VIRTUAL VILLAGERS — ISOLA GRAND EVENT COLLECTION, V18
AUTHORSHIP, CONCEPT & REVISION CREDITS

Original user-author and creative director: Lorsieab2
Collaborating writer and expansion author: ChatGPT (OpenAI)

CREDIT POLICY
* "Original concept" identifies who first supplied the premise, mechanism,
  character direction, or material for an event; it does NOT necessarily
  mean that person drafted the prose.
* "Original event text" identifies who wrote the earliest event manuscript.
* "Additional writing" identifies new dialogue, branches, outcomes and
  event text written by the named contributor.
* "Substantial revision/expansion" identifies a major later rewrite or
  extension, without transferring ownership of the original work.
* "Creative direction" credits requested themes, constraints, and outcomes.
* Editorial formatting and small grammatical fixes do not count as
  original authorship or a substantial rewrite.
* Uncertain provenance is not silently attributed to either contributor.

USER-ORIGINATED EVENT TEXT — Lorsieab2
* The Cooking Contest (VV2): original premise, original event manuscript,
  randomized cook-off framework and extensive outcome banks by Lorsieab2.
  Later additional branches, repairs and expansion text: ChatGPT.
* The Child of Legend (VV1): original premise and original event manuscript
  by Lorsieab2. Later editing and/or expansion where present: ChatGPT.
* The Meaning of Faith (VV5): original premise and original event manuscript
  by Lorsieab2. Later editing and/or expansion where present: ChatGPT.
* The Aspiring Adventurer (VV1–VV5): original event and revised event text
  supplied by Lorsieab2; the separately supplied revised version is the
  source of record. Do not reassign its writing to ChatGPT.
* The Cave Explorers (VV1), The Climb (VV2), The Troubling Signs (VV3),
  Diving for Treasure (VV3), The Mysterious Mask (VV4), The Playful
  Dolphins (VV4), The Remembrance Ceremony (all five), and The Rumblings
  (all five): original event texts and mechanics supplied by Lorsieab2
  in the separate "More Island Events" manuscript. They are NOT
  automatically claimed as ChatGPT-authored just because referenced here.

CHATGPT-WRITTEN EVENTS — WITH LORSIEAB2 CREATIVE DIRECTION
* Universal events 1–5 (Grand Isolan Scholasticon; Great Beautification
  Project; Doctors' Dilemma; Everybody's a Winner?; Great Isolan
  Storytelling Contest): event prose and branching written by ChatGPT,
  developed in response to Lorsieab2's requested scope and themes.
* VV5 woodworking showcase, potluck dispute, laundry misunderstanding,
  neighborly games, keeper of little things and festival events:
  event prose/branches by ChatGPT; overarching VV5 two-community setup,
  Guardians of Harmony naming and gameplay constraints by Lorsieab2.
* V6–V8 additional VV1–VV5 event prose, variants and codas:
  ChatGPT, with Lorsieab2's canon guidance, character preferences,
  requests for emotional complexity and patcher constraints.
* V9 "Age of Terrible Ideas": event prose, material applications,
  trial and consequence branches by ChatGPT. Core requests for intense
  conflict, fights, false guilt, courts, imprisonment, reconciliation,
  and the sixteen specific bizarre materials by Lorsieab2.
* V10 reputation, appointment, school and rivalry events: event prose
  and detailed branching by ChatGPT. Requested social outcomes and
  leadership roles, including ostracism, laughingstock, village idiot,
  mad scientist, trusted advisor, teacher and heads of professions:
  Lorsieab2. VV3–VV5-only Magic/Nature faction rule: Lorsieab2.
* V11 The Secret of Life: central concept of a life-obsessed researcher
  resembling the classic overreaching creator archetype, without outside
  character references, by Lorsieab2. Full Isola-specific story text,
  branching and additional outcomes by ChatGPT.
* V11 The Council of Impossible Ideas and The Department of Terrible
  Ideas: central direction to write wildly implausible, seemingly
  useless, laughable and occasionally successful inventions by
  Lorsieab2. Specific new invention examples, dialogue, event prose,
  branches and outcomes developed by ChatGPT unless previously
  supplied by Lorsieab2 (including the snail-shell and other
  material prompts).

SIGNIFICANT COLLABORATIVE DEVELOPMENT
Lorsieab2 originated the ongoing collection's worldbuilding requirements,
selection of games, preferred emotional and comedic range, faction and
protected-character constraints, and numerous event premises and outcome
mechanics. ChatGPT significantly expanded the event collection through
new story prose, branches, randomized dialogue, reputation systems,
continuity notes and integration guidance. These are distinct credits:
"Concept / creative direction: Lorsieab2" and "Event writing and
substantial expansion: ChatGPT" are both appropriate for many events.

ATTRIBUTION LIMITS
This credit ledger describes contributions identifiable from the user-
supplied event manuscripts and the cumulative v11 collection. It does
not claim that every paragraph has been compared against every historical
revision. Where a specific sentence's provenance is uncertain, the
original manuscript author retains the original-text credit, and
ChatGPT receives credit only for identifiable additions or substantial
expansions. The separately uploaded "More Island Events" manuscript
and "Aspiring Adventurer" manuscript remain Lorsieab2-authored source
materials; they are not silently incorporated as ChatGPT writing.

Then one of these endings (A–I). Keep the contestant identities and the chosen dish consistent throughout:

A. After only a short deliberation, there is a clear answer. (4)

D. Missing a required ingredient:

(Name) (looks under the table/rummages through the empty basket/stares at the dish in horror/insists that it must be in there somewhere/checks the trash bin). Apparently, (he/she) (forgot to include it/left it on the workstation/used the wrong ingredient/was so caught up in cooking that (he/she) never noticed/just totally forgot about it/thought (he/she) already used it). The judges (deduct points/order a quick correction/disqualify (him/her) from this round). (If points are deducted, return to A or B; if disqualified, award the other competitor the win via 4. A correction must be possible within the time limit.)

E. Sabotage!:
Just as the judges prepare to taste the dishes, someone discovers that (name1 or name2) has been tampering with (other name)'s food! (He/she) deliberately added (a disgusting lump of slime/a handful of bitter weeds/something from the latrine/an ingredient known to make villagers sick/a random pile of dirt/grass/sand/a forbidden herb) to the rival dish. (Other name) is (furious/horrified/utterly speechless/ready to start a fight). (The judges stop anyone from eating the tampered dish.) (10)

F. Sabotage gone strangely right:
(Name1 or name2) tries to sabotage (other name)'s dish by secretly adding (a strange spice/a handful of herbs/a sour fruit/an outrageously pungent ingredient/a disgusting lump of slime/a handful of bitter weeds/something from the latrine/an ingredient known to make villagers sick/a random pile of dirt/grass/sand/a forbidden herb). But when the judges taste it, something incredible happens. The dish is (even more delicious than before/perfectly balanced/unexpectedly refreshing/the best thing they've tasted all day)! The would-be saboteur (turns pale/stares in disbelief/tries not to scream/looks like (he/she) might faint). (11)

(12) Before punishing anyone, the judges ask the healers to investigate what happened. (13)

(13) Investigation results — choose one:
- The cook accidentally used contaminated water. A cracked water vessel had been left near the latrines, and no one thought to check it. The healers order the water thrown out, the vessels cleaned, and the contestants retrained in food safety. (14)
- The judges were already feeling ill that day. A few had sensitive stomachs, and one had eaten far too much before the contest. The food was innocent! (15)
- The ingredients had spoiled in the heat while everyone was arguing over the rules. Neither competitor realized anything was wrong until it was too late. (14)
- An unwashed utensil had been used for raw fish/meat/vegetables/shellfish and then for ready-to-eat food. The healers discover the mistake and explain why cleanliness matters. (14)
- The food had been kept out for too long before judging, and the warm weather did the rest. (14)
- A peculiar Isolan mushroom was mistaken for an ordinary edible one. It was not deadly, but it certainly did not agree with the judges! (14)
- The judges accidentally sampled a healer's extremely bitter/potent medicinal concoction instead of the sauce. No wonder their stomachs rebelled! (15)
- A mischievous child had swapped the judges' drinking water for an experimental coconut-and-herb potion. The child is promptly sent back to class. (15)
- A tiny frog had taken up residence inside a serving vessel. The horrified judges decide that perhaps they have seen enough cooking for one day. (15)
- The healers find convincing evidence that someone deliberately contaminated the dish. The judges confront the culprit. (10; only use when deliberate tampering is established)

(14) An honest mistake:
(Name) is mortified. (He/she) doesn't know what went wrong and is horrified that (his/her) cooking made people sick. (He/she) (apologizes over and over/buries (his/her) face in (his/her) hands/insists (he/she) would never hurt anyone/asks the healers how to prevent this from happening again). The judges (give (him/her) a stern warning/demand a new cooking lesson/decide to judge the other dish instead/cancel the contest). (If the other competitor wins, go to 4; if canceled, go to 8. Do not label an accidental mistake sabotage.)

(15) The cook is cleared:
(Name) is (relieved/utterly bewildered/annoyed that (his/her) cooking was blamed/so happy that (he/she) nearly cries). The judges apologize for jumping to conclusions and (resume judging once everyone recovers/declare the other dish the winner/postpone the contest/call off the competition). (If resumed, return to A or B; if other dish wins, go to 4; otherwise go to 8.)

(10) Deliberate sabotage confirmed:
The judges (disqualify the saboteur/order the contaminated dish destroyed/demand an explanation/ban the culprit from competing again). (Name) (R, excluding any attempt to stop the tasting of a dish already destroyed). (Other name) (Q, where the reaction makes sense). (The innocent competitor may be declared winner via 4, or the contest may be canceled via 8.) A punishment is only imposed when the sabotage is actually established.

(11) The accidental improvement:
The judges (praise the improved dish/declare it the winner by a hair/ask what on earth the secret ingredient was). The saboteur (confesses in embarrassment/tries to pretend nothing happened/is exposed by an observant judge). If the sabotage is discovered, (10); otherwise, the improved dish may win through (4), with (other name) credited as the cook. Do not reward the saboteur as though (he/she) cooked the rival's dish.

ADDITIONAL COOKING CONTEST STORYLINES — THE ACCUSATION, CONFESSION, SELF-SABOTAGE, AND RIVALRY
(These are new alternative branches. Keep all original text, banks, unsuitable containers, and existing endings above.)

G. The saboteur was framed!
Just as the judges prepare to announce their decision, someone points an accusing finger at (name1 or name2). Apparently, (he/she) was seen (lurking around the other workstation/holding a suspicious-looking bottle/arguing with (other name)/near the missing ingredients). (Name) is (furious/utterly bewildered/so shocked that (he/she) can barely speak/trying (his/her) hardest not to cry). "I didn't do it!" (he/she) insists. But who would believe that now? (16)

H. The saboteur admits it... and apologizes.
(Name1 or name2) suddenly steps forward, looking (ashamed/nervous/absolutely miserable/like (he/she) might faint). "It was me," (he/she) admits. (He/she) tampered with (other name)'s dish by adding (a disgusting lump of slime/a handful of bitter weeds/a suspicious herb/a pile of dirt/a wildly inappropriate seasoning). "I wanted to win so badly. I didn't think about what I was doing. I'm sorry." (Other name) (stares in disbelief/looks ready to explode/says nothing for a long time/asks why (he/she) would do such a thing). (17)

I. The competitor sabotages (his/her) OWN dish!
To everyone's astonishment, (name1 or name2) has deliberately ruined (his/her) own creation! (He/she) (pours something foul into the sauce/burns the dish on purpose/throws away the required basket ingredient/loads the food with a revolting amount of salt/mixes in a bitter herb at the last moment). (Other name) cannot believe (his/her) eyes. Why would anyone do that?! (18)

(16) THE FALSE ACCUSATION — investigate before judging:
The judges summon (the healers/the Chief/a few witnesses/the contest organizers) to find out what really happened. After a rather tense investigation, they discover that... (19)

(19) THE INVESTIGATION REVEALS — choose one:
- (Name) was only near the workstation because (he/she) was (returning a borrowed utensil/helping clean up/looking for (his/her) own missing basket ingredient). (He/she) is completely innocent! (20)
- Someone had planted the suspicious bottle among (name)'s belongings. An observant child saw the real culprit slip away! (21)
- The supposed evidence was actually (a bottle of harmless seasoning/a medicinal salve/a container of clean water/a particularly revolting-looking but perfectly edible sauce). The judges are rather embarrassed. (20)
- (Other name) accused (name) out of jealousy, but the investigation finds no evidence of tampering. (Other name) (admits the accusation was made in anger/refuses to apologize/looks utterly ashamed). (20)
- A third villager was responsible for the tampering and tried to make (name) take the blame. (21)
- The suspicious marks on the dish were left by (a curious child/a wandering animal/an overenthusiastic judge sampling the food too early). No sabotage occurred at all! (20)

(20) THE ACCUSED IS CLEARED:
(Name) is (relieved/angry that anyone believed the accusation/close to tears/so happy that (he/she) nearly hugs the judges). The judges (apologize publicly/give the accuser a stern warning/declare that the contest must go on). (Other name) (offers an apology/avoids (his/her) eyes/congratulates (him/her) for standing (his/her) ground/quietly wonders what will happen next). The competition (resumes judging — A or B/is postponed — 8/ends with both competitors clearing the air — 22). Do not punish or disqualify the falsely accused villager.

(21) THE REAL CULPRIT:
The real saboteur is (an envious former contestant/a disgruntled assistant/a mischievous villager/a jealous admirer of one of the chefs). (He/she) (confesses when confronted/tries to run away/insists it was just a joke/bursts into tears). The judges clear (name) of wrongdoing and (remove the culprit from the contest area/demand an apology/ask the Chief to decide on a fair punishment). If the food is safe, judging resumes (A or B); otherwise the judges (replace the dish and hold a fresh round — 23/cancel the contest — 8). No innocent competitor is penalized.

(17) THE APOLOGY — choose one:
- (Name) apologizes over and over. (Other name) is still furious, but eventually agrees to hear (him/her) out. The judges disqualify the saboteur and declare the innocent competitor the winner (4). An apology doesn't erase what happened, but perhaps it's a start.
- (Name) offers to help (other name) prepare a new dish, without taking any credit. The judges (allow a supervised rematch — 23/disqualify (name) and award the other competitor the win — 4). (Other name) (reluctantly accepts the apology/refuses to forgive (him/her) just yet/says that trust will take time to rebuild).
- (Name) admits that (he/she) was jealous of (other name)'s talent. (Other name) (is touched by the honesty/rolls (his/her) eyes/says, "You could have just asked for help!"). The judges still impose a penalty. (They award the innocent competitor the victory — 4/they cancel the contest — 8.)
- (Name) confesses only after the judges discover the tampering. The apology seems (sincere/a little too convenient/utterly desperate). The judges (disqualify (him/her) — 4/order a fresh, closely supervised round — 23). Forgiveness and the contest's rules are two different things!
NOTE: If the tampering involved a harmful or contaminated ingredient, destroy the dish and do not permit tasting. Never route a confirmed saboteur to the innocent-winner role.

(18) WHY RUIN YOUR OWN DISH? — choose one:
- (Name) was terrified of losing in front of everyone. If the dish failed on purpose, (he/she) could pretend (he/she) never really tried. Unfortunately, the judges notice the deliberate mistake. (24)
- (Name) secretly wanted (other name) to win. (He/she) (admired the rival's hard work/owed (him/her) a favor/wanted to make amends for an earlier argument). But (other name) is (touched/insulted/furious) to learn that the victory might be handed over rather than earned. (24)
- (Name) wanted to prove that (he/she) could make even the most disgusting dish taste good. Unfortunately, the experiment (fails spectacularly/somehow succeeds against all expectations). (25)
- (Name) thought the judges were being unfair and decided to make a point. The resulting dish is (utterly inedible/an outrageous mess/strangely beautiful but impossible to swallow). (24)
- (Name) was trying to hide a cooking mistake and only made things worse. Rather than admit it, (he/she) deliberately ruined the rest of the dish. (24)
- (Name) wanted to test whether the judges were paying attention. They were. Oh, they certainly were! (24)

(24) SELF-SABOTAGE DISCOVERED:
The judges (are furious/are baffled/demand an explanation/are more disappointed than angry). (Name) (confesses the truth/tries to laugh it off/looks down in shame/argues that it was (his/her) own dish to ruin). (Other name) (demands a fair contest/asks for a proper rematch/quietly accepts the victory/feels cheated out of a real challenge). The judges (award (other name) the win — 4/offer a supervised rematch — 23/call off the contest — 8). If the ruined food is unsafe, nobody tastes it.

(25) THE IMPOSSIBLE EXPERIMENT:
The judges cautiously inspect the dish. If it is safe to eat, they take a tiny bite and (spit it out immediately/declare it surprisingly delicious/argue for hours about whether it counts as food). (Name) (grins proudly/wishes the ground would swallow (him/her)/claims this was all part of the plan). The judges (score the dish fairly — A or B/order a new round — 23/disqualify the cook for breaking the rules — 4, with other name winning). If it is unsafe, do not taste it; use (24).

(22) AN UNLIKELY RECONCILIATION:
(Name1) and (name2) (exchange an awkward handshake/apologize for their accusations/agree to put the whole mess behind them/decide to settle things by cooking together). The villagers (cheer/laugh in relief/wonder whether the peace will last). The judges (resume the contest — A or B/award a joint victory — 3). Perhaps there is hope for these two yet!

(23) A FRESH, SUPERVISED ROUND:
The judges clear the workstations, replace the ingredients, and make sure no one can interfere this time. (Name1) and (name2) have one last chance to cook something worthy of the title. They (exchange fierce looks/quietly wish each other luck/argue about who gets the better utensils). Proceed to A or B, but do not start another tie-breaker or repeat a sabotage investigation indefinitely. If a tie remains, choose a winner or joint victory.

(26) BITTER RIVALS — optional aftermath following a fair win, joint win, or unresolved cancellation:
At first, (name1) and (name2) seem ready to accept the judges' decision. Then (name1) (makes a smug remark/claims the judges have terrible taste/boasts about (his/her) superior technique), and (name2) (fires back with an insult/rolls (his/her) eyes/challenges (him/her) to cook something better/accuses (him/her) of getting lucky). Before long, they are (arguing at the top of their lungs/refusing to speak to one another/trying to outdo each other at every meal/dragging their friends into the argument). It looks like Isola has gained two bitter culinary rivals! (27)

(27) THE RIVALRY CONTINUES — choose one:
- The two vow to defeat each other at next year's Cooking Contest. Until then, they (practice in secret/refuse to share recipes/keep comparing their dishes at every feast). The rivalry is far from over!
- They open competing food stalls and spend their days trying to lure away each other's customers. The villagers are (delighted by the variety/exhausted by the arguing/secretly enjoying the free samples).
- They challenge each other to an ultimate culinary showdown. No more excuses, no more second chances! (28)
- After weeks of bitter competition, they discover that they actually enjoy pushing each other to improve. Neither will admit it, of course. Perhaps they're rivals... and something like friends.
- The Chief orders them to stop fighting and cook a meal together. They (begrudgingly cooperate/argue over every ingredient/produce the best feast the village has ever tasted). They still can't stand each other, but at least everyone gets dinner!

(28) THE ULTIMATE SHOWDOWN:
Word spreads across Isola: (name1) and (name2) are going head-to-head in the ultimate culinary showdown! The villagers gather (around the cooking fire/in front of the great hut/near the feast table), while the judges prepare (three brutal rounds/a contest of speed, skill, and creativity/a feast fit for the Chief). This time, neither competitor intends to back down.

ROUND ONE — THE IMPOSSIBLE INGREDIENT:
Each competitor must prepare something using (a basket of mismatched ingredients/an ingredient neither has cooked before/a bizarre collection of roots, fruit, and fish/the same single ingredient in three different ways). (Name1) (works with frightening speed/keeps muttering a recipe under (his/her) breath/looks utterly confident), while (name2) (improvises wildly/takes a painstakingly careful approach/tries to distract (name1) with a smug grin). The judges (award the first round to (name1)/award the first round to (name2)/declare the first round a tie).

ROUND TWO — THE DISASTER ROUND:
Just as the cooking reaches its peak, (the fire goes out/a pot boils over/a sudden gust sends herbs flying/a serving vessel collapses/a curious child runs off with a key ingredient). (Name1) (recovers brilliantly/loses precious time/starts shouting instructions), while (name2) (turns the disaster into an advantage/struggles to save the dish/helps (name1) despite their rivalry). The judges (give the second round to (name1)/give the second round to (name2)/declare another tie).

FINAL ROUND — THEIR MAGNUM OPUS:
For the final round, the competitors may cook anything they want, provided it is safe to serve. (Name1) prepares (a breathtaking masterpiece/an outrageous pile of edible nonsense/a dish inspired by (his/her) childhood/a humble family recipe), while (name2) presents (a feast of impossible flavors/a deceptively simple dish/an edible work of art/a recipe no one has ever seen before). The judges take their first bites. Everyone holds their breath. (29)

(29) ULTIMATE SHOWDOWN ENDINGS — choose one, keeping the round results consistent:
- (Name1 or name2) wins by the narrowest margin imaginable! (Other name) (demands a rematch/congratulates (him/her) through gritted teeth/stands in stunned silence). The winner is crowned the ultimate Culinary Champion of Isola! (Award the established contest food and Farming reward to the actual winner.)
- The judges declare a perfect tie. Again?! (Name1) and (name2) (burst out laughing/argue that a tie is impossible/stare at each other in utter disbelief). The judges declare them joint champions. (Use the established joint-win rewards, if supported.)
- Both dishes are spectacular, but the judges cannot agree on which is better. Rather than argue all night, they invite the entire village to taste them. The villagers (choose (name1)/choose (name2)/declare both dishes equally wonderful). Award the matching single or joint victory.
- The competitors become so obsessed with winning that they forget to enjoy cooking. When the judges ask them to explain their dishes, neither has anything nice to say. The Chief (cancels the showdown/orders them to prepare a feast together/tells them to come back when they've learned some humility). No champion is declared unless a fair final judging occurs.
- The dishes are both so outrageously terrible that the judges (refuse to take another bite/ask whether this was a practical joke/declare the entire showdown a culinary catastrophe). The crowd erupts into (laughter/boos/cheers). Neither competitor wins. Better luck next time!
- (Name1) and (name2) realize that their best ideas came from watching and challenging each other. They combine their final dishes into one extraordinary feast. The judges (declare them joint champions/award a special prize for teamwork). Neither will ever admit the other helped, of course!

IMPLEMENTATION / CONTINUITY NOTES (not displayed in-game):
- Add G, H, and I to the initial A–F ending selection as optional alternatives. G is an allegation, not proven guilt. H is a voluntary confession; I is self-sabotage. These are distinct from existing E/F sabotage.
- Existing E and (10) may optionally use the apology outcomes in (17) when guilt is established. Existing F and (11) must keep credit for the improved dish with its original cook.
- (26) is an optional aftermath for outcomes (3), (4), or (8) when both competitors remain present. It must not overwrite the original food/skill rewards or cause a second award. If the contest ends without a fair result, do not invent a winner.
- For (28), store the winner/tie of each round if implementing cumulative scoring; choose a final ending consistent with those results, or explicitly let the final round determine the overall champion. Do not declare a winner who lost under the selected scoring rules.
- All unsafe tampered dishes must be withheld from tasting. Accusations require evidence; an apology does not automatically erase a penalty; an innocent competitor is never punished as a saboteur.
- A self-sabotaging competitor may be disqualified for rule-breaking, but must not be described as contaminating the rival's dish.
- Use distinct contestant names and stable pronouns. A fresh round cannot loop back into another unlimited fresh round.

New Believers -

The Meaning of "Faith"

Young (Child name) is having trouble learning (his/her) lessons. Today, (he/she) learned about the two factions that shaped Isola's history. "Like two friends, Magic and Nature share the island together," (teacher name) said. But long ago, they got into a terrible fight. They fought and fought for a long time, and their conflict almost cost them their lives. It took a lot of faith for them to rebuild their friendship. Yes, children, faith is important to life. For without faith, one cannot truly thrive."

(Name) has only two days to write an essay about what "faith" means to (him/her). But (he/she) doesn't really understand the topic. What should (name) write about?

"Faith" means perseverance in adversity:
Yes, "faith" must mean overcoming any problems (with a kind and patient heart/with maturity/with quiet courage/with strength). (Name) scribbles down (his/her) thoughts and submits (his/her) work (4). (teacher) is surprised at the amount of detail (he/she) included. (2) or (5) [the special conversation is a rare alternative, not the default]

"Faith" means sticking to what's right:
Yes, "faith" must mean fighting for (what is correct/what you believe in), even if (the world is against you/people don't always understand your perspective). (Name) scribbles down (his/her) thoughts and submits (his/her) work (4). (teacher) is surprised at the amount of detail (he/she) included. (2) or (5)

"Faith" means believing in the good of others:
Yes, "faith" must mean thinking (the best of/highly of) others, (especially if they were former friends/because people have an innate goodness to them/because people might have made a mistake/because people are not as different as one thinks). (Name) scribbles down (his/her) thoughts and submits (his/her) work (4). (teacher) is surprised at the amount of detail (he/she) included. (2) or (5)

"Faith" means believing in a higher power:
Yes, "faith" must mean that (there is something supernatural in control of one's life/there is something natural guiding one's life/there is something magical influencing the world/there are spirits watching over Isola/there is a god/there are gods/there is a guiding hand/there is something or someone watching over us). Or perhaps (the Chief/the leaders/the teachers/(his/her) parents/(his/her) elders/people themselves) have more control over one's life than one thinks. Or maybe (nothing is in control/nothing apparent is guiding our lives/one must take charge of one's own destiny)! Whatever the case, (name) believes that people must (respect the world around them/respect other people's beliefs/take charge of their own destiny/listen to those who guide them/believe what they want to believe/think for themselves). (Randomize these as COMPLETE, grammatically compatible alternatives; do not concatenate mutually contradictory claims into one sentence.) (Name) scribbles down (his/her) thoughts and submits (his/her) work (4). (teacher) is  surprised at the amount of detail (he/she) included. (2) or (5)

"Faith" depends on one's individual beliefs:
(Name) remains stumped by the cryptic question. There are a lot of things "faith" could refer to and (he/she) can't decide. Well, except for one thing: if (he/she) can't decide on a single thing, perhaps it means that "faith" means something different to different people. (He/she) decides to write (his/her) thoughts down and turns in the paper (4). (teacher) is surprised at the amount of detail (he/she) included and at (his/her) (cheekiness/utter defiance/refusal to answer the question/cleverness/intelligence/ability to think outside of the box). (2) or (5)

Question the assignment itself:
(Name) remains stumped by the cryptic question. There's a lot of things "faith" could refer to and (he/she) can't decide. Well, except for one thing: the question itself must have no right answer. Why ask it in such a vague way, then? (He/she) decides to write (his/her) thoughts down and turns in the paper (4). (teacher) is surprised at the amount of detail (he/she) included and at (his/her) (cheekiness/utter defiance/refusal to answer the question/cleverness/intelligence/ability to think outside of the box). (2) or (5)

(2) After some time, (name) gets (his/her) paper back. (3)

(3) 
(he/she) gets points for creativity/completeness/grammar/spelling/rhetoric/use of pathos/ethos/logos/vocabulary/literary analysis/phonics but not the full marks. Oh well, (name) decides to ask (teacher) later for some clarification and pointers.

(he/she) gets full marks and an Astounding Student award. (teacher) is impressed by (name)'s creativity/completeness/grammar/spelling/rhetoric/use of pathos/ethos/logos/vocabulary/literary analysis/phonics and writes a note of praise on their work.

(he/she) gets a "Try Harder" note on (his/her) work. It seems that (name) (has a lot more to learn/needs better spelling/needs to learn how to argue (his/her) points better/should alter the flow of (his/her) paper better/needs to improve (his/her) creativity/needs to improve (his/her) completeness/needs to improve (his/her) grammar/needs to improve (his/her) rhetoric/needs to improve (his/her) use of pathos, ethos, or logos/needs to improve (his/her) vocabulary/needs to improve (his/her) literary analysis/needs to work on phonics). EDITORIAL / BRANCH NOTES (not displayed in-game):
- All original option banks are retained unless a broken fragment required a grammatical alternative. Preserve the intentionally absurd containers and recipes.
- Cooking Contest: A/B/C/D/E/F are distinct initial outcomes. (5) is the joint-winner reaction bank; (9) is illness recovery; (10)-(15) are investigation/sabotage branches. Avoid routing illness into the unrelated joint-win (5).
- Cooking Contest: a required basket ingredient must be selected before cooking, and omissions should be detected before declaring a winner.
- Cooking Contest: use (6) only if a post-investigation judging decision is required; avoid punishing an innocent cook for an unrelated illness.
- Golden Child: the new classroom endings are alternatives to the original three; do not imply he must speak or be universally accepted.
- Meaning of Faith: the teacher may be imperfect, and no philosophical choice is objectively the wrong one. Keep the rare (5)/(6) outcome available to all six choices.

================================================================================
NEW MATERIAL — COOKING CONTEST: EXTRA DRAMA AND AFTERMATH
================================================================================

These are ADDITIONAL optional branches for the existing VV2 Cooking Contest.
They do not replace the existing story, contestant descriptions, recipe banks,
sabotage investigation, or endings. The initial contest may select one of these
after a suspicious incident, a joint win, a disputed result, or an ordinary win.
All names and pronouns must remain tied to the same competitors throughout.

(16) THE SABOTEUR WAS FRAMED!
The judges are just about to announce the winner when (a frightened apprentice/
an outraged spectator/a suspicious healer/a very nosy child) bursts into the
judging area.

"Wait! (Accused name) didn't do it!"

Everyone turns to look at (accused name), who (nearly drops (his/her) plate/
looks as though (he/she) might faint/folds (his/her) arms triumphantly/
stares in utter disbelief). The evidence against (him/her) seemed so convincing!
There was (a suspicious herb in (his/her) basket/a witness who claimed to have
seen (him/her) near the rival's dish/a strange stain on (his/her) apron/
a note supposedly written in (his/her) handwriting).

But apparently, (17).

(17) THE INVESTIGATION REVEALS...
- The evidence was planted by (a jealous former contestant/a disgruntled judge/
  a spectator who wanted to stir up trouble). The real culprit is confronted.
  (18)
- The supposed witness had confused (accused name) with another villager
  wearing the same outfit. Oops! (19)
- A mischievous child had been playing with the ingredients and accidentally
  created the entire misunderstanding. The child is (sent back to school/
  made to clean the workstations/given a stern lecture about telling the truth).
  (19)
- A rival competitor deliberately framed (accused name) to eliminate the
  strongest challenger. (20)
- The suspicious powder was actually (flour/pounded coconut/sugar/ground nuts).
  Someone really ought to label the containers! (19)
- A coconut fell from a tree, struck a shelf, and knocked the incriminating
  ingredient into the wrong basket. After a long, awkward silence, the judges
  agree that the coconut is probably not a criminal mastermind. (19)
- No one can establish what happened. The judges refuse to punish anyone
  without better evidence. The damaged dish is discarded, and the contest
  (continues with fresh ingredients/is postponed/is declared inconclusive).
  (21)

(18) THE REAL CULPRIT
The real culprit (tries to run away/bursts into tears/insists that it was only
a joke/claims that the whole thing was an elaborate experiment). The judges
clear (accused name)'s reputation and apologize for accusing (him/her).
(Accused name) (accepts the apology/refuses to speak to the judges/
demands a public announcement/asks only that the competition continue).
The actual culprit is (disqualified/ordered to make restitution/
temporarily barred from future contests/referred to the village leaders).
The innocent competitors receive a fair chance to finish the contest.
(If the event ends here, no winner is declared; otherwise return to ordinary
judging with clean dishes.)

(19) AN EMBARRASSING MISTAKE
(Accused name) is cleared of wrongdoing! The judges (apologize profusely/
look extremely sheepish/pretend to examine their notes/offer (him/her) a
consolation plate of snacks). (Accused name) (forgives them immediately/
demands a rematch/asks everyone to please stop staring/laughs until
(his/her) sides hurt). The competition resumes after the dishes are checked.

(20) A RIVAL'S BETRAYAL
The truth is worse than anyone expected. (Other competitor) deliberately
framed (accused name) to get (him/her) disqualified!

(Other competitor) (admits everything/denies it until the evidence becomes
overwhelming/tried to confess earlier but lost (his/her) nerve).
(Accused name) is (furious/heartbroken/speechless/strangely calm).

The judges disqualify the guilty competitor and clear the innocent one.
Whether the two ever forgive each other is another matter entirely.
(22)

(21) NO EASY ANSWER
The village spends the rest of the day arguing about what really happened.
Some insist that (accused name) is innocent. Others remain suspicious.
A few villagers decide that the whole contest has become more trouble
than it's worth.

The judges (order a clean rematch/postpone the result/declare no winner).
Nobody receives a punishment for sabotage without proof. The villagers
may not be satisfied, but at least they haven't condemned someone on a guess.

(22) AFTER THE BETRAYAL — choose one:
- The competitors never speak again. Years later, their recipes are still
  compared by everyone on the island. (23)
- The innocent competitor accepts a sincere apology, but refuses to pretend
  that nothing happened. They remain civil, if not exactly friendly.
- The guilty competitor works for weeks to make amends. (Other name)
  eventually agrees to compete against (him/her) again—fairly this time.
- Both competitors become bitter rivals. Neither will ever admit that the
  other's food is actually rather good. (23)
- The two argue so fiercely that the judges threaten to ban them BOTH.
  They immediately agree that the judges have terrible taste. (23)

(23) BITTER RIVALS
From that day forward, (name1) and (name2) become bitter culinary rivals.
One opens (a tiny food stall/a grand eatery/a suspiciously experimental
kitchen/a bakery). The other opens a competing establishment (right across
the path/next door/within shouting distance/at the other end of the village).

Every new dish becomes a challenge. Every compliment becomes an insult.
Every festival turns into an opportunity to show off.

And yet, when one rival falls ill, the other quietly leaves a warm meal
outside (his/her) hut. Neither ever mentions it.

Or perhaps they don't reconcile at all. Some grudges last longer than
the food that started them!

(Choose either this quiet resolution or the ultimate showdown at 27.)

(24) THE SABOTEUR CONFESSES... AND APOLOGIZES
(Name) can no longer bear the guilt. Just as the judges prepare to announce
their decision, (he/she) steps forward.

"I did it," (he/she) admits. "I tampered with (other name)'s dish."

The crowd (gasps/falls silent/erupts into shouting/looks thoroughly
unimpressed). (Name) (hangs (his/her) head/starts crying/looks (other name)
in the eye/struggles to get the words out).

"I'm sorry. I wanted to win so badly that I forgot why I started cooking
in the first place."

(Other name) (25). The judges (26).

(25) THE RIVAL'S RESPONSE — choose one:
- accepts the apology, but says that trust will take time to rebuild
- refuses to forgive (him/her), at least for now
- says, "You could have just ASKED me for a rematch!"
- asks whether (name) would have confessed if nobody had noticed
- quietly admits that (he/she) once considered doing something just as foolish
- bursts out laughing. "All that trouble, and you STILL couldn't beat me?"
- asks (name) to help prepare a fresh dish so the competition can end fairly
- says nothing, then offers (name) a clean cooking utensil

(26) THE JUDGES' RESPONSE — choose one:
- disqualify the saboteur, but praise (him/her) for finally telling the truth
- require (him/her) to apologize publicly and replace the ruined ingredients
- suspend (him/her) from the next competition
- allow a supervised rematch after the damaged dish is discarded
- end the contest without a winner, deciding that fairness matters more
  than an impressive feast

An apology does not automatically erase the consequences. However, the
competitors may still reconcile—or remain rivals—after the decision.

(27) THE ULTIMATE SHOWDOWN
The arguments have gone on long enough. (Name1) and (name2) demand one
final showdown to determine who is truly the greatest chef on Isola.

The judges (reluctantly agree/are delighted by the prospect/ask whether
they can PLEASE have one peaceful afternoon). Soon, the entire village
gathers to watch.

There will be THREE rounds. No more excuses. No more second chances.
And absolutely NO tampering with anyone else's food!

ROUND ONE — THE TEST OF SKILL
The competitors must (prepare an entire meal using only five ingredients/
make a dish without using fire/reproduce a recipe after hearing it only once/
prepare a feast for the village's pickiest eaters).

(Name1) (finishes early/works carefully and methodically/forgets where
(he/she) put the salt/accidentally makes a brilliant substitution).
(Name2) (makes an elegant masterpiece/creates an alarming mountain of
mush/works with extraordinary speed/has to start over).

ROUND TWO — THE TEST OF IMPROVISATION
A surprise basket is revealed. Inside are (mushrooms, coconuts, and a very
angry crab/three roots and an unfamiliar spice/a fish, some fruit, and a
single flower/an enormous pile of perfectly ordinary vegetables).

The competitors must make something edible. Preferably.

One contestant (makes a remarkable recovery/produces something
suspiciously alive/accidentally invents a new delicacy/creates a dish
that looks like it is staring back at the judges).

ROUND THREE — THE FINAL PLATE
At last, the judges ask each competitor to cook the one dish that best
represents who (he/she) is.

(Name1) chooses (a beloved childhood meal/an ambitious original recipe/
a humble bowl of stew/a dish inspired by (his/her) rival).
(Name2) chooses (an old family recipe/an impossibly elaborate dessert/
a simple dish made with great care/a dish inspired by the island itself).

The judges taste both creations. After a long silence, (28).

(28) THE ULTIMATE RESULT — choose one:
- (Name1) wins by the narrowest margin imaginable. (Name2) is devastated,
  but shakes (his/her) rival's hand. The village gains a large food award;
  the winner gains Farming experience.
- (Name2) wins decisively. (Name1) is (furious/astonished/quietly proud of
  how far (he/she) came). The village gains a large food award; the winner
  gains Farming experience.
- Both dishes are equally magnificent. The judges declare a joint victory.
  The rivals stare at each other before (bursting out laughing/arguing
  about whose half of the trophy is bigger/agreeing to share the honor).
  Both gain Farming experience if supported.
- The judges discover that the two final dishes taste best when eaten
  TOGETHER. The rivals have accidentally invented a new Isolan feast.
  They are (delighted/horrified/unwilling to admit it). The village gains food.
- A sudden downpour ruins both dishes. The judges declare the competition
  postponed. The rivals are too exhausted to argue and spend the evening
  sharing an ordinary bowl of soup. Nobody wins, but nobody seems to mind.
- A coconut lands squarely on the judges' table, smashing both plates.
  After a very long silence, someone declares the coconut the winner.
  The Chief refuses to award it the trophy.
- Both chefs refuse to concede. The judges declare the competition a draw
  and go home. The competitors continue arguing until well after sunset.
- The judges select a winner, but the losing competitor quietly congratulates
  (him/her). No speeches. No tantrums. Just a handshake and a promise to
  cook together someday.

(29) SELF-SABOTAGE
Just before the dishes are presented, (name) does something utterly
inexplicable. (He/she) deliberately tampers with (his/her) OWN dish!

(He/she) (adds a wildly inappropriate ingredient/ruins the presentation/
pours far too much spice into the pot/removes the most important part
of the recipe). The judges are (horrified/confused/suspicious/
strangely intrigued).

When questioned, (name) explains that (30).

(30) WHY WOULD ANYONE DO THAT? — choose one:
- (he/she) was afraid of winning and having everyone expect perfection forever
- (he/she) wanted (other name) to win because the rival needed the prize more
- (he/she) believed the judges had been unfair and wanted to protest
- (he/she) was tired of competing and didn't know how to withdraw
- (he/she) wanted to prove that even an awful-looking dish could taste good
- (he/she) had a sudden burst of inspiration and genuinely thought it
  would improve the food
- (he/she) misread an ancient recipe and believed the dish needed
  "one generous handful of despair"
- (he/she) wanted to see whether the judges were paying attention

(31) THE SELF-SABOTAGE RESULT — choose one:
- The judges let (name) withdraw without shame. (Other name) wins,
  but privately asks whether everything is alright.
- The judges explain that deliberately ruining a dish violates the contest
  rules. (Name) is disqualified, even if the motive was sympathetic.
- (Other name) refuses to accept a victory that was handed over in secret.
  The competitors prepare fresh dishes and agree to a fair rematch.
- Against all reason, the altered dish is delicious. The judges are
  delighted. (Name) is even more confused than they are.
- The dish is completely inedible. The judges recommend that (name)
  take a break from competitive cooking.
- The competitors admit that they have both been miserable for weeks.
  They call off the rivalry and spend the afternoon cooking for their friends.

(32) QUIET CODA — optional after any nonfatal, resolved outcome:
- By evening, the village has returned to its usual routine. Someone
  washes the dishes. Someone sweeps the judging area. The contestants
  go home, a little older and perhaps a little wiser.
- (Name1) and (name2) agree to disagree. They still argue about seasoning,
  but at least they can sit at the same table.
- The judges revise the contest rules. The next year's competition is
  remarkably uneventful. Everyone considers this a great success.
- A child asks whether all the fuss was really necessary. Nobody has
  a particularly good answer.
- The village enjoys a wonderful feast made from the leftover ingredients.
  For once, nobody cares who cooked what.

CONTINUITY NOTES:
- Route (16) only from an accusation with uncertain or falsified evidence.
  Never simultaneously establish the accused as guilty.
- Route (24) from confirmed intentional sabotage by the confessing cook.
- Route (29) only for a competitor's own dish, never the rival's dish.
- Route (27) only when both contestants are able and willing to compete.
- A contaminated dish must not be tasted after its hazard is identified.
- A contestant cannot win a prize for another contestant's cooking.
- The above numbered sections are an extension of the existing Cooking
  Contest; they are not replacements for the earlier (1)–(15) sections.

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL EVENT 1 — THE GRAND ISOLAN SCHOLASTICON
Research competition — VV1, VV2, VV3, VV4, VV5
================================================================================

The village's researchers have been arguing for days. Apparently, there
is a problem so complicated that even the most learned villagers cannot
agree on the answer.

Some believe it is a matter of mathematics. Others insist that it is
a problem of engineering. One particularly stubborn researcher believes
that the entire problem is nonsense.

At last, (researcher1) and (researcher2) agree to settle the matter
with a competition. The Chief has offered (a generous prize/a handsome
trophy/a lifetime supply of writing materials/a rather impressive coconut)
to whoever can find the best solution.

The problem is... (A).

A — THE GREAT PROBLEM (choose one):
- determining how to build a bridge across a ravine without wasting wood
- calculating the height of an ancient monument without climbing it
- designing a stable tower with an absurdly small foundation
- determining how much food the village needs to survive a long drought
- working out the safest arrangement of supports for a new building
- dividing an irregular plot of farmland fairly between several families
- predicting when the shadows of two distant monuments will meet
- finding a way to move an enormous stone using only simple tools
- determining whether an oddly shaped vessel can hold enough water
- proving that two apparently different geometric designs have equal area
- discovering why the village's measuring sticks never seem to agree
- solving an ancient puzzle carved into the ruins, which may or may not
  have been intended as a puzzle in the first place

(Researcher1) is (B), while (researcher2) is (B, different).
They have until sundown to present their work.

B — RESEARCHER APPROACH:
- methodical and painfully thorough
- brilliant, but rather absentminded
- fond of drawing diagrams on every available surface
- suspicious of any answer that seems too simple
- convinced that the ancestors already solved the problem
- determined to prove (his/her) rival wrong
- willing to admit when (he/she) doesn't know something
- prone to making wild guesses and then testing them
- obsessed with precision
- remarkably good at spotting patterns
- better at practical demonstrations than explanations
- more interested in the problem than in winning

What should the village encourage?

CHOICE: LET THEM WORK INDEPENDENTLY
The two researchers separate their materials and get to work.
(Researcher1) (fills several pages with calculations/builds a miniature
model/draws a complicated diagram/stares at the problem for hours).
Meanwhile, (researcher2) (tries a completely different method/
consults old records/makes measurements in the field/starts over twice).
Go to (1).

CHOICE: REQUIRE A PUBLIC DEMONSTRATION
The Chief declares that a solution is only useful if the village can
understand how it works. The researchers must explain their reasoning
and demonstrate their ideas before the judges.
One researcher looks delighted. The other looks as though (he/she)
would rather face a swarm of bees. Go to (2).

CHOICE: LET THEM COLLABORATE
Perhaps the researchers are wasting time trying to defeat each other.
The Chief suggests that they work together instead.
(Researcher1) (agrees immediately/hesitates/looks offended).
(Researcher2) (smiles/grumbles/asks whether the prize will be shared).
Go to (3).

(1) INDEPENDENT RESULTS — choose one:
- Both researchers arrive at the same correct answer by entirely
  different methods. They argue over whose method is more elegant,
  then reluctantly admit that both are useful. (4)
- (Researcher1) produces a brilliant solution but cannot explain it
  clearly. (Researcher2) gives a less impressive answer with a wonderful
  explanation. The judges must decide what matters most. (5)
- One researcher discovers a tiny error in the other's calculations.
  The embarrassed competitor corrects it, then finds a flaw in the
  first researcher's model. Neither gets to feel superior for long. (4)
- Both researchers confidently present the WRONG answer. A child
  points out that they have forgotten to account for the weight of
  the bridge itself. The researchers stare at each other in horror. (6)
- The researchers solve different parts of the problem. Neither can
  finish alone, but their combined work provides the answer. (3)
- Both researchers run out of time. The Chief awards no prize, but
  asks them to continue their work tomorrow. (7)

(2) PUBLIC DEMONSTRATION — choose one:
- The model works perfectly! The audience applauds. The researchers
  are (overjoyed/relieved/astonished that nobody noticed the glue).
  (4)
- The model collapses before anyone touches it. A very awkward silence
  follows. The researchers agree to investigate what went wrong. (7)
- The explanation is so complicated that the judges fall asleep.
  The design is sound, but someone must translate it into language
  ordinary villagers can understand. (5)
- A spectator asks a simple question that neither researcher considered.
  They return to their calculations, embarrassed but grateful. (7)
- The demonstration works, but only because a child has been holding
  up the model from behind the table. The judges demand a fair retest.
  (7)
- The researchers discover that their supposedly ancient measurement
  was taken using a bent stick. They agree to start over. (7)

(3) COLLABORATION — choose one:
- After a few disagreements, the researchers combine their ideas into
  a design neither could have produced alone. The village gains Tech
  points; both gain Research experience.
- They spend the entire afternoon arguing about who gets to hold the
  measuring stick. No solution is reached. (7)
- (Researcher1) discovers the theory, while (researcher2) develops a
  practical method. They share the prize and celebrate quietly.
- The collaboration reveals that the problem was based on a false
  assumption. The researchers explain this to the Chief, who is
  (impressed/disappointed/secretly relieved). The village gains Tech.
- The two become close friends and continue working together.
  Their next project is already causing arguments.

(4) THE JUDGES' DECISION — choose one:
- (Researcher1) wins for accuracy. (Researcher2) congratulates (him/her).
  The winner gains Research experience; the village gains Tech points.
- (Researcher2) wins for elegance and clarity. (Researcher1) demands
  to see the scoring rubric. The winner gains Research experience.
- The judges declare a tie. Both researchers are delighted—until they
  realize they must share the trophy.
- The judges award the prize to the child who spotted the crucial
  mistake. The researchers (laugh/applaud/look deeply embarrassed).
- No winner is declared because both solutions are useful in different
  circumstances. The village gains Tech points.

(5) THE ARGUMENT OVER WHAT COUNTS
The judges cannot agree whether the best solution should be (the most
accurate/the easiest to explain/the cheapest to build/the safest/
the most elegant/the quickest to carry out).

After a long discussion, they (publish a set of standards for future
competitions/ask the villagers to vote/award a joint victory/
postpone the decision until the designs are tested).

(Researcher1) and (researcher2) learn that solving a problem is not
always the same as deciding which solution is best.

(6) THE CHILD'S OBSERVATION
A young villager, (child name), quietly asks, "But what about (the
weight of the bridge/the missing corner/the extra support/the water
that leaks out/the shadow on the other side)?"

The researchers freeze.

Apparently, the answer was sitting right in front of them.

(Researcher1) (laughs at (himself/herself)/thanks the child/
offers to teach (him/her) more mathematics).
(Researcher2) (looks mortified/congratulates the child/
immediately begins checking every other calculation).

The child gains a little Research experience if supported.
The researchers return to work.

(7) A QUIET FAILURE
By sundown, the problem remains unsolved. Nobody has won the prize.
The researchers pack away their tools, tired and disappointed.

Still, the village now knows more about the problem than it did
yesterday. Perhaps that is progress enough for one day.

(One or both researchers gain a small amount of Research experience.)

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL EVENT 2 — THE GREAT BEAUTIFICATION PROJECT
Building competition — VV1, VV2, VV3, VV4, VV5
================================================================================

For many years, the village has been home to a monument of questionable
beauty. Some call it an important piece of Isolan history. Others call
it the ugliest thing they have ever seen.

The monument is (A).

A — THE OFFENDING STRUCTURE:
- a statue with an enormous nose and tiny little eyes
- a crooked stone figure that seems to be glaring at everyone
- a ceremonial arch that leans slightly to the left
- a building with a roof that looks like a squashed coconut
- a statue of a heroic ancestor that resembles a confused potato
- a monument with six arms, although nobody knows why
- a public fountain that has never successfully held water
- a grand staircase leading to absolutely nowhere
- a stone animal that might be a bird, a fish, or a very unhappy lizard

The Chief announces a competition to improve its appearance.
(Building contestant1) and (building contestant2) eagerly volunteer.

One wants to (restore the original design/replace the entire thing/
cover it with beautiful decorations/repair its structural flaws).
The other wants to (modernize it/make it more practical/
preserve every scratch and crack/add something truly spectacular).

Unfortunately, the villagers cannot agree on whether the monument
is ugly, charming, historically important, or all three.

What should the Chief do?

CHOICE: RESTORE IT CAREFULLY
The builders are instructed to repair the monument while preserving
its original character. They begin cleaning, measuring, and replacing
damaged stones. Go to (1).

CHOICE: GIVE THE BUILDERS CREATIVE FREEDOM
The Chief allows the builders to do whatever they think is best.
Both look delighted. The villagers begin to worry. Go to (2).

CHOICE: LET THE VILLAGERS DECIDE
The Chief calls a public meeting. Everyone is invited to propose
improvements, including those who actually LIKE the old monument.
Go to (3).

(1) RESTORATION OUTCOMES — choose one:
- The builders discover delicate carvings beneath years of dirt.
  The monument was beautiful all along! The village gains Tech.
- The repairs are completed without incident. The monument still
  looks rather strange, but it is safe and well maintained.
- A builder accidentally removes a stone that was holding up
  the statue's enormous nose. With a terrible CRACK, the nose
  falls off, rolls down the path, and comes to rest in front of
  the Chief. Nobody says a word. Go to (4).
- The builders discover that the crooked shape was intentional.
  Apparently, the monument was designed to align with a rare
  astronomical event. They abandon their plan to straighten it.
- The restoration reveals that the statue was assembled backward
  centuries ago. The villagers debate whether to correct it.
  Go to (3).

(2) CREATIVE FREEDOM — choose one:
- The builders produce a magnificent masterpiece. Even their
  harshest critics are impressed. Both gain Building experience.
- The monument is covered in so many decorations that nobody
  can tell what it used to be. The children love it.
- One builder adds a fountain. The other adds a second fountain.
  The resulting structure resembles a stone creature with a
  spectacularly runny nose.
- The builders accidentally make the monument even uglier.
  To everyone's surprise, the villagers grow rather fond of it.
- The builders argue over every detail and refuse to cooperate.
  The Chief separates their work into two competing proposals.
  Go to (5).
- The monument looks wonderful, but the new decorations block
  a frequently used village path. The builders must revise
  their design before it can be accepted.

(3) THE VILLAGE VOTES — choose one:
- The majority votes to restore the original. The builders
  obey, though some villagers are disappointed.
- The majority votes for a complete redesign. A small group
  protests that the monument belongs to their ancestors.
  The Chief pauses the work and invites them to help plan it.
- The vote ends in a perfect tie. The Chief (orders another vote/
  proposes a compromise/decides to leave the monument alone).
- A child suggests planting flowers around the monument instead.
  Everyone realizes this would be cheaper, safer, and rather pretty.
  The village gains a small Building or Farming reward.
- The villagers argue for hours. By the end, everyone is so
  exhausted that they agree to postpone the matter until next year.

(4) THE NOSE INCIDENT
The great stone nose lies in the middle of the path.

(Building contestant1) (tries to lift it/apologizes repeatedly/
insists that it was already loose). (Building contestant2)
(stares in disbelief/bursts out laughing/asks whether this
technically counts as an improvement).

The Chief (orders the nose reattached/asks the builders to
inspect the whole structure/decides the statue looks better
without it/forbids anyone from touching the monument again).

If the nose is reattached, it (fits perfectly/is slightly crooked/
falls off again the following morning).

If the nose remains off, future generations may simply assume
that the statue was always supposed to look that way.

(The builders gain a little experience if they complete a safe
repair. No reward for merely dropping a nose on the path.)

(5) THE FINAL PRESENTATIONS
(Building contestant1) unveils (a tasteful restoration/a towering
new façade/a modest but sturdy repair/an elegant stone carving).
(Building contestant2) reveals (a colorful redesign/a practical
improvement/a monument covered in tiny sculptures/a surprisingly
minimalist design).

The judges (6).

(6) JUDGING OUTCOMES — choose one:
- choose contestant1 for the better workmanship
- choose contestant2 for creativity
- declare a tie because both proposals have merit
- ask the builders to combine the best parts of their designs
- reject both proposals because neither is structurally safe
- decide that the old monument is part of the village's character
  and should remain exactly as it is

(7) QUIET ENDING
The villagers eventually return to their daily routines. Some still
think the monument is hideous. Others insist that it has never looked
better. A few have already forgotten why anyone was arguing.

But the monument remains, a reminder that beauty is not always
something everyone agrees upon.

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL EVENT 3 — THE DOCTORS' DILEMMA
Healing dilemma — VV1, VV2, VV3, VV4, VV5
================================================================================

A villager, (patient name), has been brought to the hospital in
serious condition. The healers believe that (he/she) is suffering from
(A), but they cannot agree on what to do next.

A — THE MEDICAL PROBLEM:
- a severe infection that has spread despite ordinary treatment
- an injury that threatens the use of one of (his/her) limbs
- a dangerous fever with an uncertain cause
- a complicated illness requiring a scarce medicinal ingredient
- an accident that has left (him/her) unconscious
- a condition that may worsen if treatment is delayed
- a mysterious illness that the healers have never encountered before

(Healer1) believes that the best course of action is (B).
(Healer2) disagrees, arguing that (C).

B — THE FIRST PROPOSAL:
- to attempt a risky treatment that might save the patient's life
- to use the last supply of a rare medicinal ingredient
- to transfer the patient to a distant healing site
- to continue established treatment and monitor (him/her) closely
- to isolate the patient until the cause of the illness is known
- to respect the patient's refusal of a proposed treatment

C — THE SECOND CONCERN:
- the treatment could cause serious complications
- another patient may need the medicine just as urgently
- the journey itself may be dangerous
- waiting could allow the illness to progress
- isolation may separate the patient from loved ones unnecessarily
- the patient may not have understood the consequences of refusing

To make matters worse, the village has a rule requiring (the Chief's
approval for certain treatments/the protection of scarce medicine/
isolation during suspected outbreaks/a formal decision by the healers).

The rule was intended to protect the villagers. But in this case,
following it might not be the same as doing what seems compassionate.

(If the patient is awake and able to decide, (patient name)'s informed
wishes must be established before treatment. If (he/she) cannot decide,
the healers consult an appropriate family member or representative,
while acting urgently when delay would create immediate danger.)

What should the healers do?

CHOICE: FOLLOW THE ESTABLISHED RULE
The healers follow the village's procedure, even though it may
take precious time. (Healer1) is (relieved/frustrated/anxious).
(Healer2) insists that rules exist for a reason. Go to (1).

CHOICE: SEEK AN EXCEPTION
The healers explain the situation to the Chief and request permission
to depart from the usual procedure. The Chief (listens carefully/
looks deeply troubled/asks a great many questions). Go to (2).

CHOICE: PRIORITIZE THE PATIENT'S INFORMED CHOICE
The healers explain the available treatments, their risks, and the
uncertainties. They listen to what (patient name) wants, or consult
(his/her) appropriate representative if (he/she) cannot decide.
Go to (3).

CHOICE: CALL A COUNCIL OF HEALERS
The healers gather their colleagues to review the evidence, consider
the patient's wishes, and decide what can be justified. Go to (4).

(1) FOLLOWING THE RULE — choose one:
- The approval arrives in time. The patient receives treatment.
  Go to (5).
- The rule prevents a dangerous and poorly understood treatment.
  The healers use a safer alternative. Go to (5).
- The delay causes a serious disagreement among the healers.
  By the time approval arrives, the patient's condition has changed.
  Go to (5).
- The healers discover that the rule was never intended for emergencies.
  The Chief clarifies it for future cases. Go to (5).

(2) SEEKING AN EXCEPTION — choose one:
- The Chief grants an exception after hearing the medical evidence
  and the patient's wishes. Go to (5).
- The Chief refuses, fearing that an exception would be unfair
  to other villagers. The healers seek another acceptable treatment.
  Go to (5).
- The Chief admits that (he/she) does not understand the medicine
  well enough to decide alone. The healers are asked to explain
  their reasoning before the council. Go to (4).
- The Chief authorizes emergency treatment, then orders a review
  of the village's laws so the same dilemma is less likely to recur.
  Go to (5).

(3) THE PATIENT'S VOICE — choose one:
- (Patient name) accepts the proposed treatment after asking
  several difficult questions. Go to (5).
- (Patient name) refuses the risky treatment and chooses a less
  aggressive approach. The healers respect the informed decision.
  Go to (5).
- The patient cannot communicate, but a trusted representative
  describes (his/her) previously expressed wishes. Go to (5).
- The patient asks for time to speak with loved ones. If medically
  possible, the healers give (him/her) that time. Go to (5).
- The patient says, "I don't want everyone arguing about me as
  though I'm not here." The healers fall silent and listen.
  Go to (5).

(4) THE COUNCIL — choose one:
- The healers reach a consensus after reviewing the available
  evidence. Go to (5).
- The healers disagree, but identify a reasonable compromise
  that respects the patient's wishes. Go to (5).
- One healer admits that (he/she) was mistaken about an important
  detail. The others thank (him/her) for speaking up. Go to (5).
- The discussion reveals that two patients need the same scarce
  medicine. The healers use consistent clinical criteria rather
  than favoritism to decide how it should be allocated. Go to (5).
- The council remains divided. The healers document the uncertainty
  and choose a defensible course of action. Go to (5).

(5) THE OUTCOME — select independently of the moral framing, while
remaining medically consistent with the established condition and
treatment. Do not make survival a guaranteed reward for one choice.

FULL RECOVERY:
After several difficult days, (patient name)'s condition begins
to improve. The fever subsides, (his/her) strength returns, and
the healers are finally able to breathe a sigh of relief.

(He/she) eventually makes a full recovery. The village celebrates
quietly, grateful that the treatment worked.

RECOVERY WITH LASTING DISABILITY:
(Patient name) survives, but the illness or injury has lasting
effects. (He/she) now has (reduced mobility in one limb/persistent
weakness/difficulty with certain tasks/a lasting impairment that
requires assistance).

The healers explain what they know and what remains uncertain.
The village helps (him/her) adapt, making room for (his/her) needs
without treating (him/her) as helpless.

(Patient name) (returns to familiar work in a different way/
learns a new skill/accepts help from friends/teaches others
something about patience and perseverance).

(Do not imply disability means the villager has lost dignity,
intelligence, or the ability to contribute. Apply a mechanical
limitation only if the patcher supports it coherently.)

DEATH:
Despite the healers' efforts, (patient name) grows weaker.
One evening, (he/she) dies.

The village is devastated. (Healer1) wonders whether another
decision might have changed the outcome. (Healer2) reminds
(him/her) that uncertainty cannot always be eliminated.

The villagers gather to remember (patient name). Some are
angry. Some are silent. Others simply sit together.

The healers review the case carefully—not to find someone
to blame automatically, but to understand whether anything
could have been done differently.

(Only trigger actual death through a supported game mechanic;
never narrate death while leaving the same villager alive.)

(6) AFTERMATH — choose one:
- The Chief revises the law to clarify emergency exceptions.
- The healers improve the way they explain treatments and risks.
- The village creates a fairer process for allocating scarce medicine.
- The healers remain divided, but agree to treat one another
  respectfully and review the evidence as it develops.
- (Healer1) and (healer2) quietly apologize for harsh words
  exchanged during the crisis.
- The village holds a small ceremony in honor of the patient.
- Nothing dramatic happens. The healers return to work, carrying
  what they learned into the next difficult decision.

Possible rewards: Healing experience, small Research experience,
or a modest Tech reward for improving medical procedures.
Never make the outcome depend on a simplistic "good choice = lives,
bad choice = dies" rule.

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL EVENT 4 — EVERYBODY'S A WINNER?
Parenting and children's competition — VV1 through VV5
================================================================================

The village's children have been preparing for the annual (A)
competition for weeks. Some have practiced every day. Others
only joined because their friends were participating.

A — THE CHILDREN'S CONTEST:
- storytelling
- drawing
- dancing
- singing
- solving puzzles
- making toys
- reciting poetry
- gardening
- building tiny models
- demonstrating a new skill

At last, the children gather to present their work.
The parents and teachers are delighted—until someone asks
who will receive the prize.

One parent insists that every child should be a winner.
Another argues that choosing a single winner teaches
children that effort and skill deserve recognition.

A third parent suggests canceling the entire ceremony.
Nobody pays much attention to that suggestion.

What should the adults do?

CHOICE: GIVE EVERY CHILD THE SAME PRIZE
The teacher announces that every child will receive a ribbon.
The children (cheer/look confused/whisper among themselves/
immediately begin comparing ribbons). Go to (1).

CHOICE: CHOOSE ONE WINNER
The judges carefully evaluate the entries and announce that
one child will receive the grand prize. Go to (2).

CHOICE: CREATE SEVERAL DISTINCT AWARDS
The judges decide to recognize different achievements, such as
creativity, improvement, teamwork, and technical skill.
Go to (3).

CHOICE: ASK THE CHILDREN WHAT THEY THINK
The teacher gathers the children and asks what would make the
competition feel fair to them. The answers are surprisingly varied.
Go to (4).

(1) EVERYONE WINS — choose one:
- The children are delighted. They proudly wear their ribbons
  and spend the rest of the day playing together.
- One child asks why (he/she) practiced for weeks if everyone
  was going to receive the same award. The teacher listens
  and promises clearer rules next time.
- A shy child is relieved that nobody has to feel embarrassed.
  Another child admits that (he/she) wanted to know how well
  (he/she) actually did.
- The children begin arguing over whose ribbon is shinier.
  The adults realize that equal prizes do not necessarily
  eliminate competition.
- One child proudly announces that (he/she) has won first place.
  So does every other child. The Chief has to explain that there
  cannot be thirty-seven first-place winners in a contest with
  only twelve entrants.
- The children decide to exchange ribbons with one another.
  By evening, nobody remembers which ribbon belonged to whom.

(2) ONE WINNER — choose one:
- The winner is overjoyed, while the other children applaud.
  The teacher thanks everyone for their hard work.
- A disappointed child cries. The teacher comforts (him/her)
  without pretending that losing is always pleasant.
- One parent accuses the judges of favoritism. The judges
  explain their criteria and offer to review the results.
- The winning child shares the prize with a friend. The judges
  remind everyone that the award still belongs to the winner.
- The winner admits that (he/she) doesn't enjoy competing and
  only entered to make (his/her) parents happy. The adults
  have a thoughtful conversation about expectations.
- A child who lost asks the winner for advice. The two
  begin practicing together for next year.

(3) MULTIPLE AWARDS — choose one:
- The children appreciate being recognized for different strengths.
  The ceremony ends peacefully.
- One child receives the "Most Improved" award and asks,
  "Does that mean I was the worst before?" The teacher
  hurriedly explains what the award was meant to celebrate.
- The judges invent so many categories that the ceremony
  lasts until sunset. Even the person handing out ribbons
  receives a ribbon.
- Two children disagree about whether "Most Creative"
  is better than "Best Overall." The adults admit that
  the awards need clearer descriptions.
- The children invent their own awards: "Best Friend,"
  "Funniest Drawing," and "Most Likely to Eat the Prize."
  The teachers reluctantly allow the last one.

(4) THE CHILDREN SPEAK — choose one:
- Most children vote for one winner, provided the rules are fair.
  The adults are surprised by their willingness to compete.
- Most children prefer participation ribbons. The judges
  agree, but promise honest feedback for anyone who wants it.
- The children propose a group challenge instead, where
  everyone works toward a shared goal.
- The children disagree just as much as their parents.
  The teacher sighs. Apparently, democracy is complicated.
- One child asks why adults are arguing about a contest
  that the children were enjoying perfectly well.
  The parents fall silent.

(5) A PARENTAL DISPUTE — optional rare branch:
Two parents begin arguing so loudly that the children stop
paying attention to the ceremony.

One parent (accuses the judges of favoritism/insists that
(his/her) child deserves a prize/demands a complete recount).
The other (defends the rules/argues for equal recognition/
asks everyone to calm down).

A child finally says, "Can we PLEASE just finish the contest?"

The parents (apologize/continue arguing until the Chief
intervenes/realize that they are embarrassing their children).

(6) RIDICULOUS ENDINGS — choose one:
- A frog wanders onto the judging table. The children declare
  it the winner for "Outstanding Participation." The frog
  appears entirely indifferent.
- The prize ribbon blows away in a sudden gust of wind.
  The children spend the afternoon chasing it through the village.
- The Chief accidentally awards the grand prize to the wrong
  child, then to the wrong parent, then to the schoolteacher.
  Eventually, the actual winner receives it.
- Every child receives a trophy so large that nobody can
  carry theirs home. The adults reconsider their budgeting.
- A child enters a drawing made by a coconut rolling through
  wet paint. The judges spend an embarrassing amount of time
  discussing its artistic significance.

(7) QUIET RESOLUTIONS — choose one:
- The teacher explains that losing a contest does not mean
  being less worthy of kindness or respect. The children
  return to their games.
- The winner congratulates the other children, and several
  promise to try again next year.
- The adults agree to announce the judging criteria before
  future competitions. Nobody gets everything they want,
  but everyone understands the rules.
- One child decides that (he/she) would rather create things
  for fun than compete. The teacher supports the decision.
- The parents go home, the ribbons are put away, and the
  children spend the evening laughing together.

Rewards: small Learning/Research experience for children if
supported, or a small village Tech reward for a well-run event.
Do not automatically make every child happy with the same decision.

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL EVENT 5 — THE GREAT ISOLAN STORYTELLING CONTEST
Devotion competition — VV1 through VV5
================================================================================

The village has gathered for an important celebration of Isola's
history. For generations, the elders have passed down stories
about the ancestors, the spirits, the island, and the lessons
that helped the villagers survive.

Today, two devoted villagers, (devotee1) and (devotee2), will
compete to see who can recite the ancient stories most accurately.

The rules seem simple enough: remember the story, speak clearly,
and try not to embarrass yourself in front of the entire village.

Of course, that last part may be easier said than done.

(Devotee1) is (A), while (devotee2) is (A, different).
The story they must recite concerns (B).

A — COMPETITOR PERSONALITY:
- a patient student who has practiced for months
- a confident storyteller who enjoys an audience
- a nervous novice who keeps forgetting (his/her) lines
- a stern traditionalist who insists every word matters
- a creative spirit who likes to embellish the details
- a humble devotee who simply wants to honor the ancestors
- a talented speaker with a terrible memory
- a meticulous scholar who has memorized every pause
- an eccentric villager who believes the spirits will help
- a former champion determined to defend (his/her) title
- a quiet soul who rarely speaks in public
- someone who is very good at remembering stories,
  but not necessarily understanding them

B — THE ANCIENT STORY:
- the founding of the village
- a terrible storm survived by the ancestors
- the first discovery of the island's sacred plants
- an ancient dispute settled through wisdom
- a mysterious spirit that guided lost villagers home
- the importance of sharing food during difficult times
- a brave healer who saved a village
