- a lesson about honesty and keeping promises
- a legendary journey across the sea
- a warning against greed, pride, and needless cruelty
- the origins of a curious Isolan custom
- the time the ancestors nearly destroyed a feast
  by arguing over who deserved the largest portion

What should the judges value most?

CHOICE: ACCURACY ABOVE ALL
The judges announce that every word must be recited exactly
as it was taught. (Devotee1) (nods confidently/swallows hard).
(Devotee2) (smiles/looks suddenly worried). Go to (1).

CHOICE: MEANING ABOVE MEMORIZATION
The judges announce that understanding the lesson matters
more than reciting every syllable perfectly.
One competitor looks relieved. The other mutters that
the rules have become rather suspicious. Go to (2).

CHOICE: BOTH ACCURACY AND UNDERSTANDING
The judges require each competitor to recite the story
and explain what it means. Go to (3).

(1) THE RECITATION — choose one:
- Both competitors recite the story flawlessly. The judges
  must decide whose delivery was better. (5)
- One competitor remembers every word but speaks so quietly
  that nobody can hear. The judges ask (him/her) to repeat it.
  (5)
- One competitor forgets a line and improvises a surprisingly
  convincing replacement. An elder notices immediately. (4)
- A sudden sneeze interrupts the most solemn passage.
  The competitor loses (his/her) place. The audience tries
  very hard not to laugh. (5)
- One competitor makes a tiny mistake with enormous consequences.
  (4)

(2) THE MEANING — choose one:
- The competitors offer different but thoughtful interpretations.
  The judges declare that both have learned something valuable. (5)
- One competitor gives a beautiful explanation but gets several
  details wrong. The elders praise the insight while correcting
  the mistakes. (5)
- One competitor admits that (he/she) memorized the story
  without understanding it. The judges offer to teach (him/her).
  (6)
- The competitors disagree about the story's moral. A lively
  discussion follows, but neither is declared a heretic.
  (5)
- A child asks a question that none of the adults can answer.
  The elders agree to consult the oldest records. (6)

(3) THE COMBINED TEST — choose one:
- One competitor delivers a flawless recitation and an equally
  impressive explanation. The judges are delighted. (5)
- One competitor recites perfectly but cannot explain the lesson.
  The other forgets a few words but understands it deeply. (5)
- Both competitors stumble, then help each other remember
  the missing lines. The judges debate whether cooperation
  should be rewarded in a competition. (5)
- One competitor accidentally reverses the meaning of a sacred
  instruction. The entire village freezes. (4)
- The competitors reveal that two different versions of the
  story have been taught for generations. The elders begin
  comparing records instead of declaring either contestant wrong.
  (6)

(4) THE GREAT RECITATION FUMBLE
(Devotee1 or devotee2) stands proudly before the village and
declares, in a loud and confident voice:

(C — choose one embarrassing misquotation.)

C — THE FUMBLE:
- "Thou shalt steal from thy neighbors!"
  (The original teaching said: "Thou shalt NOT steal from thy neighbors!")
- "Always betray a friend in times of need!"
  (The original teaching said: "NEVER betray a friend in times of need!")
- "The spirits command us to waste every harvest!"
  (The original teaching said: "The spirits command us NOT to waste the harvest!")
- "Honor thy elders by throwing coconuts at them!"
  (The original teaching said: "Honor thy elders by bringing coconuts to them!")
- "The wise villager eats the sacred herbs without asking questions!"
  (The original teaching said: "The wise villager NEVER eats unknown herbs
  without asking questions!")
- "A generous heart must keep all the food for itself!"
  (The original teaching said: "A generous heart must NOT keep all the
  food for itself!")
- "When in doubt, push the Chief into the pond!"
  (The original teaching said: "When in doubt, ASK the Chief for guidance!")
- "And so the ancestors decreed that all villagers must dance naked
  around the cooking fire!"
  (The original teaching said: "All villagers must dance TOGETHER
  around the cooking fire!")

For a moment, nobody moves.

Then (a child bursts out laughing/an elder drops (his/her) notes/
the Chief raises an eyebrow/the audience begins whispering/
someone asks whether the village laws have changed).

(Devotee name) (turns bright red/insists (he/she) said it correctly/
realizes the mistake halfway through a bow/looks as though
(he/she) might sink into the earth).

Go to (7).

(5) THE JUDGES' VERDICT — choose one:
- (Devotee1) wins for accuracy. (Devotee2) congratulates (him/her).
  The winner gains Devotion experience.
- (Devotee2) wins for understanding and delivery.
  (Devotee1) looks disappointed, but applauds.
  The winner gains Devotion experience.
- The judges declare a tie. Both competitors receive a small
  Devotion reward and are invited to teach the children.
- The judges award no prize, explaining that both contestants
  need more practice. The competitors agree to return next year.
- The judges decide that the two versions of the story are
  equally valuable historical traditions. Both are recorded
  for future generations; the village gains Tech points.
- The competitors are so busy congratulating each other
  that they forget to collect the prize. A child runs after them.

(6) A QUIET LESSON
After the ceremony, an elder invites the competitors to sit down.

"Stories are not merely words to be repeated," (he/she) says.
"They are memories. They are questions. Sometimes, they are warnings.
And sometimes, even the elders must admit that they do not know
everything."

The competitors listen quietly. They may not have won the contest,
but they leave with a deeper appreciation for the stories they carry.

(Both gain a little Devotion or Research experience if supported.)

(7) THE AFTERMATH OF THE FUMBLE — choose one:
- The competitor immediately corrects (himself/herself).
  The audience laughs, then applauds the honesty. The judges
  deduct a few points but allow (him/her) to continue.
- The competitor is so mortified that (he/she) runs away.
  Later, the teacher finds (him/her) and explains that one
  embarrassing mistake does not erase months of hard work.
- The judges ask the competitor to explain the actual lesson.
  To everyone's surprise, (he/she) does so beautifully.
  The judges award partial credit.
- The Chief jokingly asks whether (he/she) must now throw
  coconuts at the elders. The elders unanimously vote NO.
- A mischievous child begins repeating the incorrect version
  around the village. The teacher spends the next week
  correcting it.
- The competitor insists that the altered story is an
  improvement. The elders (laugh politely/disagree firmly/
  ask (him/her) to submit it as an original story instead).
- The other competitor quietly offers (him/her) a drink of
  water and helps (him/her) find the correct line.
  The judges are impressed by the kindness.
- The judges cannot stop laughing long enough to announce
  the winner. The ceremony is postponed until everyone
  regains their composure.

(8) RARE — THE MISSING WORD BECOMES A LEGEND
Years later, villagers still remember the day that
(devotee name) accidentally commanded everyone to
(steal from their neighbors/throw coconuts at their elders/
waste the harvest/push the Chief into the pond).

The original story is carefully preserved. The mistake
becomes a harmless joke told at village gatherings.

(Devotee name) (laughs along whenever it is mentioned/
groans every single time/secretly enjoys the attention).

Perhaps devotion includes learning to laugh at oneself.

(9) QUIET ENDING
When the ceremony is over, the villagers return to their
daily lives. The elders carefully put away their records.
The children continue asking questions.

And somewhere in the village, one thoroughly embarrassed
storyteller is practicing the word "NOT" over and over again.

REWARD NOTES:
- Use Devotion experience as the primary reward.
- Research experience or Tech points may be awarded for comparing
  historical versions and preserving accurate records.
- Do not force an embarrassing mistake to become a permanent
  personality trait or a lasting humiliation.
- The misquotations are fictional Isolan teachings, not claims
  about real religious scriptures.

================================================================================
FINAL IMPLEMENTATION AND EDITORIAL NOTES
================================================================================

1. The existing three events above this expansion remain intact:
   VV2 The Cooking Contest, VV1 The Child of Legend, and
   VV5 The Meaning of "Faith".
2. The five new events are UNIVERSAL: each may appear in VV1–VV5
   with appropriate game-specific selection and rewards.
3. Select distinct eligible contestants where the story needs two.
   Keep pronouns and contestant roles consistent throughout.
4. Treat slash-separated banks as alternatives, not literal text.
   Choose grammar-compatible complete phrases.
5. Numbered branches are narrative references, not game engine API calls.
6. An event must select one coherent resolution per path; do not
   concatenate contradictory outcomes.
7. Do not apply death, disability, permanent dislike, relationship
   flags, or skill progression unless the patcher actually supports
   them. Use the closest narrative-only alternative otherwise.
8. Do not accidentally route a player into repeated tie-breakers
   or an endless loop.
9. Preserve quiet endings as real outcomes, not merely failures
   to reach the spectacular endings.
10. Keep the intentionally absurd Isolan cooking vessels, recipes,
    eccentric villagers, and occasional ridiculous disasters.
11. For the Golden Child, communication is not synonymous with
    speech, and acceptance does not require every child to become
    his friend.
12. For the healing dilemma, the patient's wishes and clinical
    uncertainty matter; outcomes must not act as moral scores.
13. For the parenting event, children may respond differently
    to the same policy.
14. For the devotion event, an accurate recitation, thoughtful
    interpretation, and honest correction may all have value.
15. Do not rewrite the original author's prose into a uniform,
    polished, generic style. Retain its humor, melodrama, oddities,
    and Isolan charm.

END OF COLLECTION

================================================================================
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS
THE GREAT WOODWORKING COMPETITION
================================================================================

GAMEPLAY RULE: This event involves ONE TEAM of eligible, selectable villagers
from the player's tribe and ONE OFFSCREEN NPC TEAM representing the neighboring
Heathen/Isolan community. No unconverted Heathen is selected, moved, assigned a
job, converted, or given a player-villager skill flag. The Isolan team exists in
the event narrative only. "Heathen" is the historical faction label; characters
may call themselves Isolans. If a player-villager has a former-Heathen
background, that does not disqualify them from joining the player's team.

SETTING:
Relations between the two communities have grown peaceful enough for a
friendly public event. This does not imply that every disagreement or
difference in custom has vanished.

INTRODUCTION

An unexpected invitation has arrived at the village workshop.

"To our neighbors," it reads, "we propose a friendly contest to determine
which community possesses the finest woodworkers on Isola. The contest
shall be conducted with dignity, fairness, and absolutely no unnecessary
boasting."

Below the message, someone has added in a different handwriting:
"WE ARE GOING TO WIN."

(Name1), the village's (most enthusiastic builder/most meticulous
craftsperson/self-appointed artistic expert), reads the invitation aloud.
(Name2) (laughs/looks offended/starts sharpening a carving tool/
asks whether the other community knows what it has gotten itself into).

The visitors arrive carrying wood, tools, and a suspiciously large banner.
Their team leader, (Isolan leader), greets the villagers with
(a solemn bow/a friendly handshake/a dramatic flourish/an extremely
competitive smile).

A neutral judging party has been invited: (JUDGE). Neither competing
community may choose the winner.

JUDGE — choose one:
- a respected traveling artisan with no ties to either community
- three visiting elders from a distant settlement
- an impartial craftsperson who has never met either team
- a small council of visiting traders accustomed to judging handiwork
- a visiting scholar who insists on written scoring criteria
- a visiting carpenter who is notoriously difficult to impress

"The rules are simple," the judge announces. "Each team must create
something useful, beautiful, or at least recognizable. You may choose
your approach, but both teams will be judged by the same standards."

The judge reveals the assignment: (A).

A — THE ASSIGNMENT:
- carve an animal native to Isola
- make a useful piece of furniture
- construct a model of an important island landmark
- make a toy that children can actually play with
- create a decorative panel telling a story from Isola's history
- design a wooden vessel that can float
- build a ceremonial centerpiece for a village feast
- make the most beautiful object from an unpromising piece of driftwood
- construct a practical object using only a small set of tools
- create a work that represents cooperation between communities

B — THE PLAYER'S TEAM APPROACH:
- follow an established design, with careful measurements
- improvise something ambitious and completely original
- focus on strength and practical use
- create something beautiful, even if it serves no obvious purpose

CHOICES:
A. Focus on craftsmanship and precision. (1)
B. Make something wildly imaginative. (2)
C. Ask the Isolan team to agree on a few shared safety rules first. (3)
D. Propose a friendly exchange of techniques before judging. (4)

(1) THE PRECISE APPROACH
The village team measures every board and checks each joint.
(Name1) insists that even the smallest mistake could ruin everything.

The Isolan team, meanwhile, (works in near silence/sings while carving/
uses an unfamiliar but effective technique/finishes suspiciously early).

By the end of the allotted time, the village's creation is
(sturdy and elegant/technically flawless but rather plain/
so carefully measured that the team ran out of time to decorate it/
beautifully symmetrical except for one extremely noticeable leg).

Go to (5).

(2) THE CREATIVE APPROACH
(Name1) declares that the village will make something no one has ever seen.

The team produces (a magnificent carved bird with moving wings/
a chair shaped like a giant coconut/a wooden fish with a hidden compartment/
a detailed model of the island/a statue that appears to be judging its judges).

The Isolan team (looks genuinely impressed/tries not to laugh/
immediately begins adding more decorations to its own entry).

Unfortunately, (one decoration falls off/the creation is too large
to fit through the doorway/the paint has not dried/the object makes
an alarming squeaking noise whenever it moves).

Go to (5).

(3) THE SAFETY AGREEMENT
The village team proposes that both sides inspect their tools, agree on
work areas, and keep spectators away from flying wood chips.

The Isolan leader (agrees readily/acts insulted until a loose board
nearly hits (him/her)/suggests an even better arrangement).

The work proceeds (smoothly/with a few petty arguments/
under the watchful eye of the visiting judge).

The teams finish their creations without serious incident.
Go to (5).

(4) THE TECHNIQUE EXCHANGE
Before the contest begins, (name1) shows the visitors a clever way
to join two pieces of wood. The Isolan leader responds by demonstrating
(a beautiful carving technique/a clever wooden hinge/a method of
smoothing rough edges/a way to bend thin wood without breaking it).

The two teams learn something useful, then remember they are supposed
to be competing.

"Don't think this means we're going easy on you," says the Isolan leader.

"Wouldn't dream of it," replies (name1).

Go to (5).

(5) THE ISOLAN ENTRY — choose one:
- A beautifully carved family of wooden animals, each with its own
  expression. Even the judge smiles.
- A sturdy chest decorated with intricate patterns from the visitors'
  community. The judge admires its craftsmanship.
- A perfectly functional chair that is so uncomfortable nobody
  wants to sit in it.
- A delicate miniature boat that floats beautifully until a tiny
  wave sends it upside down.
- An elaborate statue whose face bears an unfortunate resemblance
  to the Chief.
- A wooden musical instrument that makes (a lovely melody/
  an ear-splitting screech/a noise suspiciously like a fart).
- A lovingly carved representation of the visitors' ancestors.
  The Isolan leader explains the meaning of each symbol.
- A wooden sculpture so abstract that the judge quietly asks
  whether it is upside down.
- A practical table that turns out to be more useful than
  either team's decorative creations.

Just as the judge prepares to evaluate both entries, (6).

(6) THE INTERRUPTION — choose one:
- Nothing happens. The judge is able to proceed without incident. (7)
- A child asks whether the teams are allowed to vote for themselves.
  The judge says no. The child asks whether bribery counts as voting. (7)
- A gust of wind blows away the scoring sheets. Both teams chase them
  through the village. (7)
- The Isolan team accuses the village of copying a carving technique.
  The village points out that it was SHOWN to them earlier. The
  misunderstanding is resolved after a rather awkward conversation. (7)
- One of the creations collapses. The judge pauses the contest and
  checks whether anyone is hurt. Nobody is injured; the damaged
  entry is evaluated according to the announced criteria. (7)
- The two team leaders begin arguing over which creation is prettier.
  The judge asks them to stop providing unsolicited commentary. (7)
- A visiting spectator offers to buy both entries. The judge
  reminds everyone that the competition is not an auction. (7)
- A tiny wooden animal falls out of a competitor's sleeve. It is
  an unfinished gift for a child, not a secret contest entry.
  The judges allow it to be set aside. (7)

(7) THE NEUTRAL VERDICT — choose one:
- The village team wins for excellent workmanship. The Isolans
  congratulate the winners, though one mutters that the judging
  criteria were clearly invented by people who love straight lines. (8)
- The Isolan team wins for artistry and originality. The village
  team is disappointed but asks how the visitors made such
  intricate carvings. (9)
- The judge declares a tie. Both teams are proud until they
  discover there is only one trophy. (10)
- The judge declares the village entry more practical and the
  Isolan entry more beautiful. Neither team can agree which
  compliment is better. (10)
- Both entries fail the required safety test. The judge refuses
  to reward an unsafe object merely because it looks impressive.
  The teams must repair their work. (11)
- The judge admits that both creations satisfy different
  purposes and awards separate prizes under the announced
  categories. (12)
- The judge selects a winner, but both teams accuse the judge
  of having terrible taste. They then agree to accept the
  decision after reviewing the scoring notes. (8 or 9, as appropriate)

(8) VILLAGE VICTORY — choose one:
- The Isolan leader shakes (name1)'s hand. "Well earned."
  The villagers invite the visitors to stay for dinner.
- The visitors demand a rematch next year. (Name1) accepts
  with an enormous grin.
- The Isolan leader says, "We could have won if our chair
  hadn't looked like an injured crab." Nobody disagrees.
- The village team offers to display both entries together,
  so everyone can appreciate the different traditions.
- A villager boasts a little too loudly. (Name2) reminds
  (him/her) that winning one contest does not make an entire
  community superior to another.

Reward: village contestants gain Building experience;
small Tech points if supported.

(9) ISOLAN VICTORY — choose one:
- (Name1) congratulates the visitors sincerely and asks for
  a lesson in their carving technique.
- (Name2) pouts for a while, then admits that the winning
  entry really was excellent.
- The village team challenges the Isolans to a rematch,
  this time with a different assignment.
- The Isolan leader is so surprised by the win that (he/she)
  forgets the acceptance speech.
- The visitors share the prize feast with the village,
  saying that a good celebration should include everyone.

Reward: modest Building experience for participating
villagers; no village victory bonus.

(10) THE TROPHY PROBLEM — choose one:
- The judge orders a second trophy made. The two teams
  spend the afternoon helping each other finish it.
- The teams agree to display the trophy in a shared meeting
  place, provided nobody secretly moves it.
- The leaders decide to alternate possession of the trophy.
  They immediately begin arguing about who gets it first.
- Someone suggests cutting the trophy in half. The judge
  looks horrified.
- The teams abandon the trophy entirely and share a meal.
  The judge is secretly relieved.

(11) THE REPAIR ROUND
The teams have one hour to repair their entries.
The village team (reinforces the joints/replaces a broken leg/
removes a dangerous splinter/starts again with a simpler design).
The Isolan team (tightens the supports/repairs a cracked panel/
removes an unstable decoration/borrows a tool with permission).

The judge (awards the safer finished entry a narrow victory/
declares a draw/decides neither entry meets the standard).
Nobody is seriously hurt. The teams gain a little practical
experience and a great deal of respect for careful workmanship.

(12) THE SHARED SHOWCASE
The judge announces that both communities have made something
worth celebrating. The villagers place the entries side by side.

Some admire the Isolan patterns. Others prefer the village's
construction. A few children care only about the wooden toys.

For the rest of the afternoon, the two communities exchange
stories, techniques, and increasingly exaggerated accounts
of how difficult the contest was.

(13) RARE RIDICULOUS ENDING — replace a peaceful (8)–(12)
ending when no injury or unresolved cheating is involved:
- A wooden statue's nose suddenly falls off. Both teams swear
  it was perfectly attached a moment ago. The judge declares
  that noses were not part of the scoring criteria.
- The trophy turns out to be a beautifully carved wooden spoon.
  The winning team uses it to serve stew.
- The judge awards the prize to a small, perfectly made stool.
  Nobody remembers which team actually built it.
  After checking the records, the mistake is corrected.
- A child asks why the statue has three ears. The sculptor
  replies, "For better listening." The child seems satisfied.
- A visiting elder declares the ugliest entry the most
  historically authentic. Everyone asks for an explanation.
  The elder says, "You had to be there."

(14) QUIET ENDING — optional after any resolved result:
- The visitors depart at sunset, carrying their tools and
  promising to return. The village workshop seems a little
  quieter afterward.
- (Name1) discovers a small wooden token left behind by
  the Isolan team: a thank-you gift for the hospitality.
- The two team leaders sit together after the contest,
  comparing sore hands and laughing about their mistakes.
- No grand friendship is formed, and nobody changes
  their beliefs. But the next time the communities meet,
  they greet each other by name.

================================================================================
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS
THE POTLUCK DISPUTE
Lighthearted cultural exchange; player tribe + offscreen Isolan visitors
================================================================================

The village has invited a group of Isolan neighbors to a shared feast.
Everyone is supposed to bring a favorite dish and a story about it.

Unfortunately, the villagers disagree about (A).

A — THE DISPUTE:
- whether the meal should begin with a blessing, a toast, or simply eating
- whether a particularly pungent herb is a delicacy or an abomination
- who gets to sit at the head of the table
- whether a sweet coconut dish counts as dinner
- whether the oldest recipe must be served first
- whether the guests are expected to bring gifts
- whether the feast should be formal or relaxed

(Name1), the village organizer, wants to (B).
The visiting Isolan organizer prefers (B, different).

B — APPROACH:
- follow the traditional customs exactly
- let everyone choose for themselves
- make a compromise
- ask the elders for guidance
- invent a new tradition together
- avoid making a fuss and just eat

What should the village do?

CHOICE A — Respect both customs separately. (1)
CHOICE B — Create a shared celebration. (2)
CHOICE C — Let the guests choose the order. (3)
CHOICE D — Hold a friendly tasting competition. (4)

(1) TWO CUSTOMS:
The hosts make room for both traditions without demanding
that either group abandon its own practice.
- The meal proceeds peacefully, and several villagers learn
  something new about their neighbors. (5)
- The arrangements are confusing at first, but a few
  friendly explanations solve the problem. (5)
- Everyone is so determined to be polite that nobody
  starts eating until a child asks whether the food
  is supposed to get cold. (5)

(2) A NEW TRADITION:
The organizers combine elements of both celebrations.
- The guests are delighted by the result. (5)
- The new ceremony is lovely but takes an absurdly long time. (5)
- One elder dislikes the changes. The organizers listen,
  then agree to keep the older tradition available too. (5)
- A child invents a ridiculous ceremonial dance.
  By the end, half the village is doing it. (5)

(3) GUESTS FIRST:
The hosts ask their visitors what would make them comfortable.
- The visitors appreciate the gesture and suggest an easy compromise. (5)
- The Isolan organizer admits (he/she) was hoping the hosts
  would decide. Both organizers burst out laughing. (5)
- The visitors choose a custom that the village already follows.
  Everyone wonders what the argument was about. (5)

(4) THE TASTING:
Both communities present their favorite dishes. A neutral
visitor agrees to judge them.
- The village wins for flavor, while the Isolans win for presentation. (5)
- The Isolans win by a hair. The village cook asks for the recipe. (5)
- The judge declares a tie and requests another helping of both. (5)
- A judge accidentally tastes a decoration instead of the food.
  The contest is paused while everyone confirms what is edible. (5)
- The judges announce that the best dish is a combination
  of both communities' recipes. The cooks are horrified. (5)

(5) ENDING — choose one:
- The guests exchange recipes and promise to cook together again.
- The villagers agree that different customs can coexist
  without anyone having to declare a winner.
- The meal ends in laughter, with everyone arguing over
  who should wash the dishes.
- A child asks for seconds of the dish everyone called disgusting.
  The cook looks deeply vindicated.
- The Isolan organizer quietly thanks (name1) for making
  the visitors feel welcome.

Rewards: small food bonus if the feast succeeds; small
Devotion or Farming experience for eligible player villagers.

================================================================================
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS
THE GREAT LAUNDRY MISHAP
A peaceful everyday comedy
================================================================================

A basket of freshly washed clothing has appeared between the
two communities. Unfortunately, nobody knows who owns it.

(Name1) discovers (A) among the laundry.

A — THE MYSTERIOUS ITEM:
- a brilliantly colored scarf
- a pair of enormous trousers
- a ceremonial sash
- a shirt with far too many sleeves
- a child's tiny knitted hat
- a cloak covered in decorative patches
- a cloth bag embroidered with flowers

An Isolan visitor insists that the item belongs to (his/her)
community. (Name2) insists it belongs to the village.

The problem is that neither can actually prove it.

CHOICE A — Ask around politely. (1)
CHOICE B — Compare the patterns and stitching. (2)
CHOICE C — Hold the item until someone claims it. (3)
CHOICE D — Put on an impromptu fashion show to find the owner. (4)

(1) ASKING AROUND:
- The owner is found immediately. Everyone apologizes
  for the confusion. (5)
- Several villagers claim the item, each with a surprisingly
  convincing explanation. (3)
- The real owner admits (he/she) left the item behind
  after visiting the other community. (5)

(2) THE STITCHING:
- An elder recognizes the distinctive stitching and
  identifies the owner. (5)
- The pattern is common to both communities. The villagers
  discover they have been sharing techniques for years. (5)
- A child recognizes the item because (he/she) accidentally
  sewed one sleeve on backward. (5)

(3) WAITING:
- The owner arrives the next morning, grateful that the
  item was kept safe. (5)
- Nobody claims it. The villagers agree to store it until
  the next gathering. (5)
- The two communities decide to make a simple system
  for labeling shared laundry. (5)

(4) THE FASHION SHOW:
(Name1) declares that the easiest way to identify the
owner is to have volunteers model the mysterious garment.
- The true owner laughs and claims it immediately. (5)
- The garment is far too small for the volunteer.
  The crowd applauds the effort anyway. (5)
- The Isolan visitor reveals that the item is actually
  a tablecloth. The volunteer slowly removes it. (5)
- A child announces that the garment belongs to the
  village's laundry basket. Nobody finds this helpful. (5)

(5) QUIET OR RIDICULOUS RESOLUTION — choose one:
- The item is returned. The neighbors share a friendly
  conversation and go about their day.
- (Name1) and the Isolan visitor exchange sewing tips.
- The owner is embarrassed but grateful. "I was looking
  everywhere for that!"
- A villager suggests a grand annual laundry festival.
  The others firmly reject the proposal.
- The incident becomes a small joke between the communities.
  Nothing more dramatic comes of it.

Reward: small Building/Research experience for an eligible
player villager who devises a useful labeling system;
otherwise narrative-only.

================================================================================
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS
THE FIELD DAY
Friendly sporting and recreational contest
================================================================================

The Isolan neighbors have invited the village to an afternoon
of games. Nobody is entirely sure who suggested the event,
but both communities seem eager to participate.

The visiting organizers have prepared (A).

A — THE GAME:
- a relay race carrying coconuts
- a contest to stack stones without knocking them over
- a guessing game involving objects hidden in baskets
- a singing competition
- a cooperative puzzle race
- a game of balancing wooden spoons
- a contest to build the tallest tower of harmless materials
- a scavenger hunt for ordinary island objects

A neutral visitor agrees to keep score. Both teams promise
to follow the rules, although one player is already asking
whether the rules are negotiable.

CHOICE A — Compete seriously. (1)
CHOICE B — Focus on having fun. (2)
CHOICE C — Mix the teams for a cooperative round. (3)
CHOICE D — Ask the children to invent an extra challenge. (4)

(1) SERIOUS COMPETITION:
- The village wins narrowly. The Isolans request a rematch. (5)
- The Isolans win decisively. The village congratulates them. (5)
- The game ends in a tie. Both teams demand a recount. (5)
- A player accidentally drops the coconut at the finish line.
  The judge applies the rule consistently. (5)

(2) FRIENDLY FUN:
- The teams cheer for each other's successes. (5)
- A participant makes a spectacularly silly mistake and
  laughs harder than anyone else. (5)
- The competitors forget to keep score. The judge shrugs
  and declares the afternoon a success. (5)

(3) MIXED TEAMS:
- The villagers discover that working together is easier
  than shouting instructions across the field. (5)
- The teams invent clever strategies by combining
  different strengths. (5)
- A minor disagreement is resolved by changing roles
  and trying again. (5)

(4) THE CHILDREN'S CHALLENGE:
- The children invent a perfectly reasonable new game. (5)
- The children insist that everyone hop on one foot
  while carrying a spoon. The adults regret agreeing. (5)
- The children declare that the winners must sing
  a song about the losers. The judge vetoes the rule.
- The children make the adults solve a puzzle they
  finished hours ago. The adults struggle terribly. (5)

(5) ENDING — choose one:
- Both communities enjoy a peaceful afternoon and
  agree to play again next season.
- The winners share their prize with the other team.
- The losing team practices quietly for next year.
- The judge announces that the real winner is friendship.
  Both teams groan. The judge laughs.
- A coconut rolls away with the score sheet attached.
  Nobody can prove who won. Everyone goes home happy.
- The two team captains shake hands, then immediately
  start arguing about next year's rules.

Rewards: small experience in a relevant supported skill,
or narrative-only. Never award player-villager skill points
to non-playable Isolan visitors.

================================================================================
VV5 EVENT INTEGRATION NOTES
================================================================================

- The player's tribe supplies only actual eligible playable villagers.
- Visiting Heathen/Isolan teams and individuals are OFFSCREEN NPCs represented
  through narrative text; do not target unconverted Heathens for gameplay actions.
- The neutral judge is not a member of either competing team.
- Do not require conversion, change of faith, or friendship as a prize.
- Winners and losers must stay consistent across all branch outcomes.
- The four VV5 events above are independent and can trigger separately.
- Keep cultural differences visible without making one group universally
  foolish, villainous, or inferior.
- Choose only one ending per path. Quiet outcomes are as valid as spectacular
  ones; the absurd endings should be rare.
- All rewards are optional and must match supported patcher mechanics.

END OF VV5 ADDITIONS

================================================================================
V6 ADDITIONS — MORE STORIES ACROSS ISOLA
Eight complete events for VV1–VV4, plus a characterization and safety guide
================================================================================

AUTHOR'S EVENT DESIGN GUIDE — HOW TO WRITE THESE VILLAGERS

The villagers are PEOPLE first and game units second. A profession is a source
of knowledge and pride, not a whole personality. A Master Scientist can be
superstitious about a favorite pencil; an Apprentice Builder can recognize a
mistake that an Expert overlooks. Children are curious, opinionated, sometimes
wrong, sometimes perceptive, and never uniformly adorable or wise. Elders
may be patient, irritable, mischievous, tired, proud, or quietly uncertain.
Nursing parents have ordinary opinions, friendships, frustrations, humor,
and skills; parenthood does not erase their previous lives.

Useful characterization banks (choose independently, with coherence):
VOICE: blunt / diplomatic / chatty / hesitant / dryly funny / formal /
      boastful / soft-spoken / suspiciously cheerful / habitually literal.
MOTIVE: wants to help / wants to be respected / fears embarrassment /
        wants to protect a friend / is curious / needs a break /
        hopes to impress a child / simply enjoys the work.
REACTION: laughs / withdraws / apologizes / doubles down / asks for proof /
          tries again / makes a joke / offers practical help.
SMALL HUMAN DETAIL: wipes dusty hands on a tunic / keeps a favorite pebble /
          hums while working / always carries spare string / hates applause /
          secretly enjoys gossip / makes tiny carvings for children.

A good event has (1) a specific situation, (2) a meaningful person-specific
reaction, (3) a consequence that follows from the choice, and (4) a final
beat that is allowed to be ordinary. Never equate a person's worth with
skill, age, fertility, faith, or usefulness. Do not write every elder as a
sage, every child as a genius, or every nursing parent as exhausted.

UNIVERSAL CASTING / PROTECTED-VILLAGER CONTRACT

Use this for every event in this manuscript, including earlier events:
- SAFE / SOCIAL: Eligible children, adults, elders, skilled villagers, and
  nursing mothers/fathers may appear in appropriate NONLETHAL scenes if the
  game supports their participation. Nursing parents may be spectators,
  conversational participants, or off-duty advisors; do not force a job
  assignment while they are occupied caring for an infant.
- DANGEROUS: Before selecting a villager for any branch that can kill,
  permanently remove, abduct, strand, or disappear them, filter by the
  actual game's protected/untargetable flags and restrictions. Exclude
  the Golden Child (VV1), the VV3 tribal Chief, all nursing mothers and
  fathers, all infants, and ANY OTHER game-protected or deliberately
  excluded villager. Also exclude story-critical, immortal, unavailable,
  already-missing, or otherwise ineligible villagers. Do not assume the
  enumerated examples are the full protected set.
- Children: never select for a deadly/disappearance branch unless the
  game explicitly permits that exact event and the user deliberately
  enables it. These eight new events do not endanger children.
- If the eligible danger pool is empty, skip the dangerous event or use
  a harmless alternative. NEVER relax the protection rules to make an
  event fire.
- Reroll selection BEFORE displaying the event; never narrate a death
  or disappearance and then discover that the selected villager was
  protected. If the engine cannot enforce the filter, disable that branch.
- Do not silently apply a job, movement, relationship, illness, death,
  disappearance, aging, or experience effect that the engine cannot do.
- When using a narrative NPC, keep them separate from player-controlled
  villagers and do not award them in-game skills.
- For VV3, the Chief may appear in safe conversation but is excluded
  from deadly/disappearance targets. For VV1, the Golden Child may
  appear in safe scenes with respectful nonverbal communication.
- Every outcome below is nonlethal unless a separate OPTIONAL hazard
  module is explicitly written and safely gated. None is needed here.

================================================================================
VV1 — A NEW HOME
EVENT: THE UNEXPECTED QUESTION
COMPLEX MULTICHOICE — children, an elder, a teacher, optional Golden Child
================================================================================

A teacher has gathered the village's children for a lesson about the island.
The topic is (A). Unfortunately, (child1) has a question that nobody expected.

A — THE QUESTION:
- If the sea is so large, why doesn't it spill over the edge?
- If a tree falls where nobody hears it, did it make a sound?
- How do we know the ancestors didn't make mistakes?
- Why do grown-ups say "in a minute" when they mean much longer?
- If everyone learns from someone, who taught the first teacher?
- Why are the oldest ruins sometimes buried beneath newer ones?
- If a bird steals our berries, does that make it a thief?
- Can two people remember the same day differently and both be sincere?

The teacher (smiles confidently/looks alarmed/asks for a moment to think/
pretends to search for a missing page). Nearby, an elder who was delivering
supplies overhears the discussion and offers to help.

The class is waiting. What should the teacher do?

CHOICE A: Answer immediately, even without certainty. (1)
CHOICE B: Turn the question into an investigation. (2)
CHOICE C: Invite the elder to share a story. (3)
CHOICE D: Ask the children to propose their own explanations. (4)

(1) THE QUICK ANSWER — choose one:
- The explanation is sensible and clear. The class is satisfied. (5)
- The teacher gives an impressive answer that is completely wrong.
  (Child2) notices a contradiction. The teacher apologizes. (6)
- The teacher admits that the answer is only a guess. The children
  appreciate the honesty more than the explanation. (5)
- The teacher becomes so tangled in words that the lesson ends with
  three more questions than it began with. (6)

(2) THE INVESTIGATION — choose one:
- The children make observations and discover a useful pattern. (5)
- Their experiment fails because someone uses the measuring string
  as a jump rope. They try again. (5)
- The class discovers that the answer depends on what the question
  means. The teacher is delighted; the children are less impressed. (5)
- A quiet child who rarely speaks offers a careful observation.
  The teacher makes room for (him/her) to explain. (5)

(3) THE ELDER'S STORY — choose one:
- The elder remembers how the same question puzzled (him/her) as a child.
  The children are fascinated. (5)
- The elder tells a magnificent story, then admits that it is a story,
  not proof. The class discusses the difference. (5)
- The elder begins with "When I was your age..." and doesn't finish
  until the children are nearly old enough to be elders themselves. (6)
- The elder and teacher disagree respectfully, demonstrating that
  grown-ups can learn from each other. (5)

(4) THE CHILDREN'S THEORIES — choose one:
- The children suggest several testable explanations. The class
  chooses one to investigate tomorrow. (5)
- (Child1) offers an ingenious idea, while (child2) spots a flaw.
  Neither takes offense. (5)
- One child insists that all unanswered questions are caused by
  invisible coconuts. The teacher asks for evidence. (6)
- The children discover they are arguing about different meanings
  of the same word. The teacher helps them clarify it. (5)

(5) A GOOD LESSON — choose one:
- The class learns that admitting uncertainty can be the beginning
  of discovery. Small Research experience for eligible learners.
- The elder thanks the children for making (him/her) think again.
  The children ask whether (he/she) will visit next week.
- A nursing parent listening nearby quietly smiles as (his/her)
  older child explains the lesson to the baby. No job assignment.
- If the Golden Child is present, he responds through an existing
  supported nonverbal gesture. The class treats his contribution
  as meaningful without requiring speech.

(6) THE CLASSROOM GOES OFF THE RAILS — choose one:
- The teacher promises to research the answer. A child writes
  "ADULTS DO NOT KNOW EVERYTHING" in enormous letters.
- The elder accidentally sits on the teacher's notes. Everyone
  spends ten minutes looking for them.
- A child asks whether the teacher is allowed to give (himself/herself)
  homework. The class votes yes.
- The lesson ends in laughter, and the teacher writes the question
  down to revisit when everyone has had time to think.

================================================================================
VV1 — A NEW HOME
EVENT: THE ELDER'S FAVORITE STONE
NO PLAYER CHOICE — quiet, comic, automatic vignette
================================================================================

An elderly villager, (elder), has spent the morning searching for
(his/her) favorite stone. It is not a precious stone. It is not even
particularly attractive. But (he/she) has carried it for years.

(Adult) offers to help, while (child) asks what makes this particular
stone so important. The elder replies, (A).

A — THE REASON:
- "I found it the day we first made this place our home."
- "It is exactly the right shape for keeping papers from blowing away."
- "My oldest friend gave it to me."
- "It looks like the Chief when (he/she) is annoyed."
- "I was going to throw it away years ago, but then I got attached."
- "It reminds me that small things can survive enormous storms."

THE OUTCOME — choose one:
- The child finds the stone beneath a basket. The elder thanks
  (him/her) and tells the story of the day it was found.
- The adult finds three similar stones. The elder rejects them all
  with the solemn expertise of a jeweler inspecting diamonds.
- The stone turns out to have been in the elder's pocket all along.
  The elder says, "Excellent. The search was a success."
- Nobody finds the stone. The elder is disappointed, but the child
  offers a different one. The elder accepts it without pretending
  that it is the same.
- A father holding his infant recognizes the stone as the
  paperweight that has been sitting on the schoolhouse table.
  He returns it after the lesson ends.
- The elder quietly explains that the stone belonged to someone
  who is no longer alive. The child sits beside (him/her) for a while.

ENDING: By sunset, the village has moved on to other concerns.
The elder (keeps the stone safe/places the replacement on a shelf/
thanks the helpers). No skill or item reward is required.

================================================================================
VV2 — THE LOST CHILDREN
EVENT: THE VETERINARY VISITOR
TWO CHOICES — healers, children, nursing parent, elder
================================================================================

A small animal has wandered into the village hospital. It is (A).

A — THE VISITOR:
- a tiny lizard that insists on sitting on the cleanest shelf
- a crab that has somehow climbed into an empty basket
- a bird that keeps stealing the healers' bandages
- a frog that appears to be supervising the medicine table
- a turtle that has chosen the doorway as its permanent residence

(Healer) notices it first. (Child) is delighted. An elder waiting
for a routine checkup remarks that the visitor has better bedside
manners than some people (he/she) knows.

The animal is not injured, but it cannot remain among clean medical
supplies. What should the healer do?

CHOICE A: Ask the children to help guide it safely outside. (1)
CHOICE B: Move it to a suitable outdoor spot personally. (2)

(1) THE CHILDREN HELP — choose one:
- The children calmly guide the animal away from the hospital.
  The healer praises their patience. (3)
- The children are so enthusiastic that the animal escapes
  through the window before they begin. (3)
- One child wants to keep it as a pet. The healer explains why
  a wild animal is usually better left in its own habitat. (3)
- The animal crawls into the child's empty sandal. The sandal
  is carefully carried outside. (3)

(2) THE HEALER TAKES CHARGE — choose one:
- The healer gently moves the visitor outdoors, then cleans
  the affected work area. (3)
- The animal evades capture with surprising agility. The elder
  gives directions from a chair like a battlefield commander. (3)
- A nursing mother visiting with her infant points out an open
  door the healer overlooked. The animal leaves on its own. (3)
- The healer discovers that the "animal" is a beautifully carved
  wooden toy. The children have been playing a joke. (3)

(3) THE ENDING — choose one:
- The hospital returns to its normal routine. The children
  learn to respect both animals and clean medical spaces.
- The elder asks whether the animal will be receiving a bill.
  The healer replies that it left before registering.
- A child makes a drawing of the visitor and gives it to the
  hospital. The healer hangs it where it won't interfere with care.
- The healer quietly thanks everyone for helping. Small Healing
  experience only if supported; otherwise narrative-only.

================================================================================
VV2 — THE LOST CHILDREN
EVENT: THE HUT-SHARING NEGOTIATION
COMPLEX MULTICHOICE — families, newcomers, elders, builders
================================================================================

The village has welcomed several newcomers, but sleeping space is tight.
A family with a nursing infant needs a quiet place to rest, an elder
prefers to remain near familiar neighbors, and two energetic apprentices
have somehow accumulated enough tools to fill an entire hut.

(Builder) has drawn a plan. (Elder) dislikes it. (Apprentice) claims
that (his/her) tools need "room to breathe." The Chief asks everyone
to come to an agreement before nightfall.

What should the village prioritize?

CHOICE A: Keep families and dependents together. (1)
CHOICE B: Arrange huts around accessibility and daily needs. (2)
CHOICE C: Build or repair more space as soon as possible. (3)
CHOICE D: Let the affected villagers negotiate their own arrangement. (4)

(1) FAMILY FIRST — choose one:
- A quiet corner is reserved for the nursing parent and infant.
  The apprentices move their tools elsewhere. (5)
- A family offers to share a larger hut with the newcomers.
  Everyone agrees on privacy and sleeping arrangements. (5)
- The plan keeps families together but leaves an elder too far
  from (his/her) usual support. The Chief requests revisions. (2)

(2) PRACTICAL NEEDS — choose one:
- The elder is placed near the hospital and familiar neighbors;
  the family receives a quieter space. (5)
- A builder notices that one path becomes slippery after rain.
  The village changes the proposed arrangement. (5)
- The villagers discover that the apparently smallest hut has
  the best ventilation. They reconsider their assumptions. (5)
- A villager asks whether anyone has actually spoken to the
  people being moved. The Chief pauses the meeting to ask. (4)

(3) MORE SPACE — choose one:
- Skilled builders repair an unused shelter in time. Modest
  Building experience for eligible workers. (5)
- The project takes longer than expected. The villagers agree
  on a temporary arrangement without separating the infant
  from the nursing parent. (5)
- The apprentices volunteer to build extra storage for their
  tools. The elder remarks that this is their first good idea. (5)
- A new doorway is installed backward. The builders fix it
  before anyone moves in. (5)

(4) NEGOTIATION — choose one:
- Everyone listens, and the villagers find a compromise that
  the Chief had not considered. (5)
- The apprentices agree to share a workshop instead of storing
  tools where people sleep. (5)
- The elder reveals that (he/she) does not mind moving, provided
  (his/her) favorite chair comes too. (5)
- The discussion becomes an argument over who snores the loudest.
  The Chief insists this is not a contest. (5)

(5) RESOLUTION — choose one:
- By evening, everyone has a safe place to rest. The village
  feels a little more like home.
- The Chief asks the builders to write down what they learned
  before the next group of newcomers arrives.
- A father thanks the villagers for asking what his
  family actually needed instead of making assumptions.
- The apprentices' tools are finally organized. One immediately
  loses a hammer.
- The elder invites the newcomers for tea and complains about
  the village's architecture with great affection.

IMPLEMENTATION: No forced relocation of nursing parents or infants.
Only perform actual housing changes if the patcher supports them.

================================================================================
VV3 — THE SECRET CITY
EVENT: THE CHIEF'S MONUMENTAL SPEECH
NO PLAYER CHOICE — Chief, elders, children, adults
================================================================================

The tribal Chief has prepared a speech celebrating the village's
progress. It is supposed to be (short and inspiring/formal and dignified/
heartfelt and personal). The entire village gathers to listen.

The Chief begins: (A).

A — OPENING:
- "My fellow villagers, today we honor the work of many hands..."
- "There comes a time in every village's history..."
- "I promise to keep this brief."
- "I have prepared a few words. Actually, quite a few."

Unfortunately, (B).

B — INTERRUPTION:
- a child loudly asks when the snacks will be served
- a gust of wind carries away the speech notes
- the Chief realizes (he/she) is holding a shopping list
- an elder starts applauding halfway through the first sentence
- a nearby bird perfectly imitates the Chief's voice
- the Chief forgets a carefully rehearsed phrase and improvises
- a father gently rocks his baby, who begins squeaking
  precisely whenever the Chief pauses

THE RESULT — choose one:
- The Chief laughs and finishes the speech in (his/her) own words.
  The villagers applaud warmly.
- The elder quietly helps retrieve the notes. The ceremony resumes.
- The child is promised snacks AFTER the speech. The Chief then
  decides that a shorter speech might be wise.
- The Chief abandons the speech and invites villagers to share
  their own stories. Several speak with unexpected eloquence.
- The Chief delivers the entire shopping list without noticing.
  The crowd is baffled but impressed by the passion shown for beans.
- The speech ends quietly. Later, the Chief tells a friend that
  leadership is sometimes less about appearing impressive than
  being willing to laugh at oneself.

PROTECTION: The Chief is always a safe speaker in this event,
never a disappearance/death target. No player choice or reward needed.

================================================================================
VV3 — THE SECRET CITY
EVENT: THE GARDENING CONTEST
TWO CHOICES — botanists, children, elder, nursing parent
================================================================================

Two skilled gardeners have developed different plans for a small
community garden near the village. (Gardener1) favors (A), while
(gardener2) favors (A, different).

A — APPROACH:
- neat rows with carefully labeled plants
- a winding garden with shaded places to sit
- hardy food plants that require little attention
- flowers chosen to attract helpful insects
- a mixture of familiar plants and experimental varieties
- a traditional layout remembered by the elders

An elder says that both plans have merit. A child asks whether
there will be room to play. A nursing mother nearby suggests a
place where parents can sit with their babies while older
children explore safely.

CHOICE A: Combine the best features into one shared garden. (1)
CHOICE B: Give each gardener a small plot to demonstrate the plan. (2)

(1) SHARED GARDEN — choose one:
- The gardeners cooperate, creating a beautiful and useful space.
  Both gain Farming experience if supported. (3)
- They argue over the path's exact shape, then compromise when
  the elder suggests a curved path with straight planting beds. (3)
- A child draws an elaborate map featuring a "secret frog kingdom."
  The gardeners reserve a small corner for wildlife. (3)
- The gardeners realize that the combined plan needs more work
  than they can manage. They simplify it rather than overpromise. (3)

(2) TWO PLOTS — choose one:
- Both gardens flourish in different ways. The villagers learn
  which designs suit which purposes. (3)
- One garden produces more food, while the other becomes a
  favorite resting place. Neither is declared a failure. (3)
- The gardeners become playful rivals and begin leaving
  compliments disguised as criticisms. (3)
- A plant grows across the dividing line. The gardeners spend
  an absurd amount of time deciding who owns it. (3)

(3) ENDING — choose one:
- The village gains a peaceful place to gather and learn.
- The elder rests on a bench, pleased that the younger villagers
  listened without copying the past blindly.
- A child presents both gardeners with a leaf labeled "BEST."
- A nursing parent enjoys the shade while talking with friends;
  the infant is never assigned a gardening task.
- The gardeners agree to compare notes again after the next harvest.

================================================================================
VV4 — THE TREE OF LIFE
EVENT: THE BREADFRUIT BREAKDOWN
COMPLEX MULTICHOICE — cooks, elders, children, skilled villagers
================================================================================

The villagers have harvested a quantity of breadfruit. It must be
prepared properly before anyone eats it, but the cooks cannot agree
on the best method.

(Cook1) insists that (A). (Cook2) argues that (A, different).
An elder claims to remember an old recipe, though the details have
become rather fuzzy. A child asks whether they can just eat dessert.

A — CULINARY PHILOSOPHY:
- roasting brings out the best flavor
- steaming produces a softer texture
- the simplest recipe is always the best
- a feast should include several seasonings
- traditional methods deserve respect
- new ideas are worth trying carefully

What should the village do?

CHOICE A: Prepare a traditional recipe with the elder's guidance. (1)
CHOICE B: Let the cooks compete with properly prepared dishes. (2)
CHOICE C: Make a shared meal using several safe preparations. (3)
CHOICE D: Ask the children to help plan the presentation. (4)

(1) THE OLD RECIPE — choose one:
- The elder remembers every step, and the meal is delicious. (5)
- The elder remembers two conflicting versions. The cooks
  test small batches rather than guessing recklessly. (5)
- The elder admits that (he/she) always disliked the recipe.
  Everyone laughs and makes something else. (5)
- The cooks preserve the recipe in the village's records. (5)

(2) THE COOK-OFF — choose one:
- Cook1 wins for flavor; Cook2 accepts the result graciously. (5)
- Cook2 wins for texture; Cook1 requests a rematch next year. (5)
- The judges declare a tie, then ask for seconds. (5)
- The dishes are both safe but so different that the judges
  decide personal preference cannot settle the argument. (5)
- The cooks become intensely competitive until an elder reminds
  them that the village was supposed to be having lunch. (5)

(3) THE SHARED MEAL — choose one:
- The cooks work together and discover a delightful combination. (5)
- The meal is simple and satisfying. Nobody makes a speech. (5)
- One cook learns a technique from the other and thanks (him/her). (5)
- The cooks agree on every step except the garnish. The argument
  lasts longer than the cooking. (5)

(4) THE CHILDREN'S PRESENTATION — choose one:
- The children arrange the food into a beautiful display. (5)
- A child creates a breadfruit sculpture shaped like the Chief.
  The resemblance is unfortunate. (5)
- The children make place cards for everyone, including a frog.
  The frog does not attend. (5)
- The cooks appreciate the help, but keep the children away
  from hot equipment and sharp tools. (5)

(5) ENDING — choose one:
- The villagers share a warm meal and exchange recipes.
- A father enjoys the food while keeping his infant
  comfortably beside him. He offers a surprisingly sharp critique
  of the seasoning.
- The elder is pleased to see an old recipe remembered, even
  if the younger cooks have changed it a little.
- The children ask for dessert. The cooks announce that the
  breadfruit WAS dessert. The children look betrayed.
- The cooks clean up together, already discussing next year's feast.

REWARDS: modest food reward and Farming experience only when supported.
Never serve raw or improperly prepared breadfruit in a success ending.

================================================================================
VV4 — THE TREE OF LIFE
EVENT: THE LULLABY
TWO CHOICES — nursing mother/father, elder, child, musician
================================================================================

A nursing (mother/father), (parent), is trying to settle (his/her)
infant for a nap. Nearby, (musician) has begun practicing a new song.
The melody is (A).

A — THE SONG:
- beautiful but extraordinarily loud
- gentle and soothing
- complicated enough to confuse the birds
- repetitive enough to drive everyone mad
- surprisingly catchy
- mostly improvised, with an alarming number of wrong notes

The baby (seems content/begins fussing/falls asleep/watches the
musician with wide eyes). An elder suggests an old lullaby, while
an older child insists that (he/she) knows a better one.

What should the villagers do?

CHOICE A: Ask the musician to adapt the song to the family's needs. (1)
CHOICE B: Invite the elder and child to perform a quiet lullaby. (2)

(1) THE ADAPTED SONG — choose one:
- The musician softens the melody. The baby settles, and the
  parent thanks (him/her). (3)
- The musician slows the song so much that everyone else
  nearly falls asleep too. (3)
- The musician asks the parent what would help. The parent
  requests a little quiet instead, and the musician agrees. (3)
- The musician tries to sing softly but keeps forgetting and
  bursting into a dramatic chorus. The group laughs and takes
  the rehearsal farther away. (3)

(2) THE FAMILY LULLABY — choose one:
- The elder sings a gentle tune remembered from childhood.
  The parent joins in quietly. (3)
- The child sings with enormous confidence and almost no sense
  of pitch. The baby seems fascinated. (3)
- The elder and child disagree over the words, then discover
  they learned different versions of the same song. (3)
- The baby falls asleep before anyone begins. Everyone exchanges
  relieved looks and agrees not to make another sound. (3)

(3) ENDING — choose one:
- The infant rests peacefully, and the parent enjoys a few
  quiet minutes among friends.
- The parent thanks the musician for listening instead of
  assuming what the family needed.
- The elder teaches the older child the old melody, preserving
  a small piece of the village's history.
- The musician writes a new verse about a baby who refuses
  to sleep. The parent laughs a little too knowingly.
- Nothing remarkable happens. A baby sleeps, the adults talk
  softly, and village life continues.

GAMEPLAY: The nursing parent and infant remain protected and
unassigned; this is a social vignette, not a job or danger event.

================================================================================
OPTIONAL DANGEROUS / DISAPPEARANCE EVENT TEMPLATE (NOT AUTO-ENABLED)
================================================================================

The eight new events above are deliberately nonlethal. For any EXISTING
or FUTURE event with a fatal or permanent disappearance outcome, use:

1. Determine the exact supported consequence first (death, permanent
   removal, temporary absence, injury, narrative-only scare).
2. Build an eligible pool using game-specific restrictions BEFORE
   choosing the named villager.
3. Exclude Golden Child, VV3 Chief, nursing mothers/fathers, infants,
   and all other protected/untargetable or story-critical villagers.
4. Prefer non-child adult candidates for dangerous branches. Do not
   target children unless explicitly supported and intentionally enabled.
5. If pool is empty, skip event or replace with a safe, nonlethal event.
6. Verify the selected target is still eligible at consequence time.
7. Apply the game effect exactly once. If application fails, do not
   narrate that the villager died or vanished.
8. Keep witnesses, helpers, and protected participants distinct from
   the target. A nursing parent may comment on a distant event but
   must not be swept into a fatal random target list.
9. Record any actual persistent effect in the game's log if supported.
10. Avoid irreversible consequences as random punishment for harmless
    choices, and never misrepresent narrative text as a completed effect.

SAMPLE PSEUDOCODE (ADAPT TO PATCHER API; NOT A DROP-IN IMPLEMENTATION):

eligible = villagers.filter(v =>
    v.isPlayable && v.isAdult && v.isAvailable &&
    !v.isNursingMother && !v.isNursingFather &&
    !v.isInfant && !v.isGoldenChild && !v.isVV3Chief &&
    !v.isStoryCritical && !v.isImmortal &&
    !v.isProtected && !v.isUntargetable &&
    !v.isMissing && gameAllowsDangerousTarget(v)
)
if (eligible.length === 0) return SKIP_OR_SAFE_FALLBACK
victim = randomChoice(eligible)
if (!stillEligible(victim)) return SKIP_OR_SAFE_FALLBACK
if (applySupportedConsequence(victim)) narrateConsequence(victim)
else narrateSafeFallback()

CROSS-GAME VOICE AND CONTINUITY CHECKLIST:
- VV1: Remember the Southern Shore and the Golden Child's protected status.
- VV2: Remember the Western Shore and the community of Lost Children.
- VV3: Respect the Secret City's leadership and protect the tribal Chief.
- VV4: Give the Tree of Life and local resources narrative weight.
- VV5: Treat the Isolans as people with customs, jokes, pride, and agency;
  unconverted Heathens remain narrative NPCs where gameplay requires it.
- Do not presume all adults are married, all elders are wise, all
  children are obedient, or all skilled villagers are socially confident.
- Include mundane, quiet endings alongside absurd or dramatic ones.
- Respect the user's original writing and existing branches above.

END OF V6 ADDITIONS

================================================================================
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS
THE KEEPER OF LITTLE THINGS
Automatic event — no player choice — quiet, comic, intergenerational
================================================================================

The village's oldest (woman/man/villager), (Elder), has been sorting through
an old wooden box. Inside are (a worn carving/a faded scrap of cloth/a
smooth pebble/a tiny toy/a carefully folded note). A child, (Child),
asks why anyone would bother keeping such an ordinary object.

"Ordinary?" (Elder) says. "This belonged to (a dear friend/my mother/
a child I once cared for/someone who taught me to build)."

Nearby, (an apprentice/a Master Builder/a nursing mother/a father)
pauses to listen. The parent gently rocks (his/her) baby, who seems
far more interested in chewing the edge of a blanket than in history.

The elder tells the story of (A).

A — THE MEMORY (choose one):
- a difficult harvest when neighbors shared the little food they had
- the first time two stubborn villagers learned to work together
- a child who made an awful carving and insisted it was a masterpiece
- an old celebration whose songs are nearly forgotten
- a misunderstanding between the village and Isolan neighbors that
  ended with an unexpected act of kindness
- a beloved teacher who always said, "Try again tomorrow"
- a grand expedition that was, in truth, a very long walk to find a lost goat

B — WHAT HAPPENS NEXT (choose one):
- The child asks a hundred questions. The elder answers ninety-nine,
  then admits (he/she) has forgotten the last answer.
- The apprentice realizes that the object's odd shape records an
  ingenious building technique. The village gains a little Tech.
- The parent shares a memory from (his/her) own childhood, and the
  baby chooses that exact moment to spit up on the family keepsake.
  The object is cleaned; the story survives.
- An Isolan visitor recognizes the same song or design from (his/her)
  community. The two compare versions without insisting one is superior.
- The child is disappointed that the story contains no monsters,
  treasure, or heroic battles. The elder promises that ordinary
  people are allowed to be interesting too.
- The elder forgets an important detail. Rather than invent an answer,
  the villagers decide to ask someone else who might remember.
- The child offers the elder a new, crooked little carving to add
  to the box. The elder accepts it with surprising solemnity.

C — CODA (choose one):
- The box is closed and returned to its shelf. Tomorrow, another
  child may ask about it.
- The villagers begin collecting stories from willing elders,
  carefully marking what is remembered and what is uncertain.
- The baby falls asleep. The adults continue talking in whispers.
- The child decides that (he/she) would like to become old someday,
  if only to own a box full of mysterious objects.
- The elder laughs at an old mistake and says, "I was young once, too."

REWARDS: Small Research or Devotion experience for eligible playable
participants, or narrative-only. The elder and parent are not placed
in danger; a nursing parent may participate conversationally without
interrupting the game's nursing state. Isolan visitors are narrative NPCs.

================================================================================
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS
THE MULTICULTURAL FESTIVAL 
Four-choice multibranch event — adults, children, elders, parents, Isolans
================================================================================

A joint festival is being planned for the village and its Isolan neighbors.
The organizers promise a pleasant afternoon of music, food, games, and
friendly conversation. Unfortunately, the planning committee consists of
(Name1), who loves schedules, (Name2), who hates schedules, and an Isolan
visitor who has brought an enormous list of suggestions.

The festival will feature (A).

A — THE MAIN ATTRACTION (choose one):
- a parade of hand-carved wooden animals
- a shared feast and recipe exchange
- a storytelling circle with elders from both communities
- a children's performance
- a friendly contest of crafts and games
- a ceremonial planting of trees
- an exhibition of family keepsakes and historical objects

The trouble begins when (B).

B — THE PROBLEM (choose one):
- two activities have been scheduled for the same place and time
- the decorations arrive in completely the wrong colors
- the village's best musician refuses to perform before sunset
- an elder objects to a tradition being changed without consultation
- the children's rehearsal is louder than the actual festival
- the food table is far too small
- the Isolan organizers believe they are guests, while the villagers
  believe the Isolans are co-hosts
- someone has accidentally printed the festival's name backward

What should the village do?

CHOICE A — Follow the original schedule as closely as possible. (1)
CHOICE B — Let the two communities redesign the festival together. (2)
CHOICE C — Give the children a chance to solve the problem. (3)
CHOICE D — Let the elders and experienced organizers mediate. (4)

(1) THE SCHEDULE:
(Name1) insists that a festival without a schedule is simply a crowd.
- The organizers rearrange the activities and everything runs on time.
  Everyone is impressed, especially (Name1). (5)
- The schedule is so strict that nobody has time to enjoy anything.
  (Name2) persuades (Name1) to add a long, unscheduled break. (5)
- The Isolan visitors point out a custom that the schedule overlooks.
  The organizers apologize and make room for it. (5)
- The schedule is accidentally used to wrap a basket of fruit.
  The festival proceeds surprisingly well without it. (5)

(2) JOINT PLANNING:
Both communities gather around the table and exchange suggestions.
- They combine their traditions into a delightful new celebration. (5)
- A lively debate reveals that both sides had misunderstood what
  the other wanted. The problem is resolved with a simple question. (5)
- Every participant insists on adding one activity. The festival
  becomes so long that the organizers split it across two days. (5)
- A few people prefer the older customs. The organizers keep a
  traditional space alongside the new festivities. (5)

(3) THE CHILDREN TAKE CHARGE:
The children form a committee, appoint (Child) as its leader, and
immediately begin issuing orders to the adults.
- The children propose an ingenious layout that solves the problem. (5)
- The children decide that all activities should involve dancing.
  The adults negotiate a more reasonable compromise. (5)
- A shy child notices that the nursing parents need a quieter area.
  The organizers make space for rest and feeding. (5)
- The committee spends its entire meeting arguing about who gets
  to ring the opening bell. The adults resume planning. (5)
- The children invent a new game involving decorated coconuts.
  Nobody knows the rules, including the children. (5)

(4) THE ELDERS' COUNCIL:
An experienced elder and an Isolan elder sit down with the organizers.
- They recall how similar celebrations were handled in years past
  and propose a practical compromise. (5)
- They disagree about an old custom, then discover that both
  versions have been practiced for generations. (5)
- One elder confesses that the old festivals were just as chaotic.
  The organizers feel strangely reassured. (5)
- The elders advise everyone to eat first and argue afterward.
  It is unexpectedly effective. (5)

(5) THE FESTIVAL BEGINS — choose one:
- The celebration is lovely. Villagers and Isolans share food,
  exchange stories, and enjoy a peaceful evening. (6)
- The opening song begins at the wrong time. Half the audience
  joins in anyway, creating a surprisingly beautiful chorus. (6)
- The decorations collapse into an enormous pile of flowers.
  Nobody is hurt. The children declare it a new attraction. (6)
- The Chief or village leader gives a brief welcome. A visiting
  child loudly asks when the snacks will be served. (6)
- Two old rivals discover they have made identical festival dishes.
  They spend the evening arguing over whose tastes better. (6)
- The festival is interrupted by a sudden shower. Everyone takes
  shelter together, then resumes once the rain passes. (6)

(6) THE UNEXPECTED AWARD — choose one:
- A neutral visitor praises the cooperation between communities.
  No formal prize is awarded.
- The children award a ribbon to the most patient adult.
  The winner looks astonished.
- The Isolan guests present the village with a small handmade
  keepsake, thanking everyone for the invitation.
- The organizers award themselves a prize for surviving the planning.
  Nobody objects.
- A child gives the festival's highest honor to the village's
  most impressive coconut. The adults quietly allow it.
- There is no award ceremony at all. Everyone is too busy enjoying
  the celebration to notice.

(7) RARE DISAGREEMENT — optional after (5):
A villager and an Isolan visitor argue about whether one tradition
was represented fairly. The organizers (invite both to explain their
concerns/apologize for an oversight/agree to change next year's plans/
acknowledge that not everyone will be satisfied). The disagreement
ends without demanding that either person abandon (his/her) beliefs.

(8) QUIET ENDING:
After sunset, the guests begin to leave. A few villagers stay behind
to put away the decorations. An exhausted parent carries a sleeping
child home; another finishes washing the dishes. The elders trade
one last story before saying goodnight.

The festival was not perfect. It may not even have been especially
well organized. But people came, laughed, argued a little, and shared
something of their lives.

Tomorrow, Isola will continue on.

REWARDS: Small Devotion, Building, Farming, or Research experience
for eligible player villagers based on the selected activities; small
food or Tech reward where supported. No automatic conversion or
control of unconverted Isolans. No deadly or disappearance branches.

================================================================================
VV5 ADDITIONAL CHARACTER AND ELIGIBILITY NOTES
================================================================================
- Unconverted Heathens/Isolans participate only as offscreen narrative
  visitors, performers, organizers, and competitors; never assign them
  a playable task or skill gain.
- Include nursing mothers/fathers as full social participants without
  interrupting feeding/carrying states or assigning impossible actions.
- Children can be observant, selfish, thoughtful, noisy, funny, or
  competent; do not make them uniformly wise or adorable.
- Elders may remember, forget, disagree, tease, or admit uncertainty.
- Skilled villagers can be technically gifted yet socially awkward,
  impatient, shy, or exhausted.
- Neither of these new events may kill or remove a villager. For all
  other deadly/disappearance events, use an explicit eligibility check
  before selecting a target and exclude the Golden Child, the VV3
  Chief, nursing parents, babies, and every other engine-protected
  villager. If no eligible villager exists, skip the dangerous branch.
- Keep the two new events independent; do not force their endings
  to alter relationships or the game's main conversion story.

END OF V7 ADDITIONS

================================================================================
V8 — THE HEART OF ISOLA
EXPANDED EMOTIONS, PERSONALITIES, RELATIONSHIPS, CONSEQUENCES & NEW EVENTS
================================================================================

AUTHOR'S INTENT:
These banks and scenes are ADDITIONS, not replacements. Keep the original
events, the bizarre objects, and the lovingly ridiculous details. A quiet
afternoon is allowed to matter. A disagreement is allowed to remain unresolved.
An old person is allowed to be wrong. A child is allowed to be petty.
A brilliant scientist is allowed to be socially clueless. A parent holding
a baby is allowed to have ambitions beyond parenting. A death is not a
punchline. Nobody is required to forgive somebody who hurt them.

IMPLEMENTATION: Choose one consistent temperament per actor, then use
compatible emotional beats. For rare escalation, choose a second beat that
actually follows the first. A villager cannot be simultaneously delighted
and devastated without a narrated reason for the change. Choose one
resolution and optional aftermath, not every outcome in succession.

PERSONALITY BANK P — FIRST IMPRESSION:
P1. famously capable, privately terrified of being found inadequate
P2. a relentlessly cheerful optimist who occasionally drives others mad
P3. a prickly perfectionist who notices every crooked line
P4. a shy observer who remembers details others overlook
P5. a theatrical storyteller with no sense of when to stop
P6. a dry-witted elder who has survived too much to be impressed
P7. an earnest young apprentice who asks seventeen questions at once
P8. a quiet caretaker whose patience has limits
P9. a competitive show-off who is surprisingly gracious in defeat
P10. a sore loser who needs time alone before apologizing
P11. an affectionate parent who is running on very little sleep
P12. a proud former rival who is embarrassed to need help
P13. a practical problem-solver who dislikes ceremonies
P14. a romantic dreamer who treats ordinary tasks as grand adventures
P15. a curious child who keeps asking the question nobody wants answered
P16. a mischievous child who knows EXACTLY what (he/she) is doing
P17. a respected elder who worries about becoming irrelevant
P18. a gifted healer who cannot bear the thought of failing someone
P19. a skilled builder who is terrible at accepting criticism
P20. a brilliant researcher who forgets that other people have feelings
P21. an awkward but sincere peacemaker
P22. a villager who is generous with objects but stingy with praise
P23. an impatient worker who secretly loves teaching
P24. a once-timid adult who has begun speaking up for (himself/herself)
P25. a villager whose confidence is almost entirely bluff
P26. a former Heathen-born villager who cherishes some old traditions
P27. an elderly prankster whose innocence is highly questionable
P28. an ordinary, competent person who has had ENOUGH excitement today

PERSONALITY BANK Q — HIDDEN NEED:
Q1. to be noticed for something other than a useful skill
Q2. to make a child proud
Q3. to prove (he/she) is still needed
Q4. to apologize without making the hurt worse
