Q5. to feel part of the community without changing everything about (himself/herself)
Q6. to be left alone for a single peaceful afternoon
Q7. to win fairly, just once
Q8. to be allowed to fail without becoming a joke
Q9. to pass on something worth remembering
Q10. to be trusted with a difficult task
Q11. to know that someone would miss (him/her)
Q12. to stop being compared with an older sibling
Q13. to be praised for effort rather than a miraculous result
Q14. to laugh again after a hard season
Q15. to keep an old promise
Q16. to hear the truth, even when it is disappointing

FEELING BANK R — SHOW, DON'T JUST LABEL:
R1. (He/she) smiles too quickly, as if hoping nobody notices the trembling.
R2. The smile reaches (his/her) eyes only after a long moment.
R3. (He/she) straightens an object that was already straight.
R4. (He/she) opens (his/her) mouth, closes it, then tries again.
R5. The apology comes out in a voice so small it is almost lost.
R6. (He/she) laughs once, sharply, and immediately regrets it.
R7. (He/she) keeps working because stopping would mean crying.
R8. The anger is quiet: no shouting, just a very deliberate silence.
R9. (He/she) folds (his/her) arms, then unfolds them, unsure what to do.
R10. For once, (he/she) has no clever remark.
R11. (He/she) looks at the crowd, searching for one familiar face.
R12. (He/she) looks absurdly proud of something no larger than a pebble.
R13. (He/she) is trying not to grin and doing an appalling job of it.
R14. The relief arrives so suddenly that (he/she) has to sit down.
R15. (He/she) says "I'm fine" with the conviction of a soggy coconut.
R16. (He/she) remembers to breathe only after the answer is announced.
R17. (He/she) rubs a thumb across an old carving, lost in thought.
R18. (He/she) stares at the empty chair a little longer than necessary.
R19. (He/she) reaches for someone's hand, then waits to see if it is welcome.
R20. (He/she) is furious, and underneath the fury, terribly embarrassed.
R21. (He/she) feels a fierce, unreasonable burst of protectiveness.
R22. (He/she) had prepared a speech. Not one word of it survives the moment.
R23. The silence is comfortable, which surprises them both.
R24. (He/she) looks almost offended by how much the kindness matters.
R25. (He/she) laughs until tears come, then has to explain that they are happy.
R26. (He/she) says "Thank you" as though the words are heavier than stone.
R27. (He/she) feels proud and disappointed at once, and makes room for both.
R28. (He/she) watches the others celebrate and quietly slips away.
R29. (He/she) is too tired to be angry anymore.
R30. (He/she) waits until everyone leaves before letting (himself/herself) cry.

DIALOGUE BANK S — IN-CHARACTER LINES:
S1. "I was wrong." / "You were." / "I know."
S2. "You don't have to forgive me." / "Good. Because I'm not ready."
S3. "I didn't ask you to fix it. I asked you to listen."
S4. "That is the ugliest thing I have ever loved."
S5. "I have made this mistake before." / "Then why did you do it again?"
S6. "You can be disappointed without being cruel."
S7. "If the ancestors wanted it perfect, they should have left instructions."
S8. "I spent three days on that!" / "Yes. We can tell."
S9. "The coconut has no qualifications to judge architecture!"
S10. "You are allowed to be proud of yourself." / "Even if I lost?"
S11. "I'm not little anymore." / "I know. That's what frightens me."
S12. "I am old, not made of dust."
S13. "Please stop calling it a masterpiece. It's a chair."
S14. "It's a TERRIBLE chair." / "It's MY terrible chair."
S15. "I wanted you to see me." / "I do. I'm sorry I didn't say it."
S16. "I still disagree with you." / "Then we finally understand each other."
S17. "We can try again tomorrow." / "What if tomorrow is worse?"
    / "Then we'll decide what to do tomorrow."
S18. "I thought winning would feel better than this."
S19. "You made room for me." / "Of course." / "It wasn't always obvious."
S20. "The baby is asleep. Nobody move. Nobody SPEAK."
S21. "Why is there a wooden nose in the soup?"
S22. "I am not crying." / "Your face is leaking." / "GO AWAY."
S23. "You could have asked for help." / "I was afraid you'd say yes."
S24. "We remember things differently. That doesn't mean either memory is worthless."
S25. "No, I won't pretend it didn't hurt. But I'd like to start again."
S26. "The winner is—" / "WAIT! THAT IS THE WRONG TROPHY!"
S27. "Is that supposed to be me?" / "...How attached are you to honesty?"
S28. "You taught me to be brave." / "I was hoping you'd be sensible, too."

CONFLICT BANK T — WHAT THE ARGUMENT IS REALLY ABOUT:
T1. Recognition: one person does invisible work and resents being overlooked.
T2. Fairness: the same rule affects two villagers differently.
T3. Identity: an old tradition matters to someone even if others find it odd.
T4. Grief: a familiar task reminds someone of a person who is gone.
T5. Fear: someone makes an overbearing choice to protect a loved one.
T6. Pride: a skilled person struggles to admit an apprentice knows more.
T7. Jealousy: praise for one child sounds like criticism of another.
T8. Exhaustion: nobody is cruel, but everybody is tired.
T9. Trust: a well-meant secret creates a very real misunderstanding.
T10. Belonging: someone is invited but not actually included.
T11. Responsibility: the person who made the mess expects others to clean it.
T12. Humor: a joke that delighted one person humiliated another.

AFTERMATH BANK U — CONSEQUENCES THAT LAST WITHOUT ALTERING GAME FLAGS:
U1. For several days, the two avoid each other. Then one asks for a tool.
    The other lends it. Neither calls this reconciliation, but it is a start.
U2. The winner displays the trophy for exactly one afternoon, then puts it
    away because the village has more important things to do.
U3. The loser keeps the judging notes. Next year, (he/she) intends to improve.
U4. A child repeats the incident with increasingly inaccurate details.
    By the third retelling, a coconut has apparently become Chief.
U5. Someone quietly repairs the damaged object and leaves it on the doorstep.
U6. The organizers rewrite the rules, and for once the rules make sense.
U7. Two rivals continue to dislike each other, but stop sabotaging one another.
U8. A parent later tells the child, "I was proud before they announced the result."
U9. An elder's story is recorded, including the parts nobody can verify.
U10. The incident becomes an annual joke; the person involved is asked
     whether (he/she) actually finds it funny before anyone repeats it.
U11. Nothing changes dramatically. The next morning, someone makes breakfast.
U12. A former rival asks, very quietly, "Would you teach me how you did that?"
U13. The village remembers the person who helped, not merely the person who won.
U14. A child decides to learn a skill for pleasure instead of prizes.
U15. The repaired statue still has a crooked nose. Everyone agrees to leave it.
U16. The two communities part without a grand speech, but greet each other
     more warmly the next time they meet.

VV1 — THE MASTER'S SMALL APPRENTICE
TWO CHOICES; children, skilled adults, elders; warm/comic/bittersweet
================================================================================

A respected Master Builder, (Builder), has been asked to repair a small
wooden bench outside the school. The job is simple enough that (he/she)
expects to finish before lunch.

Unfortunately, (Child) has decided to help.

"Why are you doing it like that?" asks (Child).
"Because it works."
"But why?"
"Because—" The builder pauses. "Because it works."

An elderly villager, (Elder), watches from a nearby seat, looking
far too entertained.

The child notices that the bench has (a wobbling leg/a loose joint/
a splintered edge/a crooked support) and proposes (a ridiculous
solution/a clever fix/a solution copied from something (he/she)
saw the builder do earlier).

CHOICE A — LET THE CHILD TRY WITH SUPERVISION. (1)
CHOICE B — FINISH THE REPAIR, THEN EXPLAIN IT. (2)

(1) THE APPRENTICE'S TURN — choose one:
- The child's idea works! The builder looks surprised, then proud. (3)
- The child's idea fails harmlessly. The builder helps (him/her)
  understand why, and they try a better method together. (3)
- The child hammers the wrong piece. The elder laughs so hard
  that (he/she) nearly falls off the old bench. (3)
- The child gets frustrated and declares that building is stupid.
  The builder admits that (he/she) has felt that way too. (3)
- The child carefully measures the wood and discovers the builder
  was about to cut the wrong length. The builder goes very quiet. (3)

(2) THE BUILDER'S TURN — choose one:
- The builder repairs the bench neatly, then lets the child
  practice on spare wood. The child beams. (3)
- The builder works too quickly to explain. The child wanders
  away, disappointed. The elder gently points this out. (3)
- The builder tries to demonstrate a perfect joint and
  accidentally glues (his/her) own fingers together. (3)
- The builder finishes, then admits that the child's question
  exposed a flaw in the original design. (3)

(3) THE ELDER'S OBSERVATION — choose one:
- "You can teach a child to hold a tool," the elder says.
  "The harder part is teaching (him/her) not to fear mistakes."
- "When I was young, my teacher shouted whenever I got something
  wrong. I remember the shouting better than the lessons."
- "I once built a whole hut backward," the elder confesses.
  "Nobody noticed until we tried to put the door in."
- "That bench will outlast the argument," the elder says.
  The builder hopes this is true.

(4) ENDING — choose one:
- The child proudly sits on the finished bench. It holds.
  For the moment, that is enough.
- The builder asks the child to help again tomorrow.
  The child replies, "Only if you answer my questions."
- The elder leaves a tiny carved wooden bird on the bench.
  Nobody knows when (he/she) made it.
- The bench collapses under a basket of coconuts. Nobody is
  injured. The builder stares at the wreckage and sighs,
  "Well. That's a useful test."
- The child falls asleep on the newly repaired bench.
  The builder quietly gathers the tools and lets (him/her) rest.

Reward: modest Building experience to the eligible builder,
small learning experience for child if supported; no death branch.

VV1 — THE LAST SONG BEFORE SUNSET
NO CHOICE; elder, parent, child; tender with optional laughter
================================================================================

As the sun begins to set, an elderly villager, (Elder), sits near the
village's gathering place, humming an old melody.

A child, (Child), asks where the song came from.

(Elder) says that (he/she) learned it from (a parent/a childhood friend/
a teacher/someone who is no longer alive). The song was once sung
(during storms/at weddings/to soothe frightened children/
while waiting for boats to return).

A nearby parent, (Parent), pauses with a sleeping baby in (his/her)
arms. (He/she) recognizes (one line/the rhythm/the melody/
none of it, but listens anyway).

DEVELOPMENT — choose one:
- The elder forgets a verse. The parent remembers it, and they
  finish the song together.
- The child invents an absurd extra verse about a dancing crab.
  The elder is scandalized for precisely three seconds.
- The elder explains that the song used to be sung by someone
  (he/she) misses. The child listens without interrupting.
- The baby wakes and begins crying. The parent hums the melody,
  and the baby gradually settles.
- The elder sings terribly off-key. The child joins in, somehow
  making the song even worse. Nobody asks them to stop.
- The child asks whether the elder will always remember the song.
  The elder says, "I hope so. But perhaps you can remember it too."

ENDING — choose one:
- The song ends. Nobody speaks for a while.
- The child promises to teach the song to other children.
- The parent thanks the elder, then carries the baby home.
- The elder laughs and says the ancestors would be horrified
  by the new crab verse. The child looks immensely proud.
- The elder hums one last line, quietly enough that only
  the nearest listeners hear it.

This event does not imply the elder is about to die.
Reward: small Devotion experience or narrative-only.

VV2 — THE PACKED HOSPITAL
FOUR CHOICES; ordinary medical work, nursing parents, comedy, care
================================================================================

The hospital is unusually busy. Nobody is in immediate danger,
but everything that can go wrong seems determined to do so.

A healer, (Healer), is trying to organize supplies. A young
apprentice, (Apprentice), has misplaced (A). Meanwhile, a parent
holding a sleepy infant is waiting for a routine consultation,
and an elderly villager has come in to complain about (B).

A — MISSING ITEM:
- a roll of clean bandages
- the hospital's only working measuring stick
- a basket of freshly washed cloths
- the inventory ledger
- a very important wooden spoon used for mixing harmless salves

B — ELDER'S COMPLAINT:
- an aching knee
- a sore shoulder
- a persistent cough that warrants assessment
- a headache that has already improved
- the fact that nobody listens when (he/she) says the benches are awful

The healer has to decide what to address first. Stable patients
are triaged according to medical need; the options below concern
organization and comfort, not delaying urgent treatment.

CHOICE A — ORGANIZE THE SUPPLIES. (1)
CHOICE B — ASK THE APPRENTICE TO LEAD THE SEARCH. (2)
CHOICE C — INVITE THE ELDER TO HELP IMPROVE THE WAITING AREA. (3)
CHOICE D — ASK THE PARENT WHAT WOULD MAKE THE WAIT EASIER. (4)

(1) THE SUPPLY SHELVES:
- The missing item is found exactly where it belongs.
  The apprentice looks personally betrayed by the shelf. (5)
- The healer creates a clear labeling system. The entire
  hospital suddenly seems less chaotic. (5)
- A stack of empty baskets falls over. The noise wakes the baby.
  Everyone freezes. (5)

(2) THE APPRENTICE:
- The apprentice finds the item and earns a quiet word of praise. (5)
- The apprentice admits (he/she) moved it to "somewhere safe"
  and has forgotten where that is. (5)
- The apprentice asks for help rather than pretending to know.
  The healer is impressed. (5)

(3) THE ELDER:
- The elder suggests a simple repair to the waiting bench.
  The healer thanks (him/her). (5)
- The elder offers a long story about how hospitals used to
  work. Some of it is useful. Much of it is not. (5)
- The elder declares the hospital's décor depressing and
  offers to carve a cheerful wooden sign. (5)

(4) THE PARENT:
- The parent asks for a quiet corner to feed the baby.
  The staff make room without treating the request as a nuisance. (5)
- The parent says (he/she) would simply like to sit down.
  A villager immediately offers a seat. (5)
- The parent jokes that the baby has better organizational
  skills than the entire hospital. The healer cannot argue. (5)

(5) THE LAST STRAW — choose one:
- The healer discovers that the missing inventory ledger
  was under (his/her) own arm all along.
- A child wanders in carrying the missing spoon, asking
  why everyone is looking for the "tiny paddle."
- The elder's carved sign reads "HOSPITAL: PLEASE REMAIN ALIVE."
  The healer requests a revision.
- The baby laughs for the first time all afternoon.
  Every exhausted adult in the room smiles.
- Nothing else goes wrong. The healer finds this suspicious.

(6) ENDING — choose one:
- The hospital returns to its usual routine. The healer
  finally sits down for a moment.
- The apprentice writes a new inventory list in enormous
  letters so nobody can possibly miss it.
- The elder leaves satisfied that somebody listened.
- The parent thanks the staff and carries the baby home.
- The healer says, "Tomorrow will be quieter." Nobody believes it.

Reward: modest Healing experience; optional Tech for inventory improvement.
No death or disappearance in this event.

VV2 — THE TIMID CHILD
TWO CHOICES; Lost Children, belonging, fear, gentle/bittersweet
================================================================================

A young villager, (Child), has been avoiding the other children.
When a caregiver, (Adult), asks what is wrong, (Child) admits
that (he/she) is afraid of (A).

A — THE FEAR:
- being left behind when the older children go exploring
- forgetting the faces of people from (his/her) old home
- being laughed at for making mistakes
- growing up and leaving the nursery
- failing at a skill that everyone else seems to understand
- being told that crying is childish

(Adult) (sits beside the child/keeps a respectful distance/
offers a cup of water/waits quietly for (him/her) to continue).

CHOICE A — TELL A STORY ABOUT BEING AFRAID. (1)
CHOICE B — ASK WHAT THE CHILD NEEDS RIGHT NOW. (2)

(1) THE STORY — choose one:
- The adult recalls a time when (he/she) was frightened
  and somebody helped. The child asks what happened next. (3)
- The adult admits that (he/she) still gets scared sometimes.
  The child looks surprised. (3)
- The adult tells a ridiculous story about mistaking a
  harmless crab for a terrible monster. The child giggles. (3)
- The adult talks too much. The child finally interrupts:
  "I didn't want a story. I wanted you to stay." (3)

(2) THE QUESTION — choose one:
- The child asks for company, not advice. They sit together. (3)
- The child wants to try the frightening activity with help. (3)
- The child says (he/she) doesn't know. The adult says that
  not knowing is an acceptable answer. (3)
- The child asks whether the adult will be angry if (he/she)
  cries. The adult says no. (3)

(3) ENDING — choose one:
- The child joins the other children for a short while,
  then returns when (he/she) has had enough.
- The child stays beside the adult until sunset.
  No grand breakthrough occurs, but (he/she) feels safer.
- The child makes a new friend who is afraid of the same thing.
- The child says, "Maybe I can be brave tomorrow."
  The adult replies, "You don't have to do everything tomorrow."
- The child takes the adult's hand, then lets go when ready.

Reward: narrative-only or small child learning experience.
Do not imply fear disappears instantly.

VV3 — THE UNFORTUNATE PORTRAIT
FOUR CHOICES; Chief protected; art, authority, pride, embarrassment
================================================================================

The villagers have decided to commission a portrait of their Chief
to commemorate an important achievement.

The Chief (politely objects/pretends not to care/is secretly thrilled/
worries the whole thing is unnecessary).

The artist, (Artist), is (a talented but impatient adult/a shy
apprentice/an elder with a very distinctive style/a Master Builder
who has never painted anything in (his/her) life).

When the portrait is unveiled, it depicts the Chief with (A).

A — ARTISTIC PROBLEM:
- a nose of astonishing proportions
- a magnificent mane of hair that the Chief does not possess
- an expression of profound constipation
- six fingers on one hand
- an enormous ceremonial hat that nobody has ever seen
- a tiny body and an inexplicably enormous head
- a suspicious resemblance to the artist's pet lizard
- a heroic pose that makes the Chief look ready to attack the sun

The crowd falls silent.

CHOICE A — PRAISE THE EFFORT AND REQUEST A REVISION. (1)
CHOICE B — DISPLAY IT AS AN UNUSUAL WORK OF ART. (2)
CHOICE C — ASK THE ARTIST TO EXPLAIN THE SYMBOLISM. (3)
CHOICE D — LET THE CHIEF DECIDE. (4)

(1) REVISION:
- The artist is relieved and fixes the obvious mistakes. (5)
- The artist is offended, then admits that the hat really
  was a bit much. (5)
- The revised portrait looks even less like the Chief.
  Everyone agrees to stop while they are ahead. (5)

(2) DISPLAY:
- The children love the portrait. The Chief slowly grows fond of it. (5)
- The Chief accepts the joke with dignity, but requests
  a less prominent location. (5)
- The portrait becomes a famous village curiosity. (5)

(3) EXPLANATION:
- The artist explains a thoughtful symbolic choice that
  the audience had misunderstood. (5)
- The artist says, "The enormous nose represents wisdom."
  The Chief replies, "Does it." (5)
- The artist admits (he/she) simply cannot draw noses. (5)

(4) THE CHIEF'S DECISION:
- The Chief thanks the artist and asks for a small revision. (5)
- The Chief displays the portrait proudly, flaws and all. (5)
- The Chief declines to display it publicly but keeps it
  as a gift, explaining the choice kindly. (5)
- The Chief declares that the village needs fewer portraits
  and more benches. The artist offers to paint a bench. (5)

(5) ENDING — choose one:
- The portrait hangs in a quiet corner, where visitors
  occasionally stop to admire—or question—it.
- The artist and Chief share a laugh after the crowd leaves.
- A child produces a much better drawing in five minutes.
  The artist asks for lessons.
- The Chief privately admits that the portrait captures
  something true, even if the nose is unforgivable.
- The portrait is put away carefully, not destroyed.
  Someday it may be a cherished reminder of this moment.

Chief is an observer/decision-maker only; never targeted for
death, disappearance, transformation, or forced movement.
Reward: small Research or Building experience for eligible artist.

VV3 — THE TALE-TELLING ELDER
NO CHOICE; Nature/Magic compatible; elders, children, parents, grief and hope
================================================================================

One evening, a child asks an elder why the village tells
the same stories every year.

"Because," the elder says, "one day there will be people
who weren't here when they happened."

A nursing parent settles nearby with a baby. A researcher
puts down (his/her) notes. A builder, still covered in dust,
sits at the edge of the gathering.

The elder begins a story about (A).

A — THE MEMORY:
- the first night the villagers spent in the Secret City
- a storm that frightened everyone but brought them closer
- a friend who taught the elder an important skill
- a disagreement that once seemed impossible to resolve
- the day the village finally found enough food
- the first child born after a difficult season
- an old mistake that the elder can finally laugh about
- someone who is gone, but whose kindness is remembered

DEVELOPMENT — choose one:
- The child asks whether the elder was ever afraid.
  "Of course," (he/she) replies. "I just kept going."
- The researcher gently corrects one detail. The elder
  laughs: "Then you tell it next year."
- The parent notices the baby has fallen asleep.
  The story continues in softer voices.
- The builder admits (he/she) had forgotten the story.
  The elder says, "That's why we tell it."
- A child asks a painfully funny question at the most
  solemn moment. The elder laughs first.
- The elder falls quiet at a difficult memory.
  Nobody rushes (him/her) to continue.

ENDING — choose one:
- The gathering disperses slowly, with villagers still talking.
- The child asks to hear the story again tomorrow.
- The elder goes home feeling less alone.
- The researcher writes down the story, leaving room for
  different versions.
- Nobody says anything profound. Someone offers the elder
  a warm meal, and (he/she) accepts.

Rewards: small Devotion or Research experience.
No Chief removal; no dangerous gameplay effects.

VV4 — THE FRUITY MASTERPIECE
FOUR CHOICES; skilled cooks, elders, children, nursing parents, absurdity
================================================================================

The village has harvested a particularly impressive breadfruit.
It is so large that three villagers insist on measuring it.

A talented cook, (Cook), announces that (he/she) will prepare
a dish worthy of the Tree of Life itself.

An elder, (Elder), insists the fruit should be cooked the old way.
A child, (Child), wants to turn it into a sculpture.
A parent carrying an infant would simply like everyone to
stop arguing so the family can eat.

CHOICE A — FOLLOW THE ELDER'S RECIPE. (1)
CHOICE B — LET THE COOK EXPERIMENT. (2)
CHOICE C — LET THE CHILD DECORATE THE SERVING AREA. (3)
CHOICE D — MAKE TWO SMALLER DISHES AND COMPARE THEM. (4)

(1) THE OLD RECIPE:
- The dish is comforting and delicious. The elder looks pleased. (5)
- The cook discovers that the elder has been omitting
  a crucial ingredient for years. They laugh about it. (5)
- The dish is plain but nourishing. The parent thanks
  the cooks and finally gets to eat. (5)

(2) THE EXPERIMENT:
- The cook invents a delicious new dish. (5)
- The dish is spectacularly ugly but tastes wonderful. (5)
- The cook uses too much spice. Everyone drinks water
  and politely avoids a second helping. (5)
- The experiment fails. The cook salvages a simpler
  meal rather than wasting the whole harvest. (5)

(3) THE CHILD'S ART:
- The child creates beautiful decorations from leaves. (5)
- The child carves a tiny face into a spare piece of
  cooked breadfruit. The elder calls it disrespectful.
  The parent calls it adorable. (5)
- The child makes a breadfruit crown and insists the
  Chief of Breadfruit must be addressed properly. (5)
- The decorations are accidentally served as garnish.
  The cooks remove the inedible pieces before anyone eats. (5)

(4) THE TASTING:
- Both recipes are delicious. The villagers enjoy the variety. (5)
- The elder's recipe wins narrowly. The cook requests
  the exact measurements for next time. (5)
- The new recipe wins. The elder is disappointed but
  asks for a small portion to take home. (5)
- A child prefers plain breadfruit and refuses to
  participate in the judging. (5)

(5) ENDING — choose one:
- The feast is a success. Everyone gets enough to eat.
- The cook and elder exchange recipes, with a few
  affectionate complaints about each other's methods.
- The parent finally sits down with the baby and a
  warm meal. The entire village is ordered not to
  interrupt for five minutes.
- The breadfruit sculpture is displayed for one afternoon,
  then eaten because it is, after all, food.
- The cook admits that a meal doesn't have to be
  impressive to be worth sharing.

Reward: modest food and Farming experience if supported.

VV4 — THE QUIET GARDENER
TWO CHOICES; elder, disabled villager if present, children, tenderness
================================================================================

An older villager, (Elder), has been tending a small garden
near the Tree of Life. The garden is not particularly productive,
but (he/she) visits it every morning.

A younger villager, (Young), asks why.

"Because I planted it with someone I loved," says the elder.

(Young) notices that the path is difficult to walk, especially
for villagers who move more slowly or need assistance.

CHOICE A — IMPROVE THE PATH WITH THE ELDER'S PERMISSION. (1)
CHOICE B — ASK THE ELDER TO TELL THE GARDEN'S STORY. (2)

(1) THE PATH:
- The villagers lay a stable, accessible path, taking care
  not to damage the plants. The elder thanks them. (3)
- The first design would uproot an important plant.
  The elder explains why it matters; the builders revise it. (3)
- A child decorates the path with pebbles. The result is
  charming but uneven. The builders adjust it for safety. (3)
- The elder insists (he/she) can manage alone. The younger
  villager respects the answer and offers help if wanted. (3)

(2) THE STORY:
- The elder recalls a friend who planted the first seeds. (3)
- The elder admits the garden was originally a failed
  experiment. "It was ugly. We loved it anyway." (3)
- The elder laughs about an argument over where to plant
  a single flower. The argument lasted three days. (3)
- The elder says some memories are private. The younger
  villager accepts that without pressing. (3)

(3) ENDING — choose one:
- The younger villager visits again the next morning.
  This time, they work quietly side by side.
- The elder gives the child a seed and explains how to care for it.
- The garden remains small. Its meaning does not.
- The elder sits in the shade, listening to the leaves.
- A flower blooms in an unexpected place. The elder smiles,
  and for a moment looks very young.

Reward: small Farming or Building experience if supported.
No implication that age or disability makes someone useless.

VV5 — GIFT OR INSULT?
FOUR CHOICES; player villagers and offscreen Isolan visitors
================================================================================

An Isolan neighbor arrives with a gift for the village:
a carefully carved wooden figure depicting (A).

A — THE FIGURE:
- the village leader with an enormous nose
- a healer holding what looks like a dead fish
- a builder standing beside a collapsed hut
- a researcher surrounded by crooked numbers
- two children arguing over a coconut
- an elder asleep beneath a tree
- a ceremonial dancer with an unfortunately large backside

The visitor looks delighted. (Player villager) looks horrified.

"Is this supposed to be a joke?" (he/she) asks.

The Isolan visitor (looks confused/smiles proudly/
realizes something is wrong but cannot guess what).

CHOICE A — ASK WHAT THE CARVING MEANS. (1)
CHOICE B — ACCEPT IT POLITELY AND DISCUSS IT LATER. (2)
CHOICE C — EXPLAIN WHY IT MIGHT BE HURTFUL. (3)
CHOICE D — RESPOND WITH A HUMOROUS GIFT. (4)

(1) EXPLANATION:
- The carving is a symbol of strength, and the exaggerated
  feature is considered an honor in the visitor's tradition. (5)
- The figure depicts a famous old story, not the person
  the villager assumed. (5)
- The visitor admits (he/she) is simply terrible at faces. (5)
- The visitor intended a gentle joke and apologizes
  when told it caused embarrassment. (5)

(2) PRIVATE DISCUSSION:
- The villagers discover the carving has a meaningful
  history and decide to display it. (5)
- The recipient admits (he/she) doesn't want it displayed.
  The group respects that choice. (5)
- The misunderstanding is resolved with a quiet conversation. (5)

(3) HONEST RESPONSE:
- The visitor apologizes sincerely and offers to remake it. (5)
- The visitor is hurt that the gift was misunderstood.
  Both sides take time to explain their feelings. (5)
- The recipient realizes (he/she) jumped to conclusions,
  and apologizes in return. (5)
- Neither side agrees about whether the joke was appropriate,
  but they part respectfully. (5)

(4) THE RETURN GIFT:
- The villagers carve a friendly caricature of the visitor.
  The visitor laughs until (he/she) cries. (5)
- The return gift is much more insulting than intended.
  The villagers immediately apologize. (5)
- The visitors propose an annual exchange of deliberately
  terrible portraits. Both communities agree. (5)
- The neutral judge from the woodworking contest is
  invited to decide whose carving is worse. The judge
  refuses to get involved. (5)

(5) ENDING — choose one:
- The carving is displayed with a note explaining its meaning.
- The gift is accepted privately, without a public display.
- The visitor returns another day with a revised carving.
- The recipient and visitor laugh about the misunderstanding
  over a shared meal.
- They do not become close friends, but each has learned
  something about the other's perspective.

Rewards: modest Building or Devotion experience for eligible
player villagers; NPC Isolans never receive playable skill flags.

================================================================================
VV5 — THE LITTLE THINGS THEY REMEMBER
NO CHOICE; aging, children, parents, former rivals, bittersweet
================================================================================

A former Heathen-born villager and an Isolan visitor meet near
the edge of the village. They recognize each other from years ago.

For a moment, neither speaks.

The visitor notices (A).

A — SMALL CHANGE:
- the villager now carries a baby
- the villager has gray in (his/her) hair
- the villager has become a skilled healer
- the villager has a child who looks just like (him/her)
- the villager walks with a little more care than before
- the villager wears a small keepsake from an earlier home
- the villager has learned to carve beautiful wooden toys

"You're different," says the visitor.

"So are you," replies the villager.

MEMORY — choose one:
- They recall a foolish childhood argument about who could
  climb the tallest tree.
- They remember an old teacher who was far stricter than
  either of them thought necessary.
- They laugh about a terrible meal they once prepared together.
- They remember a difficult season, and someone who helped
  them survive it.
- They speak of a friend who is no longer there. For a while,
  neither knows what to say.
- They realize they remember the same event differently.
  Neither insists on having the final word.

ENDING — choose one:
- The visitor asks to meet the villager's family.
  They spend a pleasant afternoon together.
- The two exchange a few stories, then go their separate ways.
  It is enough.
- A child interrupts with an extremely personal question.
  Both adults laugh, grateful for the interruption.
- The villager offers a small keepsake. The visitor accepts it
  with both hands.
- "I thought you had forgotten me," the visitor admits.
  "Never," says the villager.
- They part without resolving every difference between them.
  The next meeting will be easier.

No conversion changes. No death/disappearance. Narrative-only
or small Devotion reward to eligible player villager.

================================================================================
RARE EMOTIONAL CODAS — ATTACH TO COMPATIBLE EXISTING EVENTS
================================================================================

C1 — THE PRIZE WASN'T THE POINT
After the competition, the winner discovers that the loser has
already gone home. The winner (finds (him/her) to offer a sincere
compliment/decides to give (him/her) space/asks the judges to
acknowledge the loser's contribution).

C2 — THE ADULT APOLOGIZES TO THE CHILD
The adult kneels to the child's eye level.
"I was impatient. You didn't deserve that."
The child (forgives (him/her)/says (he/she) is still upset/
asks whether they can try again tomorrow).
The adult accepts the answer.

C3 — THE CHILD DOESN'T FORGIVE
The child hears the apology but shakes (his/her) head.
"Not yet."
The adult says, "Alright. I will do better whether or not
you're ready to forgive me."
No forced reconciliation occurs.

C4 — THE ELDER WAS WRONG
An elder gives advice that turns out to be mistaken.
At first, (he/she) insists the younger villagers must have
misunderstood. Then the evidence becomes impossible to ignore.
"Well," the elder says. "I suppose being old doesn't make
me right about everything."
A child asks whether this means the elder will stop giving advice.
"Absolutely not."

C5 — THE PARENT'S FIVE MINUTES
A nursing parent finally finds a quiet place to sit with the baby.
Someone approaches with a request, sees the exhausted expression,
and quietly turns away. For five glorious minutes, nobody needs
anything else from (him/her).

C6 — THE LOSER'S PRIVATE VICTORY
The losing contestant looks at (his/her) finished work.
It is better than anything (he/she) could have made last year.
No trophy can change that.

C7 — THE FRIEND WHO WASN'T THERE
Someone mentions a former villager who would have loved the event.
The conversation goes quiet. Then one person recalls a ridiculous
thing the absent friend once said. They laugh, and the laughter
does not erase the sadness.

C8 — THE FURY
A competitor discovers that a rival has knowingly cheated.
"Don't you DARE call it a misunderstanding," (he/she) says.
The culprit is given a chance to explain, but the organizers
do not excuse the wrongdoing merely because the culprit
feels ashamed. The result is corrected; restitution is required
where appropriate. Forgiveness is optional.

C9 — THE WRONG WINNER
The judges announce the wrong name. The crowd cheers.
The real winner stands frozen. When the mistake is discovered,
the judges correct it publicly and apologize to BOTH villagers.
The mistaken winner is allowed to feel disappointed without
being blamed for the judges' error.

C10 — THE TINY KINDNESS
A child notices that an elder has been standing for a long time.
Without being asked, the child brings a stool. The elder says
nothing at first, then reaches over and pats the child's hand.

C11 — THE JOKE WENT TOO FAR
A villager laughs at a competitor's mistake. The competitor
looks genuinely hurt. The laughter dies down. The joker
apologizes, not with a grand speech but by acknowledging
exactly what was unkind. The competitor may accept, decline,
or simply change the subject.

C12 — THE IMPOSSIBLE COMPLIMENT
A famously critical Master Builder examines an apprentice's work.
After a long silence, (he/she) says, "That joint is acceptable."
The apprentice looks as though (he/she) has been crowned ruler
of the entire island.

C13 — THE MOTHER OR FATHER WHO WANTS TO BE SEEN
A parent finishes a project while caring for an infant and
hears someone praise only the baby's cuteness.
The parent smiles, then says, "Thank you. I worked hard on
the project, too."
The other villager recognizes the oversight and offers
a genuine compliment.

C14 — THE RIDICULOUS HERO
A villager claims to have saved the entire competition by
catching a falling coconut. Another points out that the
coconut was rolling slowly along the ground.
"It was a VERY determined coconut," comes the reply.

C15 — THE QUIET TABLE
After a long day of arguments, the rivals sit down to eat.
They do not discuss the contest. One passes the salt.
The other says, "Thanks."
Nothing is solved. Something is less difficult.

C16 — THE GROWING CHILD
An elder watches a child demonstrate a skill (he/she)
once taught. The child's hands move with confidence.
"You're getting good at this," the elder says.
"I had a good teacher," the child replies.
The elder looks away before the child can see the tears.

================================================================================
STRICT TARGET SAFETY & CONTINUITY FILTER — ALL FIVE GAMES
================================================================================

BEFORE ANY DEATH OR DISAPPEARANCE EVENT:
1. Build candidate list ONLY from actual, selectable, currently present
   villagers whose game state permits the proposed consequence.
2. Exclude Golden Child (VV1); the tribal Chief or any Chief-protected
   character (VV3); babies/infants; nursing mothers AND fathers;
   villagers holding/caring for infants; and every game-specific protected
   role, quest-critical character, or engine-excluded state.
3. Exclude any villager for whom removal would corrupt an ongoing action,
   childbirth, nursing, scripted sequence, quest, or protected animation.
4. Apply any additional title-specific restrictions enforced by the game.
5. Never infer eligibility from age, skill, or narrative role alone.
6. If no eligible candidates remain, SKIP the lethal/disappearance branch
   entirely and select a nonlethal outcome or do not trigger the event.
7. Only after eligibility is established may the event name its target.
8. For events involving an infant, parent, Golden Child, or Chief, default
   to narrative, safe outcomes unless the actual game explicitly supports
   the exact nonlethal effect.
9. Never narrate a permanent removal without executing the matching
   supported engine operation; never apply the operation to a protected
   character, even if the prose seems to allow it.
10. Do not use protected characters as random casualties in another
    character's event merely to create emotional impact.

CHARACTER WRITING CHECKLIST:
- What does this person want? What do they fear losing?
- What can they do well, and what are they bad at?
- What would embarrass them? What would make them unexpectedly kind?
- Do they behave differently with children, peers, elders, or rivals?
- What would they say if nobody were watching?
- Can they be wrong without being humiliated? Can they be funny
  without becoming a caricature?
- If the story is sad, whose feelings are we actually following?
- If the story is funny, who is the joke on, and is it cruel?
- Does the ending leave room for tomorrow?

THE HEART OF ISOLA:
A child learns a new word. An elder forgets one.
A builder repairs a bench. A healer sits down at last.
A rival loses gracefully—or does not.
Someone laughs at a coconut. Someone misses a friend.
A parent carries a sleeping baby home.
The fire burns low. The village grows quiet.

Tomorrow, there will be more stories.

Isola continues on. <3


================================================================================
V9 — THE AGE OF TERRIBLE IDEAS
An original expansion for VV1–VV5. Append-only: prior text remains intact.
================================================================================

CANON & GAMEPLAY OVERRIDES (apply to earlier event text where inconsistent):
* VV1: Southern Shore. VV2: Western Shore. Neither population knows about
  the Secret City or its Chief. A village authority is a LEADER, not Chief.
* VV3: The Secret City and its tribal Chief may be mentioned. The Chief is
  protected and may appear as judge/mediator but NEVER be removed.
* VV4: Refer to village LEADERS, not a Chief. Do not import the Secret City
  as common knowledge. The Tree of Life is appropriate.
* VV5 Guardians of Harmony patch: call the formerly named Heathens ISOLANS
  throughout in-world narrative. Isolan visitors are narrative NPCs, not
  controllable unconverted villagers. 'Guardians of Harmony' is the patch name.
* A 'court', 'jail', 'prison', or 'sentence' below is an EVENT STORY device,
  not a claim that the vanilla game supports prisons or criminal mechanics.
  Never remove or freeze a playable villager unless the patch implements and
  validates it. Default imprisonment to a narrated hearing, temporary
  supervised community work, or a non-gameplay offscreen sentence.
* Protected targets: Golden Child (VV1), Chief (VV3), nursing mothers,
  fathers, infants, carried infants, quest-critical and any other
  engine-protected villagers are NEVER eligible for death/disappearance.
  If no eligible targets, reroll to nonlethal branch. Children may be
  witnesses, artists and pranksters, not arbitrarily sentenced to prison.
* Animal materials and grotesque foods are COMEDIC FICTION. Never claim
  eyeballs, guano, slugs, raw entrails, or random shell powder are safe
  foods. Where used as food, the dish is rejected, discarded or exposed
  as an outrageous misunderstanding before anyone eats it.

MATERIAL BANK M — DRAW ONE, OR TWO FOR A VERY RARE DISASTER:
M01 crushed-up snail shells — plaster filler, decorative powder, fake pearls
M02 tree bark — rough roofing, paper, stiff ceremonial collar, bitter 'tea'
M03 volcanic ash — dark pigment, ceramic additive, fake baking flour
M04 tough vines — lashings, bridges, braided hats, a knot nobody can undo
M05 animal rawhide — drum skin, tough sandals, spectacularly awful blanket
M06 animal entrails — fishing bait, grotesque art, an alarming wrapped parcel
M07 pulverized gemstones — extravagant pigment, glitter, ruinous 'seasoning'
M08 polished pearls — mosaics, inlay, marbles, priceless misplaced counters
M09 eyeballs — theatrical prop, anatomical specimen, unsettling decoration
M10 spider silk — delicate thread, miniature hammock, expensive embroidery
M11 rotten wood — fake antique, collapsing stool, suspiciously soft sculpture
M12 fermented yogurt — pungent paint experiment, sauce, unwise glue substitute
M13 yeast — dough, foaming fermentation experiment, 'living' sculpture
M14 slugs — race entrants, reluctant garden guests, horrible fake garnish
M15 slug slime — dubious polish, slippery adhesive, disastrous hair gel
M16 cave guano — fertilizer, suspicious dark powder, worst perfume ever made

MATERIAL TEXTURE BANK:
* crunchy / powdery / stringy / gelatinous / suspiciously damp / gritty /
  alarmingly glossy / unpleasantly warm / flaky / magnificently smelly /
  sticky enough to catch a coconut / surprisingly elegant / absolutely vile

SMELL BANK:
* wet stone / old socks / sour milk / a forgotten fishing basket /
  a cave that should have stayed unexplored / burnt toast / seawater /
  an inexplicably luxurious perfume / a goat

WHY DID THEY USE IT? BANK:
* 'The ancestors would have appreciated innovation.'
* 'The recipe said to add something crunchy.'
* 'It was free.'
* 'I was testing the limits of science.'
* 'The other team said I couldn't.'
* 'I mistook the jar for flour.'
* 'It sparkled.'
* 'I wanted to be remembered.'
* 'I was trying to impress my child.'
* 'I genuinely believed it would work.'
* 'The elder told me to. The elder denies everything.'
* 'I was angry and wanted the rival's invention to fail.'

COMPLICATION BANK:
* The mixture sets rock-hard around a spoon.
* A child gives the invention a better name than its inventor.
* An elder identifies a forgotten traditional use—then adds, 'Not THAT way.'
* The material was borrowed without asking, not stolen.
* The 'gemstones' turn out to be painted pebbles.
* The supposed 'eyeballs' are carved beads; the accuser refuses to apologize.
* The slug is declared an official witness and crawls off the table.
* A priceless pearl is found sewn into a completely unrelated garment.
* The creator's own notes prove the alleged sabotage was self-inflicted.
* The prototype works, but nobody can stand the smell.
* The finished object is gorgeous, and utterly unusable.
* The accused is innocent, but guilty of an unrelated minor prank.
* Three people confess, and none describes the actual incident accurately.
* A rival who hates the inventor quietly repairs the prototype overnight.

REACTION BANK (keep personalities consistent):
* The perfectionist turns red, then begins correcting the witness's grammar.
* The elderly skeptic says, 'I've lived long enough to see many fools.
  You're making a strong bid for first place.'
* The apprentice laughs, then realizes nobody else is laughing.
* The parent shifts the sleeping baby and says, 'I have been awake for
  thirty hours. Please tell me this isn't about a spoon.'
* The accused folds their arms. 'I would NEVER steal that hideous thing.'
* The inventor whispers, 'I thought you were my friend.'
* The rival says, 'I still hate your design. I didn't destroy it.'
* The leader asks everyone to speak one at a time. Nobody does.
* The child says, 'Are adults always like this?' The elder says, 'Worse.'
* The healer asks if anybody has actually been hurt. Nobody answers.
