VV1 — THE WALL
COMPLEX: four choices, two rival builders, witnesses, trial, reconciliation
================================================================================

Two builders, (Name1) and (Name2), have disliked each other ever since
(Name1) called (Name2)'s favorite bench 'an architectural apology.'

Now (Name1) unveils a revolutionary wall coating made from (MATERIAL).
(Name2) declares it (a masterpiece/a fraud/a waste of good materials/
a personal attack on the discipline of Building).

At sunrise, the wall has (cracked/turned purple/started shedding flakes/
acquired a horrifying smell/become unexpectedly beautiful).
(Name1) accuses (Name2) of sabotage. (Name2) is so furious that
(he/she) kicks an EMPTY basket across the workshop.

CHOICE A — Investigate the evidence. (1)
CHOICE B — Let the builders confront one another before the leader. (2)
CHOICE C — Ask the apprentices and children what they saw. (3)
CHOICE D — Separate the rivals and repair the wall first. (4)

(1) INVESTIGATION — choose one:
* Tool marks suggest deliberate interference, but the marks match the
  inventor's own tools. (5)
* The missing ingredient is discovered in (Name2)'s workshop; a receipt
  proves (he/she) bought an identical batch. (5)
* The wall failed because the mixture was poorly prepared. (6)
* A third builder secretly altered it to win a commission. (7)
* Nothing is conclusive. The accusation must not become a conviction. (8)

(2) THE HEARING:
The village leader convenes a public hearing. The rivals interrupt
one another so often that the leader threatens to charge them both
with wasting everyone's afternoon.
* (Name1) bursts into tears from humiliation, then grows furious
  at being seen crying. (5)
* (Name2) admits to insulting the invention, but denies touching it. (5)
* Both admit they have been spreading cruel rumors. (8)
* (Name2) shouts, 'I HATE YOUR GUTS!' and (Name1) replies,
  'THE FEELING IS MUTUAL!' The leader orders a recess. (8)

(3) THE WITNESSES:
* A child saw a figure near the wall, but cannot identify the person. (8)
* An apprentice saw (Name1) add the wrong powder. (6)
* An elder remembers (Name2) lending (Name1) a tool. The rivals had
  forgotten that brief moment of cooperation. (8)
* A child confesses to 'ruining the wall' because (he/she) dropped
  a pebble nearby. The leader explains that guilt requires evidence. (8)

(4) THE REPAIR:
* The wall is salvaged. The rivals argue about whose repair worked. (8)
* The wall collapses harmlessly into an empty work area. Nobody is hurt.
  Both builders stare in absolute silence. (6)
* (Name2) quietly helps without asking for credit. (9)
* (Name1) refuses assistance, then apologizes after exhausting
  (himself/herself). (9)

(5) THE EVIDENCE:
The leader announces that an accusation is not proof.
* (Name1) withdraws the allegation and apologizes awkwardly. (9)
* (Name2) demands a public apology and receives one. (9)
* (Name1) cannot yet apologize. The hearing ends without a verdict. (8)

(6) THE INVENTOR'S FAULT:
The notes show that (Name1) accidentally substituted (MATERIAL)
for the intended ingredient. (Name1) (laughs helplessly/looks ill/
tries to blame the handwriting/admits the mistake).
* The rival offers a correction, without gloating. (9)
* The rival gloats spectacularly. The leader orders (him/her) to stop. (8)
* The inventor admits guilt for the failure but not for the rumor. (8)

(7) REAL SABOTAGE:
A third adult admits to damaging the wall out of envy.
* The culprit pays for repairs through supervised work and apologizes. (9)
* The culprit refuses to apologize; the leader imposes proportionate
  restitution, and the rivals remain angry. (8)
* The culprit claims (Name2) put (him/her) up to it. The allegation
  is investigated separately, not treated as established fact. (8)

(8) UNRESOLVED ENDINGS — choose one:
* The rivals refuse to speak for a week, then exchange tools without a word.
* Both leave the hearing convinced they won. The leader is exhausted.
* The wall stands crooked. Nobody wants to touch it again.
* A child paints a sign reading 'PLEASE DO NOT ARGUE NEAR THE WALL.'
* The rivalry continues, but neither is allowed to make unproven accusations.

(9) RECONCILIATION ENDINGS — choose one:
* 'I still think your wall is ugly.' / 'I still think you're impossible.'
  They shake hands anyway.
* (Name1) admits the public accusation hurt more than the failed project.
  (Name2) listens, then says, 'Yes. It did.'
* The two build a better wall together and refuse to call it friendship.
* A child asks whether they are friends now. Both shout, 'NO!' in unison.
* (Name2) leaves a small bag of clean shell powder at (Name1)'s door.
  The note reads, 'Try not to ruin it this time.'

Reward: Building experience, optional modest Tech; no lethal outcomes.

================================================================================
VV1 — THE MISSING PEARL
TWO CHOICES; false guilt, family loyalty, wrong culprit
================================================================================

A polished pearl vanishes from an elder's keepsake box. Three villagers
immediately confess: an anxious apprentice, a proud adult, and a child.

The apprentice says, 'I moved the box.' The adult says, 'I broke it.'
The child says, 'I wished it would disappear, so it's my fault.'
The elder looks at all three. 'None of you has explained where it went.'

CHOICE A — Examine the box and the room. (1)
CHOICE B — Ask each person privately what they actually did. (2)

(1) SEARCH — choose one:
* The pearl is under a folded blanket. Nobody stole it. (3)
* A bird carried the shiny pearl to a nearby nest; it is retrieved
  without harming the animal. (3)
* The pearl was moved by the elder and forgotten. (3)
* A different adult took it without permission and admits it. (4)

(2) TESTIMONY — choose one:
* The apprentice feared being blamed for carelessness. (3)
* The adult tried to protect the child from punishment. (3)
* The child thought wishing for something bad made it happen. (3)
* The elder admits the pearl was never missing; it was in a second box. (3)

(3) NO THEFT:
* The child learns that feelings are not evidence of wrongdoing.
* The adult apologizes for a false confession that confused the inquiry.
* The apprentice cries with relief; the elder offers a cup of water.
* The elder says, 'Next time, everyone stop confessing before breakfast.'

(4) ACTUAL THEFT:
The adult returns the pearl, explains why it was taken, and faces
proportionate restitution. A confession does not erase the harm.
The elder may forgive, delay forgiveness, or decline it.

================================================================================
VV2 — THE EXPERIMENTAL REMEDY
FOUR CHOICES; medicine ethics, bizarre ingredients, slapstick, accountability
================================================================================

A talented but overconfident researcher has prepared an 'astonishing
new remedy' containing (MATERIAL) and (MATERIAL). A healer takes one
look at the mixture and says, 'Absolutely not.'

The researcher insists it is (a breakthrough/an ancient formula/
a harmless demonstration/a triumph of science). An apprentice admits
(he/she) has already labeled the jar 'MIRACLE CURE.'

CHOICE A — Quarantine the mixture and inspect the notes. (1)
CHOICE B — Hold a public scientific demonstration with NO ingestion. (2)
CHOICE C — Ask the researcher to explain the evidence. (3)
CHOICE D — Investigate the suspicious disappearance of hospital supplies. (4)

(1) NOTES:
* The recipe was copied incorrectly from a pottery manual. (5)
* The mixture was intended as wall pigment, not medicine. (5)
* The researcher admits (he/she) was desperate for recognition. (6)
* The apprentice altered the label as a joke and apologizes. (5)

(2) DEMONSTRATION:
* The paste hardens around a wooden spoon. 'Useful,' says a builder.
  'Not on a patient,' says the healer. (5)
* The mixture foams spectacularly. The researcher cheers; the healer
  moves the audience farther away. (5)
* Nothing happens. The researcher calls this 'remarkable stability.' (5)
* The smell empties the demonstration area. (5)

(3) EVIDENCE:
* The researcher cannot cite a single safe test. The claim is withdrawn. (6)
* A child asks why something that smells awful must be medicine.
  The researcher admits it needn't be. (5)
* A respected elder recognizes the substance as a traditional craft
  material and explains the misunderstanding. (5)

(4) THE MISSING SUPPLIES:
* The apprentice moved the bandages to make room for the jars. (5)
* A rival scientist hid a ledger, hoping to embarrass the researcher.
  The rival admits it and must repair the harm. (7)
* The supplies are found on their proper shelf. Everyone looks at
  the inventory labels with renewed suspicion. (5)

(5) COMEDIC RESOLUTION:
* The healer writes 'NOT FOR PATIENTS' on the jar in huge letters.
* The researcher offers the mixture to a builder for nonmedical testing.
* The apprentice asks whether 'miracle cure' can be renamed 'miracle glue.'
* A father holding a sleeping infant says, 'Please stop yelling
  about the slime. Some of us are trying to keep a baby asleep.'

(6) HUMBLING RESOLUTION:
The researcher admits (he/she) wanted a discovery so badly that
(he/she) ignored reasonable criticism. The healer says, 'You can try
again. But not by gambling with someone else's health.'

(7) DISCIPLINARY RESOLUTION:
The hospital holds a fair inquiry. The rival may receive a warning,
restitution, or removal from a voluntary research assignment in the
story. No actual game-role change unless supported by the patch.

================================================================================
VV2 — THE STOLEN SPOON
COMPLEX: four choices, testimony, absurd courtroom, prison debate
================================================================================

A ceremonial wooden spoon has vanished from the communal kitchen.
It is worth almost nothing, except that two families have argued
about its ownership for years. A cook accuses a rival of stealing it.
The rival shouts, 'I WOULD RATHER EAT CAVE GUANO THAN TOUCH YOUR SPOON!'

A village leader appoints an impartial elder to hear the case.

CHOICE A — Hear witnesses. (1)
CHOICE B — Search the kitchen. (2)
CHOICE C — Offer mediation. (3)
CHOICE D — Let both sides present their evidence formally. (4)

(1) WITNESSES:
* A child saw someone with a spoon but cannot identify which spoon. (5)
* A nursing mother says she borrowed a DIFFERENT spoon to prepare food,
  and resents being treated as a criminal for it. (5)
* A tired father admits he moved the spoon while cleaning. (6)
* An elder confesses because (he/she) dislikes seeing people argue,
  but has no actual knowledge of the theft. (7)

(2) SEARCH:
* The spoon is found behind a shelf. (6)
* It is discovered inside a hollow loaf of bread. Nobody can explain why. (6)
* A broken spoon is found, but it is not the missing one. (5)
* The cook had placed it in a safe box and forgotten. (6)

(3) MEDIATION:
* The families agree to share the spoon on festival days. (8)
* Both reject mediation and demand a verdict. (4)
* One family offers to make a second spoon. The other insists the
  original has sentimental value. (8)
* The mediator admits the spoon looks like every other spoon on Isola. (8)

(4) THE TRIAL:
The judge asks for evidence, not insults. The cook produces a diagram
of the crime scene that is mostly angry arrows. The rival produces
four witnesses who can only testify that the cook is 'annoying.'
* A credible witness proves the rival borrowed the spoon and forgot
  to return it. The judge orders its return and an apology. (8)
* The evidence is contradictory. No guilt is established. (7)
* The rival admits deliberately hiding the spoon out of spite. (9)
* The cook admits inventing the accusation to humiliate the rival. (9)

(5) CONFUSION:
The judge adjourns until the witnesses can distinguish a soup spoon
from a ceremonial spoon. A child asks whether the judge is paid.
'No,' says the judge. 'Clearly.' Go to (7).

(6) FOUND:
The spoon is returned. The accuser must correct the public allegation.
The accused may remain angry. A happy ending is not mandatory.

(7) WRONG GUILT:
A villager confesses to an act (he/she) did not commit, either from
fear, misplaced responsibility, or a desire to end the hearing.
The judge refuses to convict without corroboration. The confession
is withdrawn or investigated, and the villager is treated kindly.

(8) COMPROMISE:
* The families share custody of the spoon, then immediately argue
  over who gets it on Tuesdays.
* The elder carves two replicas. Both families claim the replica
  is inferior to the original.
* The rival says, 'I still hate your cooking.' The cook says,
  'At least you've finally tasted it.'

(9) ACCOUNTABILITY:
The wrongdoer faces restitution and a fair hearing. A dramatic
'prison sentence' may be narrated as a short, offscreen, non-playable
custody arrangement ONLY if appropriate to the author's patched lore;
otherwise substitute supervised kitchen work. The judge refuses to
imprison anyone over a spoon merely to satisfy the crowd.

================================================================================
VV3 — THE CHIEF AND THE PEARL
COMPLEX: four choices; Chief presides, NEVER an eligible casualty
================================================================================

A priceless decorative pearl disappears from a ceremonial display.
A young builder confesses immediately. Then an elder confesses.
Then a Master Scientist confesses, insisting the pearl was needed
for 'a critical experiment in spherical elegance.'

The Chief stares at the three suspects. 'One pearl. Three confessions.
I have a feeling this will be a very long day.'

CHOICE A — Separate the witnesses and verify their accounts. (1)
CHOICE B — Examine the display and inventory. (2)
CHOICE C — Ask why each person confessed. (3)
CHOICE D — Convene a formal hearing. (4)

(1) TESTIMONY:
* All three describe different days. Two confessions are false. (5)
* One confession matches a witness and a physical trace. (6)
* None can describe the pearl accurately. (5)
* A child reveals that the display was moved for cleaning. (5)

(2) EVIDENCE:
* The pearl rolled beneath a carved stone. (5)
* The 'pearl' was an imitation bead used in a demonstration. (5)
* A researcher had legitimately borrowed it but failed to record it. (6)
* The display was vandalized by an unrelated adult. (7)

(3) MOTIVES:
* The builder feared the Chief would blame an apprentice. (5)
* The elder thought accepting blame would restore peace. (5)
* The scientist wanted to seem important. (5)
* One adult really did take it and is ashamed. (6)

(4) COURTROOM:
* The Chief insists on evidence and dismisses unsupported claims. (5)
* A witness bursts into tears, then admits to lying to protect a friend. (7)
* The scientist tries to introduce a chart of pearl trajectories.
  The Chief says, 'We are not putting a coconut on trial.' (5)
* The Chief calls a recess when two witnesses begin shouting. (5)

(5) FALSE CONFESSION ENDING:
The Chief explains that fear, love, and shame can make people accept
blame for things they did not do. The confessions alone do not prove
guilt. The villagers are cleared where evidence warrants it.
* The elder cries from relief and embarrassment.
* The apprentice asks whether telling the truth can still be brave.
* The scientist requests permission to keep the chart. The Chief sighs.

(6) RESPONSIBILITY ENDING:
The responsible adult returns the pearl, admits the specific act,
and makes restitution. The Chief praises honesty without pretending
it erases the original wrong.

(7) MISCONDUCT ENDING:
A fair hearing establishes deliberate theft or false testimony.
The Chief orders proportionate restitution or community service.
An optional offscreen holding-cell narrative is available ONLY if
supported by the patch; never remove the Chief or protected villagers.

================================================================================
VV3 — THE DUEL OF THE TERRIBLE INVENTORS
TWO CHOICES; Nature/Magic-neutral, comic rivalry and a fight
================================================================================

Two inventors, (Name1) and (Name2), have spent years claiming the other
is a fraud. They each present a device made from (MATERIAL) and
(MATERIAL). The first promises to (keep a roof dry/make a garden thrive/
produce a beautiful melody). The second promises the same thing,
'BUT BETTER.'

When one prototype fails, the inventors begin shouting. A wooden
measuring stick is snatched away, and they start shoving one another.
Nearby adults separate them before anyone is seriously hurt.

CHOICE A — Judge the inventions under the same test. (1)
CHOICE B — Require apologies and a cooling-off period first. (2)

(1) TEST:
* One invention wins clearly. The loser storms away. (3)
* Both fail. A child suggests a simpler design that works. (3)
* Both work, but one smells like cave guano. (3)
* The devices combine into something unexpectedly useful. (3)

(2) COOLING OFF:
* One apologizes for shoving; the other admits to provoking (him/her). (3)
* Both apologize for the physical fight but refuse to forgive the insults. (3)
* One denies wrongdoing; the organizers cancel the competition. (3)
* An elder says, 'You are both too old to fight like children.'
  A child objects that children are better behaved. (3)

(3) ENDINGS:
* They reconcile over a repair project, while still arguing about tools.
* They remain enemies but agree never to become physical again.
* A third inventor wins the contest while they are arguing.
* A child gives each a trophy labeled 'MOST DRAMATIC ADULT.'
* One says, 'I thought you hated me.' The other replies,
  'I do. I also know you're brilliant.'

================================================================================
VV4 — THE ARBOR-DAY FESTIVAL
COMPLEX: four choices; cooking, art, public shame, generous endings
================================================================================

The villagers organize a festival celebrating the Tree of Life.
The organizers ask everyone to contribute something beautiful,
useful, or delicious. Unfortunately, one enthusiastic participant
interprets this as permission to use ANYTHING.

On the central table sits a creation made from (MATERIAL),
(MATERIAL), and a garnish of (MATERIAL). It (glitters/oozes/
smokes harmlessly/smells dreadful/looks unexpectedly majestic).

A child says, 'Is that food?' The inventor says, 'That depends
on how adventurous you are.' The healer says, 'IT IS NOT FOOD.'

CHOICE A — Separate safe food from art and building experiments. (1)
CHOICE B — Hold a judged competition for nonedible inventions. (2)
CHOICE C — Ask the inventor to explain the purpose. (3)
CHOICE D — Let the children organize a harmless absurdity parade. (4)

(1) SORTING:
* The villagers salvage a wonderful meal from ordinary ingredients. (5)
* The unsafe dish is discarded; the inventor is upset but accepts it. (5)
* An elder identifies the creation as an excellent fertilizer display,
  provided it is kept far from food. (5)

(2) JUDGING:
* A builder wins with a surprisingly strong plaster. (5)
* A child wins with a sculpture of the inventor looking horrified. (5)
* The judge refuses to handle the slug-slime exhibit. (5)
* A Master Farmer wins for turning guano into a clearly labeled,
  safely handled fertilizer exhibit, not food. (5)

(3) EXPLANATION:
* The inventor intended a mosaic, and someone placed it on the food table. (5)
* The inventor admits (he/she) hoped to shock the judges. (5)
* The inventor thought a spectacular failure would make people laugh. (5)
* The inventor wanted to honor an elder's odd old recipe but misunderstood
  the instructions. They make something safe together instead. (5)

(4) PARADE:
* Children parade with painted bark crowns and harmless wooden sculptures. (5)
* A child names a rotten-wood statue 'THE GREAT STINKING LEADER.' (5)
* An elder enters a sculpture of a snail and wins the crowd's affection. (5)
* The adults become so competitive that the children have to remind
  them the parade is supposed to be fun. (5)

(5) ENDINGS:
* The festival is saved. The inventor is given a separate table
  labeled 'NOT FOR EATING. SERIOUSLY.'
* The inventor is humiliated, but an elder quietly praises the effort
  while explaining why the execution failed.
* The entire village laughs together, with the inventor's consent.
* Two old rivals cooperate on next year's exhibit and deny enjoying it.
* A parent with a sleeping infant finally gets to eat a normal meal.
* The child keeps the wooden snail sculpture for years.

================================================================================
VV4 — THE ROTTEN STRUCTURE
TWO CHOICES; unjust accusation, anger, reparations, no forced imprisonment
================================================================================

A valuable structure collapses. Nobody is hurt, but the repair
will take days. The leader learns that rotten wood was used in its
construction. A respected builder accuses an unpopular apprentice
of deliberately choosing defective material.

The apprentice insists (he/she) followed the builder's instructions.
The builder shouts, 'LIAR!' The apprentice shouts back, 'YOU TAUGHT ME!'

CHOICE A — Investigate the construction records. (1)
CHOICE B — Hold a fair hearing with independent witnesses. (2)

(1) RECORDS:
* The builder approved the rotten wood and forgot. (3)
* The apprentice substituted material without telling anyone. (4)
* A third villager supplied bad wood without realizing it. (5)
* The records are inconclusive; neither can be convicted. (5)

(2) HEARING:
* A witness confirms the apprentice warned the builder. (3)
* The apprentice admits knowingly using weak wood to finish quickly. (4)
* Both made mistakes and concealed them out of embarrassment. (5)
* A false witness admits lying to protect a friend. (5)

(3) WRONGFUL ACCUSATION:
The builder must retract the accusation. The apprentice says,
'I trusted you.' The builder cannot find an answer.
* They reconcile slowly after a sincere apology.
* The apprentice accepts the apology but chooses another mentor.
* The builder is publicly embarrassed and privately remorseful.

(4) PROVEN MISCONDUCT:
The responsible adult makes restitution and helps rebuild safely.
Any prison reference is a narrative-only, offscreen possibility for
serious intentional wrongdoing if the patch's lore supports it;
ordinary negligence is not treated as a reason for imprisonment.

(5) MIXED RESPONSIBILITY:
* Both admit their mistakes and work together.
* Both remain furious but stop lying about what happened.
* An elder says, 'The structure failed. Don't let the truth fail too.'

================================================================================
VV5 — THE SLUG RACE
COMPLEX: four choices; Isolan NPC team, neutral judge, absurd law
================================================================================

At a shared village gathering, an Isolan child enters a slug in a
friendly racing contest. A child from the player's tribe claims the
slug has been secretly coated in extra slime to cheat.

The Isolan team captain is outraged. The village captain is equally
outraged. The neutral judge asks whether anyone has established
that adding slime would even help a slug.

'OBJECTION!' shouts a spectator.
'On what grounds?' asks the judge.
'IT'S DISGUSTING!'

CHOICE A — Test the race rules and inspect the course. (1)
CHOICE B — Let the children explain their accusations. (2)
CHOICE C — Offer a rematch with equal conditions. (3)
CHOICE D — Hold a formal mock trial for the adults' amusement. (4)

(1) SCIENCE:
* The slime has no measurable advantage. The slug is cleared. (5)
* The course was tilted. All racers get a fair rematch. (5)
* The slug had crawled off before the contest began. (5)
* The 'extra slime' is spilled fermented yogurt. The race is postponed
  until the course is cleaned. (5)

(2) CHILDREN:
* One child admits inventing the accusation out of jealousy. (6)
* The child genuinely misunderstood what (he/she) saw. (5)
* Both children confess to cheating, but neither has done anything. (5)
* The Isolan child says, 'I just wanted my slug to be special.' (7)

(3) REMATCH:
* The village slug wins. Both teams cheer. (7)
* The Isolan slug wins. The village child congratulates the rival. (7)
* Both slugs refuse to move. The judge declares a draw. (7)
* The slugs crawl in opposite directions. The judge retires. (7)

(4) MOCK TRIAL:
* The neutral judge demands to hear from the slug. The slug remains silent. (7)
* A villager produces a chart of slime viscosity. Everyone groans. (5)
* An elder insists the slug deserves legal counsel. The children appoint
  a very small wooden spoon as its lawyer. (7)
* The adults get so heated that the children ask them to stop. (6)

(5) ACQUITTAL:
The judge finds no proven cheating. The accusers must correct their
claims. Both communities agree that a slug's dignity has survived.

(6) APOLOGY:
The child apologizes, and the adults apologize for making the dispute
worse. The Isolan child may accept immediately or need time.

(7) RIDICULOUS ENDINGS:
* The trophy is a polished pearl too large for the slug to carry.
* The winning slug escapes with the ceremonial ribbon.
* A father remarks that his baby could have crawled the course faster.
* The children become friends; their parents remain fiercely competitive.
* The judge says, 'I trained for years to judge craftsmanship. Not this.'

================================================================================
VV5 — THE TRIAL OF THE SHATTERED PEARLS
COMPLEX: five choices; real wrongdoing, false guilt, rivals, custody, forgiveness
================================================================================

A joint Isolan-village art exhibition ends in disaster. A display of
polished pearls is found shattered, and glittering gemstone powder
has been scattered across a prized carved panel.

An Isolan artist accuses a village Master Builder. The builder
accuses an Isolan rival. A frightened apprentice confesses before
anyone asks a question. The neutral arbiter calls for order.

CHOICE A — Examine the physical evidence. (1)
CHOICE B — Interview the confessing apprentice privately. (2)
CHOICE C — Investigate both rivals' motives. (3)
CHOICE D — Convene a public hearing with neutral rules. (4)
CHOICE E — Pause the exhibition and preserve the damaged work. (5)

(1) EVIDENCE:
* A fallen shelf caused the damage. Neither rival is guilty. (6)
* One rival deliberately struck the display. (7)
* The apprentice knocked it over by accident and panicked. (8)
* The powder came from an unrelated art project. (6)

(2) CONFESSION:
* The apprentice was afraid the mentor would be punished. (6)
* The apprentice thought a confession would end the fighting. (6)
* The apprentice actually caused the damage by accident. (8)
* The apprentice saw the real culprit and is afraid to name (him/her). (7)

(3) MOTIVES:
* The rivals are bitter enemies but neither damaged the display. (6)
* One rival wanted to win so badly that (he/she) sabotaged it. (7)
* One rival made a cruel joke, not the physical damage. The joke is
  addressed separately. (6)
* The rivals were secretly collaborating on a surprise exhibit;
  their secrecy caused a misunderstanding. (6)

(4) HEARING:
* The arbiter catches a witness inventing details to protect a friend. (6)
* The accused screams, 'YOU HAVE HATED ME SINCE THE DAY WE MET!'
  The rival replies, 'YES, BUT THAT DOESN'T MAKE YOU GUILTY!' (6)
* A witness admits to tampering and offers restitution. (7)
* The arbiter refuses to treat the apprentice's fear as proof. (6)

(5) PRESERVATION:
* The pearls are recoverable and the panel can be repaired. (6)
* The artwork is permanently damaged; its maker grieves the loss. (9)
* A child finds a missing pearl inside a decorative coconut. (6)
* A visiting artisan offers a restoration method. (9)

(6) EXONERATION:
The arbiter publicly clears those against whom there is no evidence.
The rivals may apologize, remain hostile, or begin to trust each other.
The apprentice learns that confessing to a thing one didn't do can
make the truth harder to find.

(7) INTENTIONAL DAMAGE:
The culprit is identified by evidence, not rumor. The arbiter orders
restitution, a public correction and proportionate consequences.
* The culprit admits envy and apologizes sincerely.
* The culprit insists the artwork deserved it. The community condemns
  the act without demanding instant forgiveness.
* The culprit accepts an offscreen custodial sentence ONLY if this
  is supported by the author's Guardians of Harmony story mechanics;
  otherwise the sentence is supervised restitution. No playable
  villager is forcibly removed by prose alone.
* The victim refuses to reconcile. The culprit must accept that.

(8) ACCIDENT:
The apprentice admits the accident. The adults realize their furious
accusations frightened a young person into hiding the truth.
They apologize; the apprentice helps repair what can be repaired.

(9) BITTERSWEET:
The damaged art cannot be made exactly as it was. The artist may
restore it imperfectly, create a new work, or leave it unfinished.
A former rival says, 'I hated that piece. I never wanted it destroyed.'
The artist replies, 'I wish you had said that yesterday.'

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL CODA BANK — RAGE, SHAME, RECONCILIATION, CONSEQUENCES
Use with any compatible game and correct authority terminology.
================================================================================

R1: 'I hope you lose every contest you ever enter,' (Name1) snaps.
(Name2) goes pale. 'You were my friend.' Neither speaks for days.

R2: The two adults shove each other. Other adults intervene promptly.
No one is seriously hurt. The organizers suspend the contest and
require accountability before either may return.

R3: (Name1) admits to a wrongdoing nobody accused (him/her) of.
The judge says, 'That is a separate matter, and we will not pretend
it proves this charge.'

R4: An elder confesses to protect a grandchild. The grandchild says,
'Please stop. I don't want you punished for something I did.'

R5: A villager confesses because (he/she) genuinely believes a careless
thought caused the disaster. A trusted adult explains the difference
between wishing, fearing, and acting.

R6: The guilty villager is cleared by accident. (He/she) returns at
sunset and tells the truth, trembling. The wronged person asks,
'Why now?' 'Because you deserved to know.'

R7: The accused is cleared, but the accusation has cost (him/her) a
friendship. 'Being innocent doesn't give me back what you said.'

R8: A former enemy repairs the broken object in secret. The owner
recognizes the workmanship. Neither knows how to begin the conversation.

R9: The judge announces a verdict, then realizes the evidence was
misread. The hearing is reopened. The judge apologizes publicly.

R10: A pompous prosecutor drops a pile of bark-paper documents into
slug slime. The court recesses while everyone tries not to laugh.

R11: The 'prison' is a harmless, story-only holding room where an
adult culprit waits for a hearing; the guard is bored, the culprit
is indignant, and no game-state removal occurs.

R12: The accused spends a sleepless night in story-only custody before
new evidence clears (him/her). The leader apologizes and offers
restitution. The experience is not waved away as a joke.

R13: The culprit's punishment is to rebuild the object (he/she)
destroyed. The victim watches and says, 'That doesn't make us even.'
'I know,' the culprit replies.

R14: The rivals exchange apologies that are technically sincere but
extremely badly phrased. 'I'm sorry you're such an irritating person.'
'THAT IS NOT AN APOLOGY.' They try again.

R15: An elderly witness is confidently wrong. When corrected, (he/she)
says, 'I have been wrong before. Not often, mind you.'

R16: A child presents an airtight argument. The adults ignore it until
a Master Scientist repeats the same point. The child is furious.
The scientist gives the child public credit.

R17: A nursing mother or father, baby asleep in (his/her) arms, settles
the dispute with one calm observation. Everyone thanks the parent.
The parent says, 'Excellent. Now go argue somewhere else.'

R18: A villager discovers the person who hurt (him/her) is crying.
(He/she) offers water, but not forgiveness. Both acts are sincere.

R19: The rivals agree to work together. One says, 'I still despise
your handwriting.' The other says, 'I still despise your face.'
Their joint project is excellent.

R20: The supposed victim admits staging the disappearance to attract
attention. The leader is furious. The villager must repair the harm,
including apologizing to those falsely accused.

R21: The court is interrupted by a child demanding to know whether
slugs have property rights. The judge closes (his/her) eyes.

R22: The inventor's spectacular creation disintegrates into rotten
wood dust. The rival laughs, then sees the inventor's face and stops.

R23: 'I thought if I won, you'd finally be proud of me,' the apprentice
whispers. The mentor says, 'I should have told you sooner.'

R24: No one reconciles. The village still needs dinner. Two bitter
enemies pass bowls across the table without speaking. It is a start.

R25: The whole argument began because somebody labeled a jar of
crushed snail shells 'PEARLS.' The inventor refuses to change the label.

R26: The neutral judge resigns from all future slug-related litigation.
A child asks whether this includes snails. The judge says, 'Especially.'

================================================================================
EDITORIAL / IMPLEMENTATION NOTE
================================================================================

All scenes are narrative drafts, not a tested patcher script. Random
choices should be weighted: ordinary, funny and tender outcomes common;
violent confrontations, severe betrayal and custody rare. Verify each
branch's evidence, guilty party, and verdict stay consistent. Never
select a protected character for permanent loss. Keep children and
nursing parents available for characterization without turning them
into casualty targets. Where a game has no legal system, a hearing is
an improvised village council, not a claim of a canonical courthouse.

An inventor fails. A rival gloats. A child notices the truth.
An elder admits a mistake. Someone laughs too loudly. Someone cries.
A broken thing is mended, or isn't. Tomorrow there is another contest.

ISOLA CONTINUES ON. <3

================================================================================
V10 — REPUTATIONS, EXILE FROM THE DINNER TABLE, AND UNLIKELY GREATNESS
================================================================================

CANON GATE — APPLY BEFORE ANY BRANCH:
VV1: Southern Shore. No knowledge of the Secret City, Chiefs, Magicians,
Naturalists, or the later Isolan/HEATHEN faction. Refer to community leaders.
VV2: Western Shore. Do not introduce knowledge of the Secret City, Chiefs,
Magicians, Naturalists, or the later Isolan faction. Refer to leaders.
VV3: Secret City. Chief exists and is protected. Nature/Magic traditions
and Naturalist/Magician identities may appear.
VV4: Tree of Life. Nature/Magic identities may appear as inherited or
known traditions; do not assert the VV3 Chief governs this village.
VV5: New Believers, with Guardians of Harmony patch. Call the other
community ISOLANS, never Heathens. Nature/Magic traditions may appear
when narratively appropriate, but do not imply every Isolan practices them.
In VV1, VV2, VV4, VV5 use "village leaders," not "Chief."
Head-of-field and school appointments are STORY HONORS unless the patcher
explicitly supports a matching role/flag. Never silently replace a
game's canonical leadership or specialty mechanics.

REPUTATION STATES (narrative, not permanent engine traits):
REP-1: temporarily ostracised / excluded from communal celebrations
REP-2: laughingstock after a public blunder
REP-3: cruelly called "the village idiot" by some villagers
REP-4: self-styled "mad scientist," eccentric but not necessarily dangerous
REP-5: trusted advisor to the village leaders
REP-6: teacher or mentor at the local school
REP-7: head doctor/healer
REP-8: head scientist/researcher
REP-9: head farmer
REP-10: head builder
REP-11: head devotee / priest / priestess / spiritual leader
REP-12: head magician (VV3–VV5 only)
REP-13: head naturalist (VV3–VV5 only)
REP-14: vindicated after being wrongly blamed
REP-15: respected despite never becoming popular
REP-16: publicly disgraced, privately forgiven by one person

RULES: Only grant titles after plausible demonstrations of competence,
service, election, mentorship, or a supported appointment. A terrible
mistake does not magically qualify someone as head doctor. A villager
can be laughed at without deserving humiliation; portray the
consequences of cruelty. Ostracism is temporary and revisitable.
"Village idiot" is an insulting nickname used by characters, not
the narrator's judgment or an intelligence statistic.

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL VV1–VV5 — (NAME1)'S NO GOOD, VERY BAD DAY
COMPLEX MULTIBRANCH: accusation, ostracism, truth, consequences
================================================================================

A villager named (Name1) is accused of (A). By sunset, nearly
everyone knows the story. Unfortunately, nearly everyone knows
a DIFFERENT version of it.

A — THE ACCUSATION:
- ruining a festival centerpiece with slug slime
- pulverizing someone else's polished pearls for paint
- stealing a pouch of gemstones
- replacing good timber with rotten wood
- contaminating a display with cave guano
- secretly sabotaging a rival's sculpture
- lying about who invented the new snail-shell plaster
- mocking a child's carefully made gift

(Name1) is (furious and defiant/ashamed and silent/
bewildered by the accusation/too proud to ask for help).
(Name2), the accuser, is (genuinely convinced/jealous/
repeating a rumor/covering up (his/her) own mistake).

CHOICE A — HOLD A FAIR HEARING BEFORE IMPOSING PENALTIES. (1)
CHOICE B — ASK BOTH SIDES TO PRODUCE EVIDENCE. (2)
CHOICE C — LET THE VILLAGE COUNCIL IMPOSE A SHORT COOLING-OFF PERIOD. (3)
CHOICE D — ASK A TRUSTED ELDER TO MEDIATE. (4)

(1) HEARING:
- Witnesses contradict one another. Go to (5).
- An apprentice produces a labeled jar proving the material
  was borrowed with permission. Go to (6).
- (Name1) angrily insults the council. The hearing continues;
  rudeness is not proof of guilt. Go to (5).

(2) EVIDENCE:
- The supposed stolen pearls are found inside a hollow coconut. (6)
- A smear of slug slime implicates the wrong person because
  three villagers used the same tool. (5)
- A written confession appears, but the handwriting belongs
  to someone else. (7)
- The evidence shows (Name1) knowingly sabotaged the work. (8)

(3) COOLING-OFF PERIOD:
The leaders ask the disputants to avoid each other for two days,
without cutting anyone off from food, shelter, healthcare, or family.
- The pause prevents a fight, but rumors worsen. (5)
- (Name1) feels ostracised and begins to withdraw. (9)
- (Name2) realizes the punishment was premature and asks
  for the case to be reopened. (6)

(4) MEDIATION:
- The elder uncovers a misunderstanding. (6)
- The elder is biased toward (Name2). A second, neutral
  mediator is appointed. (5)
- The two agree on what happened but not what it meant. (10)

(5) INSUFFICIENT PROOF:
The council announces that suspicion is not a verdict.
- Some villagers apologize. Others continue whispering. (9)
- A child asks why everyone believed the worst so quickly. (9)
- The accuser becomes furious and refuses to accept the finding. (10)

(6) EXONERATION:
(Name1) is cleared. The leaders correct the public record.
- The village celebrates the discovery, but (Name1) does
  not feel ready to celebrate with them. (11)
- (Name2) admits the mistake and apologizes without excuses. (11)
- (Name1) refuses the apology for now. (11)

(7) FALSE CONFESSION:
A third villager admits writing the confession.
- (He/she) hoped to protect a friend. (12)
- (He/she) wanted attention and did not expect the rumor
  to become serious. (12)
- (He/she) genuinely believed (he/she) was responsible,
  having confused two similar jars. (12)

(8) ACTUAL WRONGDOING:
(Name1) admits the sabotage after the evidence is presented.
- (He/she) repairs the damage and loses the competition prize. (13)
- (He/she) is suspended from judging future contests for a time. (13)
- (He/she) refuses to apologize. The community maintains
  proportionate boundaries. (13)

(9) TEMPORARY OSTRACISM — choose one:
- Nobody sits beside (Name1) at the feast. An elderly
  villager moves over and says, "There's room here."
- Children repeat the accusation as a cruel rhyme.
  Their teacher intervenes and explains why it is wrong.
- A friend leaves a meal at (Name1)'s door but is too
  frightened to stay. (Name1) feels grateful and hurt.
- (Name1) begins to believe the rumors. A healer reminds
  (him/her) that public opinion is not evidence.
- The leaders formally end the exclusion and invite
  (Name1) back, acknowledging that trust may take time.
Go to (11).

(10) UNRESOLVED CONFLICT:
- The rivals continue to hate each other's guts, but agree
  not to interfere with each other's work.
- One says, "I don't forgive you." The other replies,
  "I didn't ask you to. I asked you to stop lying."
- They work together during an emergency, then resume
  avoiding each other afterward.
- The leaders require a neutral witness whenever the two
  share materials until the dispute is resolved.

(11) REPUTATION AFTERMATH — choose one:
- (Name1) becomes a respected advocate for fair hearings.
- (Name1) remains unpopular, but is no longer excluded.
- A former accuser asks for advice; (Name1) chooses
  whether to help.
- The villagers remember the scandal long after the
  truth is established. (Name1) learns to live with
  the discomfort without pretending it is fair.
- A child publicly says, "You were right." It means
  more than the council's entire speech.

(12) THE CONFESSOR'S CONSEQUENCES:
- The confessor must publicly correct the story and help
  repair the damage.
- The confessor is embarrassed by the attention and learns
  to ask questions before assuming guilt.
- The confession itself becomes a village joke; a teacher
  stops the teasing when it turns cruel.
- The confessor explains why (he/she) was frightened.
  The adults listen rather than treating the lie as a joke.

(13) ACCOUNTABILITY:
- Restitution is accepted, but forgiveness is not immediate.
- The wrongdoer becomes a temporary laughingstock, then
  slowly earns trust by doing ordinary reliable work.
- The wrongdoer refuses to improve and is excluded from
  managing shared supplies for a while.
- An apprentice says, "You taught me better than this."
  That hurts more than the formal penalty.

================================================================================
UNIVERSAL VV1–VV5 — THE VILLAGE IDIOT AND THE IMPOSSIBLE INVENTION
TWO CHOICES, THEN MANY OUTCOMES; comic to triumphant
================================================================================

(Name1) has become famous for proposing dreadful ideas:
a chair held together with fermented fruit juice, a roof made from
tree bark and rotten wood, and a ceremonial necklace
fashioned from (polished pearls/pulverized gemstones/
crushed-up snail shells).

After the latest failure, some villagers cruelly call
(Name1) "the village idiot."

(Name1) hears them.

Then (he/she) announces a new experiment involving
(volcanic ash and crushed snail shells/spider silk and
tough vines/yeast and fermented yogurt/tree bark and
animal rawhide).

CHOICE A — OFFER SUPERVISION AND A FAIR TEST. (1)
CHOICE B — TELL (NAME1) TO STOP BEFORE ANYONE IS HURT. (2)

(1) THE TEST:
- The material works surprisingly well for a harmless
  decorative application. (3)
- The invention fails, but the observations reveal
  something useful about material strength. (3)
- The experiment creates a horrible smell. The judges
  retreat in an orderly fashion. (4)
- The inventor has accidentally reinvented a technique
  an elder has used for forty years. (5)
- The invention is impressive but unsafe for its intended
  use; the village declines to deploy it. (3)

(2) THE WARNING:
- (Name1) feels humiliated and stops presenting ideas.
  An elder later encourages a safer project. (5)
- (Name1) angrily declares (himself/herself) a MAD SCIENTIST
  and starts wearing a ridiculous leaf-covered hat. (4)
- The leaders establish sensible testing rules rather
  than forbidding experimentation entirely. (3)
- A child asks why mistakes are funny when adults make
  them but shameful when children do. (5)

(3) THE RESULT:
- (Name1) earns genuine respect for careful observation.
- The invention wins a small prize, and the former
  mockers apologize.
- The inventor accepts the criticism and returns with
  a better design next season.
- A Master Scientist offers mentorship, insisting
  that enthusiasm is not a substitute for evidence.

(4) THE MAD SCIENTIST:
- (Name1) insists the bubbling yogurt is "ALIVE!"
  The healer replies, "That is not the reassuring
  statement you think it is."
- (Name1) wears a dramatic cloak woven from tough vines.
  It gets caught on a door.
- (Name1) names a jar of slug slime "THE FUTURE."
  The village leaders ask that it be kept outside.
- A rival admits that the experiment was clever.
  (Name1) faints theatrically.
- (Name1) becomes known as the village's eccentric
  experimentalist, not as someone inherently foolish.

(5) THE HUMAN MOMENT:
- "I can survive being wrong," says (Name1).
  "I just wish you wouldn't enjoy it so much."
- An elder says, "Some of your ideas are dreadful.
  That doesn't make you dreadful."
- A child offers (Name1) a tiny wooden trophy
  labeled MOST IMPROVED FAILURE.
- The loudest critic admits to once building a bridge
  that led directly into a pond.
- (Name1) keeps experimenting, but begins recording
  failures as carefully as successes.

Potential reputation: REP-2, REP-3 (as cruel label only),
REP-4, REP-14 or REP-15. No automatic leadership promotion.

UNIVERSAL VV1–VV5 — THE UNLIKELY ADVISOR
FOUR CHOICES; an unlikely villager gains the leaders' trust
================================================================================

A difficult dispute has stalled the village council:
(A). Several Master villagers have offered conflicting
opinions. A quiet villager, (Name1), has been listening.

A — THE DISPUTE:
- how to divide a scarce harvest fairly
- whether to rebuild a damaged workshop or a damaged path first
- how to settle a long-running rivalry between artisans
- whether to fund an ambitious but poorly tested invention
- how to allocate teaching time between beginners and experts
- how to preserve an elder's old records without changing them

(Name1) is (a shy apprentice/an overlooked parent/
a former laughingstock/an elderly gardener/a quiet
researcher/a villager whose earlier advice was ignored).

CHOICE A — INVITE (NAME1) TO SPEAK. (1)
CHOICE B — ASK FOR A WRITTEN PROPOSAL. (2)
CHOICE C — HOLD A PUBLIC DISCUSSION. (3)
CHOICE D — APPOINT A SMALL, DIVERSE ADVISORY GROUP. (4)

(1) SPEAK:
- (Name1) offers a simple, effective compromise. (5)
- (Name1) freezes under attention. A friend helps
  (him/her) explain the idea. (5)
- (Name1) points out an overlooked risk. (5)

(2) WRITE:
- The proposal is detailed and persuasive. (5)
- The proposal contains a flaw; (Name1) corrects it
  when questioned. (5)
- The proposal is written on tree bark because
  (Name1) could not find paper. It works anyway. (5)

(3) DISCUSS:
- The crowd agrees on a sensible compromise. (5)
- A loud rival interrupts. The leaders enforce
  speaking turns. (5)
- A child asks an unexpectedly perceptive question. (5)

(4) GROUP:
- Several perspectives reveal a better plan. (5)
- The group disagrees but records the tradeoffs honestly. (5)
- The meeting lasts so long that a parent brings
  the baby, a blanket, and a snack. (5)

(5) APPOINTMENT — choose one:
- The leaders invite (Name1) to serve as a trusted
  advisor for a trial period. (6)
- The leaders thank (Name1) but decide the proposal
  needs more evidence. (6)
- (Name1) declines a formal title but agrees to
  offer advice when asked. (6)
- The leaders adopt the proposal and credit (Name1)
  publicly. (6)

(6) PERSONAL ENDING:
- (Name1) is proud but worries about the responsibility.
- A former critic says, "I underestimated you."
  (Name1) replies, "Yes. You did."
- (Name1) brings the first official advice to the
  leaders: "Please make these meetings shorter."
- The title changes little. (Name1) still returns
  home to help with supper.
- A child asks whether advisors get crowns.
  (Name1) says, "Unfortunately, only headaches."

Potential reputation: REP-5. VV3 may consult its Chief,
but the Chief is not replaced or randomly targeted.
Elsewhere use "leaders" exclusively.

UNIVERSAL VV1–VV5 — THE TEACHER WHO FAILED 
NO CHOICE; teaching appointment, children, embarrassment, redemption
================================================================================

(Name1), a highly skilled (builder/healer/farmer/researcher/
devotee), has been invited to teach at the local school.

The first lesson concerns (a practical skill/a difficult
question/a village tradition/a demonstration with safe
materials). The pupils are excited.

The lesson goes wrong when:
- a child asks a question (Name1) cannot answer
- the demonstration collapses into a pile of rotten wood
- (Name1) discovers (he/she) has brought the wrong notes
- the children know more about the subject than expected
- a child politely points out that the teacher is contradicting
  (himself/herself)
- a snail crawls across the teaching materials, stealing
  everyone's attention

(Name1) (blushes/gets defensive/laughs at (himself/herself)/
takes a moment to recover).

DEVELOPMENT — choose one:
- (Name1) admits not knowing and promises to investigate.
- (Name1) apologizes for snapping at a student.
- (Name1) turns the failed demonstration into a lesson
  about testing and revision.
- (Name1) asks an elder to share a complementary
  perspective on the topic.
- (Name1) realizes teaching is harder than doing,
  and seeks guidance from an experienced instructor.

ENDING — choose one:
- The students ask (Name1) to return next week.
- (Name1) becomes a regular school teacher after
  demonstrating patience and reliability.
- (Name1) decides to remain an occasional guest teacher.
- A child announces that this was the best lesson
  because "the teacher made the biggest mistake."
- (Name1) leaves exhausted, then begins planning
  a better lesson before reaching home.

Potential reputation: REP-6, only when earned.

UNIVERSAL VV1–VV5 — THE GREAT APPOINTMENT SCANDAL
COMPLEX MULTIBRANCH; head doctor/scientist/farmer/builder/devotee
================================================================================

The village leaders announce that a respected senior worker
will be selected to coordinate (A). It is a responsibility,
not a magical promotion in skill.

A — THE OFFICE:
- head doctor / senior healer
- head scientist / lead researcher
- head farmer / harvest coordinator
- head builder / works supervisor
- head devotee / spiritual organizer
- priest or priestess / ceremonial caretaker
- spiritual leader / community counselor
- head magician (VV3–VV5 ONLY)
- head naturalist (VV3–VV5 ONLY)

If a role is not culturally or mechanically supported in the
current game, replace it with an informal mentor/coordinator
of a supported field. Never generate Magician/Naturalist in VV1–VV2.

Two qualified candidates, (Name1) and (Name2), are nominated.
They (respect each other/secretly despise each other/
used to be friends/are parent and adult child/
have been rivals since their apprenticeship).

A third villager, (Name3), is (a meticulous record-keeper/
an opinionated elder/a worried apprentice/a former critic).

CHOICE A — ASSESS COMPETENCE AND SERVICE. (1)
CHOICE B — ASK THE WORKERS WHO KNOW THE CANDIDATES. (2)
CHOICE C — GIVE BOTH A SUPERVISED TRIAL. (3)
CHOICE D — INVESTIGATE AN ALLEGATION BEFORE VOTING. (4)

(1) MERIT REVIEW:
- (Name1) has excellent skill but poor teamwork.
  The leaders request a plan for improvement. (5)
- (Name2) has quietly mentored others for years.
  The record impresses the council. (5)
- Both are qualified; the decision rests on the
  office's current needs. (5)

(2) WORKER FEEDBACK:
- Apprentices praise (Name1)'s patience. (5)
- Several workers complain that (Name2) takes
  credit for others' work. The claims are checked. (5)
- The workers prefer a shared leadership arrangement. (5)
- A child nominates the village's pet lizard.
  The nomination is respectfully declined. (5)

(3) TRIAL:
- (Name1) organizes a complex project beautifully. (5)
- (Name2) handles a mistake with honesty and care. (5)
- Both perform well but disagree over priorities. (5)
- One candidate refuses to delegate and becomes
  exhausted; the leaders offer feedback. (5)

(4) ALLEGATION:
- The allegation is supported by records. The candidate
  receives a fair hearing and may be disqualified. (6)
- The allegation is false and was spread by a rival. (7)
- The allegation stems from a misunderstanding. (7)
- Evidence remains inconclusive; the appointment
  is delayed rather than treating suspicion as guilt. (6)

(5) APPOINTMENT:
- (Name1) is appointed after demonstrating competence. (8)
- (Name2) is appointed after demonstrating competence. (8)
- Both are assigned complementary coordinating duties. (8)
- Neither is appointed; the leaders reopen nominations. (8)

(6) SCANDAL:
- The responsible candidate admits wrongdoing and
  accepts a temporary bar from leadership. (9)
- A falsely accused candidate insists on a public
  correction before returning to work. (9)
- The investigation reveals poor record-keeping,
  not deliberate wrongdoing; the procedures are fixed. (9)

(7) VINDICATION:
- The accused candidate is cleared. The accuser
  apologizes and corrects the rumor. (9)
- The candidate is cleared but declines the office,
  saying the process has exhausted (him/her). (9)
- The candidate accepts the appointment while
  requesting clearer future procedures. (9)

(8) REACTIONS:
- The winner is jubilant; the loser is furious
  and refuses to attend the celebration.
- The loser congratulates the winner sincerely,
  then cries alone later.
- The winner admits to feeling frightened rather
  than triumphant.
- The new coordinator's first act is to give
  the apprentices credit for their work.
- The winner delivers a speech so long that
  an elderly villager falls asleep.

(9) LONG AFTERMATH:
- A disgraced candidate earns trust back over time
  through consistent work, not one grand apology.
- The former rivals become effective colleagues
  without becoming friends.
- The village remembers the appointment as a lesson
  in fairness rather than a triumph for one faction.
- The new head appoints a former rival as an advisor,
  after discussing clear boundaries.
- The loser declares the process corrupt and spends
  a week sulking. A child asks whether sulking
  counts as a profession.

Title granted only to eligible, suitably skilled adult
characters and only where the role fits the game's lore.
Nursing parents can advise or participate safely without
being made casualties or forcibly reassigned.

================================================================================
VV3–VV5 ONLY — THE MAGICIAN AND THE NATURALIST
FOUR CHOICES; ideology, rivalry, cooperation, two traditions
================================================================================

A respected Magician and a respected Naturalist have been
arguing for weeks over the best way to (A).

A — THE PROJECT:
- restore a neglected garden
- decorate a ceremonial gathering place
- explain an unusual weather pattern
- teach children about the island
- protect a fragile grove
- design a beautiful public monument

The Magician insists on (ritual/symbolism/creative intuition/
traditional magical practice). The Naturalist argues for
(observation/soil testing/ecological balance/
careful natural methods).

Neither is necessarily foolish. Both are unbearably smug.

CHOICE A — LET EACH PRESENT A PROPOSAL. (1)
CHOICE B — REQUIRE A SHARED PLAN. (2)
CHOICE C — ASK A NEUTRAL ELDER TO MEDIATE. (3)
CHOICE D — LET APPRENTICES TEST SAFE PARTS OF BOTH IDEAS. (4)

(1) PRESENTATIONS:
- Both proposals have strengths and weaknesses. (5)
- The Magician gives a magnificent speech but forgets
  to explain the practical steps. (5)
- The Naturalist brings seventeen charts and no
  one can read the tiny labels. (5)

(2) SHARED PLAN:
- The two work together grudgingly and achieve
  an unexpectedly lovely result. (5)
- They spend an hour arguing about whose name
  should appear first on the plaque. (5)
- They agree to divide tasks by expertise. (5)

(3) MEDIATION:
- The elder reminds them that disagreement is
  not a reason to insult one another. (5)
- The elder is secretly enjoying the argument
  and has to be reminded to mediate. (5)
- The elder admits both traditions matter to
  different people for different reasons. (5)

(4) TESTS:
- The apprentices identify a practical combination. (5)
- Neither proposal works as expected; both experts
  must revise their assumptions. (5)
- A child asks whether the argument itself is
  part of the ceremony. Nobody answers. (5)

(5) OUTCOMES:
- They reconcile and become occasional collaborators.
- They still hate each other, but now respect
  each other's competence.
- One publicly admits (he/she) was wrong.
  The other nearly faints.
- Their feud becomes a running village joke,
  until the leaders ask the audience to stop
  encouraging the shouting.
- The Magician is recognized as head magician
  after a long record of service, while the
  Naturalist becomes head naturalist.
- Neither receives a title. Both are told
  to spend a week learning how to listen.
- One says, "I will never agree with you."
  The other replies, "Good. I'd worry if you did."

No Magician/Naturalist roles in VV1 or VV2. In VV3
the Chief is protected and is not displaced by this event.
In VV4/VV5, village leaders preside instead.

================================================================================
ADDITIONAL OUTCOME BANK — SOCIAL RISE, SOCIAL FALL, AND COMEBACKS
================================================================================

O1. OSTRACISED FOR A WHILE:
(Name1) finds that the usual conversations stop when
(he/she) approaches. A friend finally says, "I don't
know what to believe." The uncertainty hurts almost
as much as the accusation.

O2. THE LAUGHINGSTOCK:
The disastrous invention becomes a joke at every feast.
At first (Name1) laughs along. Then one day (he/she)
doesn't. Someone finally notices.

O3. THE "VILLAGE IDIOT":
A cruel nickname catches on. A teacher challenges it:
"One foolish decision does not define a person."
A child repeats the lesson more loudly than expected.

O4. THE MAD SCIENTIST:
(Name1) establishes an "INSTITUTE OF IMPROBABLE
MATERIALS" in a shed. Its first experiment involves
fermented fruit juice, yeast, and a very expensive pearl.
The village leaders demand an inventory.

O5. TRUSTED ADVISOR:
After several wise recommendations, (Name1) is asked
to advise the leaders regularly. (He/she) accepts,
then discovers the position involves listening to
arguments about whose goat ate whose tree bark.

O6. SCHOOL TEACHER:
The former troublemaker becomes a teacher and warns
the children not to repeat (his/her) mistakes.
Naturally, they ask for detailed instructions.

O7. HEAD DOCTOR:
The experienced healer is selected to coordinate
care. (He/she) insists that the title is less
important than training, hygiene, and listening
to patients.

O8. HEAD SCIENTIST:
The new research lead announces a strict rule:
"NO MORE UNLABELED POWDERS."
Someone asks whether this includes pulverized gemstones.
"ESPECIALLY THOSE."

O9. HEAD FARMER:
The village's most dependable farmer is asked to
coordinate the harvest. The first decree is that
nobody may use cave guano as table seasoning.

O10. HEAD BUILDER:
The Master Builder receives the office after
years of dependable work. (He/she) celebrates
by repairing the same crooked bench that
started (his/her) career.

O11. HEAD DEVOTEE / PRIEST / PRIESTESS / SPIRITUAL LEADER:
The community honors a villager known for patient
service and wisdom. The villager declines an
elaborate crown and asks for a quieter ceremony.

O12. HEAD MAGICIAN — VV3–VV5 ONLY:
The Magician's appointment is celebrated with
an elaborate ritual. The ceremony goes perfectly
until the decorative vines catch on a stool.

O13. HEAD NATURALIST — VV3–VV5 ONLY:
The Naturalist is appointed after years of careful
stewardship. The first public address is interrupted
by a slug crawling over the notes. The Naturalist
calls it "an excellent teaching opportunity."

O14. PUBLIC VINDICATION:
The council corrects a false accusation before
the entire village. (Name1) accepts the apology
but says, "I wish you had asked me first."

O15. NO FORGIVENESS YET:
The wrongdoer apologizes sincerely. The injured
person says, "I believe you're sorry. I am
still angry." The apology remains valid;
the anger remains real.

O16. SECOND CHANCE:
The village grants (Name1) another opportunity
under sensible supervision. (He/she) succeeds
quietly and resists the urge to make a speech.

O17. PERMANENT RIVALRY, PEACEFUL COEXISTENCE:
The two villagers never become friends. They
do, however, stop trying to ruin each other's
work. The village considers this tremendous progress.

O18. HUMILIATING TITLE:
A child announces that (Name1) is now "HEAD OF
THE DEPARTMENT OF TERRIBLE IDEAS." (Name1)
bows deeply and demands an office.

O19. A FRIEND RETURNS:
After days of avoidance, someone sits beside
(Name1) and offers a piece of bread. They
do not discuss the scandal. Not yet.

O20. THE ELDER'S RECOGNITION:
An elderly villager who once mocked (Name1)
says, "I was wrong about you." (Name1)
answers, "I've waited years to hear that."

O21. WRONG GUILT:
(Name1) confesses to damaging the workshop,
convinced that (his/her) experiment caused it.
A careful investigation shows the damage
predated the experiment. The relief leaves
(Name1) shaking.

O22. GUILT WITHOUT A CRIME:
(Name1) admits taking the last piece of bread.
The council points out that it was offered
freely. (Name1) demands to be punished anyway.
An elder prescribes an afternoon of rest.

O23. THE PROMOTION NOBODY WANTED:
The leaders offer (Name1) a senior role.
(Name1) declines, saying, "I like the work.
I don't like meetings." The leaders respect
the choice.

O24. THE TEACHER AND THE RIVAL:
(Name1) offers to teach (Name2) a difficult skill.
(Name2) refuses twice, then arrives before dawn
on the third day, pretending to be passing by.

O25. THE PUBLIC MELTDOWN:
A defeated contestant shouts until (his/her)
voice cracks, then bursts into tears.
The organizers give (him/her) space.
Later, (he/she) apologizes for the insults,
not for feeling disappointed.

O26. THE VERY BAD STATUE:
The village unveils a commemorative statue
of its new advisor. The advisor stares at it.
"Why do I have seven teeth?"
The sculptor replies, "Artistic freedom."

O27. THE UNEXPECTED HEAD:
A quiet worker is appointed head of a field
after years of unglamorous service. The
loudest candidate complains. An apprentice
says, "You never noticed who did the work."

O28. THE FAILED REDEMPTION:
(Name1) makes one impressive gesture, then
immediately returns to old habits. The
village declines to restore (his/her)
responsibilities until behavior improves.

O29. THE COST OF MOCKERY:
A child asks the adults why they are laughing
at someone who is trying to learn. Nobody
has a satisfying answer.

O30. THE REPUTATION THAT OUTLIVES THE SCANDAL:
Years later, a young villager asks whether
the famous snail-shell incident really happened.
(Name1) sighs. "Which version?"

================================================================================
V10 PATCHER IMPLEMENTATION SAFEGUARDS
================================================================================

- Use a reputation descriptor only when the event actually establishes it.
- Titles are narrative honors unless explicitly supported by game mechanics.
- Assign head-of-field roles to suitably qualified adult villagers, with
  culturally plausible selection. Do not overwrite canonical roles.
- VV3 Chief remains protected and cannot be replaced by a random appointment.
- Magician and Naturalist titles, factions, and knowledge are gated to VV3–VV5.
- No Secret City or Chief knowledge in VV1–VV2.
- VV5 neighboring faction is called Isolans (Guardians of Harmony patch).
- In VV1, VV2, VV4 and VV5, use "leaders" instead of "Chief."
- Ostracism, ridicule, and temporary restrictions never remove access to
  food, water, shelter, necessary medical care, or family.
- Avoid turning "village idiot" into an objective game attribute; it is
  a hurtful insult the story can criticize or subvert.
- Prison/custody, if referenced elsewhere, remains narrative-only unless
  the patcher supports the mechanic; do not fabricate incarceration flags.
- Never choose Golden Child, VV3 Chief, infants, nursing mothers/fathers,
  or any engine-protected character for death/disappearance branches.
- If a lethal/disappearance branch has no eligible target, skip it.
- All story outcomes should remain consistent with the evidence and
  the actual action taken in the branch.

THE VILLAGERS AREN'T JUST WORKERS.
THEY ARE PEOPLE WHO CAN BE RIDICULOUS, CRUEL, BRAVE, WRONG,
GENEROUS, ENVIOUS, FORGIVING, UNFORGIVING, AND WONDERFUL.

ISOLA CONTINUES ON. <3


================================================================================
V11 — FULLY WRITTEN EVENTS: THE SECRET OF LIFE & THE IMPOSSIBLE IDEAS
Universal VV1–VV5; medieval-equivalent Isolan materials and language
================================================================================

EDITOR'S NOTE: The following are NEW standalone events, not replacements for
any existing story. Each numbered ending is actual event-popup prose. Choose
one listed outcome within a branch unless the branch explicitly chains to
another popup. The Creator is the only divine title used here.

================================================================================
EVENT V11-1 — THE SECRET OF LIFE
A brilliant researcher's dangerous obsession; serious, absurd and bittersweet
================================================================================

ELIGIBILITY: Pick (Name1), an adult villager with Research experience, and
(Name2), a different eligible adult. Exclude all engine-protected villagers
from removal or injury outcomes. This event is narrative-only unless the
patcher supports the named changes. Never resurrect a dead villager by text
alone. Any apparently revived animal must have been alive all along.

FIRST POPUP:

(Name1) has spent years studying the mysteries of living things. (He/she)
knows how roots drink from the earth, how wounds mend, and how a tiny seed
becomes a towering tree. Yet one question torments (him/her): Why does life
end, and could it ever be persuaded to return?

Lately, (Name1) has been gathering bones, clay, rawhide, dried herbs, and
strange jars from the farthest corners of Isola. (His/her) workshop is lit
long after everyone else has gone to bed. At dawn, (Name2) discovers a
carefully stitched animal-shaped figure lying beneath a sheet.

"It is not what you think," says (Name1).

"I haven't said what I think," replies (Name2).

"Then please continue not thinking it."

(Name1) explains that (he/she) intends to discover the secret of restoring
life itself. (Name2) must decide what to do.

CHOICE A: Permit a supervised, harmless demonstration. -> A1–A6.
CHOICE B: Demand that the experiments stop. -> B1–B6.
CHOICE C: Ask the healers and elders to investigate. -> C1–C7.
CHOICE D: Ask (Name1) what (he/she) is really trying to bring back. -> D1–D6.

-------------------- CHOICE A: THE DEMONSTRATION --------------------

A1 — THE MIRACLE THAT WASN'T:
At sunset, (Name1) unveils a motionless little creature resting on a bed of
leaves. (He/she) raises both hands and cries, "BEHOLD!" The creature suddenly
opens its eyes, scrambles upright, and races out the door. The villagers
scream. (Name1) begins weeping with triumph. Then the head healer examines
the abandoned bed and announces that the creature had merely been sleeping.
"So," asks a child, "you invented waking up?" (Name1) spends the rest of
the evening insisting that this is still a significant discovery.
Outcome: no revival; no reward; village laughter.

A2 — THE GREAT UNVEILING:
The sheet comes off. Beneath it stands a figure of clay, bones, carved wood,
and rawhide, held upright by a complicated arrangement of vines. "My creation
will WALK!" (Name1) declares, pulling a cord. One leg rises. The entire
figure topples forward and scatters across the floor. A small child begins
applauding. "It walked once!" (Name1) shouts. "It fell," says (Name2).
"All great journeys begin with a first step!" "That was its last step."
Outcome: failed experiment; small Research experience for observing the
mechanical problem, if supported.

A3 — THE WRONG SORT OF LIFE:
(Name1) places a sealed pot before the gathering and announces that the
substance inside has become alive. The pot bubbles ominously. An experienced
farmer sniffs it and identifies a mixture of fermented fruit and yeast.
"Then why is it moving?" (Name1) demands. "Because it's fermenting," the
farmer replies. (Name1) considers this for a long time. "Then I have not
conquered death," (he/she) admits, "but I may have improved our bread."
Outcome: modest food or Research reward, if supported; no resurrection.

A4 — THE UNEXPECTED LESSON:
The experiment fails, but one of the healers notices that (Name1)'s careful
records show how different plant fibers hold a dressing together. The
healers test the fibers safely and discover a useful way to bind splints.
(Name1) is congratulated. "I set out to master the boundary between life and
death," (he/she) says grandly. "And instead," replies the healer, "you've
helped someone keep a broken arm still. That is no small thing."
Outcome: Healing or Research experience; no magical effect.

A5 — THE FRIGHTFUL NOISE:
(Name1) has built a tall frame covered in animal hide. At the decisive
moment, (he/she) pulls a vine, releasing a concealed stone weight. The frame
shudders and emits a terrible groan. Half the audience flees. The other half
stays long enough to see (Name1) emerge from behind the curtain, laughing.
"IT MOVED!" (he/she) cries. "You pulled it!" says (Name2). "YES! IT MOVED
BECAUSE I PULLED IT!" Nobody is certain whether the experiment succeeded,
failed, or was intended as a joke. (Name1) refuses to clarify.
Outcome: no reward; no injuries.

A6 — THE QUIET FAILURE:
The villagers watch as (Name1) carefully follows every step in (his/her)
notes. Nothing moves. Nothing breathes. Nothing changes. At last, (Name1)
covers the figure again. "I was so certain," (he/she) whispers. (Name2)
helps clean the workshop without offering a clever remark. The following
morning, (Name1) begins writing a new page: WHAT I LEARNED FROM BEING WRONG.
Outcome: Research experience, if supported; reputation for diligence.

-------------------- CHOICE B: THE WARNING --------------------

B1 — THE OUTRAGED GENIUS:
"You cannot forbid the pursuit of knowledge!" (Name1) cries. (He/she)
throws a dramatic cloak over (his/her) shoulders, announces the founding of
THE GREAT HALL OF FORBIDDEN DISCOVERIES, and storms away. The cloak catches
on the doorframe. (Name1) must return for it. "THIS CHANGES NOTHING!"
(he/she) declares. The villagers agree that it changes very little.
Outcome: no reward; self-appointed eccentric title, narrative-only.

B2 — A REASONABLE BOUNDARY:
(Name2) explains that curiosity is welcome, but taking shared supplies
without permission and frightening the village are not. (Name1) argues,
then reluctantly agrees to keep a proper inventory and demonstrate only
harmless experiments in public. A week later, the workshop door bears a
new sign: DO NOT ENTER UNLESS YOU HAVE A QUESTION WORTH ASKING. Someone adds
beneath it: OR YOU ARE RETURNING OUR BOWLS.
Outcome: research continues under ordinary village rules.

B3 — THE SECRET WORKSHOP:
(Name1) agrees far too quickly. Soon, strange noises are heard from a shed
behind the trees. (Name2) investigates and finds (Name1) surrounded by
wooden birds, clay frogs, and a mechanical crab made of shells. "I said I
would stop trying to bring the dead back," (Name1) says defensively. "I
never promised to stop making things walk." The crab scuttles half an inch
before falling over. (Name2) decides this is an improvement.
Outcome: harmless mechanical tinkering; possible Building experience.

B4 — THE HUMILIATION:
The village laughs at (Name1)'s work, and a few people begin calling
(him/her) a fool. (Name1) quietly locks the workshop and stops attending
research lessons. Days later, (Name2) finds a pile of torn notes beside
the door. "You can tell me my idea is wrong," (Name1) says. "You needn't
tell me I am worthless." (Name2) apologizes and invites (him/her) to help
with a smaller, safer project. It takes time before (Name1) agrees.
Outcome: no instant reconciliation; eventual return to research.

B5 — THE SPECTACULAR RENUNCIATION:
(Name1) gathers the village and solemnly announces that (he/she) is
abandoning all foolish experiments. To prove it, (he/she) throws a bundle
of drawings into the fire. The drawings are damp and refuse to burn.
(Name1) tries again. The fire goes out. A child observes that this may be
(his/her) most successful experiment yet: the discovery of a way to
extinguish flames. (Name1) begins taking notes before anyone can stop
(him/her).
Outcome: Research experience, if supported; no dangerous fire effects.

B6 — A HARD PROMISE:
(Name1) confesses that the experiments have consumed (his/her) days and
nights. "I thought if I worked hard enough, I could make the world obey me,"
(he/she) says. The villagers help dismantle the dangerous parts of the
workshop, leaving the books, tools, and harmless models intact. (Name1)
returns to ordinary work. Sometimes (he/she) still watches the stars with
an expression nobody can quite read.
Outcome: obsession eases; no forced permanent career change.

-------------------- CHOICE C: THE INVESTIGATION --------------------

C1 — THE HEALERS' VERDICT:
The healers examine every jar, drawing, and specimen. At last, the eldest
speaks. "Your observations are careful. Your conclusions are not." (Name1)
looks wounded. "You have not found a way to return life," the healer
continues, "but you have found many questions worth studying properly."
(Name1) agrees to work alongside the healers. (He/she) insists on keeping
the enormous handwritten title THE SECRET OF LIFE above the desk.
Outcome: modest Healing/Research experience; supervised study.

C2 — THE MISSING BONES:
The investigation reveals that several bones used in the figure came from
an old communal teaching collection. (Name1) insists (he/she) meant to
return them. "After your creation walked away?" asks (Name2). "Naturally."
The elders order the collection restored and ask (Name1) to apologize to
the teacher who had been searching for it. (Name1) does so, although
(he/she) continues to describe the matter as a regrettable misunderstanding
between two great minds.
Outcome: restitution; no theft flag unless supported.

C3 — THE DANGEROUS MIXTURE:
A healer discovers that (Name1) has combined unfamiliar plants in a sealed
jar. Rather than opening it near the crowd, the healers isolate the jar and
safely dispose of the mixture. (Name1) is furious at first, then ashamed
when (he/she) realizes how little (he/she) knew about its contents. "I
wanted to discover something extraordinary," (he/she) admits. "Then begin
by learning what you already have," the healer replies.
Outcome: no exposure or injury; safety rules adopted.

C4 — THE GREAT DEBATE:
The elders hold a public discussion on whether a discovery is valuable
simply because it is possible. The farmers argue about resources, the
healers about care, the researchers about freedom, and the children about
whether the clay creature would need to eat. By sunset, no one agrees on
the meaning of life, but everyone agrees that (Name1) must stop borrowing
bowls without asking. The meeting is declared a partial success.
Outcome: no reward; improved village research rules.

C5 — THE UNWELCOME TRUTH:
A healer gently asks why (Name1) is so determined to reverse death. (Name1)
tries to explain the experiments, the measurements, the drawings. Finally,
(he/she) stops. "Because I miss someone," (he/she) says. The workshop falls
silent. The healer sits beside (him/her). No grand discovery is made that
evening, but for the first time in months, (Name1) does not sit alone.
Outcome: quiet grief scene; no resurrection.

C6 — THE ACCIDENTAL MASTERPIECE:
One of the investigators accidentally knocks a wooden arm on the floor.
It springs upright, strikes a hanging bell, and startles everyone. (Name1)
leaps to (his/her) feet. "YOU SEE? IT LIVES!" The builder examines the
arm and discovers an ingenious tensioned-vine mechanism. "It doesn't
live," the builder says, "but it could lift water." (Name1) is offered
help adapting the mechanism. (He/she) accepts, after demanding that the
water-lifter be named THE AWAKENING.
Outcome: Building or Research experience; no resurrection.

C7 — THE UNANSWERED QUESTION:
The elders determine that no law has been broken, but the experiment has
frightened the village. They ask (Name1) to explain the work honestly and
listen to the objections. (Name1) does both, with visible difficulty.
The elders allow harmless research to continue. The question that started
it all remains unanswered, and (Name1) must learn to live with that.
Outcome: no reward; research continues.

-------------------- CHOICE D: THE REASON BEHIND IT --------------------

D1 — THE EMPTY PLACE:
(Name1) looks at the stitched figure for a long time. "There used to be
someone who asked me what I was studying every morning," (he/she) says.
"Now nobody does." (Name2) does not pretend to have an answer. Instead,
(he/she) offers to visit the workshop each morning and ask about the day's
work. It is a small promise, but (Name1) accepts it.
Outcome: compassionate resolution; no resurrection.

D2 — THE FEAR OF DYING:
"I am afraid," (Name1) admits. "One day everything I have learned will
disappear with me." (Name2) suggests teaching the younger researchers.
(Name1) objects that the children will ask foolish questions. "Then you'll
have plenty in common," (Name2) replies. The next week, (Name1) begins a
lesson on living things. It is interrupted by seventeen questions and one
frog. (Name1) enjoys it more than expected.
Outcome: Research experience; potential teaching role, narrative-only.

D3 — THE PROMISE:
(Name1) explains that (he/she) once promised a loved one to discover why
living things die. The promise became an obsession. (Name2) asks whether
the loved one would have wanted (him/her) to stop living in order to keep
it. (Name1) cannot answer immediately. That evening, (he/she) closes the
workshop early and joins the village meal.
Outcome: obsession softens; no magical effect.

D4 — THE PUREST CURIOSITY:
"Nobody," (Name1) replies, genuinely puzzled. "I simply wanted to know
