whether it could be done." (Name2) stares at the clay figure. "You spent
six months building a dead goat out of sticks because you were curious?"
"It is not a goat," (Name1) replies indignantly. "It is a prototype."
"A prototype of what?" (Name1) opens (his/her) mouth, then closes it.
For the first time, (he/she) realizes the question deserves an answer.
Outcome: no reward; comic self-reflection.

D5 — THE WRONG LESSON:
(Name1) proudly reveals that (he/she) has been trying to create a creature
that will do all the village's chores. "Then why give it claws?" (Name2)
asks. "For gathering fruit." "And teeth?" "For cracking coconuts."
"And three heads?" "TO MULTIPLY ITS EFFICIENCY!" (Name2) quietly suggests
that a basket and two willing workers might be more practical. (Name1)
accuses (him/her) of lacking vision.
Outcome: no reward; no actual creature created.

D6 — A NEW PURPOSE:
(Name1) confesses that (he/she) wanted to do something no one else had
ever done. (Name2) reminds (him/her) that discoveries need not be grand to
matter. Months later, (Name1) finishes a careful guide to the plants and
animals of Isola. A child reads the first page and asks a question that
(Name1) cannot answer. (He/she) smiles. "Excellent. We shall find out."
Outcome: Research experience; enduring teaching legacy.

================================================================================
EVENT V11-2 — THE INVENTION SHOWCASE
An invention fair where nonsense, brilliance and humiliation collide
================================================================================

FIRST POPUP:

The village has agreed to hold an exhibition of new inventions. (Name1),
a notoriously imaginative researcher, arrives carrying a bundle of
sketches so large that (he/she) cannot see over it. (Name2), a practical
builder, offers to help sort the proposals.

"I have discovered seventeen ways to improve village life," announces
(Name1). "Have you tested any of them?" asks (Name2).

"Of course not! That would spoil the surprise."

The first proposal is (IDEA). The villagers must decide how to respond.

IDEA BANK — choose one; all use pre-industrial materials:
- a bridge woven from spider silk and vines
- a chair that scratches the sitter's back with wooden fingers
- a rain-catching roof made entirely of enormous leaves
- a wooden device that turns itself off whenever someone starts it
- a special frame intended to force coconuts to grow square
- a cart that is pulled by snails
- a hollow coconut meant to catch rain before it falls
- a statue designed to frighten storms away
- a writing system made entirely of pictures of frogs
- a tree planted upside down to make its fruit easier to pick
- a machine for teaching fish to climb trees
- a stone wheel with corners, "so it won't roll away"
- a basket that can carry itself downhill but not uphill
- a pair of sandals with little umbrellas for each toe
- a bell that rings whenever the village is too noisy
- a device for stirring soup using the motion of a goat's tail
- a very long spoon for eating from someone else's bowl
- a rope ladder that is longer at the top than at the bottom
- a new kind of door that can only be opened when it is already open
- a water wheel placed on dry land "to save water"

CHOICE A: Give the inventor supplies for a small, supervised test. -> A1–A8.
CHOICE B: Invite everyone to vote on the idea. -> B1–B7.
CHOICE C: Ask the inventor to explain exactly what problem it solves. -> C1–C8.

-------------------- CHOICE A: TEST IT --------------------

A1 — THE UNLIKELY TRIUMPH:
The villagers gather expecting disaster. Instead, the little contraption
works beautifully. The bridge holds a basket of stones; the leaf roof
keeps a bench dry; or the wooden mechanism performs its humble task without
breaking. (Name2) checks the result twice. "Well," (he/she) says, "I owe
you an apology." (Name1) folds (his/her) arms. "I shall accept it in the
form of more materials." The village agrees to improve the design before
using it for anything important.
Outcome: modest Building/Research experience; no unsafe instant deployment.

A2 — THE ABSOLUTELY USELESS SUCCESS:
The device works exactly as (Name1) promised. It turns itself off, rings
when someone shouts, or moves a stone from one side of a table to the
other. Everyone waits for the useful part. "That is the useful part,"
(Name1) explains. "But why?" asks (Name2). "Because now we know it can be
done." The council awards the invention a certificate for TECHNICAL
SUCCESS AND COMPLETE PRACTICAL IRRELEVANCE.
Outcome: no resources; comic reputation.

A3 — THE EXPENSIVE FAILURE:
After three days of work, the contraption collapses into a heap of vines,
wood, and broken shells. (Name1) stares at the wreckage. "The theory was
sound," (he/she) says. "The legs weren't," replies (Name2). The council
orders that future demonstrations use scraps before consuming precious
supplies. (Name1) calls the rule oppressive. The farmers call it sensible.
Outcome: optional small resource loss only if explicitly configured.

A4 — THE UNEXPECTED DISCOVERY:
The invention fails magnificently. While trying to repair it, however,
(Name2) notices that a particular knot holds far more weight than the
others. The builders test the knot, improve it, and teach it to the village.
(Name1) claims credit for the discovery. (Name2) points out that the
original machine was supposed to teach fish to climb trees. "And yet,"
(Name1) replies, "look how far science has come."
Outcome: Building experience; no trained fish.

A5 — THE SNAIL PROBLEM:
The cart moves! The crowd cheers! The snails continue moving with
magnificent determination. By sundown, the cart has advanced the width of
a coconut. (Name1) announces that the transportation problem has been
solved. (Name2) asks how long a journey to the other side of the village
would take. (Name1) requests several weeks to calculate the answer.
Outcome: no reward; no animal injury.

A6 — THE FROG ALPHABET:
(Name1) unveils a new writing system containing sixty-three drawings of
frogs. "This symbol means harvest," (he/she) explains. "And this one
means rain." (Name2) points to a third frog. "What does that mean?"
(Name1) hesitates. "I forgot." The villagers vote to keep their existing
writing system. One child, however, begins using the frog symbols to pass
secret messages to friends. Within a week, the children understand it
better than its inventor.
Outcome: Learning/Research experience; no official language change.

A7 — THE TREE THAT REFUSES:
(Name1) plants a young tree with its roots pointing toward the sky.
"Now the fruit will grow underground where we can reach it!" (he/she)
announces. The farmers gently explain that roots are not merely branches
with poor ambition. (Name1) is unconvinced until the tree begins to wilt.
The farmers replant it properly. "Perhaps," (Name1) admits, "trees have
strong opinions about which way is up."
Outcome: tree saved; no harvest penalty.

A8 — THE TEST THAT NEVER ENDS:
The invention almost works. Then it almost works again. By the seventh
attempt, (Name1) has announced seven separate breakthroughs, none of which
have occurred. (Name2) suggests stopping for the day. "But the next test
could change everything!" (Name1) protests. "Then it can change
everything tomorrow," replies (Name2). The council establishes a sensible
limit on how long one person may occupy the workshop.
Outcome: no reward; future testing rules.

-------------------- CHOICE B: LET THE VILLAGE VOTE --------------------

B1 — THE LANDSLIDE:
The villagers vote overwhelmingly against the invention. (Name1) is
stunned. "You have rejected the future!" (he/she) cries. An elder points
out that the future may return with a better design. (Name1) vows to do
just that, but only after giving a long speech about how history will
remember this injustice. Nobody stays for the entire speech.
Outcome: proposal declined; no punishment.

B2 — THE CHILDREN'S FAVORITE:
Every adult votes against the invention. Every child votes for it. The
children win by a single vote after an elderly villager changes (his/her)
mind, saying that a frog-shaped rain catcher would brighten the village.
(Name1) is delighted. (Name2) insists that the first version be small,
cheap, and harmless. It becomes the most beloved useless object in Isola.
Outcome: decorative invention; no mechanical benefit.

B3 — THE POPULAR DISASTER:
The crowd loves the idea. The builders, who must actually construct it,
do not. "A bridge of spider silk!" cries one admirer. "A masterpiece!"
"Have you ever tried collecting enough spider silk to make a bridge?"
asks (Name2). The admirer has not. After a long argument, the council
approves a miniature demonstration instead. The tiny bridge holds one
pebble. Everyone cheers as though an entire river has been crossed.
Outcome: no full-scale bridge; possible Research experience.

B4 — THE RIGGED BALLOT:
(Name1) wins the vote by a suspicious margin. Investigation reveals that
(he/she) has submitted several ballots under the names of (his/her)
inventions. "THE GREAT RAIN CATCHER IS A STAKEHOLDER!" (he/she) protests.
The council voids the extra ballots and orders a fair vote. (Name1)
accepts the ruling with the dignity of someone who has been caught very
publicly cheating at something extremely silly.
Outcome: reputation damage; no permanent exclusion.

B5 — THE TIE:
The vote is evenly divided. The council asks (Name1) to explain the idea
once more. After the explanation, three supporters change their votes to
"no," four opponents change theirs to "yes," and one villager announces
that (he/she) no longer understands what a coconut is. The council
postpones the decision. (Name1) calls this a historic victory.
Outcome: proposal postponed.

B6 — THE QUIET SUPPORTER:
The crowd laughs until a respected elder rises. "I remember when people
laughed at the first person who tried to carry water in a woven vessel,"
(he/she) says. "This idea may be dreadful. But laughter is not proof."
The council agrees to test a small version. (Name1) thanks the elder,
then immediately proposes a second invention involving upside-down goats.
The elder sits down again.
Outcome: supervised test later; no guaranteed success.

B7 — THE MOST IMPORTANT VOTE:
After hours of arguing, the villagers vote not on the invention but on
whether (Name1) should be allowed to speak at the next meeting. The
motion passes, narrowly. (Name1) takes the result as evidence that the
village fears (his/her) genius. (Name2) quietly suggests that speeches
should be shorter than the inventions they describe.
Outcome: no mechanical change.

-------------------- CHOICE C: WHAT IS IT FOR? --------------------

C1 — THE PERFECTLY SIMPLE ANSWER:
"To make work easier," says (Name1). (Name2) asks whose work. (Name1)
looks at the drawing. "Mine, obviously." It turns out the contraption
would save (Name1) ten steps each morning while requiring two other
villagers to operate it all day. The council declines the proposal.
(Name1) complains that nobody appreciates efficiency.
Outcome: no reward.

C2 — THE ANSWER NOBODY EXPECTED:
(Name1) explains the problem clearly, demonstrates a simple model, and
shows exactly how much labor the invention might save. (Name2), prepared
for nonsense, is startled. "Why didn't you say all that at the start?"
"Because," says (Name1), "I thought the dramatic unveiling would be more
impressive." The council approves a cautious trial. For once, the most
ridiculous-looking proposal has a perfectly sensible purpose.
Outcome: modest Building/Research experience; trial only.

C3 — THE INFINITE EXPLANATION:
(Name1) begins with the history of coconuts, proceeds to the movement of
the stars, and somehow arrives at the mating habits of crabs. Three hours
later, (Name2) interrupts. "WHAT DOES THE MACHINE DO?" (Name1) pauses.
"I was just getting to that." The meeting is adjourned before sunset.
Outcome: no reward.

C4 — THE PHILOSOPHICAL MACHINE:
"It solves the problem of meaning," (Name1) announces. (Name2) studies
the wooden box. "How?" "By proving that even an apparently useless thing
can have a purpose." "And what is its purpose?" "To prove that."
(Name2) rubs (his/her) temples. The village's philosophers are delighted.
The builders are not.
Outcome: no resource benefit; possible Devotion/Research discussion.

C5 — THE INVENTOR ADMITS DEFEAT:
(Name1) stares at the drawings for a long time. "I don't know," (he/she)
finally says. The admission surprises everyone, including (Name1).
Instead of laughing, (Name2) asks which part of the idea first seemed
interesting. Together they identify a smaller question worth testing.
(Name1) goes home with fewer grand plans and one genuinely good one.
Outcome: Research experience, if supported.

C6 — THE JOKE THAT WASN'T:
The crowd bursts into laughter as (Name1) explains the device. (Name1)
does not laugh. "I worked on this for months," (he/she) says quietly.
The room grows uncomfortable. (Name2) apologizes for the ridicule, then
explains the actual flaws in the design. (Name1) listens, still hurt, but
asks for help improving it. The invention may never work. The apology
still matters.
Outcome: no reward; respectful criticism replaces mockery.

C7 — THE JOKE THAT WAS:
(Name1) solemnly announces a new device for carrying water without getting
wet. It is a bowl. (Name2) stares. The council stares. (Name1) maintains
a perfectly serious expression until an elder begins explaining how bowls
work. Then (Name1) collapses with laughter. "I HAVE BEEN WAITING ALL WEEK
FOR SOMEONE TO SAY THAT!" (he/she) wheezes. The council bans practical
jokes from the next invention fair. The children immediately begin
planning their entries.
Outcome: no reward; harmless prank.

C8 — THE IDEA THAT CHANGES EVERYTHING:
(Name1) admits that the machine is impossible as drawn. "But what if we
used the same arrangement of vines to lift a heavy stone?" asks (Name2).
The builders try it. To everyone's surprise, the arrangement works. The
original invention is abandoned, and a useful lifting frame takes its
place. (Name1) watches the first stone rise and grins. "I told you my
ridiculous ideas might work." "One part of one idea," (Name2) corrects.
"I SHALL TAKE IT!" replies (Name1).
Outcome: Building/Research experience; no instant puzzle completion.

================================================================================
EVENT V11-3 — THE COUNCIL OF CONUNDRUMS
The village formalizes a very bad habit
================================================================================

FIRST POPUP:

After a string of disastrous experiments, (Name1) proposes forming a
special council dedicated to investigating ideas that everyone else
considers absurd. (Name2) points out that this describes nearly every
idea (Name1) has ever had.

"Precisely!" says (Name1). "We are already experts."

The council would accept proposals from any villager, including children,
but would need clear rules about money, materials, and safety.

CHOICE A: Establish the council with strict testing rules. -> A1–A5.
CHOICE B: Let the council operate freely for one day. -> B1–B6.
CHOICE C: Reject the council but invite one proposal. -> C1–C5.

A1 — THE FIRST SUCCESS:
The council's first proposal is a better way to tie bundles of firewood.
Nobody is impressed until the farmers discover that the knot saves time.
(Name1) unveils a grand banner reading THE DEPARTMENT OF TERRIBLE IDEAS.
(Name2) asks whether the name is still appropriate. "More than ever,"
(Name1) says. "We have proved that terrible ideas deserve investigation."
Outcome: modest Building or Farming experience.

A2 — THE SAFETY RULE:
The council produces a thick list of regulations. Rule One: Do not test
bridges with people standing on them. Rule Two: Do not taste unknown
powders. Rule Three: Do not borrow the healers' supplies without asking.
Rule Four: Do not add a goat to an experiment merely because a goat is
available. (Name1) objects vehemently to Rule Four. The council adopts
it unanimously.
Outcome: no reward; safer demonstrations.

A3 — THE CHILD PRODIGY:
A child proposes a small shelter that keeps seedlings shaded during the
hottest part of the day. The adults politely explain why it might fail.
The child asks to test it anyway. A week later, the sheltered seedlings
are thriving. (Name1) announces that the child is a genius. The child
asks whether that means (he/she) can skip chores. It does not.
Outcome: Farming/Research experience; child not assigned adult labor.

A4 — THE PAPERWORK:
(Name1) discovers that the council requires records, receipts, and
meetings. (He/she) spends the entire week writing proposals about how to
reduce the amount of writing required for proposals. The first suggestion
is to appoint a second secretary. The second secretary immediately
resigns. (Name2) quietly enjoys the spectacle.
Outcome: no reward.

A5 — THE HUMBLE DISCOVERY:
Most proposals fail, but the villagers learn to ask better questions and
to record what happened rather than what they hoped would happen. At the
season's end, (Name1) presents a book containing twenty-nine failures and
one useful discovery. "A poor record," (he/she) admits. "A splendid
beginning," replies (Name2).
Outcome: Research experience; no miracle invention.

B1 — THE GOAT-POWERED SOUP STIRRER:
A goat wanders past the cooking fire. (Name1) ties a long wooden spoon to
a loose frame beside it, hoping the goat's tail will stir a pot. The goat
walks away. The spoon falls into the soup. The cook declares the
experiment over and bans (Name1) from the kitchen until further notice.
Outcome: no harm to the goat; no reward.

B2 — THE UNSTOPPABLE SCRATCHER:
The back-scratching chair works perfectly. Unfortunately, its wooden
fingers continue scratching after the sitter stands up. The chair
scratches the air for several minutes before its vine cord unwinds.
(Name1) calls it tireless. (Name2) calls it haunted. The chair is retired
before anyone tries to improve it with sharper fingers.
Outcome: no injury; no reward.

B3 — THE STORM-FRIGHTENING STATUE:
The villagers erect a frightening statue intended to scare away storms.
That night, rain pours over the entire village. (Name1) announces that the
statue has worked: "The storm was so frightened it wept!" (Name2) asks
whether there is any result (Name1) would consider failure. (Name1)
thinks carefully. "No," (he/she) admits.
Outcome: no weather effect.

B4 — THE BASKET OF TOMORROW:
(Name1) unveils a basket designed to roll downhill by itself. It does.
Unfortunately, the basket rolls directly into a pond. (Name1) declares
that this proves the principle of effortless transport. The fishermen
return the basket and ask that the next demonstration take place farther
from their nets.
Outcome: no reward; no lost food.

B5 — THE GREAT COCONUT DEBATE:
A proposal to grow square coconuts divides the village. The builders want
fruit that stacks neatly. The farmers insist that trees do not care about
storage. The cooks ask whether the fruit will taste different. (Name1)
finally produces a wooden box and places an ordinary coconut inside.
"BEHOLD!" (he/she) cries. "A square coconut container!" The council
votes to award (Name1) half a prize for solving half a problem.
Outcome: no actual square fruit; possible Building experience.

B6 — THE DAY ENDS:
By sunset, the village has acquired a frog alphabet, three broken stools,
a basket in a tree, and a handwritten petition demanding that (Name1)
stop calling everything a breakthrough. (Name1) announces that the day
has been an overwhelming success because nobody can remember being bored.
(Name2) admits that this, at least, is true.
Outcome: no reward; experiment day ends.

C1 — THE GOOD ONE:
The single proposal is a simple tool for lifting hot cooking pots without
burning one's hands. The cook tests it and approves. (Name1) is so shocked
that (he/she) forgets to make a speech. (Name2) quietly writes down the
date for future generations.
Outcome: small Building/Research experience.

C2 — THE VERY BAD ONE:
(Name1) proposes putting a roof over the ocean to prevent rain from
wetting the fish. (Name2) asks how much wood this would require. (Name1)
answers, "All of it." The council rejects the proposal. (Name1)
accuses them of lacking ambition. The builders accuse (Name1) of lacking
an understanding of oceans.
Outcome: no reward.

C3 — THE MISUNDERSTOOD ONE:
A shy villager suggests hanging small bells along a path to help people
find their way in darkness. The council initially laughs, imagining an
entire forest of bells. Then a healer points out that a few bells near
an important turn might genuinely help. The idea is tested on a short,
safe path. (Name1) is delighted that someone else's absurdity has become
useful.
Outcome: small Building/Healing experience.

C4 — THE OLD IDEA:
The proposal is presented with great ceremony. An elder recognizes it
immediately as a tool used by (his/her) grandparents. (Name1) is crushed.
"So I invented nothing?" (he/she) asks. "You rediscovered something
worth remembering," replies the elder. The villagers invite the elder to
teach the forgotten method.
Outcome: Research experience; recovered traditional knowledge.

C5 — THE NEXT YEAR:
The council rejects the proposal but asks (Name1) to return next season
with a better-tested version. (Name1) agrees, then produces a second
bundle of sketches from beneath (his/her) cloak. (Name2) stares in horror.
"I thought we were only considering one idea!" "We are," says (Name1).
"These are merely its forty-seven improvements." The meeting ends
immediately.
Outcome: no reward; recurring comic character arc.

================================================================================
V11 IMPLEMENTATION & CONTINUITY NOTES
================================================================================

- V11-1, V11-2 and V11-3 are separate event IDs; never merge their A/B/C
  branches. V11-1 also has D branches.
- All outcome labels are unique within their event; branch selection is
  mutually exclusive unless a future implementation explicitly adds a chain.
- Random IDEA bank text is used only in the first popup of V11-2. Some
  outcomes feature specific inventions as independent, fixed gags; they do
  not claim to resolve whichever IDEA was rolled in the introduction.
- If stronger continuity is desired, match fixed invention endings to their
  corresponding IDEA entries, or introduce them as separate demonstrations.
- Awards are deliberately described by type, not fabricated numeric amounts.
  Use the existing game's scaling and event reward mechanics if enabled.
- No modern electricity, machinery, laboratories, outside fictional names,
  or technology beyond plausible Isolan craft appears in these stories.
- No villager is actually resurrected. Mechanical models and apparently
  living creatures are explained without requiring new supernatural rules.
- Do not apply death, injury, reputation, title, occupation, or inventory
  changes without game-engine support and valid eligibility checks.
- Criticism of a bad idea should not imply the inventor is worthless.
- Preserve the original V1–V10 material above exactly as written.

================================================================================
V13 — THE CRITICS, THE IMPOSSIBLE, AND THE GLORIOUSLY OFFENSIVE
================================================================================

AUTHORSHIP AND IDEA CREDIT — V13
Original creative direction, requested competition critic archetypes, and
five specific outcome concepts: Lorsieab2.
Original event text, new characters, individual dialogue, additional
outcomes, branching and implementation notes below: ChatGPT (OpenAI).
The Cooking Contest's original text and randomized framework remain
credited to Lorsieab2. These are OPTIONAL ADD-ON branches, not rewrites
of the original. The other competition add-ons preserve the authorship
of their existing base events as identified in the V12 credit ledger.

CANON AND TECHNOLOGY:
Every person, institution, device and metaphor must be intelligible to
an Isolan village using craft, agriculture, simple mechanical devices,
herbal medicine, oral history and written records. No outside fictional
character references, electrical power, modern engines or contemporary
celebrity names in event dialogue. VV1 Southern Shore; VV2 Western Shore;
VV3 Secret City, where the Chief exists; VV4 Tree of Life; VV5 New
Believers with the Guardians of Harmony naming of the other community
as Isolans. Magic and Nature factions/identities only in VV3–VV5.
The Chief is never assigned as a random critic or contestant. In VV5
an Isolan visitor is an offscreen NPC, not a player-controlled villager.

================================================================================
V13-A — VV2 THE COOKING CONTEST: THE JUDGE WHO FEARED NO DISH
OPTIONAL INTEGRATION INTO LORSIEAB2'S ORIGINAL EVENT
================================================================================

INTRODUCE AS ONE OPTIONAL JUDGE BEFORE THE ORIGINAL JUDGING PHASE.
A famous visiting cook, (Judge), has been asked to examine the finalists'
work. (He/she) has served feasts for entire villages, trained more
apprentices than (he/she) can count, and once rejected a banquet because
the soup tasted like boiled sandals. (He/she) has an extraordinary palate,
a terrifying voice, and absolutely no patience for carelessness.

JUDGE TEMPERAMENT (one):
- thunderous, theatrical, and almost impossible to impress
- quiet and devastating, speaking in a whisper that frightens everyone
- warm toward earnest apprentices, merciless toward boastful masters
- astonishingly fair, but prone to comparisons involving dead fish
- perpetually furious at wasted food and unhygienic preparation

JUDGE'S FIRST REMARK (one):
- "I've eaten better meals during a famine! At least then I was grateful!"
- "This fish died twice. Once in the sea, and once in your hands."
- "You call this a stew? I've seen puddles with greater ambition."
- "Beautifully presented. Pity you forgot to make it edible."
- "Who seasoned this? A frightened snail?"
- "A fine dish. I'm almost angry that I have nothing to complain about."
- "Do not tell me how much effort it took. Tell me why it tastes good."

CHOICE A: Invite the judge to taste both dishes publicly. (A1–A6)
CHOICE B: Ask the judge to give constructive instruction first. (B1–B5)
CHOICE C: Allow the finalists to challenge the judge's standards. (C1–C5)
CHOICE D: Hold a blind tasting, with the judge unaware of the cooks. (D1–D5)

A1 — THE THUNDERCLAP:
(Judge) takes one mouthful of (Name1)'s stew, sets down the spoon, and
asks whether the cook is trying to feed the villagers or punish them.
(Name1) protests that it is an ancient family recipe. "Then your
ancestors deserve an apology!" The crowd roars. Later, the judge
privately explains exactly what went wrong and offers another lesson.
Result: temporary laughingstock; optional mentorship, no forced dislike.

A2 — THE PERFECT DISH:
(Judge) tastes (Name2)'s dish. The hall falls silent. (He/she) tastes
it again, then a third time. "Well," (he/she) mutters, "that's
infuriating." Asked what is wrong, (Judge) replies, "Nothing."
(Name2) wins the judge's praise; use the original contest's winner
routing and award rules, not an extra duplicate reward.

A3 — THE DISASTER THAT EVACUATED THE VILLAGE:
The lid is lifted from (Name1)'s covered pot. A thick, eye-watering
cloud rolls across the judging table. The judge staggers backward.
"CLOSE IT! CLOSE THE WRETCHED THING!" The cooks flee, the spectators
scatter, and the entire village retreats to higher ground until the
smell disperses. The dish consists of fermented fish, old cabbage,
tree resin and a seasoning that (Name1) refuses to identify.
The food is discarded. The judges postpone the competition. Nobody
is harmed, but the village speaks of THE GREAT STENCH for generations.
Result: no food reward; optional temporary embarrassment; no automatic
punishment without investigation.

A4 — THE REVOLTING MASTERPIECE:
(Name2) presents a grey, quivering mound covered in dark green sauce.
(Judge) closes (his/her) eyes in despair, then tastes a tiny portion.
The expression changes. "It's... delicious." The audience gasps.
"I hate everything about looking at it," says (Judge), "but I
would gladly eat another bowl." Result: surprising praise; judging
continues according to the original contest's framework.

A5 — THE JUDGE IS WRONG:
(Judge) loudly condemns a dish for containing a bitter herb. A quiet
healer points out that the herb was carefully prepared and is a
traditional delicacy. The judge tastes it properly, pauses, and
apologizes before everyone. "Even I can be wrong. Remember that."
Result: respected judge; cook vindicated; no automatic win.

A6 — THE FURIOUS APPRENTICE:
(Name1) finally snaps. "If you know so much, why don't YOU cook?"
(Judge) calmly accepts. (He/she) prepares a modest dish from the
remaining ingredients, explaining every step. It is exquisite.
(Name1) may apologize, study in silence, or vow to surpass the judge.
Result: rivalry or mentorship; the judge does not enter as a contestant.

B1 — THE LESSON:
(Judge) has the cooks prepare a simple broth. The boastful finalist
tries to hide an elaborate garnish in it. "NO!" bellows the judge.
"First learn to feed someone. Then learn to impress them."
Both improve, and the competition resumes.

B2 — THE KINDNESS BENEATH THE RAGE:
A nervous apprentice drops a spoon. (Judge) kneels to retrieve it and
says quietly, "A mistake is not a disgrace. Refusing to learn is."
When a senior cook laughs, the judge turns on (him/her): "And what
exactly have YOU taught anyone today?" The room falls silent.

B3 — THE USELESS LESSON:
(Judge) explains how to judge a stew by scent. Unfortunately, a
nearby cauldron of spoiled shellfish overwhelms every nose in the
hall. "My lesson," says the judge grimly, "has been defeated."

B4 — THE TEACHER IS TESTED:
A child asks why the judge shouts at people. (Judge) pauses, admits
that shouting is not always helpful, and conducts the next lesson
without raising (his/her) voice. Everyone finds this more frightening.

B5 — THE OLD RECIPE:
An elderly villager shares a simple family recipe. (Judge) listens,
tastes, and asks for the method to be recorded so it is not lost.
Result: culinary knowledge preserved; optional Research experience.

C1 — THE CHALLENGE:
(Name1) accuses the judge of valuing reputation over flavor. The
judge orders a blind tasting and agrees to be bound by the result.
(Name1)'s dish wins. "You were right," the judge says. "Don't
become insufferable about it." (Name1) immediately becomes insufferable.

C2 — THE OUTBURST:
(Name2) screams that the judge is a tyrant. The judge replies,
"Perhaps. But the stew is still burnt." The organizers separate
the pair, allow both to cool down, and resume fairly.

C3 — THE BRIBE:
A finalist attempts to slip the judge a polished pearl. (Judge)
raises it for the whole crowd to see. "Is this supposed to improve
the taste?" A fair investigation follows; the judges do not punish
the other contestant for the attempted bribe.

C4 — THE BET:
The finalists challenge the judge to identify ingredients while
blindfolded. (He/she) identifies every herb, then confidently calls
one ingredient "river mud." It is actually mashed beans. The
villagers never let (him/her) forget it.

C5 — THE CRITIC'S CRITIC:
A visiting elder politely reviews the judge's review, identifying
three unsupported claims and two excellent observations. The judge
is speechless. The elder offers to teach (him/her) how to accept
criticism. Result: hilarious role reversal, possible friendship.

D1 — THE SURPRISE:
The judge praises the dish of a cook (he/she) had publicly dismissed.
When the identity is revealed, (Judge) congratulates the cook openly.

D2 — THE FAVORITE LOSES:
The village's celebrated cook loses to an unassuming apprentice.
(Judge) says, "The spoon does not care about your title." The
apprentice nearly drops the trophy.

D3 — THE DRAW:
The judge cannot separate the two dishes and calls for a second
blind tasting. Use the original contest's tie-breaker branches.

D4 — THE FATAL FLAW IN THE PLAN:
Both cooks have made exactly the same dish after secretly borrowing
the same recipe. The judge suspects collusion; a hearing establishes
that both were independently taught by the same grandmother.

D5 — THE GREAT CRITIC'S HUMILIATION:
The judge mistakenly praises a bowl of plain boiled roots as the
most imaginative dish in the contest. The roots were intended as a
side dish. The audience erupts. "At least," says the judge,
"someone here knows when to leave a good ingredient alone."

INTEGRATION: These branches replace or embellish a judging scene only.
Do not stack contradictory endings or issue duplicate food/skill rewards.
Original Cooking Contest: concept, framework and initial writing by
Lorsieab2; V13 critic and these branches written by ChatGPT from
Lorsieab2's requested severe-critic archetype.

================================================================================
V13-B — UNIVERSAL COMPETITION CRITICS: THE PANEL OF IMPOSSIBLE STANDARDS
ADD-ON TO BUILDING, ART, RESEARCH, STORYTELLING, FARMING, DEVOTION
================================================================================

For each contest, optionally introduce one celebrated but formidable
expert. Their harshness is a CHARACTER TRAIT, not evidence they are
always right. If the base contest has a neutral judging requirement,
this expert serves on an impartial panel or gives commentary only.

BUILDING — MASTER STONEWRIGHT (NAME3):
An elderly craftsperson known for inspecting walls by tapping them
with a knuckle. "I've seen better foundations under a sleeping pig!"
(He/she) can also recognize brilliant construction and will personally
apologize when a supposedly impossible design proves sound.

SCULPTURE/BEAUTIFICATION — MASTER CARVER (NAME3):
A renowned sculptor who insists that art should have intention,
balance, and something worth looking at. "Is it a person? A potato?
Or a potato suffering from terrible news?" (He/she) secretly loves
works that are ugly but strangely moving.

RESEARCH/INVENTION — THE STERN SCHOLAR (NAME3):
A senior researcher who asks the same question of every invention:
"What does it do, and how do you know?" The scholar will reject a
beautiful fraud, praise an ugly success, and demand that failed tests
be recorded instead of concealed.

STORYTELLING/POETRY — THE KEEPER OF VERSES (NAME3):
A famous poet and historian with an astonishing memory. (He/she)
notices every misplaced word, faulty rhyme, and unintentional insult.
"You have managed to rhyme 'moon' with 'coconut.' I would like to
know how, and I am afraid of the answer."

FARMING — THE HARVEST MASTER (NAME3):
An experienced grower who distrusts impressive speeches about crops.
"Show me the roots. The roots never flatter anybody." This judge
may recognize practical genius in a seemingly foolish technique.

DEVOTION/CEREMONY — THE ELDER OF RITES (NAME3):
A respected keeper of ceremonies who values sincerity and accuracy.
"A loud voice is not the same thing as a wise heart." (He/she) may
rebuke a performer for disrespect or praise a new interpretation that
honors the tradition's spirit. In VV3–VV5, optional Magic/Nature
specialists may join, but never in VV1–VV2.

OPTIONAL CHOICES FOR ANY COMPETITION:
A. Accept the expert's judgment and request specific feedback.
B. Ask for a second, independent judge.
C. Let the contestants defend their decisions before the audience.
D. Require a practical demonstration rather than relying on reputation.

GENERIC ENDINGS (one appropriate to the contest):
1. THE EXPERT IS VINDICATED: The flaw appears during the demonstration.
   The contestant is embarrassed but learns how to correct it.
2. THE EXPERT IS HUMBLED: The supposed flaw turns out to be intentional
   and effective. The expert publicly acknowledges the oversight.
3. THE CROWD DISAGREES: The expert dislikes the work, but the village
   adores it. The judges explain that popularity and technical merit
   are different measures; neither is automatically worthless.
4. THE BITTER RIVAL: The contestant rejects the criticism, vows to
   return next year, and spends months practicing.
5. THE UNEXPECTED MENTOR: The judge offers instruction after seeing
   determination beneath the errors.
6. THE SHAMEFUL INSULT: The judge crosses from critique into cruelty.
   The organizers demand an apology and appoint a fair replacement.
7. THE STUNNING MASTERPIECE: The judge is rendered speechless for the
   first time in living memory. A child asks whether (he/she) is ill.
8. THE POINTLESS PERFECTION: The work is technically flawless but
   serves no discernible purpose. The judge awards skill, not utility.

================================================================================
V13-C — UNIVERSAL: "IT'S ALIVE!"
SENTIENT CONSTRUCT / LIFE-CREATION SCARE — THREE CHOICES
================================================================================

(Name1), a gifted but secretive researcher, unveils a human-shaped
figure built from carved wood, clay, tough vines, animal rawhide,
polished stones and intricate joints. (He/she) insists it is more
than a sculpture. The figure turns its head, lifts an arm, and
whispers something nobody can quite understand.

The crowd falls silent. Then the figure takes three steps.

CHOICE A — Praise (Name1) for creating something alive. (A1–A4)
CHOICE B — Demand an inquiry into forbidden practices. (B1–B4)
CHOICE C — Examine the construct before making any claims. (C1–C5)

A1 — THE COLLAPSE:
The villagers cheer that (Name1) has created life like the spirits
of old. The construct raises its arms in triumph, takes a fourth
step, and collapses into an untidy pile of wood and clay. The
researcher weeps over the pieces. An apprentice quietly asks whether
this means the creature has died. Nobody knows how to answer.

A2 — THE NEW CREATOR:
(Name1) is praised as a maker blessed by the spirits. (He/she)
accepts the title with alarming enthusiasm and orders a ceremonial
crown. Before the crown arrives, the construct falls apart. The
crowd debates whether divine favor has a very short duration.

A3 — THE HEARTBREAK:
The construct appears to recognize its maker. It reaches toward
(Name1) and makes a sound like a child trying to speak. Then its
joints give way. (Name1) spends the night rebuilding it, sobbing
that (he/she) had barely begun to know (his/her) creation.

A4 — THE FRAUD:
A hidden apprentice emerges from behind a screen, admitting to
pulling concealed vine cords. The audience boos. (Name1) says it
was meant as a demonstration of clever mechanisms, not life itself.
The village must decide whether the presentation was dishonest.

B1 — THE ACCUSATION:
A devotee shouts, "WITCHCRAFT!" Another calls it necromancy.
A third says that if the spirits wished wood to walk, trees would
have legs. The leaders insist on a hearing, not a mob. (Name1)
may explain the mechanism, admit uncertainty, or refuse to speak.
No punishment is imposed solely on rumor.

B2 — BLASPHEMY AGAINST NATURE:
A respected elder argues that making a thinking creature from
lifeless materials would violate the natural order. Another elder
asks whether making a canoe also violates nature. The debate lasts
until the construct collapses. Neither side considers the matter
settled. In VV3–VV5, a Naturalist may voice the objection.

B3 — BLASPHEMY AGAINST MAGIC (VV3–VV5 ONLY):
A Magician accuses (Name1) of imitating sacred acts without
understanding their consequences. A rival Magician replies that
fear is not proof of wrongdoing. The figure crumbles before either
can complete the argument. The village calls for patient study.

B4 — THE APOLOGY:
(Name1) admits having exaggerated the claim of sentience. (He/she)
explains that the movement came from cleverly balanced joints and
concealed tension in the vines. The frightened villagers are
relieved, then annoyed. (Name1) apologizes for the deception.

C1 — THE HIDDEN CORDS:
The builders discover fine cords behind the figure. The construct
was a marvelous puppet. (Name1) becomes a respected maker of moving
sculptures, though the devotees remain suspicious of the performance.

C2 — THE WIND:
The figure moved because a strong gust filled a hidden sail and
released a wooden catch. Its whispered words were wind through a
reed. The researcher looks disappointed. The builders look delighted.

C3 — THE UNEXPLAINED:
The scholars cannot identify what made the figure move. Its eyes
seemed to follow them, and it spoke a name nobody had taught it.
Before further examination, the joints fail. The pieces are kept
for study. The event ends in mystery, not a proven resurrection.

C4 — THE LITTLE CREATURE:
A small animal scurries out from the hollow chest. Everyone screams.
(Name1) screams loudest. The movement was real, but the intelligence
belonged to a very irritated lizard. The village names it Professor.

C5 — THE LESSON:
(Name1) insists that a few moments of movement were worth years of
work. The healers ask whether (he/she) had considered the welfare
of any being (he/she) might truly create. For once, (Name1) has no
answer. (He/she) begins recording ethical questions alongside
experimental observations.

RESULTS: narrative awe, disgrace, debate, grief, mentor status or
research experience where supported. Do not actually spawn a sentient
NPC or apply death/resurrection without supported mechanics.

================================================================================
V13-D — UNIVERSAL: THE PHYSICS-DEFYING BUILDER
FOUR CHOICES — APPARENTLY IMPOSSIBLE ARCHITECTURE
================================================================================

(Name1), a builder famous for ignoring conventional advice, unveils
a structure that seems to defy everything the village knows about
weight and balance. A heavy stone platform appears to hang over an
empty space with nothing beneath its far edge.

"That cannot stand," declares the Master Stonewright.
"It already is," replies (Name1).

CHOICE A — Inspect the hidden supports. (A1–A3)
CHOICE B — Test it with a gradually increasing load. (B1–B4)
CHOICE C — Open it to the public immediately. (C1–C3)
CHOICE D — Ask the inventor to explain the design. (D1–D3)

A1 — THE COUNTERWEIGHT:
The builders uncover an ingenious system of buried stones and
interlocking beams. The platform is safe within carefully measured
limits. The critic congratulates (Name1) for seeming to defy the
world while actually understanding it better than anyone expected.

A2 — THE ROOTS:
A living tree's roots anchor the structure. It is beautiful and
clever, but the Naturalist (VV3–VV5 only; otherwise a farmer) warns
that the tree's growth could change the balance. The design is
modified to protect both the structure and the tree.

A3 — THE MIRAGE:
The supposed unsupported edge is an illusion created by a hidden
slope and clever stonework. The villagers laugh and praise the
craftsmanship. The judge declares the illusion a work of art.

B1 — THE MIRACLE:
The platform holds an astonishing load without bending. (Name1)
receives the highest praise of the builders, and the village adopts
the new technique after independent testing.

B2 — THE EMBARRASSMENT:
A single basket of coconuts makes the platform tip into the sand.
Nobody is hurt. The Master Stonewright folds (his/her) arms.
"You have indeed defied the laws of building. Unfortunately, the
building has objected."

B3 — THE UNSETTLING SUCCESS:
The structure remains upright even after its apparent supports are
removed. The builders cannot explain why. They fence it off for
study rather than pretending that mystery proves it is safe.

B4 — THE EXPERT WAS WRONG:
The judge's calculations predict failure, but repeated safe tests
show the design works. The judge checks the assumptions, discovers
a mistake, and apologizes. (Name1) becomes an advisor to builders.

C1 — THE PARADE:
The crowd rushes onto the platform. A steward stops them before it
is overloaded. The event is postponed until proper testing. (Name1)
is furious but later admits the steward acted wisely.

C2 — THE STUNT:
(Name1) walks across the platform while the crowd gasps. It holds.
The judge says, "A successful gamble is not the same as a sound
proof." A formal inspection follows.

C3 — THE COLLAPSE:
The platform tilts and settles into soft earth before anyone steps
on it. (Name1) becomes a laughingstock and returns to the drawing
board, insisting that the next version will be even stranger.

D1 — THE EXPLANATION:
(Name1) presents an intricate account of load, balance and buried
counterweights. The crowd learns something valuable. A child asks
whether the builder can make a roof that stays dry in the rain.
"That," says (Name1), "would be considerably harder."

D2 — THE NON-EXPLANATION:
(Name1) says the building stands because it "wants to." The
Master Stonewright stares. "I require a better explanation."
(Name1) replies, "So do I." The building is closed pending study.

D3 — THE IMPOSSIBLE CLAIM:
(Name1) insists that (he/she) has overturned the very order of the
world. The judges discover a concealed stone brace. (Name1) admits
it was a dramatic exaggeration. The design remains impressive.

================================================================================
V13-E — UNIVERSAL: THE STRANGE STATUE
THREE CHOICES — SCULPTURE, AFFECTION, AND ARTISTIC CRITICISM
================================================================================

(Name1) unveils a sculpture made from driftwood, volcanic ash,
pulverized limestone and several extremely unfortunate-looking
pearls. It depicts (a beloved elder/a heroic fisher/a sacred tree/
a sleeping cat/a legendary island explorer).

Its proportions are dreadful. One eye is enormous. The other seems
to be looking toward a different village. The left arm is longer
than the entire body. Yet something about the sculpture is oddly
lovable.

The Master Carver stares at it for a full minute.
"I cannot decide whether to praise you or bury it."

CHOICE A — Enter it into the sculpture competition. (A1–A4)
CHOICE B — Ask the villagers what it makes them feel. (B1–B4)
CHOICE C — Let the artist revise the work. (C1–C3)

A1 — THE PEOPLE'S FAVORITE:
The judges award it low marks for craftsmanship. The villagers,
however, adore it and begin leaving flowers at its feet. The
organizers establish a separate People's Favorite award.

A2 — THE SCANDAL:
The sculptor wins. A rival demands to know whether the judges have
eyes. The Master Carver replies, "Unfortunately, yes. I shall
never forget what I have seen." The award stands after a fair vote.

A3 — THE UNINTENDED INSULT:
The elder depicted in the sculpture sees the enormous nose and
bursts into laughter. "You have captured my finest feature!"
(Name1) is relieved. The elder requests a second statue for home.

A4 — THE UNFORGIVING CRITIC:
The Master Carver declares it an offense against stone, wood and
sight itself. Later, (he/she) is discovered secretly sketching it.
"For study," the critic insists. Nobody believes (him/her).

B1 — THE CHARM:
Children insist the statue looks friendly. Adults begin to agree.
It becomes the village's unofficial good-luck monument.

B2 — THE NIGHTMARE:
A child bursts into tears upon seeing it at dusk. The sculpture is
moved to a sunny spot where it looks considerably less threatening.

B3 — THE MEMORY:
An elderly villager says the crooked face reminds (him/her) of a
lost friend. The audience falls quiet. The artist decides to keep
the flaws exactly as they are.

B4 — THE DIVISION:
Half the village loves the statue; the other half wants it hidden.
The leaders choose a public place where it can be admired without
blocking anyone's path. The argument continues for years.

C1 — THE RUINED IMPROVEMENT:
(Name1) corrects the proportions. The sculpture becomes technically
excellent and completely forgettable. The villagers beg for the
crooked version back.

C2 — THE DOUBLE STATUE:
The artist displays the original beside the corrected version.
The judges debate which is better. A child says, "That one looks
right. This one looks happy." Nobody can improve on the answer.

C3 — THE ARTIST'S CHOICE:
(Name1) refuses to change a thing. "It is ugly," (he/she) admits.
"But it is mine." The Master Carver grants a reluctant nod.

================================================================================
V13-F — UNIVERSAL: THE VERSE THAT CHANGED HISTORY
FOUR CHOICES — RECITATION, OFFENSE, FAME, AND SCHOLARLY DEBATE
================================================================================

At the annual gathering of stories, (Name1) has been invited to
recite a beloved (poem/song/verse/ceremonial teaching/ancestral
chronicle). The text has been repeated for generations. (Name1)
has practiced for weeks and is determined to do it justice.

Unfortunately, the first line comes out wrong.

ERROR BANK (choose one):
- (Name1) replaces a solemn word with a term for animal droppings.
- (Name1) accidentally praises a historical villain instead of a hero.
- A forgotten pause changes a blessing into an apparent insult.
- A misremembered rhyme makes the ancestors sound like greedy goats.
- (Name1) sings a funeral verse to the melody of a drinking song.
- The performance accidentally implies that the village's founders
  were extraordinarily bad at farming.
- (Name1) repeats an obscure archaic word with a very embarrassing
  modern meaning.
- (Name1) improvises a verse that sounds like a personal attack on
  the village's most respected elder.

CHOICE A — Continue confidently. (A1–A4)
CHOICE B — Stop and apologize. (B1–B4)
CHOICE C — Explain the new interpretation. (C1–C4)
CHOICE D — Invite the scholars to debate the performance. (D1–D5)

A1 — THE NEW SONG:
The crowd bursts into laughter. Instead of stopping, (Name1)
finishes the entire verse in the same ridiculous style. By morning,
children are singing it everywhere. Within a generation, the comic
version has become a beloved festival song, though the original is
still preserved by the elders.

A2 — THE OFFENSE:
An elder rises and says the performance dishonors the ancestors.
(Name1) is mortified. A discussion follows, distinguishing honest
mistakes from deliberate mockery. The elder accepts an apology but
asks that the original verse be taught accurately.

A3 — THE REVELATION:
The mistaken line causes the audience to hear a familiar teaching
in a startling new way. The Keeper of Verses calls it "an accident
of considerable wisdom." The scholars record both versions.

A4 — THE LEGEND OF THE GOAT:
(Name1)'s mispronunciation becomes a story about a legendary goat
that supposedly saved the village. Nobody remembers who first told
it. Years later, a scholar attempts to prove that the goat existed.

B1 — THE GRACEFUL RECOVERY:
(Name1) admits the error, begins again, and performs beautifully.
The audience applauds the courage to correct a mistake.

B2 — THE WORSE APOLOGY:
In apologizing, (Name1) accidentally repeats the offensive phrase
three times, each louder than the last. The Keeper of Verses begs
(him/her) to stop speaking. The village remembers the apology more
vividly than the original error.

B3 — THE HUMILIATION:
(Name1) flees the stage in tears. A teacher later reminds the
crowd that public laughter can wound even when nobody means harm.
A few villagers visit (Name1) and offer encouragement.

B4 — THE COMPASSION:
The elder whose honor was supposedly insulted stands and recites
the next line alongside (Name1). Together they finish the poem.
The crowd is moved to silence.

C1 — THE BRILLIANT INTERPRETATION:
(Name1) explains that the altered wording was intended to question
whether the old heroes were flawless. The Keeper of Verses praises
the thoughtful reading while noting that the original text should
still be accurately identified.

C2 — THE DESPERATE EXCUSE:
(Name1) claims the ancestors spoke entirely in goat metaphors.
A historian asks for evidence. (Name1) has none. The audience
laughs; (Name1) admits the mistake.

C3 — THE HEATED DISPUTE:
A devotee argues that the verse must never change. A scholar
insists that reinterpretation keeps traditions alive. The leaders
allow both to speak and commission a record of the debate.

C4 — THE NEW TRADITION:
The villagers begin performing the solemn original at dawn and
the comic reinterpretation at sunset. Both become annual customs.

D1 — THE SCHOLARLY CRITIQUE:
Three scholars produce an exhaustive commentary on the mistaken
line. It is longer than the original poem. A child asks whether
they have all lost their minds.

D2 — THE GREAT DISAGREEMENT:
The Keeper of Verses declares the error unforgivable. Another
scholar calls it a masterpiece. Their argument becomes a yearly
public debate that draws enthusiastic crowds.

D3 — THE LOST ORIGINAL:
An elder reveals that the supposedly incorrect wording appears in
an ancient copy of the verse. Nobody knows which version came
first. (Name1) becomes famous for rediscovering a forgotten line
entirely by accident.

D4 — THE BITTER SATIRIST:
(Name1) embraces the controversy and writes increasingly outrageous
verses mocking village pretensions. Some villagers adore the work;
others are offended. The leaders protect peaceful criticism while
asking (Name1) not to spread false accusations as fact.

D5 — THE LEGACY:
Generations later, children learn both the traditional verse and
"(Name1)'s Version." Scholars still argue over its meaning.
The original embarrassment has become part of Isola's history.

================================================================================
V13-G — UNIVERSAL: THE INEXPLICABLE IDEA
THREE CHOICES — INVENTION LOTTERY
================================================================================

(Name1) presents a proposal so peculiar that the village council
cannot determine whether it is brilliant, foolish, or a joke.
(Name1) is entirely serious.

IDEA BANK (roll one, then choose a logically compatible ending):
I01. Make a raft from tightly tied empty gourds.
I02. Carry water uphill in a long chain of hollow reeds.
I03. Train birds to deliver messages tied to their legs.
I04. Build a bridge whose planks are suspended by woven vines.
I05. Keep food cool in a shaded clay vessel surrounded by damp sand.
I06. Use polished shells to reflect daylight into a dark hut.
I07. Make a chair that follows its owner everywhere.
I08. Teach fish to recognize the sound of a dinner bell.
I09. Invent a spoon with a second spoon attached to it.
I10. Plant every seed upside down to confuse the roots.
I11. Construct a self-opening door that opens only when nobody approaches.
I12. Paint the village's shadows to keep them from wandering away.
I13. Create a hat large enough to shelter the entire village.
I14. Replace all spoken greetings with an elaborate dance.
I15. Build a cart with square wheels because circles are "overused."
I16. Train snails to carry the harvest one grain at a time.
I17. Invent a ceremonial bell that makes no sound.
I18. Build a ladder that can be climbed from either end.
I19. Store wind in sealed clay jars for a windless day.
I20. Place a wooden sign reading "RAIN" beside the crops to encourage clouds.

CHOICE A — Give the inventor a modest budget and a safe trial. (A1–A5)
CHOICE B — Ask the inventor to defend the idea before the council. (B1–B5)
CHOICE C — Reject the proposal and invite a revised version. (C1–C5)

A1 — IT WORKS:
The council is astonished. The idea solves a practical problem
with ordinary materials, though the inventor's original explanation
was nonsense. (Name1) is offered a chance to teach the method.
Use only for I01–I06, I08 or I18 with suitable testing.

A2 — IT SORT OF WORKS:
The device functions, but only under such specific conditions that
it is scarcely useful. The villagers praise the effort and decline
to build another. Suitable for I07, I11, I13, I15 or I18.

A3 — THE ABSOLUTE FAILURE:
The project fails instantly. (Name1) insists that the failure proves
the idea is "ahead of its time." The council requests evidence.
Suitable for any idea, especially I09–I20.

A4 — THE UNEXPECTED SIDE EFFECT:
The device fails at its stated purpose but proves unexpectedly
useful for (drying herbs/shading seedlings/teaching apprentices/
decorating a festival). (Name1) accepts a small prize while
continuing to insist the original idea was better.

A5 — THE GREAT LAUGH:
The entire audience assumes the proposal is comedy and applauds.
(Name1) is devastated to discover that nobody believed (him/her).
A sympathetic elder offers to review the idea seriously.

B1 — THE GENIUS:
(Name1) gives a clear demonstration and anticipates every objection.
The council approves a supervised trial. The stern scholar grudgingly
admits that appearances can deceive.

B2 — THE NONSENSE:
(Name1) explains that the invention works because "everything
wants to be somewhere else." The council asks for a testable
explanation. (Name1) offers a drawing of a smiling coconut.

B3 — THE INSULTED INVENTOR:
When the crowd laughs, (Name1) shouts, "YOU LAUGHED AT MY LAST
INVENTION TOO!" A builder replies, "Your last invention was a
hole." "AN IMPROVED HOLE!" (Name1) screams.

B4 — THE BETTER QUESTION:
A child asks what problem the invention solves. (Name1) cannot
answer. After a long silence, (he/she) admits that the invention
was made simply to see whether it could be done.

B5 — THE COUNCIL IS STUMPED:
The leaders debate for hours and finally admit they cannot decide
whether the invention is possible. They ask the researchers to
test it rather than inventing a verdict.

C1 — THE QUIET COMEBACK:
(Name1) returns weeks later with a smaller, more practical design.
The council approves it. (Name1) says nothing, but smiles broadly.

C2 — THE SPITEFUL MASTERPIECE:
(Name1) spends months perfecting the rejected design. It works,
but is still completely useless. The inventor demands a parade.

C3 — THE FORTY-SEVEN REVISIONS:
(Name1) returns with forty-seven revised sketches. The leaders
ask which one is best. "All of them," says (Name1).
The meeting ends abruptly.

C4 — THE LOST OPPORTUNITY:
A visiting artisan independently demonstrates the same useful
principle months later. The council realizes it dismissed (Name1)
too quickly and apologizes.

C5 — THE FINAL WORD:
(Name1) abandons the invention and returns to ordinary work. Years
later, the village still jokes about it. (Name1) finally laughs too,
then admits that the ridicule hurt at the time.

V13 — INTEGRATION AND GAMEPLAY NOTES
================================================================================

* The V13-A cooking judge is a distinct, unnamed Isolan original character
  archetype, not an outside celebrity. Integrate into the user's VV2 event
  without replacing the user's original prose, branches or prize rules.
* V13-B critics may be added to existing competitions (including the
  universal storytelling and beautification contests and VV5 woodworking
  showcase). Respect each event's existing neutral-judge requirements.
* V13-C is a narrative construct event. No real resurrected villager,
  new living entity or magic power is added without engine support.
* V13-D describes apparently impossible structures. The narrative can
  leave mysteries unresolved, but should not claim a new physics engine.
* V13-E and V13-F are standalone event designs, with optional competition
  integration. Their winners and awards must follow the parent event's
  supported mechanics where integrated.
* V13-G idea-specific outcomes must be compatible with the rolled idea;
  never resolve a rain sign as a working water-cooling jar.
* A severe judge must be fallible and accountable. No cruel treatment
  is automatically portrayed as correct or deserved.
* VV3–VV5 only for Magician/Naturalist identity or claims of offenses
  against those traditions. VV1–VV2 never mention those factions.
* No game-specific protected characters become random victims; no
  mechanical changes occur unless implemented and tested in the patcher.
* Attribution: the five requested outcome premises and request for
  competition-specific severe critics belong to Lorsieab2. The full
  prose and newly developed branches in V13 belong to ChatGPT.
