================================================================================
V14 — THE GOLDEN CHILD'S NEW WONDERS (VV1: A NEW HOME ONLY)
================================================================================

ORIGINAL CONCEPTS & CREATIVE DIRECTION: Lorsieab2 — the Golden Child
solves equations beyond his peers, explains advanced subjects simply,
composes a song alone, invents an instrument, summons rainbows and storms,
waters and regrows crops/berry bush, and helps children discover plants,
animals, fungi, stars, planets and hidden places. Also requested more
age-appropriate actions suggesting an adult's insight in a child's body.
ADDITIONAL CONCEPTS, EVENT PROSE & EXPANSIONS: ChatGPT — the further
child-scholar, music, empathy, play, invention and social scenarios below.

SCOPE AND IMPLEMENTATION:
* VV1 only. Requires the Golden Child to exist and be available. He is
  the actor in every event; never choose a random child in his place.
* These are 25 distinct repeatable Golden Child actions / event scenes,
  not 25 newly implemented engine features. Use only effects supported
  by the user's patcher. Never assume a new skill or map unlock exists.
* The boy remains a child: he plays, gets distracted, makes mistakes,
  enjoys praise, and does not have to solve every village problem.
* For discoveries, choose a locally plausible object and avoid asserting
  that an actual new planet or species has been permanently added to the
  game world unless implemented. Rain/storm events need no new weather
  simulation unless supported; crop/berry effects require a valid target.
* The words 'physics' and 'equation' in design notes refer to observations
  and mathematical reasoning, not post-medieval machinery or notation.

01. THE IMPOSSIBLE EQUATION
Older pupils struggle over a problem of quantities, proportions and
unknowns. The Golden Child borrows charcoal and fills a flat stone with
careful marks. The researchers check his answer three times. It is
correct. Asked to explain, he demonstrates with pebbles, then draws a
flower beside the result. Outcome: large technology reward if supported.

02. THE WORLD'S SIMPLEST EXPLANATION
A teacher loses the younger children during a difficult lesson. The
Golden Child lays out leaves, sticks and stones, turning the same subject
into a game. Soon even the smallest child understands. Choose subject:
mathematics, motion, geography, history, philosophy, medicine, farming,
plants, stars, building or the island's mysteries. Outcome: technology
reward or descriptive increase in the pupils' enthusiasm.

03. THE LITTLE PHILOSOPHER
Elders argue whether wisdom comes from knowledge, experience or kindness.
The Golden Child asks, 'If you know everything but never help anybody,
are you really wise?' The elders have no immediate answer. One eventually
thanks him for reminding them what their arguments were supposed to serve.
Outcome: reflective scene; optional small technology reward.

04. THE LIVING MAP
With a stick in the dirt, the Golden Child draws the Southern Shore,
including an old path and a little-used stream. A scouting party confirms
some of the details. The boy adds fish to the coastline and asks whether
he can go swimming now. Outcome: optional technology reward; no automatic
new navigable map area.

05. THE LESSON NOBODY EXPECTED
An apprentice fails the same task again and again. The Golden Child
breaks it into tiny steps, watches patiently, and cheers when the
apprentice succeeds. The apprentice insists the boy did not do the work
for (him/her): he made it possible to learn. Outcome: one eligible
villager gains a modest amount in the relevant existing skill.

06. THE SONG OF ISOLA
Sitting beneath a tree, the Golden Child hums a tune no one recognizes.
By afternoon he has composed verses and a refrain. Other children join
in, then the adults. The melody becomes a familiar accompaniment to
work and play. Outcome: small technology reward; possible lasting lore
mention without requiring audio playback.

07. THE INSTRUMENT THAT NEVER EXISTED
The boy binds hollow reeds, a gourd, shells and stretched fibers into an
instrument no musician recognizes. Its strange voice makes an elder
remember a half-forgotten lullaby. The boy is delighted by the sound,
but even more delighted that the elder begins singing. Outcome: small
technology reward or village morale flavor.

08. THE TINY CONDUCTOR
The Golden Child teaches three groups of children different parts of a
song. The first attempt is an awful tangle of noise. He giggles, adjusts
their entrances, and tries again. The second performance is beautiful.
Outcome: children gain a memorable shared activity; optional small tech.

09. THE RAINBOW MAKER
After a passing shower, the Golden Child points toward the sky. One
rainbow appears, then a second, and then a faint third arch. The children
race to see where the colors touch the earth. The adults disagree about
whether he summoned the sight or simply knew when to look. Outcome:
cosmetic weather wonder; no guaranteed resource reward.

10. THE LITTLE STORMCALLER
On a hot afternoon, the Golden Child claps toward a distant cloud.
Thunder answers. Rain drums on the village roofs. The boy looks pleased,
then startled by the volume, and hurries under shelter with everyone
else. Outcome: weather-flavor scene; optional beneficial rainfall if the
engine supports it. The storm must not injure protected villagers.

11. THE THIRSTY GARDEN
The Golden Child sees the farmers staring at dry soil. He closes his
eyes and lifts his palms. A soft rain falls over the crops while the
nearby paths remain dry. The farmers cheer as the plants recover.
Outcome: restore or regrow crops ONLY if the patcher supports crop
replenishment and the crop plot is an eligible active target; otherwise
narrative blessing with no unsupported resource change.

12. THE BERRY BUSH'S BLESSING
The boy sits beside the nearly bare berry bush, touches a leaf and hums.
Blossoms appear, followed by ripe berries. He immediately eats one and
offers another to the youngest child nearby. Outcome: replenish berry
bush if it exists and is eligible; otherwise use a harmless narrative
version. Do not replenish an unavailable or permanently exhausted source
without explicit patch support.

13. THE CURIOUS LITTLE EXPLORER
The Golden Child leads his playmates into a familiar part of the jungle
and notices something the adults overlooked. Random discovery: a tiny
flower, unusual beetle, patterned fungus, unfamiliar mushroom, hidden
clearing, odd stone or animal track. The children carefully show a
researcher. Outcome: modest technology reward; discovery is descriptive
unless a collectible or map feature exists.

14. THE STARS HAVE NAMES
The Golden Child lies on his back and points out how stars shift through
the seasons. He identifies a faint wandering light that the adults had
mistaken for an ordinary star. The researchers begin observing it, while
the boy invents a funny name for it. Outcome: technology reward; no
unsupported claim that a new celestial body is created.

15. THE LITTLE HEALER
An elder struggles beneath a heavy basket. The Golden Child helps the
elder sit in the shade, fetches water, and calls a healer rather than
pretending he can cure everything. The healer praises his judgment.
Outcome: modest health recovery for an eligible villager if supported.

16. THE CHILD WHO SETTLED AN ARGUMENT
Two builders quarrel over a roof support. The Golden Child arranges
sticks to show a third way to distribute the weight. Both builders test
the model and discover that it holds. He asks whether they can stop
arguing now because it is making his head hurt. Outcome: optional
construction progress or technology reward.

17. THE GOLDEN CHILD'S FIRST DEBATE
A researcher asks why anyone should study what they do not understand.
The Golden Child replies, 'If we only learned things we already knew,
we would never learn anything new.' The researcher laughs and invites
him to the next discussion. The boy asks whether there will be snacks.
Outcome: small technology reward or pure character scene.

18. THE LITTLE ARCHITECT
The Golden Child builds a miniature village from mud and twigs. A master
builder notices drainage channels, strong foundations and paths wide
enough for carrying baskets. The boy is busy making tiny beds for his
imaginary residents. Outcome: technology or construction reward.

19. THE GOLDEN CHILD'S HISTORY LESSON
An elder muddles the order of two events from the tribe's past. The
Golden Child gently corrects the account and reenacts the story with
clay figures. The elder checks the old records and admits the boy was
right. Outcome: small technology reward and an amused elder.

20. THE QUESTION THAT CHANGED EVERYTHING
The Golden Child asks why the village performs a routine task in such a
complicated way. Nobody remembers. A few trials reveal a simpler method.
The boy is proud until he discovers that the new method means he must
help with the chores too. Outcome: modest or large technology reward.

21. THE LITTLE PEACEMAKER
Two children refuse to speak after a quarrel. The Golden Child asks each
to explain what hurt without interruption. They apologize and return
to their game. Asked where he learned such diplomacy, he says, 'I just
wanted us to play together.' Outcome: friendship and morale flavor.

22. THE GOLDEN CHILD'S GREAT EXPERIMENT
The boy plants seeds in several small patches, varying the soil and
amount of water while keeping careful marks on a stone. Farmers notice
that his simple comparison reveals which conditions help seedlings grow.
Outcome: modest food or farming-skill reward, if supported.

23. THE LITTLE INVENTOR'S DISASTER
The Golden Child unveils a contraption of vines, shells, reeds and
balanced stones. It works for three glorious seconds and collapses into
a heap. He studies the wreckage and announces, 'I think I know what went
wrong!' The builders groan as he starts again. Outcome: small technology
reward despite failure; no injury.

24. THE BOY WHO UNDERSTOOD GRIEF
The Golden Child finds a mourner alone near the graves. He offers a
flower and sits quietly. After a while, the villager begins sharing
memories of someone lost. The boy listens until sunset. Outcome: gentle
narrative scene; no claim that grief is instantly cured.

25. THE CHILD WHO REFUSED TO BE AN ADULT
Impressed scholars summon the Golden Child to a long council meeting.
He listens politely, answers a difficult question, and then spots his
friends playing outside. 'Can I go now?' he asks. Before they finish
replying, he is running toward the game. Outcome: pure character scene;
reinforces that extraordinary insight does not erase childhood.

OPTIONAL VARIATION RULES:
* Each action may have a quiet, humorous or celebrated narration variant.
* Adult reactions can range from pride and wonder to skepticism, envy,
  protective concern, or embarrassment; do not assume every adult agrees.
* A failed experiment can still teach a useful lesson. A miracle can be
  followed by ordinary childlike behavior, laughter or tiredness.
* For explanations, randomly choose a discipline appropriate to the
  game's world, using stones, shells, sticks, water, shadows and stars
  as teaching aids rather than modern scientific instruments.
* Avoid assigning the Golden Child an adult profession or aging him up.

ISOLA CONTINUES ON. <3

============================================================
NEW GAME-EXCLUSIVE ISLAND EVENTS — VV2, VV3, VV4, VV5
============================================================
Creative direction: Lorsieab2 (request for additional exclusive events, established Isola setting, and relocation of the child-chief event from VV2 to VV3).
New event concepts, dialogue, and outcome writing: ChatGPT.
These are optional, repeatable island-event designs. Select only outcomes whose prerequisites are satisfied. Names and pronouns should be resolved by the game. No outcome should reduce stored food or tech below zero.

VV2 — THE LOST CHILDREN

1. THE SECRET BEHIND THE WATERFALL
A researcher notices markings in a sheltered recess beside the Great Falls.
A. Investigate.
A1. The marks describe a forgotten path. Gain a large tech reward.
A2. They are the drawings of a mischievous child who is delighted to have fooled the scholars.
A3. A loose stone reveals old ornaments. Gain a small tech reward.
A4. Slippery rock sends the researcher tumbling into shallow water. Lose health; companions pull the researcher to safety.
the villager finds a priceless treasure chest!
B. Leave the recess undisturbed.
B1. An elder praises the researcher's respect for uncertain ground.
B2. Another researcher later claims to have solved the mystery, but cannot explain the markings.
B3. Rain washes away the markings. Their meaning becomes a favorite subject of debate.

2. THE HOSPITAL'S MOST DIFFICULT PATIENT
A stubborn villager insists that going to the hospital is unnecessary despite being visibly exhausted.
A. Ask an experienced healer to speak with the patient.
A1. Patient listening restores trust. Restore some health.
A2. The patient argues with every healer, then becomes the hospital's loudest supporter.
A3. The patient notices badly organized supplies. The embarrassed healers reorganize the room. Gain a small tech reward.
B. Let the villager rest privately while healers remain available.
B1. The villager recovers and thanks everyone for respecting the request.
B2. The villager grows weaker and finally accepts treatment. Lose health, then restore some.
B3. At night, the villager quietly visits the hospital and begs the healers not to tell anyone.

3. THE GREAT TOTEM DISPUTE
Two villagers quarrel about which totem deserves the greatest honor.
A. Hold a celebration for the totems.
A1. The celebration becomes a beloved festival.
A2. Rival speeches last so long that the food grows cold. Lose a little food.
A3. A child asks why the village must choose only one. The adults fall silent, then laugh.
B. Ask an elder to mediate.
B1. The elder recalls the founders' journey and brings both sides together.
B2. The elder accidentally insults both factions and must apologize publicly.
B3. The elder proposes a shared ceremony. Gain a small tech reward as its history is recorded.

VV3 — THE SECRET CITY

4. THE CHILD WHO CLAIMED TO BE CHIEF
A child of the Secret City appears before the Tribal Chief wearing a crown of woven grass and declares that the village has been governed incorrectly. The child demands the right to rule for one day. The Chief considers the challenge while the gathered villagers struggle not to laugh.
A. Let the child govern for one day under the Chief's supervision.
A1. THE TINY TYRANT: The child decrees that everyone must play. The Chief asks who will gather supper. After a hungry afternoon, the young ruler reluctantly withdraws the decree.
A2. THE UNEXPECTED REFORMER: The child asks why supplies are carried back and forth so often. The Chief allows the farmers to test a better arrangement. Gain a small food reward; the Chief publicly praises the child's observation.
A3. THE ROYAL FEAST: The child demands a feast fit for a ruler. The Chief places the child in charge of gathering and preparing it. Lose a small amount of food; the child learns what such orders cost.
A4. THE PEOPLE'S CHIEF: Two quarrelling adults are ordered to share their tools. The solution works, much to the Chief's delight and the adults' embarrassment.
B. Have the Chief explain the responsibilities of leadership.
B1. The Chief invites the child to help an exhausted farmer. By sunset the child admits that leadership is harder than wearing a crown.
B2. The child challenges the Chief to a public debate and makes a surprisingly clever argument. The Chief graciously acknowledges it, whereupon the child forgets the subject entirely.
B3. The child happily withdraws the challenge in favor of hide-and-seek. The Chief solemnly accepts the abdication and returns to governing.

5. THE CHIEF'S IMPOSSIBLE QUESTION
The Tribal Chief asks the village's scholars to settle a dispute: what makes a decision wise when every choice has a cost?
A. Invite representatives of different traditions to debate.
A1. A naturalist and a magician unexpectedly agree that patience matters more than certainty. Gain tech.
A2. The speakers argue until a child asks who will tend the neglected gardens. Lose a little food; the debate ends abruptly.
A3. The Chief listens to a healer's account of a difficult decision and announces that compassion must have a voice.
B. Let the Chief decide alone.
B1. The Chief makes a careful judgment and earns respect.
B2. The decision is unpopular but later proves necessary.
B3. The Chief admits uncertainty and calls for more evidence, impressing the researchers.

6. THE WEATHER DANCE THAT WENT WRONG
A confident performer claims to have perfected a new weather dance.
A. Allow the performance.
A1. Rain arrives, but only after the dancer has exhausted every spectator. The farmers celebrate. Gain food.
A2. A gust of wind carries away ceremonial decorations. The audience applauds, thinking it part of the performance.
A3. No rain falls. The dancer insists that the clouds are simply late.
A4. A brief shower falls on the wrong patch of ground. The researchers argue whether it counts.
B. Ask for a demonstration without grand promises.
B1. The dancer admits the ritual was mostly improvisation and learns humility.
B2. An elder turns the performance into a children's game.
B3. Careful observations of wind and clouds lead to a useful prediction. Gain tech.

7. THE CITY'S TWO HISTORIES
Two scholars uncover contradictory accounts of the Secret City's past.
A. Preserve both accounts.
A1. Comparing the stories reveals a forgotten event. Gain large tech.
A2. The scholars discover that each account was written to flatter its author.
A3. The village stages both versions as plays, and neither scholar likes the casting.
B. Choose the account that seems most credible.
B1. A careful comparison of evidence produces a reliable record. Gain tech.
B2. A newly discovered inscription contradicts the chosen version, to everyone's embarrassment.
B3. The rejected scholar writes a scathing poem that becomes more popular than either history.

8. THE STATUE THAT JUDGED ITS MAKERS
During work on the great statue, a builder claims its expression changes depending on who looks at it.
A. Investigate the claim.
A1. Shadows explain the illusion. Gain tech.
A2. A child moves a polished shell, making the face seem to wink. The crowd roars with laughter.
A3. The workers interpret the face as a reminder to finish their task. Increase construction progress slightly.
B. Declare the statue's expression a mystery.
B1. Villagers begin leaving gifts nearby, until the Chief reminds them the workers need supplies more.
B2. Two poets compose rival interpretations. The resulting argument becomes village entertainment.
B3. A worried builder confesses to carving the eyebrows unevenly. The village decides the flaw gives the statue character.

VV4 — THE TREE OF LIFE

9. THE UNWELCOME LANDSCAPER
A young villager insists that the Tree of Life needs a personal gardener and begins arranging stones, flowers, and moss around its roots.
A. Encourage the gardener.
A1. The arrangement improves drainage and protects fragile seedlings. Gain a small tech reward.
A2. The gardener accidentally blocks a water channel. The villagers correct the mistake together.
A3. The flowers attract colorful insects, delighting the children.
B. Ask the gardener to consult the elders first.
B1. The gardener learns how carefully the Tree must be tended.
B2. An elder adopts the idea and creates a shared gardening ritual.
B3. The gardener sulks, then returns with a detailed sketch that impresses the builders.

10. THE MASK THAT WOULDN'T STAY HIDDEN
An old mask keeps appearing in unexpected places, although every villager denies moving it.
A. Watch the mask overnight.
A1. A curious animal has been dragging it around. The children name the creature the Mask Keeper.
A2. A child confesses to moving it as part of a game and apologizes.
A3. Wind through a hollow opening makes the mask whistle eerily. Gain a small tech reward from the discovery.
B. Hold a ceremony to return it to a safe place.
B1. The ceremony brings the village together.
B2. The mask slips off its stand and lands on the officiant's head. Everyone laughs, including the officiant.
B3. A storyteller invents an elaborate legend that becomes a favorite bedtime tale.

11. THE GREAT WATER ARGUMENT
Two farmers disagree about how best to carry water to a thirsty patch of ground near the Tree.
A. Test both methods.
A1. A channel of carefully fitted stones proves efficient. Gain food or a small tech reward.
A2. Both methods fail because neither farmer noticed a blocked inlet.
A3. A child combines the ideas and succeeds. The farmers reluctantly share the credit.
B. Ask the builders to decide.
B1. The builders propose a sturdy solution and gain respect.
B2. Their elaborate design takes longer than simply carrying the water by hand.
B3. The builders admit the farmers know the ground better and work together.

12. THE DOLPHINS' STRANGE GIFT
A villager finds a smooth, oddly shaped object washed ashore after dolphins were seen playing offshore.
A. Study the object.
A1. It is an unusual shell. The researchers document it. Gain tech.
A2. It is an ordinary stone polished by waves, but the children treasure it anyway.
A3. A skilled craftsperson turns it into a charming ornament.
B. Return it to the sea.
B1. A dolphin surfaces nearby; the children insist it has come to say thank you.
B2. The object washes ashore again the next day, starting a village joke.
B3. The villagers hold a small celebration of the sea's mysteries.

VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS / GUARDIANS OF ISOLA

13. THE FEAST OF TWO TRADITIONS
A converted Isolan family and a village family each offer to host a feast, but neither agrees on how it should be prepared.
A. Combine the traditions.
A1. The meal becomes a new annual celebration. Gain a little food through shared preparations.
A2. The cooks produce an astonishingly strange dish that tastes unexpectedly wonderful.
A3. The two head cooks quarrel, then discover their children have already eaten everything.
B. Hold two smaller feasts.
B1. Guests attend both and exchange stories late into the night.
B2. A scheduling mishap leaves both feasts short of cooks. Lose a little food.
B3. The families decide that next year they will cook together.

14. THE OLD BELIEF AND THE NEW QUESTION
A young Isolan asks an elder whether accepting a new community means forgetting the people who came before.
A. Invite the elder to share the old stories.
A1. The villagers record the stories respectfully. Gain tech.
A2. A forgotten song is revived and taught to the children.
A3. A disagreement arises, but careful listening leads to a new understanding.
B. Ask the young Isolan to write a reflection.
B1. The essay moves both communities.
B2. The child writes an unintentionally funny comparison of the two traditions. The adults laugh kindly and discuss it.
B3. The child admits that the question remains unanswered, and the elder praises the honesty.

15. THE CONVERT WHO MISSED HOME
A recently converted Isolan is seen sitting alone, staring toward a familiar part of the island.
A. Invite the villager to speak.
A1. The villager shares memories of family and old customs. Neighbors offer companionship.
A2. The villager admits to fearing rejection. A former rival offers a sincere apology.
A3. The villager asks for time alone and later returns to join the others.
B. Arrange a shared activity.
B1. Working side by side helps old tensions soften. Gain a small food reward.
B2. A misunderstanding briefly renews an argument before everyone apologizes.
B3. The villager teaches the children a traditional craft, preserving a piece of Isolan history. Gain tech.

16. THE GUARDIANS' DISAGREEMENT
Two respected villagers disagree over what it means to protect Isola: guarding its traditions or allowing them to change.
A. Hold a council.
A1. The council agrees that remembrance and change can coexist. Gain tech.
A2. The debate grows heated until a child asks why protecting the island should require hurting one another.
A3. An elder proposes that both sides collaborate on a lasting record of their history.
B. Give each side a task to demonstrate its vision.
B1. One group restores an old gathering place; the other builds a new one. Both become beloved.
B2. Their efforts compete for supplies, forcing a difficult compromise. Lose a small amount of food.
B3. The groups unexpectedly exchange ideas and produce a better plan than either had proposed alone.

END OF NEW GAME-EXCLUSIVE EVENTS


================================================================================
V17 — THE FIVE SHORES COMPETE: COMPETITIONS FOR EVERY VIRTUAL VILLAGERS GAME
================================================================================

CREDITS: Lorsieab2 proposed making the full competition suite available in
VV1, VV2, VV3, VV4 and VV5, rewriting each to reflect its setting, and
specifically proposed VV5 Heathen/Isolan competitors and judges with political
tensions. The original VV2 Cooking Contest's premise, mechanics and text are
by Lorsieab2. The game-specific adaptations, fresh dialogue, branching,
political plots and integration notes below are written by ChatGPT.
Existing universal competitions and their previous credits remain intact.

INTEGRATION / NO DUPLICATE EVENTS:
* Cooking Contest: VV2 uses Lorsieab2's ORIGINAL full event and its optional
  V13 harsh-judge module. VV1, VV3, VV4 and VV5 use the new adaptations below;
  they are not mislabeled as the original VV2 manuscript.
* Other competitions: Grand Isolan Scholasticon (research), Great Beautification
  Project (art/sculpture), Everybody's a Winner? (children's games), Great
  Isolan Storytelling Contest (devotion/history), and the established
  woodworking, farming, invention and performance contest concepts can run
  in all five games with the setting and branch modifiers below.
* Choose a single contest, one game-specific setting and one branch set per
  trigger. V13 Panel of Impossible Standards can optionally supply a critic;
  do not award the same prize twice or run contradictory outcomes together.
* No VV3 Tribal Chief in VV1, VV2, VV4 or VV5. No Magic/Nature specialists
  in VV1 or VV2. In VV5, an unconverted Heathen/Isolan is an event-only NPC,
  not a selectable worker. Do not automatically force conversion or imply
  that one contest settles the political relationship.
* Keep participants age-eligible and exclude protected roles according to
  each game's existing patcher rules. No unsupported permanent titles or
  structure changes without an engine implementation.

COMMON COMPETITION STRUCTURE
INTRO: Two villagers (Name1) and (Name2) enter a competition before an
excited crowd. In VV5, one competitor may instead be an event-only Isolan
visitor (Visitor), and a judge may belong to either community.
CHOICE A — Trust the appointed judges and let the contest proceed.
CHOICE B — Require a blind or anonymous test of the entries.
CHOICE C — Invite the competitors to explain or demonstrate their work.
CHOICE D — Allow a mixed panel of elders, practitioners and ordinary
villagers to judge, where the game setting makes that meaningful.

UNIVERSAL CONSEQUENCE POOL (choose only a compatible one):
U1: A boastful master loses to a nervous novice and offers a grudging lesson.
U2: The severe judge delivers a devastating critique, then recognizes the
    contestant's ingenuity and publicly apologizes.
U3: A judge's favorite wins despite a clear flaw; the crowd demands a fair
    second test, which may vindicate or overturn the result.
U4: The most hideous entry wins the audience's affection but not the technical
    prize. Its maker is delighted to be loved and furious to have lost.
U5: A beautifully finished entry fails its practical test; an ugly but useful
    entry wins. The losers spend the evening arguing about what 'best' means.
U6: A child inadvertently points out a flaw missed by every master.
U7: A contestant recites a familiar verse so badly that it becomes a popular
    comic song; an elder insists the original must still be taught accurately.
U8: A competitor withdraws to help a rival whose work is falling apart;
    the crowd remembers the kindness longer than the announced winner.
U9: The critic is caught accepting a gift. A second panel hears the evidence;
    no contestant is punished without proof of involvement.
U10: Nobody wins because the judges discover an unsafe method. A practical
     lesson is held instead; prizes are postponed, not confiscated.

================================================================================
V17-A — VV1: SOUTHERN SHORE FESTIVAL OF SKILL
================================================================================

The villagers of the Southern Shore celebrate the work that helped their
settlement survive. Competitors display their creations near the village
center while elders tell the children how much knowledge was once lost.
The Golden Child, when present, may watch, applaud or offer a simple question;
he is never forced into an adult competitor slot.

COOKING — THE FIRST GREAT FEAST:
(Name1) prepares a familiar fish-and-root stew; (Name2) offers an unusual
fruit, herb and roasted shellfish dish. A visiting elder who once fed the
entire village during a lean season takes the judging spoon.
"If this is meant to honor our survival," (he/she) says, "it must first be
survivable." The crowd laughs uneasily.
A: Public tasting. A1: The elder praises a humble broth over an extravagant
   feast; food reward. A2: A rancid pot produces a stench so dreadful that
   everyone flees the square; discard it and postpone judging.
B: Blind tasting. B1: An apprentice defeats the master cook. B2: Both dishes
   use the same ancient recipe; the judge awards a tie and asks for stories
   of the ancestors who taught it.
C: Explain the recipes. C1: One contestant reveals a clever preservation
   method; Research reward. C2: An unsafe ingredient is identified and
   removed before tasting; no food reward, no illness.

BUILDING — A SHELTER FOR THE NEXT STORM:
Two builders construct small demonstration shelters from vines, wood and
stone. A stern master builder kicks the first support and it falls over.
"A roof is not a roof if it meets the ground before the rain does!"
A: Test them with carried water: one leaks, one remains dry.
B: Let the builders defend their methods: the strangest support arrangement
   appears to defy ordinary balance, but survives repeated load tests.
C: Ask children to inspect the shelters: they notice a low doorway that
   would trap an elder carrying a basket; both teams revise their plans.

RESEARCH / INVENTION — THE STONE TABLE OF QUESTIONS:
Researchers demonstrate practical tools and mathematical puzzles. The
harsh scholar dismisses (Name1)'s odd lever until it lifts a stone that
three adults could not budge. Another invention merely flings a coconut
into the sea. The scholar demands a record of both results.

SCULPTURE — THE FACE OF THE SOUTHERN SHORE:
A carved ancestor figure is technically exquisite; the rival's crooked,
wide-eyed clay sculpture looks as if it has swallowed a frog. Children
adore the second. The elders may award craftsmanship to one and affection
to the other, or ask the makers to collaborate on a memorial.

STORY / SONG — THE WORDS WE BROUGHT WITH US:
The contestants retell the settlers' history. One recitation is accurate;
the other contains an embarrassing accidental insult to a revered elder.
A: Correct it gently; the elder teaches the original version.
B: Preserve the comic version as a separate festival song, clearly labeled
   as parody rather than a replacement for the tribe's history.

FARMING — THE SEED AND SOIL TRIAL:
Two growers demonstrate beds of differing soils and watering patterns.
One grows leaves quickly but poor roots; the other produces a smaller yet
healthier harvest. Reward food only when an actual usable harvest exists.

CHILDREN'S GAMES — THE GREATEST LITTLE CHAMPION:
The children race, sing and build tiny houses. A strict judge criticizes a
crooked mud hut until its maker explains that it is a house for a snail.
The judge is left with nothing sensible to say.

================================================================================
V17-B — VV2: WESTERN SHORE AND THE LOST CHILDREN
================================================================================

The village gathers beneath the sound of the Great Falls. The Lost Children
help prepare decorations, keep score and, sometimes, interfere spectacularly.
The existing Lorsieab2 Cooking Contest is the canonical VV2 cook-off: use its
full randomized dishes, personalities and outcomes, with the V13-A judge
module optionally added. The following are new VV2-specific variants for
other competitions, NOT substitutes for the original cooking event.

BUILDING — THE BRIDGE THAT SHOULD NOT STAND:
A competition to span a shallow stream produces a beautiful bridge and an
impossibly thin structure of woven reeds. The master builder calls the
second "a fishing net with delusions." The reed bridge supports three
adults, then sways so alarmingly that nobody volunteers to be fourth.
A: Reward the safe, reliable bridge.
B: Permit a supervised second test: the unusual design may prove sound or
   require reinforcement; do not let children test an unverified bridge.

RESEARCH — THE GREAT FALLS RIDDLE:
Scientists compete to explain the waterfall's currents using carved
models. A Lost Child solves the final problem with pebbles and is delighted
when the experts admit the answer is correct. Tech reward; no new puzzle
is declared complete unless the patcher supports it.

SCULPTURE — THE TOTEM'S UNFORTUNATE COUSIN:
A contestant unveils a statue so hideous that a child asks whether it is
sick. The maker insists it represents courage in the face of ridicule.
A stern carver dismisses it, then notices that every child has brought
flowers for the "poor little thing." Award separate skill and audience
honors, or let the carver learn humility.

STORY / SONG — THE FOUNDERS' BALLAD:
The contestants sing about the path to the Western Shore. One accidentally
sings that the founders fled from an enormous turnip. The children repeat
that version for weeks; the historians demand that the true account also
be preserved.

FARMING — THE GREAT BERRY CHALLENGE:
Growers compete over berry harvest methods. A careful contestant preserves
the bush; an impatient rival strips too many branches and is disqualified
from the practical prize. Food changes must follow the game's supported
berry/food mechanics.

CHILDREN'S GAMES — THE CHILDREN'S JURY:
The Children are asked to award prizes for play, craft and teamwork.
One insists everyone wins; another argues that winning means nothing if
nobody can lose. The adults help them announce separate, honest awards.

================================================================================
V17-C — VV3: THE SECRET CITY'S GREAT TRIALS
================================================================================

The Tribal Chief presides ceremonially over the Secret City's festival.
The Chief does not enter as a randomly chosen contestant or judge. Nature
and Magic practitioners may participate as rivals, allies or skeptical
observers, according to the active tribe and unlocked content.

COOKING — THE CHIEF'S TABLE:
Two cooks prepare a ceremonial meal. A celebrated critic from the city's
older traditions declares that a bowl of soup "has the courage of a wet
leaf and the flavor to match." The Chief insists the judging be fair.
A: Invite the critic's public tasting. A1: The critic praises an outsider's
   unfamiliar seasoning. A2: The critic insults a traditional dish and
   must apologize after an elder explains its significance.
B: Blind tasting. B1: The critic unknowingly favors the rival tradition.
   B2: Both meals are excellent and the Chief orders a shared feast.
C: Permit a cooking demonstration. C1: An inventive method improves food
   preparation; tech reward. C2: An appalling fermented dish empties the
   plaza; the Chief declares a temporary ban on uncovered cooking pots.

BUILDING — THE STATUE AND THE IMPOSSIBLE ARCH:
The contestants propose repairs near the city's monumental works. One
uses an unfamiliar arch whose stones seem ready to fall upward. The stern
stonewright demands proof. A controlled load test vindicates the builder,
or exposes a hidden support that must be disclosed before judging.

RESEARCH — MAGIC, NATURE AND MEASUREMENT:
A scholar claims a weather pattern is entirely predictable; a practitioner
of another tradition challenges the method. The Chief orders both sides to
record observations. One may be vindicated, both may learn, or a child may
notice that neither measured the wind. No faction automatically wins.

SCULPTURE — THE CHIEF'S MOST UNFLATTERING LIKENESS:
A sculptor presents a solemn portrait of the Chief. A rival unveils a
lopsided figure with a nose like a bent yam. The Chief laughs, then asks
why the second statue is surrounded by children who adore it.
A: Judge technical merit. B: Let the public vote. C: Let the Chief speak
   without giving (him/her) unilateral authority over the prize.

STORY / SONG — THE TWO HISTORIES:
A poet from each tradition recounts the city's past. One accidentally
turns a sacred refrain into a joke; another quietly challenges an
inaccurate account. The Chief may appoint a joint oral-history project,
permit separate interpretations, or ask for a public correction.

FARMING — THE GARDEN OF RIVAL METHODS:
Nature-oriented growers favor careful soil stewardship; a Magic-oriented
entrant proposes a ceremonial approach paired with practical cultivation.
Blind harvest measurement may favor either or reveal that both methods
need revision. Food rewards depend on actual results.

CHILDREN'S GAMES — THE CHIEF FOR A MOMENT:
The child who once claimed to be Chief is invited to judge a children's
contest under the real Chief's supervision. (He/she) announces that the
first prize is "being allowed to play again." The Chief calls this the
wisest decree of the day. This is a crossover, not a second copy of the
standalone VV3 event.

================================================================================
V17-D — VV4: FESTIVAL BENEATH THE TREE OF LIFE
================================================================================

The contests take place near the Tree of Life, with care not to damage its
roots, waterways, nearby wildlife or ritual grounds. A critic who demands
that beauty justify itself is about to meet a village that disagrees.

COOKING — THE ROOTS AND RIVER FEAST:
Two cooks prepare meals from safely gathered foods. The feared judge
announces, "If the Tree of Life could taste this, it would shed its leaves
in protest." The crowd gasps; the judge insists (he/she) meant the stew.
A: Taste publicly. A1: An ugly green dish proves magnificent. A2: A
   catastrophically pungent pot drives everyone away from the feast;
   it is discarded and the cooks must start again.
B: Blind tasting. B1: A modest cook beats the village favorite.
   B2: The judge recognizes a childhood recipe and becomes emotional.
C: Explain the gathering methods. C1: The judges reward a cook who
   protected fragile plants. C2: One ingredient is identified as unsafe
   and removed; no one eats it.

BUILDING — THE WATER'S UNLIKELY PATH:
Two builders propose channels that carry water around, not through, the
Tree's roots. One structure seems to defy the slope of the land. A careful
test reveals an ingenious siphon-like arrangement or a concealed source of
higher water; the builders explain it using only observable materials and
simple craft. No magical violation of physics is asserted as fact.

RESEARCH — THE QUESTION OF THE TREE:
Scholars compete to explain changes in the Tree's health. The severe
research judge dismisses an apprentice's observations until a series of
carefully marked leaves proves the apprentice's case. Tech reward, with
no automatic puzzle progression.

SCULPTURE — THE UGLIEST GUARDIAN:
A child-sized carved guardian is spectacularly ugly, with uneven eyes,
three chins and a grin that looks painfully sincere. The master carver
calls it "an insult to wood." Villagers begin leaving flowers beside it.
A: Award technical excellence elsewhere. B: Give the sculpture a place
   in the village. C: Ask the artist to explain its meaning; the critic
   may apologize or remain unconvinced.

STORY / SONG — THE TREE REMEMBERS:
Two storytellers recite the village's history. One confuses an ancient
blessing with a request for the Tree to grow enormous feet and walk away.
The children collapse with laughter. An elder records the error as a
festival joke while preserving the true verse.

FARMING — THE WATERKEEPERS' TRIAL:
Two growers compete to nourish the garden without draining the Tree's
water supply. The winner demonstrates sustainable irrigation, not simply
the largest immediate harvest. Food reward only if crops truly improve.

CHILDREN'S GAMES — THE MASK-MAKERS' PARADE:
Children fashion harmless ceremonial masks. One looks terrifying; its
maker says it represents a frightened creature who wants a friend.
The harsh judge softens and awards the child a special storytelling honor.

================================================================================
V17-E — VV5: THE FESTIVAL OF TWO COMMUNITIES
================================================================================

This version explicitly allows event-only Heathen/Isolan visitors to be
competitors, judges, spectators, teachers, and political critics. They
are never automatically converted, made player-controlled or assigned
player skills. Use "Isolan" as the community's preferred name where the
Guardians of Isola patch applies; historical "Heathen" is a faction
reference, not a compulsory insult in dialogue. A competition may occur
before full reconciliation as a tense encounter, or afterward as a
fragile celebration, according to progression.

COMMON VV5 CAST (choose as needed):
(Name1) — eligible player-tribe competitor.
(Visitor) — offscreen competitor from the Isolan community.
(Judge1) — a player-tribe elder or expert.
(Judge2) — an Isolan elder or expert, offscreen.
(Mediator) — a mutually acceptable neutral figure, if available.

VV5 POLITICAL CHOICES FOR ANY CONTEST:
A. Let the hosting community appoint the entire judging panel.
B. Require equal representation and publish the criteria beforehand.
C. Hold an anonymous test, hiding the entrants' community affiliations.
D. Invite the two teams to cooperate on one final entry instead.

VV5 POLITICAL OUTCOMES (compatible with all contest types):
P1 — THE INSULT: A judge calls the rival tradition backward. The visitors
     threaten to leave. The organizers can demand a public apology or
     appoint a new judge; the competition resumes only if both sides agree.
P2 — THE ACCUSATION: The losing team alleges favoritism. A review finds
     either a real irregularity, a sincere misunderstanding, or no proof.
     No guilt or punishment is assigned before the review.
P3 — THE SECRET ALLIANCE: Two competitors have quietly been exchanging
     techniques for months. Their neighbors call it betrayal; the pair
     insist that sharing a recipe or craft does not surrender a belief.
P4 — THE SYMBOL: A winning entry includes a sign sacred to one community.
     Some cheer; others feel appropriated. The maker explains intent,
     listens to objections, and may revise the design without being forced.
P5 — THE UNEXPECTED VOTE: An Isolan judge gives first place to the player's
     villager. A player-tribe judge favors the Isolan entry. Both face
     angry whispers and defend the evidence behind their decisions.
P6 — THE BOYCOTT: A faction refuses to compete under the proposed rules.
     The organizers can postpone, negotiate or proceed with fewer entries;
     the absence is not falsely described as reconciliation.
P7 — THE SHARED VICTORY: A joint entry wins, but both communities argue
     over whose name should appear first. A child proposes writing both.
P8 — THE DEFEAT WITH DIGNITY: The losing competitor congratulates the
     winner publicly, then privately admits that the result still hurts.
P9 — THE TROPHY DISPUTE: The communities cannot agree where to display the
     award. They may alternate custody, make two commemorative carvings,
     or leave it with the actual winner regardless of affiliation.
P10 — THE UNWANTED HERO: A critic praises the rival community's work and
      becomes unpopular at home. The critic refuses to retract a fair
      judgment merely to satisfy the crowd.

COOKING — THE QUESTIONABLE FEAST:
(Name1) and (Visitor) prepare signature dishes. A formidable Isolan cook
joins the panel and tastes (Name1)'s stew. "This fish has suffered enough,"
(he/she) announces. (Judge1) replies, "Then judge the flavor, not the
family that cooked it." The crowd goes silent.
A: Open tasting. A1: (Visitor)'s dish wins fairly, and a furious villager
   accuses the judges of surrendering tradition. A2: (Name1)'s dish wins,
   but an Isolan elder calls it stolen from an old shared recipe.
B: Blind tasting. B1: Each community's harshest critic unknowingly praises
   the other's cook. B2: A bowl is mislabeled; organizers discover it and
   repeat the tasting before announcing a winner.
C: Shared cooking. C1: A magnificent joint feast wins over most of the
   audience, though a few refuse to eat. C2: A fermented experiment smells
   so appalling that BOTH communities evacuate the square together. Their
   first unanimous agreement: never cook that again.
D: Let cooks defend their traditions. D1: The cooks discover a common
   ancestral method. D2: They disagree respectfully and serve separate
   dishes, with neither forced to renounce the original recipe.

BUILDING — THE BRIDGE BETWEEN NEIGHBORS:
The two teams build rival spans over a shallow crossing. One favors old
Isolan stonework; the other uses a novel woven support. The master builder
calls the latter "a nest pretending to be a bridge." A fair load test
may vindicate either. If the two teams collaborate, the final structure
can symbolically join the communities without automatically ending their
political disagreements. Optional: use existing VV5 woodworking showcase
as the base event rather than triggering a duplicate building contest.

RESEARCH / INVENTION — WHO OWNS A DISCOVERY?:
A player-tribe scholar and an Isolan investigator present nearly identical
water-measuring devices. Each claims to have invented the method first.
A: Ask both to demonstrate. B: Examine dated notes and witnesses.
C: Credit independent discovery if evidence supports it.
D: Invite joint refinement. Possible endings: honest coincidence,
misremembered teaching, deliberate copying proved by evidence, or a
shared improvement that neither could have made alone. Tech reward only
for genuine new knowledge, not merely political victory.

SCULPTURE — THE TWO FACES OF ISOLA:
One artist carves a dignified ancestor; the other creates a wildly ugly
figure holding hands with two tiny villagers. The severe critic sneers,
"It looks like a yam trying to make peace." The audience loves it.
A: Award craft skill separately from symbolic meaning.
B: Ask the artists to explain the imagery before voting.
C: A mixed jury may split, agree, or uncover an unintentional insult.
D: A collaborative memorial may be accepted, rejected or redesigned;
   no community's sacred imagery is used without discussion.

STORY / SONG — WHO GETS TO TELL ISOLA'S HISTORY?:
An Isolan storyteller and a player-tribe devotee recite accounts of the
same historical event. The dates and heroes differ. A famously merciless
keeper of verses corrects both, then accidentally misquotes a sacred
line and becomes the laughingstock of the festival.
A: Name one account the winner; the losing side may protest.
B: Compare sources and publicly identify what is known and uncertain.
C: Preserve two attributed versions in the village record.
D: Compose a new shared song, while allowing each community to keep its
   own older verses. A comic misrecitation may become a festival legend,
   but it must not silently overwrite sacred history.

FARMING — THE HARVEST AND THE BORDER:
Two teams cultivate adjacent plots. An Isolan elder accuses the visitors
of diverting water; a player-tribe farmer claims the Isolans planted too
close to the boundary. A measured inspection can reveal an honest error,
a real diversion, or a natural change in the stream. The winning harvest
is judged by yield, care for soil and fairness of water use. A joint
irrigation agreement may follow, but only if both sides consent.

CHILDREN'S GAMES — THE CHILDREN'S PEACE, THE ADULTS' QUARREL:
Children from the two communities compete in games, songs and little
crafts. The children happily exchange prizes; their parents begin
arguing over which community taught the winning game first.
A: Allow the children to choose the rules and finish playing.
B: Appoint a mixed panel of patient adults.
C: Pause to address an actual fairness concern without humiliating any
   child. Outcomes: a joyful joint celebration; a sincere apology;
   an unresolved adult disagreement; or a child asking why grownups
   cannot lose as gracefully as children.

FINAL VV5 EPILOGUES (choose only one):
E1: The visitors depart with a trophy and a few new friends. Some elders
    still distrust the other community, but the children plan a rematch.
E2: The festival ends in argument. A judge's careful written account
    prevents rumors from becoming accepted history.
E3: The harshest critics meet privately and exchange recipes, techniques
    and insults with surprising affection.
E4: The trophy is broken during the closing ceremony. Both communities
    accuse each other, then discover that a goat knocked it over.
E5: No grand reconciliation occurs. Two competitors simply agree to
    practice together next season. For now, that is enough.

================================================================================
V17 IMPLEMENTATION AND ATTRIBUTION NOTES
================================================================================

For every game, retain the underlying event's original authorship and
mechanics. The VV2 Cooking Contest remains Lorsieab2's original; the
four new cooking adaptations are ChatGPT's additional writing based on
Lorsieab2's request to extend the contest across the series. The VV5
political framework and permission to include Isolan/Heathen judges and
competitors were specifically requested by Lorsieab2; the particular
characters, dialogue, branches and outcomes in V17 are ChatGPT writing.
Do not change established puzzle flags, invent permanent population
members from event-only visitors, or double-count contest prizes.

ISOLA CONTINUES ON. <3

================================================================================
V18 ADDITION — THE GRAND FESTIVAL OF TWELVE TRIALS
ALL FIVE GAMES — COMPLETE BRANCHING COMPETITION EVENTS
================================================================================

CONTRIBUTION CREDITS
Original request and twelve competition concepts: Lorsieab2.
Replacement of “Brain-Off” with Scholasticon/Decathlon terminology: Lorsieab2.
Event narratives, choices, randomized outcome prose, and adaptations: ChatGPT.

GLOBAL RULES
Each event is independent and repeatable; choose eligible contestants by game.
Nonlethal contests stop when participants are injured or unwilling.
No competition should automatically override a protected character’s role.
VV3 Tribal Chief may judge or officiate, not be replaced by a child.
VV5 Isolan/Heathen participation depends on the state of reconciliation.
Game-specific framing below applies to each of the twelve events.

GAME-SPECIFIC FESTIVAL FRAMING
VV1: Southern Shore; practical survival, memory of the founders, and the
Golden Child’s occasional noncompetitive observations or demonstrations.
VV2: Western Shore; the Lost Children can cheer, ask questions, or join
age-appropriate contests; totems and the Great Falls inspire themes.
VV3: Secret City; Tribal Chief presides; Magic and Nature supporters may
dispute methods, then cooperate or accept an impartial ruling.
VV4: Tree of Life; themes honor ecology, conservation, and stewardship;
performances and playing fields must not damage the Tree.
VV5: The village and Heathens/Isolans may enter as competitors, judges,
or audiences, with naming determined by the Guardians of Isola patch.
Mixed judging panels can provoke allegations of bias, but may also
create surprising trust across communities.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
FESTIVAL EVENT 01 — THE WARRIORS’ TOURNAMENT
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible warriors.

Two experienced villagers demand a tournament to decide whose discipline best protects the village. Blunted wooden weapons, padded shields, and a healer stand ready.

A — Permit a supervised contest.
B — Replace combat with demonstrations of defense and restraint.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — The veteran wins every bout, then offers to teach the defeated apprentice. The crowd cheers louder for the lesson than for the victory.
A2 — A quiet farmer defeats the favored warrior with a clever feint. The favorite demands a rematch, but the judges uphold the result.
A3 — A contestant loses control and strikes too hard. The judges stop the tournament; the offender must apologize and help the healer.
A4 — Two finalists refuse to yield. They agree to share the prize and train the village together.
B1 — The contestants demonstrate disarming, retreating, and protecting children. The judges declare that courage need not require injury.
B2 — The crowd complains that the contest is boring until a mock ambush reveals the champion’s extraordinary vigilance.
B3 — One boastful fighter fails to defend a basket of coconuts from three giggling children. Nobody lets (him/her) forget it.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:
P1 — A judge from the other community praises the winning rival despite
pressure from (his/her) own supporters. The crowd slowly applauds.
P2 — An accusation of favoritism forces the judges to disclose their
criteria; the disputed result is upheld, reversed, or declared a tie.
P3 — Competitors from both communities secretly practice together and
publicly acknowledge each other when the results are announced.

MECHANICAL NOTES: Reward a modest, game-scaled amount of tech, food,
or appropriate skill experience for constructive success; embarrassment
may change reputation without automatically causing injury or death.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
FESTIVAL EVENT 02 — THE MARTIAL ARTS TOURNAMENT
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible martial artists.

The village holds a contest of balance, grappling, footwork, and controlled unarmed technique. No striking vulnerable areas is permitted.

A — Judge by successful technique.
B — Judge by grace, restraint, and teaching.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — The smallest competitor redirects the strongest without hurting (him/her), winning the tournament and an astonished silence.
A2 — Two competitors become hopelessly tangled and roll into the sand. The judges call a draw; the spectators call it a masterpiece.
A3 — An arrogant contestant ignores the rules and is disqualified, despite having the loudest supporters.
B1 — The winner stops mid-bout to help an opponent who has stumbled. The judges reward sportsmanship.
B2 — A patient instructor teaches a nervous child a safe balancing stance; the child proudly demonstrates it at the closing ceremony.
B3 — A supposedly flawless master slips while bowing. The rival catches (him/her), and the two become friends.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:
P1 — A judge from the other community praises the winning rival despite
pressure from (his/her) own supporters. The crowd slowly applauds.
P2 — An accusation of favoritism forces the judges to disclose their
criteria; the disputed result is upheld, reversed, or declared a tie.
P3 — Competitors from both communities secretly practice together and
publicly acknowledge each other when the results are announced.

MECHANICAL NOTES: Reward a modest, game-scaled amount of tech, food,
or appropriate skill experience for constructive success; embarrassment
may change reputation without automatically causing injury or death.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
FESTIVAL EVENT 03 — THE GREAT QUESTION
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible philosophers and scholars.

The judges present one question: What makes a village truly prosperous? Who owns a discovery? Can a promise become unjust? Is courage possible without fear?

A — Reward the most persuasive answer.
B — Invite the whole village to question the answers.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — An elder gives an elegant speech, but a child’s brief answer moves the audience more deeply.
A2 — A contestant argues that prosperity means full food stores; another points toward the lonely and forgotten. The judges cannot easily choose.
A3 — The winner admits uncertainty and explains how to test the claim. The scholars praise intellectual humility.
B1 — The discussion uncovers an unfair village custom, and the council agrees to reconsider it.
B2 — Every answer creates three more questions. The competition ends at sunset without a winner but with a thriving new study circle.
B3 — One villager responds with a joke so apt that it becomes a proverb.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:
P1 — A judge from the other community praises the winning rival despite
pressure from (his/her) own supporters. The crowd slowly applauds.
P2 — An accusation of favoritism forces the judges to disclose their
criteria; the disputed result is upheld, reversed, or declared a tie.
P3 — Competitors from both communities secretly practice together and
publicly acknowledge each other when the results are announced.

MECHANICAL NOTES: Reward a modest, game-scaled amount of tech, food,
or appropriate skill experience for constructive success; embarrassment
may change reputation without automatically causing injury or death.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
FESTIVAL EVENT 04 — THE PHILOSOPHERS’ DUEL
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible philosophers.

Two thinkers publicly dispute whether truth comes from reason, experience, tradition, or revelation. Each must fairly restate the other’s position before replying.

A — Let the judges name a victor.
B — Require both debaters to exchange positions.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — The favored orator wins the audience but loses the scholars, who catch a contradiction.
A2 — A timid apprentice calmly dismantles a celebrated argument. The defeated master asks to become (his/her) student.
A3 — The debate becomes personal. The moderator ends it and demands apologies before any prize is awarded.
B1 — The rivals discover their disagreement rested on two meanings of the same word.
B2 — One contestant argues the opposing case so well that (he/she) changes (his/her) own mind.
B3 — Both refuse to concede, but collaborate on a new village school of thought.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:
P1 — A judge from the other community praises the winning rival despite
pressure from (his/her) own supporters. The crowd slowly applauds.
P2 — An accusation of favoritism forces the judges to disclose their
criteria; the disputed result is upheld, reversed, or declared a tie.
P3 — Competitors from both communities secretly practice together and
publicly acknowledge each other when the results are announced.

MECHANICAL NOTES: Reward a modest, game-scaled amount of tech, food,
or appropriate skill experience for constructive success; embarrassment
may change reputation without automatically causing injury or death.

FESTIVAL EVENT 05 — THE ALCHEMISTS’ TRIAL
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible researchers.

Researchers compete to identify unknown powders, separate harmless mixtures, make useful dyes, and explain the changes they observe. The healers forbid tasting unknown substances or making dangerous fumes.

A — Reward the most useful preparation.
B — Reward the clearest explanation and safest method.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — A contestant produces a durable plant dye that transforms village weaving.
A2 — An apparent failure yields a surprisingly effective clay sealant. The judges argue whether accidents count as discoveries.
A3 — A pompous researcher makes a foul-smelling paste. The crowd retreats, and the judges award no prize.
B1 — A careful apprentice identifies a mislabeled mineral before anyone uses it. The healers declare (him/her) the true champion.
B2 — The most colorful demonstration is exposed as trickery; the least impressive one is scientifically sound.
B3 — The finalists disagree about the cause of a reaction and devise a fair experiment instead of quarreling.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:
P1 — A judge from the other community praises the winning rival despite
pressure from (his/her) own supporters. The crowd slowly applauds.
P2 — An accusation of favoritism forces the judges to disclose their
criteria; the disputed result is upheld, reversed, or declared a tie.
P3 — Competitors from both communities secretly practice together and
publicly acknowledge each other when the results are announced.

MECHANICAL NOTES: Reward a modest, game-scaled amount of tech, food,
or appropriate skill experience for constructive success; embarrassment
may change reputation without automatically causing injury or death.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
FESTIVAL EVENT 06 — THE PARADOX CHALLENGE
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible scholars.

The contestants face a puzzle that seems to contradict itself: a liar’s statement, a divided heap of grain, an endless sequence of smaller steps, or a riddle about a ship rebuilt plank by plank.

A — Demand a definite solution.
B — Allow contestants to explain why the puzzle is misleading.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — A contestant spots a hidden assumption and resolves the puzzle to thunderous applause.
A2 — The judges accept a clever answer until a child finds a counterexample. The prize is withheld.
A3 — Two scholars reach different answers because they interpret a word differently. Both are declared correct under their stated assumptions.
B1 — A quiet thinker demonstrates that the riddle cannot be answered without defining its terms.
B2 — The contestants argue until dusk, then agree that recognizing a genuine uncertainty is progress.
B3 — One villager solves the paradox by eating the grain used to demonstrate it. The philosophers are furious; the farmers are delighted.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:
P1 — A judge from the other community praises the winning rival despite
pressure from (his/her) own supporters. The crowd slowly applauds.
P2 — An accusation of favoritism forces the judges to disclose their
criteria; the disputed result is upheld, reversed, or declared a tie.
P3 — Competitors from both communities secretly practice together and
publicly acknowledge each other when the results are announced.

MECHANICAL NOTES: Reward a modest, game-scaled amount of tech, food,
or appropriate skill experience for constructive success; embarrassment
may change reputation without automatically causing injury or death.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
FESTIVAL EVENT 07 — THE SIXTEENTH ARIA
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible singers.

The village challenges singers to perform the notoriously long Sixteenth Aria composed by (random living or deceased adult villager). It contains unexpected turns, difficult intervals, and verses few can remember.

A — Require a faithful full performance.
B — Allow interpretation, but require the entire aria.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — An unassuming singer performs every verse perfectly. The composer weeps with pride.
A2 — The composer forgets the ninth verse while correcting a contestant. The audience erupts in laughter.
A3 — A contestant reaches the final note, only to discover the judges disagree on whether an old refrain counts as a verse.
B1 — A bold singer transforms the aria into a moving lament. The composer initially protests, then embraces the performer.
B2 — A disastrous rendition becomes a wildly popular children’s song, much to the composer’s horror.
B3 — Two singers finish together after one quietly helps the other remember a missing line. The judges award a shared victory.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:
P1 — A judge from the other community praises the winning rival despite
pressure from (his/her) own supporters. The crowd slowly applauds.
P2 — An accusation of favoritism forces the judges to disclose their
criteria; the disputed result is upheld, reversed, or declared a tie.
P3 — Competitors from both communities secretly practice together and
publicly acknowledge each other when the results are announced.

MECHANICAL NOTES: Reward a modest, game-scaled amount of tech, food,
or appropriate skill experience for constructive success; embarrassment
may change reputation without automatically causing injury or death.

B1 — A rough, imperfect poem about a lost loved one moves the village to tears.
B2 — A playful poem about a goat becomes a beloved festival chant.
B3 — A rival plagiarizes an old verse and is exposed by its elderly author, who is sitting among the judges.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:

Teams answer questions about medicinal plants, village history, constellations, food storage, building materials, and the geography of Isola.

B1 — A farmer recognizes a seed that none of the scholars can name.
B2 — A brilliant theorist cannot tie a useful knot. The builders celebrate.
B3 — The winning team includes a novice, an elder, and a former rival; their different knowledge proves decisive.

FESTIVAL EVENT 10 — THE BALL TOURNAMENT

Teams compete with a tightly wrapped fiber-and-hide ball, trying to carry or pass it across a marked field without dangerous contact.

A — Play for the championship.

A1 — A spectacular final pass wins the match at the last moment.
A2 — A disputed boundary call causes uproar until a neutral child reveals where the ball landed.
A3 — The favored team loses because its captain refuses to pass to anyone else.
B1 — A shy villager scores after teammates deliberately make room for (him/her).
B2 — The ball bursts open. The match becomes an impromptu competition to make a better one.
B3 — Two rivals discover they make an excellent team and end their feud.

FESTIVAL EVENT 11 — THE STICK GAME
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible athletes.

Competitors use smooth wooden sticks to guide a small ball toward marked goals, testing precision, timing, and cooperation. The playing field is cleared of bystanders.

A — Hold a fast team match.
B — Hold a precision challenge.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — An unlikely team executes a perfect sequence of passes and wins.
A2 — A contestant sends the ball into the judges’ ceremonial fruit basket. The match is paused for laughter.
A3 — An aggressive player is penalized for reckless swinging and learns to control (his/her) strength.
B1 — A patient elder wins with three astonishingly accurate strokes.
B2 — The strongest contestant repeatedly overshoots while a novice succeeds through gentle control.
B3 — A clever builder designs a fairer goal marker that becomes standard in future games.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:

FESTIVAL EVENT 12 — THE HOOP TOURNAMENT
Games: VV1 / VV2 / VV3 / VV4 / VV5
Contestants: eligible athletes.

Villagers roll woven hoops along winding courses, toss smaller rings toward pegs, and attempt graceful catches.

A — Award the fastest completion.

RANDOMIZED OUTCOMES (choose according to player choice):
A1 — A young competitor wins by choosing a careful route instead of rushing.
A2 — A hoop escapes downhill and leads half the village on an absurd chase.
A3 — Two finalists finish together and demand a rematch.
B1 — An inventive contestant uses a new throwing stance and wins the judges’ admiration.
B2 — A master athlete misses every peg; a laughing child lands the winning ring.
B3 — A warped hoop makes the contest unfair. The contestants insist on replacing it before proceeding.

VV5 SPECIAL POLITICAL VARIANTS:

CROSS-EVENT GRAND FINALS — THE ISOLAN DECATHLON
An optional multi-discipline festival combines ten randomly selected
trials from the twelve events above. A champion must demonstrate more
than one kind of talent; teams can win through complementary strengths.

D1 — The overall favorite dominates the physical contests but loses the
Scholasticon round to an unassuming novice.
D2 — A boastful champion refuses help and loses to a cooperative team.
D3 — A mixed VV5 team wins; both communities argue over whose victory
it is until the champions insist the prize belongs to everyone.
D4 — The judges cannot agree whether poetry outweighs stick-game skill.
They introduce separate laurels for mind, art, and athletic achievement.

The Grand Isolan Scholasticon is the dedicated scholarly festival;
the Isolan Decathlon is the mixed-discipline festival.
END OF V18 ADDITION

================================================================================
V20 ADDITION — ISOLA'S WORLD, THE DOCTORS' DILEMMA, AND IMPOSSIBLE RELICS
================================================================================

CREATIVE CREDITS
Original concepts and requested outcomes: Lorsieab2.
Additional event concepts, prose, choices, and outcome elaboration: ChatGPT.
The original contributions and credits in the preceding collection remain intact.

CANON AND SETTING GUIDE — APPLIES THROUGHOUT THE COLLECTION

Isola is a mysterious ancient tropical, Polynesian-inspired tribal island.
Ordinary village life is grounded in coastal and island environments: beaches,
reefs, lagoons, volcanic stone, waterfalls, forests, fruit-bearing trees, vines,
coconut palms, carved wood, bamboo, gourds, shell, woven fibers, clay, stone,
bone, thatch, and natural pigments. Its inhabitants are capable, thoughtful
people with complex indigenous traditions of healing, navigation, astronomy,
agriculture, mathematics, architecture, philosophy, storytelling, and music.
Do not casually substitute medieval European courts, castles, knighthood,
monarchies, or industrial institutions. Disputes can be heard by elders or
appropriate village leaders; confinement may occur in a guarded communal
shelter rather than a European-style dungeon.

Game-specific canon must be respected. The Golden Child belongs to VV1;
the Lost Children and Great Falls to VV2; the Tribal Chief to VV3; the
Tree of Life to VV4; and the Heathens/Isolans and their fraught relationship
with the player's village to VV5. Magician/Naturalist choices appear only
where the game's setting and patch support them (VV3–VV5). No VV3-style
Tribal Chief is silently inserted into other games.

INTENTIONAL EXCEPTIONS: Bizarre, supernatural, impossible, wildly inventive,
and deliberately anachronistic things ARE welcome as rare story premises.
A modern artifact, a gravity-defying building, an apparently living construct,
or an impossible medical revival should feel extraordinary precisely because
the everyday island world is not modern. Villagers describe unfamiliar objects
through their own experiences, not through knowledge they do not possess.
Use ordinary game-scaled food/tech/skill rewards and apply the established
protected-villager exclusions unless a specific event says otherwise.

================================================================================
THE DOCTORS' DILEMMA — ADDITIONAL MEDICAL CRISES (VV1–VV5)
================================================================================

Each numbered crisis is a separate rare event variant. Use eligible adult
villagers unless the event expressly concerns a mother and child. Where a
patient's wishes can be obtained, their choice takes priority in the story.
For VV5, an Isolan healer or elder may offer disputed ancestral knowledge.

D1 — TWO LIVES, ONE DECISION
A pregnant villager lies gravely ill; the healers fear for both mother and
unborn child. A dangerous intervention might save them both, but a gentler
approach might leave too little time.
A. Attempt the dangerous intervention.
  A1. Both survive; the mother later honors the physician in her child's name.
  A2. The mother survives but the child dies; a solemn mourning follows.
  A3. The child survives but the mother dies; the family remembers her courage.
  A4. Neither survives; the healer questions whether the decision was right.
  A5. Both survive, though the mother requires prolonged care.
B. Choose the gentler treatment.
  B1. Both recover against expectation.
  B2. The mother survives but loses the baby.
  B3. The condition worsens, demanding a desperate intervention.
  B4. Both die; villagers disagree about whether waiting was a mistake.
C. Explain the risks and ask the mother.
  C1. She elects intervention; both survive.
  C2. She chooses gentler care; her recovery is prolonged and uncertain.
  C3. Her relatives object to her decision, but the healers honor her wishes.

D2 — THE TREATMENT THAT DOES NOT EXIST
A researcher has proposed a treatment that exists only in calculations and
sketches. Nobody has ever performed it.
A. Attempt the proposed technique.
  A1. It works; the village celebrates a major medical discovery.
  A2. It relieves symptoms but does not cure the illness.
  A3. The experiment fails and the patient dies.
  A4. The patient recovers, but evidence suggests the treatment was irrelevant.
B. Research the theory first.
  B1. A fatal flaw is discovered before anyone is harmed.
  B2. The scholars devise a safer approach and earn a large tech reward.
  B3. The patient dies while the researchers are still deliberating.
  B4. The scholars demonstrate that the original proposal cannot work.

D3 — THE EXPERIMENTAL CURE
Several villagers have developed an unfamiliar illness. A healer proposes
a new plant-based preparation with unknown effects.
A. Offer the remedy to patients who agree to try it.
  A1. Several recover; the healer is praised.
  A2. Some improve, while others develop troubling complications.
  A3. No one improves, but the researchers learn about the illness.
  A4. A patient dies, prompting a public examination of the healer's conduct.
B. Continue established supportive care.
  B1. Most recover gradually.
  B2. The illness spreads and the hospital is overwhelmed.
  B3. Another healer finds a more reliable approach.
C. Study the remedy further.
  C1. The effective ingredient is identified.
  C2. A harmful ingredient is discovered.
  C3. A rival scholar steals the notes and claims the discovery.

D4 — THE HALF-TRANSLATED REMEDY
A healer finds ancient writing: "Gather the leaves beneath the moon,
crush them with the pale stone, and never—" The rest has worn away.
A. Reconstruct and attempt the remedy.
  A1. The interpretation is correct and the patient recovers.
  A2. The missing words warned against a dangerous plant.
  A3. The supposed remedy is actually a recipe for ceremonial pigment.
  A4. The name of a long-forgotten healer is recovered.
B. Ask scholars and elders to examine the inscription.
  B1. A single symbol transforms the meaning of the instructions.
  B2. Three scholars produce three incompatible translations.
  B3. An elder recognizes the missing phrase from an old chant.
VV5 variation: An Isolan elder knows the rest but demands respect for
the community's knowledge before sharing it.

D5 — THE LAST DOSE
Two patients require the same scarce medicine. There is enough for one.
A. Treat the more gravely ill patient.
  A1. Both survive, one without the medicine.
  A2. The treated patient survives; the other deteriorates.
  A3. The medicine fails, and the physicians grieve.
B. Treat the patient most likely to benefit.
  B1. The chosen patient recovers.
  B2. Both die despite the attempt.
  B3. The untreated patient unexpectedly recovers.
C. Search for more ingredients.
  C1. An expedition finds enough for another dose.
  C2. The expedition returns too late.
  C3. A child notices the needed plant growing near the hospital.

D6 — THE PHYSICIAN WHO REFUSED
A master healer refuses a risky procedure; an apprentice believes it is
the patient's only chance.
A. Support the master: the patient recovers; or dies; or later findings
prove the master's caution justified.
B. Support the apprentice: the procedure succeeds; fails; or is wisely
halted when a previously unseen danger becomes apparent.
C. Convene the healers: they reach consensus; argue too long; or hear a
third solution from a quiet assistant.

D7 — THE PATIENT WHO KNEW TOO MUCH
A sick researcher insists the healers have misidentified the illness.
A. Investigate: the patient is right; a different illness is discovered;
or the theory is disproved.
B. Continue the original treatment: it works; fails; or an apprentice
finds evidence that contradicts both diagnoses.

D8 — THE MEDICINE THAT WORKS TOO WELL
A new pain remedy leaves patients too sleepy to eat or work.
A. Continue cautiously: the healers improve it; a patient develops
complications; or the village begins overusing it.
B. Suspend it: the cause of the drowsiness is discovered; patients protest
the loss of relief; or a safer remedy is found.

D9 — THE HEALER'S SECRET
A respected healer confesses privately to a serious mistake that left
a surviving patient with lasting symptoms.
A. Disclose the mistake: the patient forgives; demands accountability;
or the hospital adopts safer records and training.
B. Conceal it: the truth emerges later; another healer repeats the error;
or the guilty physician confesses publicly.

D10 — THE CHILD WHO CORRECTED THE MASTERS
A young apprentice quietly challenges a diagnosis.
A. Listen: the apprentice is right; mistaken but thoughtful; or notices
a third illness.
B. Dismiss the child: the masters later discover the child was right;
the patient recovers but the apprentice loses confidence; or an elder
apologizes and offers mentorship.

D11 — THE ANCIENT OATH
An old healing oath seems to prohibit a treatment that might save a life.
A. Honor it: another cure works; the patient dies; or the prohibition
turns out to have been mistranslated.
B. Permit the treatment: the patient survives amid controversy; it fails;
or the village reexamines what its medical duty means.
VV5 variation: The oath comes from the Isolans' ancestors, and the two
communities dispute its meaning.

D12 — THE DOCTOR WHO COULD NOT SAVE EVERYONE
Several injured villagers arrive at once after a disaster.
A. Prioritize immediate, treatable danger: many survive; a family
questions a death; or the hospital establishes a new emergency system.
B. Treat in arrival order: chaos follows; a critical patient worsens;
or everyone is stabilized through remarkable luck.
C. Let healers choose independently: they coordinate well; conflict
dangerously; or an apprentice organizes supplies and saves time.

================================================================================
