THE DOCTORS' DILEMMA — BETWEEN LIFE AND DEATH (VV1–VV5)
================================================================================

(Name1) collapses in the village center. (His/Her) breath is faint,
(his/her) skin cold, and the healers say that (he/she) is dying NOW.
The gathered villagers must decide what to do before time runs out.

A. ESTABLISHED SCIENTIFIC HEALING
 A1 — The Physician's Triumph: Careful treatment stabilizes (Name1);
      (he/she) eventually recovers fully. Healing experience gained.
 A2 — A Life Forever Changed: (Name1) survives with lasting weakness
      and breathlessness; (he/she) develops a dislike of running.
 A3 — One Last Conversation: Consciousness returns just long enough for
      (Name1) to speak final words to a loved one, then (he/she) dies.
 A4 — The Unanswered Question: (Name1) dies despite the healers' efforts.

B. THE POWERS OF NATURE (VV3–VV5 ONLY)
 B1 — The Forest's Mercy: Flowers open around (Name1), whose breathing
      returns; (he/she) recovers completely.
 B2 — Rooted Between Worlds: Living roots enclose the motionless patient
      in suspended animation. Days later (he/she) awakens, weak but alive.
 B3 — The Price of Renewal: (Name1) survives but suffers enduring
      sensitivity to heat, sunlight, and exertion.
 B4 — Nature Reclaims Its Own: Flowers bloom, but (Name1) dies.

C. MAGICAL RESTORATION (VV3–VV5 ONLY)
 C1 — The Impossible Revival: Light surrounds the patient; (Name1)
      rises briefly from the ground and awakens fully healed.
 C2 — The Sleeping Miracle: Magic suspends the decline, leaving (Name1)
      motionless until a later awakening.
 C3 — The Broken Spell: Breathing returns, but intermittent weakness
      and strange sensations persist.
 C4 — The Price of Power: The ritual fails and (Name1) dies.

D. SPIRITUAL INTERVENTION
 D1 — The Ancestors Answer: (Name1) awakens and describes familiar voices
      calling (him/her) back; the village celebrates.
 D2 — A Final Blessing: (Name1) briefly awakens to give a blessing
      and then dies. The words enter the village's oral history.
 D3 — The Long Vigil: The patient lies in a deathlike stillness until
      sunrise, then awakens; determine full or partial recovery.
 D4 — Silence from Beyond: The prayers continue, but (Name1) dies.

E. MAKESHIFT RESCUE
 E1 — The Apprentice's Miracle: An apprentice improvises a rescue with
      available supplies and skilled guidance; (Name1) fully recovers.
 E2 — Saved, But Not Unscathed: (Name1) survives with limited stamina
      and a permanent dislike of running.
 E3 — A Narrow Escape: The improvised effort buys time for a master
      healer to stabilize (Name1); determine full or partial recovery.
 E4 — The Cost of Desperation: The attempt fails; (Name1) dies.

F. FORBIDDEN TECHNIQUES
 F1 — The Unnatural Survivor: (Name1) awakens with cold skin and a
      barely detectable heartbeat. Some call (him/her) a miracle;
      others call (him/her) an abomination. Permanent half-alive trait.
 F2 — The Walking Dead: Movement returns but awareness seems absent.
      (Name1) wanders and responds faintly to familiar voices.
      The village debates whether this is life at all.
 F3 — The Miracle They Condemned: (Name1) recovers fully, but the
      healer who saved (him/her) is accused of violating sacred laws.
 F4 — The Forbidden Failure: (Name1) dies; the healer faces a public
      hearing over the forbidden intervention.
 F5 — The Patient Who Remembered Death: (Name1) survives and claims
      to recall dying, causing scholarly and spiritual controversy.

G. LAST-RESORT EXPERIMENTAL INTERVENTION
 G1 — Against All Odds: (Name1) recovers fully; large Tech reward.
 G2 — The Long Road Back: Survival comes with permanent limitations
      and dislike of running.
 G3 — The Living Sleeper: The patient enters suspended animation;
      days later (he/she) awakens with randomized recovery.
 G4 — The Last Words: (Name1) regains consciousness briefly, says
      goodbye, and dies.
 G5 — The Uncertain Victory: (Name1) survives, but the healers cannot
      tell whether their intervention was responsible.

OPTIONAL LONG-TERM FOLLOW-UPS
 L1 — The Villager Who Returned Wrong: The half-alive survivor
      struggles with ordinary life; villagers may offer compassion,
      shun (him/her), or seek a cure.
 L2 — The Village That Feared Its Savior: The rescuer faces a hearing,
      ending in vindication, disgrace, exile, or unexpected acclaim.
 L3 — The Sleeper Who Awoke: A patient wakes after years of suspended
      animation and finds that the village has changed.
 L4 — The Second Death: A half-alive villager collapses again; a new
      choice determines revival, ordinary death, or further suspension.
 L5 — The Unexpected Cure: A scholar finds a way to restore ordinary
      life; the patient may accept, refuse, or recover only partially.

DESIGN NOTES: The zombie-like outcome should be exceptionally rare.
Suspended animation is a temporary state, not an automatic full cure;
on awakening, select an appropriate later outcome. Keep the events
fictional and do not treat their magical or improvised cures as real
medical guidance.

================================================================================
THE IMPOSSIBLE RELICS OF ISOLA — RARE EVENT FAMILY (VV1–VV5)
================================================================================

RANDOM RELIC POOL — THE PLAYER MAY KNOW THE REAL OBJECT; THE VILLAGERS DO NOT
 1. A smooth glowing black rectangle (smartphone).
 2. A little box of voices (battery-powered radio).
 3. A tiny fire-making wand (disposable lighter).
 4. Two transparent seeing-stones joined by metal (eyeglasses).
 5. A writing stick that needs no ink bowl (ballpoint pen).
 6. A circle that remembers numbers (digital wristwatch).
 7. A sealed cylinder marked with unknown symbols (battery).
 8. A metal vessel containing strangely preserved food (food tin).
 9. A portrait of impossible precision (printed photograph).
10. A singing shell with a turning handle (wind-up music box).
11. A tube emitting cold light without flame (LED flashlight).
12. A tiny metal-and-resin token with no visible purpose (USB drive).
These are intentionally anachronistic. Do not give the villagers modern
names for the objects in dialogue or narration from their perspective.

EVENT R1 — THE THING THAT SHOULD NOT EXIST
(Name1) finds a peculiar object under the soil. It has precise edges,
unfamiliar materials, and no purpose anyone can identify. One elder
calls it a gift; another fears strangers have reached Isola.

A. DESTROY IT
 A1 — The Shattered Mystery: The object is smashed. Researchers lament
      the lost evidence; (Name1) is blamed for bringing it.
 A2 — The Thing That Would Not Break: It survives repeated blows and
      is buried amid fearful rumors. Add to buried-relic pool.
 A3 — The Destruction That Saved Them: An unpleasant odor or startling
      reaction sends the villagers away; nobody agrees whether the
      attempt prevented danger or created it.
 A4 — The Accidental Discovery: A useful component inspires a new
      island-made tool. (Name1) is suddenly praised as an inventor.

B. TRY TO USE IT
 B1 — The Impossible Tool: (Name1) discovers a practical function;
      craftspeople attempt to imitate it using island materials.
 B2 — The Voice from Nowhere: The object speaks or makes a strange
      noise; some villagers flee, others pray, and panic spreads.
 B3 — The Hero of the Village: (Name1) uses it to solve a problem
      without understanding how it works. Public acclaim follows.
 B4 — The Catastrophic Misunderstanding: A ceremony accidentally
      activates the object, igniting a theological dispute.

C. GIVE IT TO THE RESEARCHERS
 C1 — The Scholar's Triumph: Its construction inspires several
      practical discoveries. Large Tech reward; (Name1) is honored.
 C2 — The Great Scholarly Embarrassment: Masters confidently identify
      it as a ceremonial relic until a child finds its actual use.
 C3 — The Forbidden Question: Scholars propose that distant people
      possess far greater technology; elders demand silence.
 C4 — The Accused Discoverer: Unfamiliar markings lead villagers to
      accuse (Name1) of communicating with outsiders.

D. BURY IT
 D1 — Forgotten Beneath the Earth: The object passes into legend.
      Add to buried-relic pool for later generations.
 D2 — The Second Discoverer: (Name2) digs it up years later; an
      elderly witness cries, "That thing again?!"
 D3 — The Accusation: A witness reports the burial as suspicious.
      (Name1) must explain (his/her) actions to the elders.
 D4 — The Relic That Returned: The object reappears near the huts;
      nobody admits moving it. Was it sabotage, chance, or a curse?

EVENT R2 — THE TRIAL OF THE IMPOSSIBLE RELIC
Following a suspicious discovery, (Name1) is brought before a council
of elders. The object rests on a woven mat. Some accuse (him/her) of
spying; others defend (his/her) curiosity.

A. INVESTIGATE FORMALLY
 A1 — Innocent: Evidence clears (Name1), who is publicly vindicated.
 A2 — The Hidden Visitor: Strange footprints and materials suggest
      someone else has recently visited the island.
 A3 — The False Confession: Frightened, (Name1) admits to something
      untrue; another witness eventually exposes the mistake.
 A4 — The Unexpected Spy: Another villager planted the object to
      discredit (Name1).

B. CONFINE (NAME1) IN A GUARDED SHELTER
 B1 — The Wrongful Prisoner: (Name1) is innocent. Later revelations
      bring shame upon the council and demands for restitution.
 B2 — The Prisoner's Discovery: In confinement, (Name1) recalls a
      crucial clue about the object's arrival.
 B3 — The Village Divided: Supporters demand release while fearful
      villagers demand continued confinement.
 B4 — The Escape: (Name1) flees into the forest; the village cannot
      decide whether this proves guilt or desperation.

C. DEFEND (NAME1) PUBLICLY
 C1 — The Vindicated Explorer: The accusation collapses; (Name1)
      receives public thanks for the discovery.
 C2 — The Unanswered Suspicion: (Name1) is freed, but rumors persist.
 C3 — The Courageous Elder: An elder argues that fear must not become
      an excuse for cruelty, winning grudging respect.
 C4 — The Genuine Conspiracy: The relic was planted deliberately,
      although (Name1) may still be innocent.

EVENT R3 — HAS ISOLA BEEN DISCOVERED?
A second impossible object appears. Then a third. The villagers fear
that powerful strangers know the island's location.

A. PREPARE FOR POSSIBLE INVASION
 A1. Watch posts and patrols are organized.
 A2. A lookout mistakes an animal for an invader, causing panic.
 A3. A patrol discovers signs of an unfamiliar landing party.
 A4. The preparations exhaust food and labor; nobody arrives.

B. SEARCH FOR THE SOURCE
 B1. Explorers discover an old wreck with more impossible objects.
 B2. A cave contains a secret collection of strange artifacts.
 B3. An unfamiliar campsite appears recently abandoned.
 B4. The objects may have washed ashore from a distant land.

C. KEEP THE DISCOVERIES SECRET
 C1. The elders conceal the relics, but rumors persist.
 C2. A researcher records the discovery despite an order of silence.
 C3. Someone steals a relic for personal gain.
 C4. A child innocently reveals the secret to everyone.

D. ATTEMPT TO COMMUNICATE
 D1. A great shore signal receives no answer.
 D2. A distant light appears over the water.
 D3. An unfamiliar object appears in apparent response.
 D4. The signal attracts attention the elders deeply regret.

EVENT R4 — THE RELIC THAT OUTLIVED ITS DISCOVERER
Years or generations after (Name1) first found the object, (Name2)
discovers it again. The original discoverer may now be elderly or dead.
Randomize one outcome:
 R4-1 — The Forgotten Genius: (Name2) solves the mystery and receives
        acclaim once denied to (Name1).
 R4-2 — Justice Across Generations: Village records reveal that
        (Name1) was wrongfully confined; the elders clear (his/her) name.
 R4-3 — The Legendary Artifact: The object becomes a cherished relic
        despite nobody understanding its purpose.
 R4-4 — The Great Deception: A supposed ancient marvel was fabricated
        by an earlier villager as an elaborate prank.
 R4-5 — The Discovery That Changed Everything: A later generation
        understands part of its construction. Large Tech reward.
 R4-6 — The Curse That Wasn't: The object proves entirely harmless.
 R4-7 — The Curse That Was: It activates unexpectedly after decades.
 R4-8 — The Last Witness: An elder admits to having concealed the truth
        about the object's arrival for many years.

GAME-SPECIFIC RELIC VARIANTS
VV1: The Golden Child inexplicably recognizes a function no adult knows.
VV2: One Lost Child recognizes a detail from a half-remembered past.
VV3: The Tribal Chief weighs suppression against inquiry; scholars,
     magicians, and naturalists disagree over what the object means.
VV4: An artifact among the Tree of Life's roots sparks concern that
     outsiders have disturbed a sacred place.
VV5: Villagers and Heathens/Isolans accuse one another of concealing
     artifacts. An Isolan elder may reveal old knowledge, be accused
     of spying, or become the first to defend an accused rival.

OPTIONAL REPUTATION AND HISTORY CODAS
 P1. Reluctant Hero: A destroyer accidentally discovers a useful tool.
 P2. Disgraced Scholar: A confident expert misidentifies the relic.
 P3. Political Prisoner: A wrongfully confined villager is cleared years later.
 P4. False Prophet: A claimant of divine authority is exposed.
 P5. Inventor's Curse: Reproducing a mechanism leads to sorcery accusations.
 P6. Secret Society: Researchers meet privately to study the objects.
 P7. The Great Panic: Rumors disrupt work and empty the village stores.
 P8. First Contact: Exceptionally rarely, evidence shows the object
     was deliberately left by someone who knows Isola exists.

IMPLEMENTATION NOTES
Track relic type, discoverer, buried/destroyed/studied state, whether
the discoverer was accused, and any subsequent vindication. Rediscovery
should refer to the same relic rather than spontaneously inventing an
unrelated object. Use elders, guarded huts, village councils, and oral
history in place of European prisons and royal trials. Avoid asserting
that every strange object proves invasion: shipwrecks, drift, hoaxes,
and unexplained phenomena should remain viable explanations.

END OF V20 ADDITION
================================================================================
V21 ADDITION — THE POTTERS' TRIALS AND THE GREAT ISOLAN CRAFTS FAIR
================================================================================
CREDITS: Original requests for ceramic pottery/statue making and a crafts fair:
Lorsieab2. New scenarios, choices, prose, and outcome branches: ChatGPT.
SETTING: VV1–VV5; tropical Polynesian-inspired Isola. Clay, sand, ash,
water, pigments, shells, woven fiber, reeds, wood, stone, and carefully tended
firing pits are ordinary materials. Truly impossible effects are deliberately
rare mysteries. Each event is repeatable, with eligible named villagers.

EVENT C1 — THE GREAT POTTERS' TRIAL (ALL FIVE GAMES)

At the village clearing, (Name1) sets a newly shaped clay vessel upon a woven
mat. A respected craftsperson inspects its rim, balance, decoration, and
usefulness. Other villagers crowd around, eager to see whether it survives
firing. (Name1) asks permission to enter the annual pottery contest.

A. ENTER THE VESSEL IN THE PRACTICAL CRAFT CATEGORY.
 A1 — The Perfect Water Jar: The pot holds water without leaking and is easy
      to carry. The judges praise (Name1)'s skill; gain modest Tech points.
 A2 — A Beautiful Sieve: The vessel is covered in tiny holes. (Name1) calls
      it a water jar; the judge calls it a colander. The farmers immediately
      request copies for washing gathered food. Small Tech reward.
 A3 — The Great Collapse: The unfired vessel slumps into a shapeless mound.
      (Name1) is mortified; an apprentice gently offers to help rebuild it.
 A4 — The Unbreakable Bowl: The bowl survives a fall onto packed earth.
      The judges celebrate, until someone discovers it cannot be lifted
      without spilling everything inside. Mixed reputation.
 A5 — The Vessel with Two Mouths: Nobody can explain the double spout until
      a healer discovers it is useful for measuring small amounts of water.
      (Name1) becomes an unexpectedly celebrated innovator.

B. ENTER THE VESSEL IN THE ARTISTIC CATEGORY.
 B1 — The Ancestors' Faces: The carved figures are so expressive that an
      elder recognizes a long-dead relative. The crowd falls respectfully
      silent; (Name1) is awarded first place.
 B2 — The Ugliest Masterpiece: The pot resembles a lopsided crab with an
      enormous nose. The judges recoil; the children adore it. Years later,
      its likeness is found in nearly every hut.
 B3 — The Painted Catastrophe: During firing, the colors change unexpectedly.
      The judges call it ruined; a visiting craftsperson calls it beautiful.
      The village splits into rival schools of taste.
 B4 — The Stolen Pattern: (Name2) accuses (Name1) of copying a family motif.
      The elders investigate and discover coincidence, theft, or a shared
      ancestral source. Choose a fitting reputation coda.
 B5 — The Speaking Pot: When the wind passes over the vessel, it makes a
      mournful musical note. (Name1) insists this was intentional; no one
      believes (him/her), but the musicians beg to borrow it.

C. ASK THE MASTER POTTER TO JUDGE AND TEACH.
 C1 — The Merciless Critic: The judge lists twelve flaws without taking a
      breath. (Name1) nearly cries, then spends days improving the work.
      (Name1) gains a crafting-related Tech reward and new respect.
 C2 — The Critic Is Wrong: The judge condemns the shape, but a farmer
      demonstrates its remarkable usefulness. The judge apologizes publicly.
 C3 — The Apprentice's Triumph: A child quietly suggests reinforcing the
      base. The resulting pot wins; (Name1) credits the child before all.
 C4 — The Judge's Favorite: The judge praises a friend's inferior pot.
      Outraged competitors demand a new panel; the council may uphold or
      overturn the result.

EVENT C2 — THE STATUE THAT DIVIDED THE VILLAGE (ALL FIVE GAMES)

(Name1) unveils a new statue made from fired clay, carved wood, or volcanic
stone. The sculpture is intended to honor the island's history, but its
unusual proportions provoke an argument before the unveiling is even over.

A. DISPLAY IT PROUDLY IN THE VILLAGE CENTER.
 A1 — Beloved Monstrosity: The statue is staggeringly ugly, yet somehow
      endearing. Villagers leave flowers beside it; it becomes a landmark.
 A2 — The Unflattering Portrait: The subject recognizes (himself/herself)
      and is deeply offended. (Name1) must apologize, defend artistic freedom,
      or reveal that the resemblance was accidental.
 A3 — The Masterwork: The statue is praised for balance and expression;
      (Name1) becomes a distinguished artisan. Gain Tech points.
 A4 — The Terrible Omen: A sudden crack splits the figure at the unveiling.
      Some villagers fear an omen; others blame poor firing. A debate follows.
 A5 — The Impossible Balance: A top-heavy statue stands upright despite
      every builder's prediction. Researchers inspect the base; perhaps
      (Name1) is brilliant, perhaps the island is playing a trick.

B. INVITE THE PUBLIC TO IMPROVE IT.
 B1 — Many Hands: Children add shells and pigments. The statue becomes a
      beloved community work, though (Name1) loses sole credit.
 B2 — The Artistic War: Two factions repaint the figure every night.
      The elders finally demand that both sides stop.
 B3 — The Unexpected Genius: A quiet apprentice repairs its weak base and
      transforms the statue into a masterpiece. (Name1) shares the prize.
 B4 — The Ruined Treasure: An enthusiastic helper damages the statue;
      (Name1) must decide whether to forgive (him/her).

C. PLACE IT AT A QUIET SHRINE OR GARDEN.
 C1 — The Secret Favorite: Villagers begin visiting the sculpture in private;
      its popularity grows slowly into a lasting tradition.
 C2 — The False Miracle: Rainwater trickles from the statue's eyes. Some
      call it weeping; a child finds a crack collecting water from above.
 C3 — The Real Mystery: The statue casts a shadow in an unexpected direction
      at sunset. The scholars cannot explain it; no answer is imposed.
 C4 — Forgotten Art: Plants grow around the statue until it is rediscovered
      by a later generation and hailed as an ancient masterpiece.

EVENT C3 — THE GREAT ISOLAN CRAFTS FAIR (ALL FIVE GAMES)

Woven mats line the clearing. Potters display bowls and jars; weavers offer
baskets and patterned cloth; woodcarvers show paddles, ornaments, and masks;
craftspeople bring shell jewelry, musical instruments, toys, and ingenious
household tools. Visitors barter food, materials, and favors. A loud,
merciless judge has been invited to award the festival's highest honor.

A. HOLD A JURIED COMPETITION.
 A1 — The Judge Who Feared No Craft: The judge calls a bowl 'a coconut
      shell pretending to be important' and a basket 'a nest with delusions.'
      Then (he/she) praises a shy child's tiny woven fish as the best work
      at the fair. The crowd erupts in applause.
 A2 — The Great Upset: A novice defeats a celebrated master. The master
      demands a recount; the judges confirm the result.
 A3 — The Perfectly Useless Wonder: A contraption is exquisite, intricate,
      and entirely incapable of doing what its maker claims. The judges award
      it a special prize for imagination.
 A4 — The Rigged Prize: A judge secretly favors a relative. A witness
      exposes the bias; the council orders a new judging panel.
 A5 — The Shared Victory: Two rivals create an extraordinary object together
      and refuse to claim individual credit. Both receive recognition.

B. ENCOURAGE BARTER AND SHARED TEACHING.
 B1 — A Village of Artisans: Techniques spread across households. Modest
      Tech reward; several villagers gain crafting experience.
 B2 — The Great Barter Disaster: One villager trades an entire basket of
      food for a 'rare sacred pebble' that turns out to be ordinary stone.
 B3 — The Invention Exchange: A potter and weaver combine techniques to
      create an unexpectedly useful container. Large Tech reward.
 B4 — The Lost Heirloom: A treasured family ornament vanishes amid the crowd.
      The search ends in recovery, an accusation, or an awkward discovery
      that its owner put it in the wrong basket.
 B5 — The Children's Fair: Children begin trading miniature clay animals,
      shell necklaces, and painted stones. The adults realize their fair has
      been thoroughly outshone.

C. MAKE THE FAIR A CEREMONIAL FESTIVAL.
 C1 — The Night of a Hundred Lamps: Safe, sheltered firelight illuminates
      the exhibits as singers recite the makers' names. The festival becomes
      an annual tradition.
 C2 — The Uninvited Storm: Rain sweeps through the clearing. Potters rush to
      protect unfired work, while the weavers rescue their dyes and fibers.
      A hastily built shelter inspires a new communal structure.
 C3 — The Mask That Chose Its Owner: A ceremonial mask repeatedly falls
      toward the same villager. Some see a sign; others find a crooked stand.
 C4 — The Feast of Reconciliation: Two feuding households exchange handmade
      gifts and begin speaking again.
 C5 — The Fair Nobody Could End: The celebrations run so long that farming
      and building are neglected. The elders establish sensible festival
      hours next time.

GAME-SPECIFIC ADAPTATIONS
VV1 — SOUTHERN SHORE: The fair celebrates the village's first thriving
settlement. The Golden Child may make an astonishing miniature sculpture or
solve a kiln-design problem, then wander off to play with the other children.
VV2 — WESTERN SHORE: The Lost Children contribute tiny clay animals and
painted shells. A competition may honor the founders, the totems, or the
Great Falls; an elder discovers a child's apparently childish design is
more practical than a master's.
VV3 — SECRET CITY: The Tribal Chief opens the fair and arbitrates disputes.
Magicians and naturalists may create competing works inspired by their
traditions, but neither faction must be portrayed as inherently superior.
VV4 — TREE OF LIFE: Sculptors and potters incorporate fallen leaves and
non-damaging natural motifs. A dispute arises when someone wants to remove
living material from the Tree; the villagers must choose preservation or
artistic ambition.
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS: Villagers and Heathens/Isolans can enter as makers,
judges, critics, traders, and spectators. Rival groups accuse each other of
stealing designs; an Isolan judge may fairly award a prize to a villager,
or a village judge may publicly praise an Isolan rival. A mixed team may
win the highest honor, provoking either reconciliation or a boycott.

OPTIONAL LONG-TERM CODAS
1. The Ugly Statue Becomes a Legend: Children retell its origin for years.
2. The Disgraced Judge Returns: A former critic seeks a chance to judge fairly.
3. The Forgotten Potter: A later generation rediscovers an overlooked vessel.
4. The Artisan's Apprentice: A novice becomes a master and honors the teacher.
5. The Great Design Feud: Two families dispute ownership of a decorative motif.
6. The Festival of Two Communities (VV5): An annual joint fair becomes a
   fragile but cherished symbol of peaceful coexistence.
7. The Impossible Vessel: An inexplicable pot seems to preserve water, sing,
   or balance beyond reason; the mystery remains deliberately exceptional.

END OF V21 ADDITION

================================================================================
V22 ADDITION — DRAWING, STRENGTH, POTION-MAKING, AND PARENTING
================================================================================

CREDITS
Original event concepts and requested parenting mechanics: Lorsieab2.
Event scripts, additional branches, and detailed outcomes: ChatGPT.
All earlier material and its authorship credits are preserved unchanged.
IMPORTANT: The five event-title alterations requested after v21 are NOT
applied in this edition. They remain pending until the user has reviewed v22.

EVENT V22-1 — THE GREAT ISOLAN DRAWING COMPETITION (VV1–VV5)

The artists of Isola assemble beneath the shade of broad tropical leaves.
There are bowls of crushed berry pigments, charcoal sticks, colored earth,
barkcloth, smooth wood, and prepared leaves. A row of judges sits before
a growing collection of drawings. (Name1) has entered the competition,
although (he/she) has not yet decided what to draw.

An enthusiastic organizer announces, "May the finest artist win!"
From the back, someone mutters, "May the judges know what art is."

WHAT SHOULD (NAME1) DRAW?

A. A LANDSCAPE OF ISOLA
 A1 — The Island in a Single Picture: The drawing captures the reefs,
      palms, and distant hills so beautifully that even the judges
      fall silent. (Name1) wins; gain Artistic skill and reputation.
 A2 — The Rain's Contribution: A tropical shower washes the pigments
      into dramatic streaks. (Name1) calls the result "The Storm."
      One judge loves it; another calls it an accident. Random prize.
 A3 — The Accidental Map: An explorer recognizes a detail that resembles
      a little-known route through the island. Gain exploration progress
      or a modest Tech reward; the contest result is disputed.
 A4 — The Wrong Island: (Name1) paints a magnificent beach but places
      the waterfall on the wrong side. The judges praise the technique
      while the audience laughs at the geography.

B. A PORTRAIT OF A FELLOW VILLAGER
 B1 — A Living Likeness: The subject is moved by the careful portrait.
      (Name1) receives first prize and Artistic skill.
 B2 — The Nose Controversy: The subject objects that (his/her) nose
      looks enormous. (Name1) insists the likeness is accurate.
      The village debates whether accuracy is always kind.
 B3 — The Accidental Insult: (Name1) portrays a respected elder with
      an expression of great wisdom; the elder thinks it looks sleepy.
      The artist must apologize or explain the intention.
 B4 — The Unexpected Friendship: A portrait of a lonely villager
      prompts a warm conversation. The prize is modest, but both
      villagers gain friendship and (Name1) gains Artistic skill.

C. A SCENE FROM IMAGINATION
 C1 — The Visionary: A striking imagined landscape astonishes the
      judges. (Name1) earns a special prize for creativity.
 C2 — The Unidentifiable Thing: The judges cannot agree whether the
      drawing depicts a fish, a bird, or a coconut with legs.
      The children adore it. Gain popularity but no prize.
 C3 — The Picture That Changed: At sunset the image seems subtly
      different. A scholar blames shifting light; a mystic disagrees.
      Rare supernatural mystery; no confirmed explanation.
 C4 — The Storyteller: The picture inspires an improvised tale that
      captivates the audience. Gain Artistic skill and a storytelling
      reputation, even if the drawing itself does not win.

D. EXPERIMENT WITH AN UNUSUAL TECHNIQUE
 D1 — A New Pigment: (Name1) finds a way to make a more durable color
      from island materials. Gain Tech and Artistic skill.
 D2 — The Beautiful Disaster: The colors run together into a muddy
      smear. A judge politely describes it as "energetic."
      (Name1) receives no prize and a little embarrassment.
 D3 — The Pattern of Light: Layered pigments produce a remarkable
      effect in the afternoon sun. Gain Artistic skill and a prize.
 D4 — The Great Copying Scandal: Another contestant claims (Name1)
      stole a design. A fair inquiry clears (Name1), reveals shared
      inspiration, or uncovers deliberate copying.

OPTIONAL CODA: A child's simple drawing of the village may defeat
every adult entry. The result is celebrated, disputed, or taken as
a reminder that art need not be complicated.

EVENT V22-2 — THE GREAT ISOLAN STRENGTH TRIALS (VV1–VV5)

To celebrate the village's hardworking builders, farmers, and gatherers,
the elders announce a friendly strength competition. Coconuts are stacked,
water-filled clay jugs are carefully inspected, sturdy fallen logs are
marked, and a large stone is placed at the edge of the clearing.
(Name1) volunteers. Several spectators immediately begin arguing about
which feat should count as the greatest demonstration of strength.

WHAT CHALLENGE SHOULD (NAME1) ATTEMPT?

A. THE COCONUT CARRY
 A1 — The Walking Coconut Mountain: (Name1) carries an astonishing
      number of coconuts across the finish line. Gain Physical skill
      and Champion of the Coconut Carry reputation.
 A2 — The Clever Basket: (Name1) uses a huge woven carrier and wins.
      The judges argue whether cleverness counts. The elders decide
      either to honor the feat or to introduce a separate ingenuity prize.
 A3 — The Great Coconut Avalanche: The stack tumbles harmlessly into
      the sand. Children race to collect the runaway coconuts while
      (Name1) bows to the laughing crowd.
 A4 — The Patient Champion: A rival starts with more coconuts but drops
      them. (Name1)'s slower, steadier effort wins the contest.

B. THE WATER-JUG LIFT
 B1 — Steady as Stone: (Name1) holds two filled clay jugs longer than
      anyone else. Gain Physical skill and a prize.
 B2 — The Unfortunate Splash: A cracked jug gives way, soaking (Name1).
      The crowd roars with laughter; the organizers replace the jug
      and offer a fair retry.
 B3 — The Surprise Winner: A quiet villager with excellent balance
      defeats several boastful competitors. (Name1) learns that control
      matters as much as brute force.
 B4 — A Sensible Withdrawal: (Name1) notices a strained grip and
      stops before injury. No prize, but an elder praises good judgment.

C. THE BOULDER OR LOG CHALLENGE
 C1 — The Strongest on the Shore: (Name1) moves the marked stone or
      fallen log farther than any competitor. Gain Physical skill.
 C2 — The Leverage Lesson: A smaller competitor demonstrates a clever
      wooden lever. The judges award a separate prize for engineering;
      gain Tech and Physical skill for observing.
 C3 — Stuck in the Sand: The log sinks into soft sand. (Name1) and a
      rival must work together to free it. Gain Cooperation reputation.
 C4 — The Overconfident Champion: (Name1) boasts loudly, then cannot
      budge the load. A child remarks, "Perhaps your words were heavier."

D. THE WILLING-VILLAGER CARRY
 A consenting adult volunteer agrees to be carried a short, marked
 distance over even ground; organizers may cancel the event if unsafe.
 D1 — A Perfect Partnership: (Name1) carries the volunteer steadily
      and wins. Both celebrate; gain Physical skill.
 D2 — The Talking Passenger: The volunteer offers nonstop advice
      about posture and foot placement. (Name1) finishes but is
      thoroughly embarrassed; the audience is delighted.
 D3 — The Sudden Switch: The volunteer proves stronger than (Name1)
      and jokingly offers to reverse roles. The crowd applauds.
 D4 — Teamwork Over Glory: (Name1) decides the carry is unwise and
      helps organize a safer team challenge instead. Gain respect.

E. THE GRAND STRENGTH CIRCUIT
 E1 — The All-Around Champion: A balanced performance across coconut
      carrying, water-jug holding, and log moving earns the grand prize.
 E2 — Too Fast, Too Soon: (Name1) exhausts (himself/herself) early and
      must rest; no injury, but an important lesson about pacing.
 E3 — The Unlikely Victory: An overlooked competitor wins through
      steady technique and endurance. Gain Physical skill and respect.
 E4 — The Disputed Finish: Two contestants finish together. The
      elders declare a shared victory or call for a lighthearted rematch.

MECHANICS: Use reasonable loads and voluntary participation. These
are festival feats, not permission for children to carry heavy stones
or for villagers to injure one another. Award Physical skill only
when the outcome demonstrates practice, technique, or successful effort.

EVENT V22-3 — THE GREAT ISOLAN POTION-MAKING TRIALS (VV1–VV5)

The healers and herbalists assemble around low tables laden with island
plants, clean water, gourds, stone bowls, and carefully labeled samples.
The judges announce several possible contests. Some reward a useful
preparation; others are demonstrations of harmless magical craft.
(Name1) chooses an entry. A rival declares that (his/her) mixture
will be "the most powerful potion Isola has ever seen."

WHAT KIND OF POTION SHOULD (NAME1) MAKE?

A. THE MOST EFFECTIVE HEALING PREPARATION
 A1 — The Careful Healer: Using established methods and careful
      observation, (Name1) makes a useful preparation. The judges
      award Healing skill and a prize.
 A2 — The Promising Discovery: The mixture appears helpful in
      controlled demonstrations, but the judges insist on further
      study before calling it a cure. Gain Tech and Healing skill.
 A3 — The Beautiful Failure: The potion smells wonderful and looks
      magnificent but performs no better than plain water in the
      agreed tests. No healing prize; gain modest research experience.
 A4 — The Misidentified Leaf: A judge notices an ingredient mix-up
      before anyone uses the preparation. The entry is disqualified;
      the healer gains a lesson in careful identification.

B. THE STRONGEST POTION
 B1 — Strength of the Spirit: A fictional, magical preparation
      briefly grants an adult contestant unusual vigor. The effect
      fades harmlessly; (Name1) wins a rare prize.
 B2 — The Boastful Brew: A rival proclaims extraordinary power,
      but a simple test reveals that the potion does nothing.
      The audience applauds the judge's deadpan expression.
 B3 — The Potion That Worked Too Well: A harmless fictional calming
      mixture leaves a judge sleepy through the awards ceremony.
      The organizers rule that "strongest" is not always "best."
 B4 — The Impossible Result: A glowing mixture behaves in a way
      no researcher can explain. It is sealed for further study;
      rare supernatural mystery and Tech reward.

C. A POTION THAT DOES ONE SPECIFIC THING
 The judges randomly request a harmless effect: vivid color change,
 a pleasant fragrance, a long-lasting bubble, a safe cooling
 sensation, or an explicitly magical but temporary glow.
 C1 — Exactly as Requested: The potion performs its assigned task
      reliably. (Name1) wins; gain Research skill.
 C2 — Almost Right: The color changes beautifully but to the wrong
      shade, or the fragrance fades too quickly. Small skill gain.
 C3 — The Endless Bubbles: The mixture produces far more bubbles
      than intended. The judges struggle to keep straight faces.
 C4 — The Unexpected Utility: The mixture fails its assigned task
      but proves useful for marking baskets or decorating barkcloth.
      Gain a modest Tech reward, but not first prize.

D. THE MOST EFFECTIVE ANTIDOTE OR REMEDY
 D1 — The Thoughtful Researcher: (Name1) correctly matches a known
      remedy to a simulated case. Gain Healing and Research skill.
 D2 — The Cautious Scholar: (Name1) refuses to make unsupported
      claims. The judges commend the honesty and award a research prize.
 D3 — The Great Mislabeling: Two harmless demonstration samples
      are swapped; an apprentice notices before judging. The
      organizers improve their labeling system.
 D4 — The Rival's Sabotage: A competitor tampers with the labels.
      An inquiry exposes the trick; the culprit loses reputation.

E. THE EXPERIMENTAL OR FORBIDDEN BREW
 E1 — A New Principle: A careful experiment reveals a useful
      property of an island plant. Gain Tech; no untested clinical use.
 E2 — The Potion of Hiccups: A rare comic magical mishap gives an
      adult judge harmless hiccups until sunset. No prize.
 E3 — The Temporary Transformation: A rare magical potion briefly
      changes the brewer's hair color or makes (his/her) voice squeak.
      The effect wears off; the crowd is astonished.
 E4 — The Council Intervenes: The elders stop a reckless demonstration
      before anyone is harmed. (Name1) may accept the correction and
      gain Research skill, or sulk and lose reputation.

DESIGN NOTES: Never imply that untested remedies are proven cures.
Healing contests use observation, simulated cases, safe demonstrations,
or well-established preparations. Explicitly magical effects are
exceptional to the island's ordinary technology. Do not supply real
toxic recipes or harmful dosing instructions.

EVENT V22-4 — THE PARENT'S LESSON (VV1–VV5)

(Name2), a child in the village, has been testing the patience of the
adults. (Name1), the adult currently responsible for guiding (him/her),
decides it is time to teach an important lesson.

(Name1) may be a biological parent, adoptive parent, guardian, foster
caregiver, or trusted adult mentor. No blood relationship is required.
The story must remain warm, all-ages, and free of creepy implications.
The child's mistakes are playful or understandable, never cruelly
humiliating. The adult may be mistaken too.

WHICH LESSON SHOULD (NAME1) TEACH?

A. HONESTY
 A1 — Truth with Kindness: (Name2) admits to accidentally spilling
      berries and helps clean up. (Name1) praises the honesty and
      explains that truth and kindness belong together.
      Parenting skill: SIGNIFICANT GAIN; child gains Honesty.
 A2 — The Painfully Honest Critic: At the village meal, (Name2)
      announces that the cook's stew tastes like "wet sandals."
      "But you said always to tell the truth!" (Name1) blushes.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN until a successful follow-up lesson.
 A3 — The Gentle Correction: After the embarrassing comment,
      (Name1) explains how to speak honestly without being unkind.
      (Name2) apologizes and tries again.
      Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN; child gains Tact.
 A4 — The Little Investigator: (Name2) begins asking everyone
      whether they are telling the truth. (Name1) must teach the
      difference between honesty and accusing people without reason.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN if uncorrected; MODERATE if corrected.

B. PATIENCE
 B1 — The Patient Gardener: (Name2) tends a seedling for days
      and proudly celebrates its first leaves.
      Parenting skill: SIGNIFICANT GAIN; child gains Patience.
 B2 — The Waiting Game: (Name2) sits under an unripe fruit tree
      for hours, expecting the fruit to fall directly into (his/her)
      mouth. "I'm being patient!" (Name1) is speechless.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN until corrected.
 B3 — Patience and Action: (Name1) explains that waiting can
      include preparation and care. (Name2) begins watering the tree.
      Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN.
 B4 — The Impatient Teacher: (Name1) grows frustrated while teaching
      patience, then notices the irony and apologizes.
      Parenting skill: SMALL GAIN if (Name1) models patience afterward;
      otherwise NO GAIN.

C. RESPONSIBILITY
 C1 — The Reliable Helper: (Name2) takes care of a manageable task,
      remembers it without prompting, and helps a neighbor.
      Parenting skill: SIGNIFICANT GAIN; child gains Responsibility.
 C2 — The Coconut Inspector: Asked to help organize coconuts, (Name2)
      devises an elaborate inspection system and refuses to let
      anyone eat without approval. The village is amused and hungry.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN until boundaries are clarified.
 C3 — A Job Well Shared: (Name1) helps the child make a simple plan
      and share the work. The coconuts reach the cooks on time.
      Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN.
 C4 — The Helpful Troublemaker: (Name2) begins reorganizing other
      villagers' belongings without asking. (Name1) must explain
      that responsibility also means respecting others' choices.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN if uncorrected; MODERATE if corrected.

D. KINDNESS AND SHARING
 D1 — The Quiet Kindness: (Name2) notices a lonely child and
      invites (him/her) to play. (Name1) is deeply moved.
      Parenting skill: SIGNIFICANT GAIN; child gains Kindness.
 D2 — Sharing Everything: (Name2) gives away all the family's
      prepared snacks, including the portion meant for (Name1).
      The adult must explain thoughtful sharing and boundaries.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN until corrected.
 D3 — The Thoughtful Gift: (Name2) learns to ask before giving
      away belongings and makes a small gift from fallen shells.
      Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN.
 D4 — The Self-Appointed Kindness Officer: (Name2) orders everyone
      to be nice and interrupts every disagreement. The villagers
      gently remind the child that kindness cannot be commanded.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN if the child remains bossy.

E. RESPECT FOR NATURE
 E1 — The Careful Explorer: (Name2) learns to observe small animals
      without disturbing them and helps protect a young plant.
      Parenting skill: SIGNIFICANT GAIN; child gains Nature knowledge.
 E2 — The Leaf Collector: Trying to "care for every tree," (Name2)
      gathers a mountain of fallen leaves and blocks the hut entrance.
      (Name1) laughs and helps sort them for compost.
      Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN if lesson completed.
 E3 — The Well-Meant Mistake: (Name2) tries to water every plant
      at once and makes a muddy mess. (Name1) teaches moderation.
      Parenting skill: SMALL or MODERATE GAIN if corrected.
 E4 — The Little Guardian: (Name2) starts scolding adults for
      picking fruit. (Name1) explains sustainable harvesting.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN until the child understands.

F. PERSEVERANCE AND HUMILITY
 F1 — The Second Attempt: After a failed weaving project, (Name2)
      practices and completes a simple basket.
      Parenting skill: SIGNIFICANT GAIN; child gains Perseverance.
 F2 — Never Give Up Asking: (Name2) repeatedly requests extra fruit
      because (Name1) said "never give up." The adult must clarify
      the difference between persistence and pestering.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN until corrected.
 F3 — The Boastful Apprentice: (Name2) learns a new skill and
      immediately declares (himself/herself) the greatest artisan
      on Isola. A gentle lesson in humility follows.
      Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN only if the child learns.
 F4 — The Teacher Learns: (Name2) asks a thoughtful question that
      exposes (Name1)'s own mistake. (Name1) listens, admits it,
      and models humility. Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN.

G. THINK BEFORE ACTING
 G1 — A Wise Pause: (Name2) stops before a harmless prank and
      considers how a friend would feel. (Name1) is proud.
      Parenting skill: SIGNIFICANT GAIN; child gains Judgment.
 G2 — The Endless Thinker: (Name2) spends an entire afternoon
      deciding which coconut to choose. "I'm thinking first!"
      (Name1) explains that decisions need not take forever.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN until corrected.
 G3 — The Prank That Became a Gift: (Name2) changes a mischievous
      idea into a playful surprise everyone enjoys.
      Parenting skill: MODERATE GAIN; child gains Creativity.
 G4 — The New Troublemaker: (Name2) interprets "think ahead" as
      "plan pranks more carefully." The parent is horrified when
      a trail of coconut shells leads to an elaborate harmless trick.
      Parenting skill: NO GAIN; possible small skill LOSS if the
      adult responds poorly. A later corrective event may repair it.

PARENTING MECHANICS — IMPORTANT, AUTHOR-DIRECTED RULE
The designated adult does NOT automatically gain Parenting skill merely
for attempting a lesson. Skill changes depend on what the child actually
learns and how well the adult guides or corrects the situation:
 * Successful lesson and demonstrated understanding: significant gain.
 * Partial understanding or constructive follow-up: small/moderate gain.
 * Comic misunderstanding left uncorrected: no gain.
 * Mischief/troublemaking caused by the lesson and left unresolved:
   no gain; optional small loss if the adult handles it especially badly.
 * Adult recognizes an error, responds respectfully, and successfully
   teaches a better lesson: moderate gain.
 * Child teaches the adult something useful: gain only if the adult
   listens, responds constructively, and improves their parenting.
Child outcomes may confer appropriate traits, but do not permanently
brand a child as bad; future lessons can lead to growth and redemption.

CROSS-GAME ADAPTATIONS
VV1 — SOUTHERN SHORE: Drawings celebrate the new settlement. The Golden
Child may enter a contest as a child, surprising the judges, but should
still be playful. Parenting scenes can involve ordinary villagers.
VV2 — WESTERN SHORE: The Lost Children may draw remembered places,
enter child-friendly arts events, or learn lessons from their caregivers.
The Great Falls and totems may inspire art.
VV3 — SECRET CITY: The Tribal Chief may open the competitions or
mediate a dispute. Nature and Magic practitioners may propose
different approaches to the potion trials without guaranteeing either
side victory.
VV4 — TREE OF LIFE: Art may celebrate the Tree; responsible artists
must not damage its living parts. Strength competitions can support
community building projects. The Tree may inspire harmless mysteries.
VV5 — NEW BELIEVERS: Villagers and Heathens/Isolans may compete,
collaborate, disagree over judging, or honor one another's craft.
An Isolan caregiver may teach a child or be invited to share a lesson.
The event should not imply that either community lacks intelligence
or parental care.

LONG-TERM CODAS
1. The Artist's Apprentice: A former contest loser becomes a beloved
   teacher of young artists.
2. The Champion Who Learned Humility: A strength champion later
   loses to a patient novice and offers sincere congratulations.
3. The Potion That Became Tradition: A useful, carefully studied
   preparation enters the healers' records.
4. The Child Who Remembered: Years later, a grown villager repeats
   the same lesson to a younger child, with either wisdom or irony.
5. The Trouble That Became Talent: A mischievous child channels
   cleverness into art, research, or helpful invention.
6. The Shared Prize: A VV5 mixed-team victory helps ease a
   longstanding village-Isolan disagreement.

END OF V22 ADDITION



===============================================================================
V23 ADDITION — PRANKS, MISUNDERSTANDINGS, AND EVERYDAY CHAOS
===============================================================================
CREDITS: Core premises and requested title edits by Lorsieab2; branching
narratives and elaborations by ChatGPT. All five games. Ancient tropical,
Polynesian-inspired Isola is the ordinary setting; deliberate supernatural
exceptions are possible. These events are age-appropriate.

EVENT V23-1 — THE PRACTICAL JOKE
(Name1) decides that the village has become much too serious. (He/She)
prepares a harmless trick for (Name2), using baskets, shells, woven mats,
painted gourds, or other ordinary island materials.

CHOICE A — PLAY A SIMPLE TRICK
A1. The Perfect Prank: (Name2) jumps, then laughs so hard that the whole
    village joins in. Both gain a little happiness.
A2. The Wrong Audience: An elder receives the prank by mistake and gives
    (Name1) a long lecture. (Name2) finds this hilarious.
A3. The Prankster Pranked: (Name2) anticipated everything and replaces the
    surprise with a carved sign reading, "TRY AGAIN."
A4. The Great Misunderstanding: A mysteriously moving basket convinces
    several witnesses that an ancestral spirit is visiting.

CHOICE B — MAKE AN ELABORATE ILLUSION
B1. The Vanishing Villager: Clever screens and woven mats make (Name2)
    appear to vanish. The researchers spend an embarrassing afternoon
    investigating the trick.
B2. The Haunted Hut: A hollow bamboo tube produces eerie noises until
    (Name1) is caught giggling behind the hut.
B3. The Accidental Discovery: The prank reveals an unusual echo or air
    current; researchers gain a little Tech.
B4. The Village Panic: The illusion is too convincing. Villagers flee,
    then demand an apology and help restoring the interrupted work.

CHOICE C — RECRUIT ACCOMPLICES
C1. The Great Conspiracy: Several pranksters maintain straight faces
    until one bursts out laughing and ruins the surprise.
C2. The Betrayal: A child announces the entire plan to the target.
C3. The Prank War: (Name2) organizes a friendly retaliation; unlock
    THE PRANK WAR.
C4. The Apology: The prank accidentally hurts someone's feelings.
    The pranksters apologize and make amends; friendship improves.

EVENT V23-2 — THE ILL-TIMED SNEEZE
(Name1) develops an irresistible tickle in (his/her) nose at precisely
the wrong moment. Choose one randomized scene:
S1. The Ceremonial Disaster: A thunderous sneeze interrupts a solemn
    speech. The speaker attempts to continue while everyone snickers.
S2. The Ruined Feast: (Name1) sneezes directly over a freshly prepared
    communal dish. The cook discards the affected portion and starts
    again, grumbling the entire time. Small food loss.
S3. The Starch Cloud: A sneeze sends finely ground starch into the air,
    leaving two respected elders looking like pale statues.
S4. The Interrupted Announcement: A speaker's dramatic revelation is
    drowned out. Nobody agrees what was said.
S5. The Chain Reaction: A startled villager drops a basket; a nearby
    animal bolts; a clay pot tips over. The entire sequence takes
    seconds and becomes a favorite village story.
S6. The Omen: Superstitious witnesses insist the sneeze was a warning.
    A calm researcher offers an ordinary explanation.
S7. The Lucky Interruption: The sneeze makes a healer look twice at
    a mislabeled basket, preventing a minor mistake.

EVENT V23-3 — THE STRANGE COINCIDENCE
A coconut falls exactly as an elder mentions falling fruit. A bird
calls when a child predicts a bird call. A fire goes out when a
storyteller speaks of darkness. Some laugh; others are unsettled.

CHOICE A — LAUGH IT OFF
A1. Nothing Unusual: Everyone enjoys the coincidence and returns to work.
A2. The Skeptic's Embarrassment: A researcher dismisses the stories just
    as another extraordinary coincidence occurs.
A3. The Child's Explanation: A child discovers a loose branch, familiar
    bird calls, and a gust of wind. The mystery dissolves.

CHOICE B — INVESTIGATE
B1. The Hidden Prankster: Someone carefully arranged the occurrences.
B2. The Natural Explanation: Wind and animal behavior account for them.
B3. The Unanswered Mystery: All but one incident can be explained.
B4. The Unexpected Discovery: Investigators find an overlooked spring,
    cave opening, or useful plant. Small Tech reward.

CHOICE C — TREAT IT AS A WARNING
C1. False Alarm: The villagers prepare for disaster; nothing happens.
C2. Lucky Warning: Precautions accidentally protect stored food from
    an unrelated heavy rain.
C3. The Great Panic: Rumors grow until someone insists Isola itself
    will vanish by sunset. It does not.
C4. The Prophecy Fulfilled: Exceptionally rarely, an actual strange
    phenomenon occurs. The researchers record it without claiming
    to understand it.

EVENT V23-4 — THE MISSING COCONUTS
Several coconuts disappear from the food stores. (Name1) suspects theft.
A. Question witnesses.
 A1. A child hid them for a treasure hunt and returns them apologetically.
 A2. An innocent villager is accused, then publicly cleared.
 A3. A witness remembers that the coconuts were moved for a feast.
B. Search the island.
 B1. An animal carried several coconuts into the undergrowth.
 B2. A forgotten storage basket is discovered behind a hut.
 B3. The coconuts appear arranged in a mysterious spiral.
C. Set a harmless watch.
 C1. The watchers catch friendly pranksters and demand they return them.
 C2. The watchers fall asleep; the coconuts reappear anyway.
 C3. The watch becomes a village game and everyone forgets the mystery.

EVENT V23-5 — THE FALSE ALARM
(Name1) races into the village shouting, "SOMETHING TERRIBLE IS HAPPENING!"
A. Investigate.
 A1. The 'monster' is a fallen tree silhouetted against the sky.
 A2. The 'ghostly voice' is wind passing through hollow bamboo.
 A3. The 'volcanic cloud' is dust kicked up by running villagers.
 A4. A real fallen branch threatens a path; the villagers clear it.
B. Sound the alarm.
 B1. Everyone abandons work and runs about in confusion.
 B2. The emergency response is impressively orderly.
 B3. An elder demands an explanation and receives a sheepish apology.
 B4. The alarm exposes an unrelated prank.
C. Ask for details first.
 C1. The misunderstanding is resolved before panic spreads.
 C2. (Name1) grows increasingly embarrassed while explaining.
 C3. The explanation sounds more implausible than the warning.
 C4. Something genuinely extraordinary is found, very rarely.

EVENT V23-6 — THE PRANK WAR
(Name1) and (Name2) have exchanged harmless jokes for days. Now each
is determined to have the last laugh.
A. Declare a winner.
 A1. One admits defeat graciously and congratulates the other.
 A2. The reluctant elder judge announces a tie and flees.
 A3. Both competitors are caught by their own tricks.
B. Escalate with another harmless joke.
 B1. The entire village laughs at a brilliantly timed surprise.
 B2. The joke fails spectacularly and the pranksters are embarrassed.
 B3. A valuable clay vessel is broken; the pranksters repair or replace it.
 B4. An elaborate trick accidentally helps solve a practical problem.
C. End the rivalry.
 C1. The two arrange a joyful communal celebration.
 C2. Both apologize to anyone inconvenienced.
 C3. The rivalry becomes an annual friendly tradition with clear rules.

ADDITIONAL SHORT-TITLE EVENT SEEDS
THE FALSE PROPHECY — An ordinary coincidence is mistaken for a warning.
THE VANISHING BASKET — A misplaced basket causes wild speculation.
THE SUSPICIOUS SHADOW — A moving shadow sparks a supernatural rumor.
THE GREAT MIX-UP — Similar-looking baskets swap owners at a feast.
THE UNFORTUNATE TIMING — An innocent interruption ruins a grand reveal.

CROSS-GAME NOTES
VV1: Golden Child phenomena may be genuinely magical, but ordinary
pranks remain ordinary. VV2: Lost Children may play gentle jokes
and learn to apologize. VV3: The Tribal Chief may judge disputes;
Nature and Magic practitioners may interpret a mystery differently.
VV4: Pranks must not damage the Tree of Life. VV5: Mischief may spark
misunderstandings between villagers and Heathens/Isolans, but both
groups can prank, investigate, laugh, apologize, and reconcile.

PARENTING AND AGE-APPROPRIATE LANGUAGE
Use "father" in ordinary prose, not "nursing father" (a technical
game designation). Mothers may nurse infants. Parenting skill is
available to any designated caregiver, including unrelated guardians.
All child-centered humor must remain wholesome, never creepy.
The child may learn correctly, misunderstand comically, or become
mischievous, but remains capable of learning and growing. Preserve
the parenting outcomes and skill rules already in the manuscript.

END OF V23 ADDITION

================================================================================
V24 ADDITION — THE REVOLUTIONARY INVENTION / THE REVOLUTIONARY DISCOVERY
================================================================================
CREDITS
Original premise, example discoveries, randomized social/physical consequences,
and context-sensitive title direction: Lorsieab2.
Event dialogue, additional examples, choice trees, sequel mechanics and prose:
ChatGPT. Preserve all preceding credits.

APPLICABILITY AND TITLE RULE
Available in VV1–VV5. Select exactly one of these event titles each time:
  THE REVOLUTIONARY INVENTION — a villager deliberately makes, develops,
  designs, composes, devises, or introduces something new.
  THE REVOLUTIONARY DISCOVERY — a villager observes, uncovers, recognizes,
  proves, finds, or explains something that already exists.
If a villager first discovers a phenomenon and then builds a device to use
it, choose the title according to the central action of that particular
trigger. Neither title is a placeholder for the other.

The everyday village remains an ancient, tropical, Polynesian-inspired
island society. Ingredients, tools, materials, and villagers' vocabulary
should reflect that setting. Deliberately impossible or anachronistic
breakthroughs are rare exceptions. Villagers do not casually know modern
names for unfamiliar objects. These events may involve an object, fluid,
gas, light, a fleeting phenomenon, a mathematical method, a performance,
a social convention, or a purely abstract idea. The event is not limited
to something solid that can be held.

OPENING — THE REVOLUTIONARY INVENTION
For days, (Name1) has been experimenting with a peculiar idea. Now
(he/she) has summoned the villagers to witness the result. A woven mat
covers the work, and a nervous apprentice stands nearby holding a gourd
of water in case something goes wrong.

"This," announces (Name1), "may change everything."

An elder asks whether it will at least be useful.
(Name1) hesitates. "That remains to be seen."

OPENING — THE REVOLUTIONARY DISCOVERY
While working near the [beach / forest / garden / waterfall / fire pit /
research area / night sky], (Name1) notices something nobody in the
village has ever described before. After repeated observations, (he/she)
concludes that it may change the way Isola understands the world.

The researchers demand proof. The elders demand an explanation.
A child asks whether the discovery is edible.

RANDOM SUBJECT TABLE — CHOOSE ONE OR COMBINE COMPATIBLE ENTRIES

FOOD AND COOKING
01. TWO LAYERS OF BREAD WITH A COOKED MEAT FILLING — A flat round of
    seasoned meat between two soft pieces of bread, with leaves or
    relish if available. The villagers debate whether it is a meal
    or merely an improperly assembled loaf. (Hamburger-like.)
02. THINLY CUT GARDEN LEAVES AND VEGETABLES IN A TANGY DRESSING —
    The cook claims raw plants can be an entire dish. Some are delighted;
    others demand to know when the actual food will arrive. (Salad-like.)
03. FLAT ROUND CAKES — A cook presses dough into circles and cooks it
    on a heated stone. It may be soft, crisp, thick, thin, sweet, or
    folded around other food. (Pancake/tortilla-like.)
04. CRUNCHY FOLDED SHELLS — Thin cooked cakes are bent while warm and
    filled with meat, beans, fish, or vegetables. The first taster
    cracks the shell and showers the ground with dinner. (Taco-like.)
05. THE BUBBLING DRINK — A sealed or naturally effervescent drink
    releases startling bubbles. A child declares that the drink is
    trying to escape. (Soda-like; use fictional harmless preparation.)
06. THE DELICATE FISH PLATE — Exceptionally fresh fish is cut into
    elegant thin pieces and arranged with painstaking care. A hungry
    villager asks why the cook has forgotten the fire. (Sashimi-like.)
07. SWEETENED SNOW OR ICE — Rare ice or snow obtained by a fantastic
    accident, a high mountain, or magical intervention is mixed with
    fruit or sweetness. It melts before half the judges can taste it.
    (Sorbet/ice-cream-like; ordinary tropical Isola has no casual snow.)
08. A NEW BREADFRUIT PREPARATION — The fruit is roasted, pounded,
    steamed, or shaped in an unprecedented way. Some call it genius;
    others say it is still just breadfruit.

SCIENCE, MEDICINE, AND NATURAL PHENOMENA
09. A COLOR-CHANGING LIQUID — A flower extract shifts color when
    mixed with different substances. Scholars investigate what the
    change signifies; a prankster wants to color everyone's soup.
10. A GAS THAT MAKES A STRANGE SOUND — A sealed vessel produces a
    peculiar hiss when opened. The inventor is more impressed than
    the villagers, who insist the vessel is simply rude.
11. AN UNUSUAL CRYSTAL — A transparent mineral bends light in an
    unexpected way, leading to optical experiments and wild rumors.
12. A NEW HEALING PREPARATION — A healer proposes an unfamiliar
    remedy. Responsible tests may show promise, no benefit, or harmful
    effects; do not automatically claim it cures every illness.
13. A NEW RULE OF NATURE — (Name1) identifies a repeatable pattern
    in water, falling fruit, shadows, plants, or tides. It is a
    discovery even if nothing physical has been manufactured.
14. A FLOATING BALL OF LIGHT — A rare, explicitly impossible or
    magical phenomenon appears over a bowl of water or carved stone.
    Scholars cannot decide whether to study it or run away.

ASTRONOMY, MATHEMATICS, AND ENGINEERING
15. THE STAR-TRACKING GEARS — Interlocking carved wheels and markers
    model the movement of stars or planets. The mechanism may help
    with calendars or navigation, or jam during its unveiling.
16. THE COUNTING WHEELS — A system of pebbles, notches, cords, or
    rotating disks helps perform arithmetic. A master scholar praises
    it; a child computes the answer faster in (his/her) head.
17. THE MEMORY STONE — A strangely responsive slab seems to hold
    symbols, sounds, or recollections. It is deliberately anachronistic
    or supernatural, not ordinary village technology. Some fear it
    contains a spirit; others call it the greatest archive imaginable.
18. THE WATER-LIFTING MACHINE — A wooden and woven-fiber mechanism
    raises water from a lower pool to a garden. It may work beautifully
    or soak its inventor and everyone watching.
19. THE UNLIKELY BRIDGE — An unusual arrangement of timber, vines,
    and stone supports surprising weight, until an overlooked knot
    becomes the most important detail in the village.
20. THE NEW GEOMETRY — A researcher proves a surprising relationship
    among shapes using shells, cords, and marks in the sand.
21. THE BETTER CALENDAR — A villager finds a more reliable way to
    anticipate seasons, tides, or astronomical events.

TRADE, FINANCE, AND IDEAS
22. THE SHELL ACCOUNTING SYSTEM — A new method records exchanges,
    debts, food shares, and communal supplies with shells or knots.
    Merchants rejoice; others complain that every coconut now has
    paperwork, though no paper is involved.
23. THE FAIR EXCHANGE RULE — A villager proposes a new principle for
    barter. It may reduce arguments or start the longest argument yet.
24. THE IMPOSSIBLE PROOF — A philosopher offers a startling argument
    that overturns a familiar assumption. The village debates it for
    days; the fisherman keeps fishing.
25. THE SHARED WORK PLAN — A new arrangement of tasks makes building
    and harvesting more efficient, unless nobody agrees who starts.

ART, CULTURE, AND FADS
26. THE NEW DANCE — (Name1) invents a peculiar sequence of steps,
    turns, and claps. Within days, everyone may be doing it, including
    elders who insist they disapprove.
27. THE NEW CLOTHING STYLE — A fresh way to weave, dye, tie, or drape
    island fabrics catches attention. Some adore it; others say it
    looks like someone got tangled in a fishing net.
28. THE NEW HAIRSTYLE — A striking braid, ornament, or arrangement
    becomes a fashion sensation—or a brief embarrassment.
29. THE NEW MUSIC — A rhythmic pattern, song form, or unfamiliar
    instrument captures the village's imagination.
30. THE NEW GAME — (Name1) invents a contest using shells, stones,
    sticks, or woven balls. The rules become more controversial than
    the game itself.
31. THE NEW GREETING — A greeting gesture or phrase spreads rapidly,
    then causes confusion when visitors do not recognize it.
32. THE FAD OF THE WEEK — An absurd but harmless habit takes over the
    village: matching shell necklaces, balancing coconuts, dramatic
    bows, or elaborate names for ordinary chores.

CHOICES — BOTH EVENT TITLES USE THESE, WITH APPROPRIATE WORDING

A. PRESENT IT TO THE WHOLE VILLAGE
 A1 — THE SENSATION: The crowd erupts in cheers. Within days nearly
      everyone adopts the invention or celebrates the discovery.
      (Name1) gains a celebrated reputation and relevant skill.
 A2 — THE LAUGHINGSTOCK: The demonstration fails hilariously.
      (Name1) is mocked, then receives an apology if later vindicated.
 A3 — THE SHRUG: Nobody is particularly interested. The crowd
      disperses and the invention is forgotten for now.
 A4 — THE HERO: The innovation solves a longstanding village problem.
      (Name1) receives an honor and substantial relevant skill gains.
 A5 — THE OUTRAGE: Villagers denounce the novelty as ridiculous,
      improper, wasteful, or alarming. A public debate follows.
 A6 — THE NEW FAD: Everyone imitates it enthusiastically, but the
      enthusiasm may disappear after a few days.

B. SUBMIT IT TO THE RESEARCHERS
 B1 — THE MASTER SCHOLAR: The idea withstands scrutiny; (Name1) is
      invited to become one of the island's leading scholars.
 B2 — THE UNEXPECTED FLAW: An apprentice spots a serious problem.
      The invention is revised, or the discovery is reinterpreted.
 B3 — THE SCHOLARLY FEUD: Two researchers claim credit for the
      breakthrough. The elders must settle the dispute.
 B4 — THE IMPOSSIBLE RESULT: Repeated tests confirm something that
      seems to defy ordinary explanation. A rare mystery begins.
 B5 — THE FALSE DISCOVERY: A careful experiment disproves the claim.
      (Name1) is disappointed but gains knowledge from the attempt.
 B6 — THE ACCIDENTAL ADVANCE: The original idea is useless, but
      the failed experiment reveals something genuinely valuable.

C. TRY TO IMPROVE OR REPEAT IT
 C1 — THE PERFECTED DESIGN: A flaw is corrected; the invention
      becomes reliable or the discovery becomes reproducible.
 C2 — THE SHATTERING: A brittle model cracks into countless pieces.
      The inventor survives, embarrassed and covered in fragments.
 C3 — THE EXPLOSION: A rare fictional experimental mishap produces
      a startling bang and destroys the demonstration apparatus.
      Nearby villagers retreat; damaged equipment must be replaced.
 C4 — THE DISAPPEARANCE: The object or phenomenon vanishes before
      the scholars can document it. Nobody believes the witnesses.
 C5 — THE DISINTEGRATION: An exceptionally rare impossible device
      crumbles into dust, leaving no usable remains.
 C6 — THE SECOND MIRACLE: Repetition works even better than the
      first attempt. The village celebrates a genuine breakthrough.
 C7 — THE SMALL IMPROVEMENT: The second version is less spectacular
      but more useful. (Name1) learns the value of patient work.

D. KEEP IT SECRET OR SHARE IT WITH ONE PERSON
 D1 — THE HIDDEN GENIUS: The invention quietly improves the lives
      of a few villagers before the rest learn of it.
 D2 — THE STOLEN CREDIT: A rival unveils the same idea first.
      A witness or notebook may eventually establish the truth.
 D3 — THE TRUSTED APPRENTICE: A young learner improves the design;
      both are credited and gain relevant skill.
 D4 — THE LOST IDEA: The only record is damaged or misplaced.
      The innovation vanishes from village memory.
 D5 — THE RELUCTANT CELEBRITY: The secret leaks and (Name1) becomes
      famous despite desperately wishing to be left alone.
 D6 — THE DANGEROUS RUMOR: Villagers mistake secrecy for evidence
      of sorcery or dealings with outsiders. An elder investigates.

E. OFFER IT TO THE ELDERS FOR A VILLAGE DECISION
 E1 — OFFICIAL ADOPTION: The elders approve practical use, training,
      or preservation of the discovery in the village records.
 E2 — THE PROHIBITION: The elders ban it, fearing waste, conflict,
      or harm. Some villagers continue debating the decision.
 E3 — THE LONG DEBATE: The council argues until sunset and reaches
      no conclusion. (Name1) falls asleep during the final speech.
 E4 — THE PUBLIC TEST: A fair demonstration settles the question,
      though the outcome may be triumph, embarrassment, or mystery.
 E5 — THE COMPROMISE: Limited use is permitted while researchers
      continue investigating; reputations remain divided.

CROSS-CUTTING REACTION TABLE — APPLY AFTER ANY COMPATIBLE OUTCOME
 R1. EVERYONE ADOPTS IT: The innovation becomes part of village life.
 R2. EVERYONE SHUNS IT: The idea becomes a cautionary tale.
 R3. THE INVENTOR IS CALLED MAD: The villagers dismiss (Name1),
     possibly until a later event proves (him/her) right.
 R4. COMPLETE INDIFFERENCE: Villagers resume their daily chores.
 R5. HERO'S WELCOME: A celebration honors (Name1).
 R6. CHIEF SCHOLAR: (Name1) gains a high scholarly reputation, if
     appropriate to the game; this is an academic distinction, not
     an automatic claim to VV3's Tribal Chief position.
 R7. IMITATION CRAZE: Copycat inventions appear everywhere.
 R8. ACCUSATION OF TRICKERY: A hearing clears or censures (Name1).
 R9. RIVALRY: A second inventor attempts to outdo the first.
 R10. GENERATIONAL LEGACY: A later villager studies the surviving
      plans, memories, or records and builds on the idea.

RARE LONG-TERM FOLLOW-UPS
 L1 — THE FORGOTTEN INVENTOR: Years later, (Name2) recreates an old
      invention and becomes famous. A surviving witness remembers
      (Name1), prompting a dispute over who deserves credit.
 L2 — THE DISCOVERY REDISCOVERED: A later researcher finally
      understands an observation once dismissed as nonsense.
 L3 — THE FAD THAT NEVER ENDED: A once-ridiculous fashion, dance,
      or greeting becomes a beloved local tradition.
 L4 — THE FAD THAT VANISHED: Nobody remembers why everyone once
      wore three shells on one wrist. A child finds an old portrait.
 L5 — THE DANGEROUS SUCCESS: The invention works, but the village
      discovers that success brings new responsibilities or problems.
 L6 — THE APPRENTICE'S IMPROVEMENT: A younger villager makes the
      original idea simpler, safer, or more useful; both receive credit.
 L7 — THE SHATTERED LEGACY: The only surviving model breaks; the
      village must decide whether to reconstruct it from memory.
 L8 — THE INVENTOR WHO WAS RIGHT: A previously ridiculed villager
      is publicly vindicated and receives an apology.

GAME-SPECIFIC VARIANTS
VV1 — The Golden Child may understand an impossible principle
      without being able to explain how; adults still debate it.
VV2 — The Lost Children may contribute unusual memories or clever
      improvements, and Great Falls can inspire water inventions.
VV3 — The Tribal Chief may arbitrate competing claims. Nature and
      Magic practitioners may offer different interpretations.
VV4 — Discoveries may involve the Tree of Life, but ordinary
      experiments must not casually damage its sacred roots.
VV5 — Villagers and Heathens/Isolans may compete, collaborate, or
      accuse each other of stealing an idea. Joint recognition,
      apology, and reconciliation are possible outcomes.

MECHANICS AND CONTINUITY
- Choose a subject, determine INVENTION versus DISCOVERY, then select
  the matching title. Never use a combined slash title in the game UI.
- Apply one principal branch and, where compatible, one reaction or
  physical mishap. Avoid contradictory outcomes (e.g., an object both
  fully destroyed and still used daily unless rebuilt).
- Match rewards to the subject: cooking, research, healing, building,
  parenting, or other existing skill systems; add Tech or Food only
  where the story justifies it. A dance fad is not automatically a
  scientific breakthrough.
- Track inventor/discoverer, subject, acclaim, rejection, records,
  destruction, and rediscovery for later sequels.
- Keep accidents non-graphic and appropriate for all ages.
- If a culinary idea resembles a familiar modern dish, its Isolan
  creator has still invented it independently. Avoid casually
  attributing its historical real-world origin to ancient Isola.

END OF V24 ADDITION

================================================================================
V26 — THE MATERIALS OF ISOLA: EXPANDED VARIETY RULES
================================================================================
Original creative direction and material suggestions: Lorsieab2
Expansion and event-writing implementation: ChatGPT (OpenAI)

This module applies to existing and future events involving food, strange stews,
potions, remedies, science, inventions, architecture, clothing, art, and trade.
It supplements the event text without changing any established character,
choice, outcome, title, author credit, or story continuity.

MATERIAL SELECTION RULES
- Do not default repeatedly to slugs, snail shells, or decaying timber.
- Select ingredients by purpose, setting, availability, and tone. Choose a
  different combination for different events, even when their mechanics match.
- Distinguish edible ingredients from decorative, industrial, medicinal,
  ritual, and hazardous materials. Nonfood substances are never ordinary
  ingredients in edible dishes. Strange or contaminated stews may be rejected,
  discarded, or used as cautionary comedy rather than served.
- A character may experiment with a dangerous material in fiction without
  the narrative supplying a real-world preparation method.
- Snow, glass, rubber, metal, paper, and unusual reagents may be rare,
  imported, magically discovered, or deliberately anachronistic; the event
  must acknowledge that when it matters.
- Keep inventions and discoveries surprising. Avoid using the same three
  ingredients as the punchline in multiple consecutive events.

FOOD, DRINKS, AND CULINARY DISCOVERIES
Breadfruit flour; grated taro; mashed yam; roasted sweet potato; green
banana; ripe banana; coconut milk; coconut cream; coconut water; coconut
flesh; shredded coconut; palm sugar; wild honey; sugarcane syrup; fruit
juice; fermented fruit; sour citrus; guava; papaya; mango; starfruit;
passionfruit; sea grapes; seaweed; edible flowers; fragrant leaves;
banana leaves used as wrappers; fresh herbs; toasted seeds; ground nuts;
rice where available; edible roots; wild mushrooms identified as safe;
fish; smoked fish; shellfish; sea salt; brine; ocean water used only in
appropriate cooking processes; river water made safe for drinking; and
crushed ice or snow in exceptional settings.

Sample combinations:
* Sweet breadfruit cakes wrapped in banana leaves with guava syrup.
* Roasted taro with coconut cream, herbs, and a pinch of sea salt.
* A chilled fruit drink made from papaya, citrus, and crushed ice.
* Smoked fish with sea grapes, toasted seeds, and fragrant leaves.
* A tart fermented-fruit sauce that the entire village debates.
* Thin breadfruit crisps served with a savory herb-and-coconut dip.
* A layered sweet made with mashed yam, fruit pulp, and wild honey.
* A bubbling beverage that is either a triumph or a sticky disaster.

HEALERS, HOSPITALS, AND STRANGE STEWS
Clean cloth; ripped-up cloth for nonsterile crafts; fresh bandages; woven
grass splints; smooth bamboo; boiled water; clean river water; sea salt;
medicinal herbs; tree sap; resin; wax; clay jars; ceramic bowls; ground
mineral pigments; plaster for casts or repairs; healing potions; previously
brewed remedies; strange stews; alchemical reagents; white powder of
unknown identity; smelly salts; sulfur; fish oil; plant oils; aromatic
flowers; moss; feathers used as tools; and preserved hospital supplies.

Sample fictional outcomes:
* A plaster-and-bamboo support is surprisingly comfortable.
* A carefully labeled herbal remedy becomes the hospital's pride.
* A mislabeled white powder forces the healer to discard a batch.
* A pungent salt jar clears the room before anyone tests it.
* A sticky resin dressing inspires a better storage container.
* An experimental stew smells so awful that nobody volunteers to taste it.

BUILDING, ENGINEERING, AND CRAFT
Fallen timber; driftwood; seasoned hardwood; flexible branches; bamboo;
palm fronds; woven reeds; woven grass; coconut shells; coconut fibers;
coir rope; barkcloth; ripped-up cloth; animal skins; animal hides; animal
leather; animal fur; feathers; fish scales; lizard scales; shells;
pebbles; crushed rocks; gravel; dirt; mud; river clay; wet sand; dry
sand; limestone; plaster; resin; tree sap; rubber; natural latex;
charcoal; ash; glass; sea glass; metal; copper; bronze; iron; stone
blades; polished stones; paper; woven mats; pigments; and carved bone.

Sample combinations:
* Fallen timber beams tied with coconut-fiber rope.
* A rainproof roof of overlapping palm fronds and resin-sealed reeds.
* A flexible bridge of bamboo, woven grass, and braided coir.
* A decorative wall of river stones, mud plaster, and sea glass.
* A wind-driven mechanism using wood, leather straps, and metal gears.
* A waterproof basket woven from reeds and sealed with tree sap.
* A paper-and-bamboo star chart protected in a carved wooden case.
* A springy sandal made with rubber, woven cloth, and soft leather.

ALCHEMY, NATURAL HISTORY, AND PECULIAR SPECIMENS
Sulfur; volcanic ash; mineral crystals; powdered limestone; white
powder; smelly salts; alchemical reagents; resin; rubber; ambergris;
rafflesia musk; fermented fruit; rotting meat; dung; pig sweat; animal
entrails; animal skins; animal fur; lizard scales; fish scales; shed
feathers; firefly wings; butterfly chrysalises; insect bits; grubs;
worms; empty cocoons; river sediment; ocean salt; sand; glass; pebbles;
crushed rocks; tree sap; plant dyes; strange stews; and unidentified
bottles from forgotten ruins.

These are fictional specimen and material options, not an invitation to
put all of them into food or medicine. A researcher might catalogue them,
compare their odors, study their textures, preserve them, or mistakenly
confuse one specimen with another. A ridiculous invention may involve
them without implying that it is useful or safe.

CLOTHING, ART, RITUAL, AND SOCIAL FADS
Barkcloth; paper; dyed cloth; torn cloth; woven grass; woven reeds;
palm fronds; banana leaves; coconut fibers; polished coconut shells;
feathers; animal fur; hides; leather; fish scales; lizard scales;
shell beads; sea glass; colored sand; clay pigments; mineral powder;
charcoal ink; flower petals; fragrant herbs; resin; carved wood;
hammered metal ornaments; and polished pebbles.

Sample combinations:
* A ceremonial cape of dyed barkcloth and iridescent fish scales.
* A fashionable hat woven from palm fronds and bright feathers.
* A mosaic portrait made of sea glass, sand, and colored pebbles.
* A ridiculous new dance performed in jingling coconut-shell anklets.
* A paper-and-reed lantern festival that becomes a village tradition.
* A leather-and-feather costume that everyone either loves or mocks.

REVOLUTIONARY INVENTION / REVOLUTIONARY DISCOVERY INTEGRATION
When either event is rolled, choose the subject FIRST and draw materials
from the appropriate bank, not from one generic list. A new food should
feature plausible foodstuffs; a bridge should feature structural
materials; a strange chemical discovery can use rare specimens; a
clothing craze should emphasize fibers, dyes, and ornament. Let the
specific materials shape the accident, public reaction, and legacy.

Examples:
* A glass lens framed in bronze reveals tiny creatures in pond water.
* A clay vessel, sea salt, and an unusual cooling technique produce
  a surprising new way to preserve food.
* A machine built from driftwood, rubber, and metal springs leaps
  across the workshop and is declared either genius or a menace.
* An artist's layered paper and mineral pigments create a new
  technique for recording maps and stories.
* A curious scholar notices patterns in firefly flashes and begins
  recording observations on barkcloth.
* A tailor invents a spectacularly impractical feathered cape that
  becomes the season's most copied fashion.

END OF V26 MATERIALS REVISION
